# Pāli: A Workbook

Version 1.2 · 17 November 2026, for use with the same version of the grammar.

This is the companion volume to *Pāli: A Reference Grammar*. It carries the drills and their worked answers, one set per chapter, in the grammar's order. Nothing here introduces a rule that the grammar does not already state; if an exercise seems to require something you have not met, the chapter it belongs to is where to look.

The two volumes are numbered alike: the exercises for chapter 6 of the grammar are "Exercises: Numerals" here, and the worked answers for them are "Answers: Numerals" at the back. Nothing needs cross-referencing beyond the chapter's name.

Read the answer only after attempting the exercise. The point is practical, not moral: the answers are discursive on purpose, and several of them turn on a distinction that only becomes visible once you have got it wrong. An answer read cold teaches a fact. An answer read after an attempt teaches the reason the fact is worth having.

Every Pāli sentence in this volume is canonical, and cited. None has been composed for the exercise. That constraint has a cost, since a drill made of real sentences cannot isolate one grammatical point as cleanly as an invented one, and it is worth paying, because a sentence you parse here is a sentence of the Buddha's word that you have read, not a demonstration of one.

The answers give a word-by-word gloss, an idiomatic rendering, and then a short discussion naming the classes at work. In print the gloss is set as the grammar sets it, each Pāli word above its English and its grammar tag; the grammar's "How to Use This Book" explains the layout. Where an exercise reuses a sentence from the grammar's exposition, the answer says so: meeting a sentence again with no surrounding prose to lean on is different work from meeting it the first time, and it is deliberate.

## How a set is arranged

Each set runs in four stages, and the four come from the canon. A monk masters the teaching, and four things are then said of what he has mastered: it is *sotānugata*, "followed by ear"; *vacasā paricita*, "rehearsed aloud"; *manasānupekkhita*, "gone over in the mind"; and *diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdha*, "well penetrated by view" (AN 4.191 — A ii 185). These are four operations, not four intensities of one: exposure, recitation, recall without the text, and analysis, and the order is theirs, not mine. Understanding comes fourth. The grammar's "How to study a classical language" prints the passage in full with its gloss and argues the point; what the formula does here is decide what a set asks first and what it asks last.

| Stage | What its items ask | Difficulty |
|---|---|---|
| ***Sotānugata*** — met by ear | The line is given, with its gloss. Read it aloud, mark the long vowels and the doubled consonants, and say what the marked form is. | easy |
| ***Vacasā paricitā*** — rehearsed aloud | Recite from the Pāli; then rebuild the line from the gloss alone. | easy to moderate |
| ***Manasānupekkhitā*** — gone over in the mind | Text covered. Recall the cell, the form, the rule — including from chapters already finished. | moderate |
| ***Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā*** — penetrated by view | Adjudicate an ambiguity, split a compound, decide what the evidence licenses, put English back into Pāli. | difficult |

Nothing is withheld in stage 1, so an item there cannot be got wrong for want of information, by design: the first meeting with a sentence is a meeting and not a test. Stage 2 is aloud throughout. Vowel length and the doubled consonants are half the morphology of this language, and on the page they are a single mark. *Bhikkhunā* and *bhikkhūnaṁ* are the instrumental singular and the genitive plural of one word, they each turn up a few hundred times in the four Nikāyas, and what separates them is one long vowel. Said aloud, they are not the same word at all.

Stage 3 holds the short form-drills, the ones that give an ending and ask what it is, and also the items reaching back to earlier chapters. In stage 4 the set stops being a drill. At least one of its items asks you to produce Pāli instead of parsing it, a harder task and a different one.

The sub-headings inside the stages (*Recall*, *Identify*, *Split the compound*, *Read at length*) say what each small group of items does. They are the same group names the mature sets have always used.

The difficulty ordering is measured, not felt. A drill is hard or easy largely by how often its reader has already met the words it turns on, and that is a countable thing: across the four Nikāyas, the five hundred commonest word-forms account for half of all running words, and the ten thousand commonest for a little under seven-eighths. Stage 1 works with forms from the head of that curve, stage 4 from well down it. The count cannot show whether the *rule* an item drills is difficult, and that stays a judgement.

## Coming back

Every set ends with a short **Coming back** list, naming which of its items to re-attempt after three days, after ten, and after thirty.

The reason is the best-attested finding in the literature on how adults study, and it contradicts what most people actually do. Of ten study techniques reviewed by Dunlosky and colleagues, only two were rated of high utility: testing yourself, and spreading the sessions out. A later synthesis of 242 studies and 169,179 participants ranked the same two first again, and found that the techniques learners actually prefer, rereading and highlighting, are among the least effective of the ten.[^wb-spacing] Retrieval beats re-study by about half a standard deviation.[^wb-retrieval] So a set worked through once on the day the chapter was read has been enjoyed, not learned.

The three intervals are a practical choice and not a finding. What the evidence supports is that the sessions be separated at all, with the gaps lengthening as the material holds; three, ten and thirty days is one workable implementation of that and there is nothing sacred in the numbers. Missing one is not a failure. Doing the whole set again in one sitting the night before you want it is.

The tradition arrived here first, and by a different road. *Sajjhāya*, "recitation, rehearsal", was the technique that carried this canon for the centuries before it was written down, and it consists of recall from memory, daily, sustained over years: retrieval and spacing at once. The grammar makes the same observation and adds the caution that goes with it, which holds here too: nobody in that tradition was doing cognitive psychology, and that the practice turns out to be well designed is a happy accident, not an argument.

## What the method rests on

A workbook that grades its exercises and schedules their review owes the reader an account of where the grading and the schedule came from. This is that account, briefly; the grammar's chapter carries the evidence in full.

Four considerations shaped this volume. **The stages are canonical**: AN 4.191, quoted above, read as four operations in a fixed order. **Retrieval and spacing** are why the answers sit in a separate half of the book, not beneath each question, and why every set schedules its own review.[^wb-spacing] [^wb-retrieval] **Production** is why each set now ends by asking for Pāli, not about it: Swain's work on French-immersion learners found that thousands of hours of comprehensible input still left persistent gaps in grammar, which is a finding about what mere reading does not do.[^wb-output] And **volume of reading** is why the drills are not the destination. Two meta-analyses of extensive reading report medium to large gains in comprehension, rate and vocabulary, with adults gaining more than children; the practical consequence is that Appendix B's reading pathway should be begun when Part IV is done and not when the book is finished.[^wb-reading] Nation's threshold of about 98% of running words known is why that pathway is graded, and why the sets here are too.[^wb-coverage]

None of this is imported wholesale. The four-stage spine is the canon's, the measurements behind the grading are of the canon, and the modern literature does one job: it says which of the several defensible ways of arranging a workbook is likely to reward the hours you put into it.

## Exercises: Script and Pronunciation

Nothing in this chapter can be learned silently. Every item below is meant to be **said aloud** before it is written down, and several of them cannot be answered any other way.

The four stages run in the order the chapter argues in. First, three lines given complete, weights and all, to be heard, not worked out. Then two to recite and then write down, because the diacritics are learned by the hand as much as by the eye. Then the letters and the vowels, from memory: how many letters a word has, which is a different question from how many characters are printed, and whether a vowel is long, where the answer is often "it depends on what follows". Then whole lines, divided and weighed by you. Every Pāli line is canonical and cited; none has been composed. Worked answers are at the back.

### Sotānugata — script and pronunciation

Nothing is withheld. Each line below is printed twice: as it stands, and divided into syllables with the heavy ones in bold. These are three of the lines a Theravāda Buddhist hears most often in a lifetime, and the point of having them here is that you should hear the weights in them before you can name one.

1. Namo tassa bhagavato arahato sammāsambuddhassa. (MN 91 — M ii 140)

    na·**mo** **tas**·sa bha·ga·va·**to** a·ra·ha·**to** **sam·mā·sam·bud·dhas**·sa

    "Homage to him, the Blessed One, the perfected one, the fully awakened one."

    Say it three times at chanting pace, giving every heavy syllable about twice the length of a light one. Then, without looking, say where the light syllables run longest without a heavy one among them, and where the heavy ones do the same.

2. Suṇātu me, bhante, saṅgho. (Kd 1 — Vin i 95)

    su·**ṇā**·tu **me** **bhan**·**te** **saṅ**·**gho**

    *suṇātu* let it listen (3 sg. imper.) · *me* to me (dat./gen. sg.) · *bhante* venerable sirs (voc.) · *saṅgho* the Sangha (m. nom. sg.)

    "Let the Sangha listen to me, venerable sirs."

    This is the opening of every formal act of the Sangha. Say it aloud. Only two syllables in it are light; find them. Then say why the *e* of *bhante* is heavy although nothing follows it, and why *saṅgho* is written with *ṅ* and not *n*.

3. Sabbapāpassa akaraṇaṁ, kusalassa upasampadā; (Dhp 183 — Dhp p. 27)

    **sab**·ba·**pā**·**pas**·sa a·ka·ra·**ṇaṁ**, ku·sa·**las**·sa u·pa·**sam**·pa·**dā**

    *sabbapāpassa* of all evil (n. gen. sg.) · *akaraṇaṁ* the not-doing (n. nom. sg.) · *kusalassa* of the wholesome (n. gen. sg.) · *upasampadā* the taking up (f. nom. sg.)

    "Not doing any evil; taking up what is wholesome."

    Say it aloud. Two syllables in this line are heavy for a reason that is not a long vowel and is not a doubled consonant. Find them, and say what makes each heavy.

### Vacasā paricitā — script and pronunciation

Recite until the line is fluent. Then close the book and **write it out**, and only then check what you wrote against the printed line, letter by letter. Count your own errors instead of correcting them silently: the count is the exercise.

4. Itipi so bhagavā arahaṁ sammāsambuddho vijjācaraṇasampanno sugato lokavidū anuttaro purisadammasārathi satthā devamanussānaṁ buddho bhagavā. (AN 6.10 — A iii 285)

    "Such is the Blessed One: perfected, fully awakened, accomplished in knowledge and conduct, holy, knower of the world, supreme guide for those fit to be trained, teacher of gods and humans, awakened, blessed."

    Recite it, then write it. Check every long vowel and every doubled consonant. Then say which of your errors you could have heard, had you been reciting properly, and which you could not.

5. Ayaṁ itthannāmo itthannāmassa āyasmato upasampadāpekkho. (Kd 1 — Vin i 95)

    *ayaṁ* this · *itthannāmo* so-and-so, one of such-and-such a name (m. nom. sg.) · *itthannāmassa āyasmato* of Venerable so-and-so (m. gen. sg.) · *upasampadāpekkho* seeking full ordination (m. nom. sg.)

    "This person, so-and-so, is seeking full ordination under Venerable so-and-so."

    Recite it, then write it from memory. This line has more traps in it than any other in the set, and they are all traps of the pen, not of the ear. When you have checked it, say how many doubled consonants and how many long vowels it holds.

### Manasānupekkhitā — script and pronunciation

Cover the chapter. These are answered from memory or not at all.

**How many letters?** For each word, say how many Pāli letters it has, and name any digraph.

6. *dukkha*
7. *amhi*
8. *saṅgha*
9. *daḷha*
10. *sammāsambuddho*

**Long or short?** For each form, say whether the marked vowel is long or short, and why.

11. the *e* of *bhante*
12. the *e* of *mettā*
13. the *o* of *soṇḍo*
14. the *a* of *arahaṁ*

### Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — script and pronunciation

**Split and weigh.** Divide each line into syllables, mark every heavy syllable, and say what makes each heavy one heavy. Nothing is printed for you here.

15. Manopubbaṅgamā dhammā, manoseṭṭhā manomayā; (Dhp 1 — Dhp p. 1)
16. …poriyā vācāya samannāgato vissaṭṭhāya anelagalāya atthassa viññāpaniyā… (DN 4 — D i 114)
17. Yassa nakkhamati, so bhāseyya. (Kd 1 — Vin i 95) — Divide and weigh it. Then two questions the line answers only if you say it: what is *nakkhamati* made of, and what happens to the *e* of *bhāseyya*? Say whether that second answer changes the weight of the syllable it is in.

Where the rules do not settle it.

18. Yassāyasmato khamati … so tuṇhassa; (Kd 1 — Vin i 95) — Take only the last two words. Divide *tuṇhassa*, and say how many letters it has. Then say what the chapter's list of undecidable clusters has to do with your answer, and whether anything here forces you to choose.

**Put the diacritics back.** Producing the marks is harder than reading them, and here the sitting-still work of this chapter shows.

19. Three words, stripped of everything above and below the line. Restore them, and then say which one you could not have restored from the pronunciation alone, and why.

    (a) sammasambuddhassa

    (b) sangho

    (c) anelagalaya

### Coming back — script and pronunciation

**Day 3.** Items 1–3 aloud, and 11–14. Vowel length is the first thing to go, and it goes unnoticed.

**Day 10.** Items 6–10 from memory, and item 15.

**Day 30.** Items 4 and 19. If the recollection still comes out with its long vowels intact, the chapter has done its work.

## Exercises: Sandhi

This chapter's skill is taking a written form apart, so most of what follows runs backwards from the printed word to its pieces. That is the direction a reader actually needs, and it is the harder one: forming a junction is a rule applied, undoing one is a hypothesis tested.

Two working cautions apply throughout. A division is only right if both pieces are words that occur elsewhere, the test the chapter draws from Geiger's phantom glide. And not every adjacent pair of vowels has been through anything, so "no junction here" is a legitimate answer and one of the items below wants it.

Every Pāli line is canonical and cited; none has been composed. Worked answers are at the back.

### Sotānugata — sandhi

Sandhi happens in the mouth before it happens on the page, so this stage is entirely aloud. Each item gives the joined form, its pieces and its meaning, and asks only that you say both and hear what the join is for. Say the pieces slowly, then say them at speaking speed. The junction is what speaking speed does.

The first three are the canon doing this exercise on itself: the Vinaya's word-analysis quotes a rule's word as the rule prints it, joined, and then takes it apart.

1. Yo panāti yo yādiso … Eso vuccati "yo panā"ti. (Bu Pj 1 — Vin iii 23)

    *yo panāti* < *yo* whoever + *pana* moreover + *iti* (the quotative) · *yādiso* of whatever kind · *eso* this · *vuccati* is called

    "'Whoever': whoever, of whatever kind … This is what is called 'whoever'."

    Say *yo pana iti*, slowly. Then say it at speed. Where did the long *ā* come from, and which of the two words does it belong to?

2. Bhikkhūti bhikkhakoti bhikkhu, (Bu Pj 1 — Vin iii 24)

    *bhikkhūti* < *bhikkhu* a monk + *iti* · *bhikkhakoti* < *bhikkhako* an almsman, a beggar + *iti* · *bhikkhu* a monk (m. nom. sg.)

    "'A monk': he is a monk because he lives on alms."

    The same quotative twice in five words, on two different vowels. Say each pair apart and then joined. One vowel lengthens and one does not. Say which, and then say why not.

3. "Parimaṇḍalaṁ nivāsessāmīti sikkhā karaṇīyā"ti. (Bu Sk 1 — Vin iv 185)

    *parimaṇḍalaṁ* evenly all round · *nivāsessāmīti* < *nivāsessāmi* I shall dress + *iti* · *sikkhā* the training · *karaṇīyā* to be done

    "'I shall dress evenly all round': this is the training to be done."

    A third vowel before the same quotative. Say *nivāsessāmi iti* and then the joined form. Then look up *nivāsessāmi* in your memory of the last three items and say what the three have in common.

4. Esevanto dukkhassā. (SN 12.51 — S ii 84)

    *esevanto* < *eso* this + *eva* just + *anto* end (m. nom. sg.) · *dukkhassā* of suffering (n. gen. sg.)

    "Just this is the end of suffering."

    Three words inside one. Say *eso eva anto* slowly, then quickly, until you hear it arrive at *esevanto* on its own. Then say how many vowels were lost and how many survived.

### Vacasā paricitā — sandhi

Recite until the line is fluent. Then cover the Pāli and rebuild it from the gloss alone, aloud, junctions and all. Rebuilding is the work.

5. …yena mithilā yena brahmāyu brāhmaṇo tenupasaṅkami; upasaṅkamitvā brahmāyuṁ brāhmaṇaṁ abhivādetvā ekamantaṁ nisīdi. (MN 91 — M ii 135)

    *yena* by which (m. inst. sg.) · *mithilā* Mithilā · *brahmāyu* Brahmāyu · *brāhmaṇo* the brahmin (m. nom. sg.) · *tenupasaṅkami* < *tena* by that + *upasaṅkami* he approached · *upasaṅkamitvā* having approached · *brahmāyuṁ* Brahmāyu (acc.) · *brāhmaṇaṁ* the brahmin (acc.) · *abhivādetvā* having bowed · *ekamantaṁ* to one side · *nisīdi* he sat down

    "…he went to Mithilā, to the brahmin Brahmāyu; and having approached, he bowed to the brahmin Brahmāyu and sat down to one side."

    This is the stock narrative formula for one person going to another, and you will meet it hundreds of times. Recite it until it is automatic, because the reading time it saves later is enormous.

6. Pātimokkhanti ādimetaṁ mukhametaṁ pamukhametaṁ kusalānaṁ dhammānaṁ. Tena vuccati pātimokkhanti. (Kd 2 — Vin i 103)

    *pātimokkhanti* < *pātimokkhaṁ* the Pātimokkha + *iti* · *ādimetaṁ* < *ādiṁ* beginning (acc.) + *etaṁ* this · *mukhametaṁ* < *mukhaṁ* face, foremost + *etaṁ* · *pamukhametaṁ* < *pamukhaṁ* chief + *etaṁ* · *kusalānaṁ dhammānaṁ* of wholesome states (m. gen. pl.)

    "'Pātimokkha': this is the beginning, this is the face, this is the fore of wholesome states. That is why it is called the Pātimokkha."

    Five junctions, and one niggahīta in every one of them, so this line is worth saying aloud, not just reading. Rebuild it from the gloss; then say which of the five comes out differently from the other four, and what decided it.

### Manasānupekkhitā — sandhi

Cover the chapter. These are answered from memory or not at all.

**Name the junction.** For each form, say which of the four cases it is: vowel + vowel, vowel + consonant, niggahīta + vowel, or niggahīta + consonant.

7. *natthi*
8. *ahampi*
9. *tañca*
10. *nappajānāti*

**Undo the junction.** Give the two words or elements that produced each form, and name what happened.

11. *tenupasaṅkami*
12. *sveva*
13. *etadavoca*
14. *appekacce*
15. *yāvadeva*
16. *evañhi*

### Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — sandhi

**Read the line.** Give a word-by-word gloss and an idiomatic rendering, and account for every junction, including any place where two vowels meet and nothing has happened.

17. Manopubbaṅgamā dhammā, manoseṭṭhā manomayā; (Dhp 1 — Dhp p. 1)

**Undo four junctions in one line.** The Vinaya's rule against theft quotes what a king says to a thief he has caught. It is four sentences and the editions print them as four words.

18. Corosi bālosi mūḷhosi thenosīti. (Bu Pj 2 — Vin iii 47) — Give the words that produced each of the four, name what happened at each join, and say why the last of the four is longer than the other three.

**Make the junction.** Every item so far has run from the page to the pieces. Now run it the other way, which is harder, and which is the only way to find out whether you know the rule or merely recognise its results.

19. Join each of these into the single form the canon actually prints, and name the change at each seam. Say the answer aloud before you write it.

    (a) *tena* + *upasaṅkami*

    (b) *eso* + *eva* + *anto*

    (c) *coro* + *asi*

    (d) *nivāsessāmi* + *iti*

**Where the test does not reach.**

20. The chapter's test for a proposed division is that both pieces must be words that occur elsewhere. Apply it to the Vinaya's resolution in item 1, *yo panā* + *ti*, and report what it returns for *panā*. Then say what the result settles, and what it does not, and why the chapter's footnote calls this a qualification of the test, not an exception to it.

### Coming back — sandhi

**Day 3.** Items 1–4 aloud again. The quotative is the junction you will meet most and the one most easily unlearned.

**Day 10.** Items 7–16 from memory, and item 19, which is the same material produced instead of parsed.

**Day 30.** Items 5 and 19. If the narrative formula in 5 still comes out whole, you have the sentence that opens a third of the canon.

## Exercises: Nouns

The chapter's argument was that the declensions are fewer than they look, because masculine and neuter differ only in three cases and everything else is variation on three paradigms. These exercises test that claim from the reader's side, which is the side that matters: given a form, how much can you actually tell?

The third stage is the point of the set. A great many Pāli endings are ambiguous, and knowing *which* ambiguity you are facing is a more useful skill than knowing a paradigm by heart, because it shows you what to go looking for in the rest of the sentence. Every Pāli line below is canonical and cited; none has been composed. Worked answers are at the back.

### Sotānugata — nouns

Nothing is withheld here. Each line comes with its gloss and its rendering, and the work is to say it and to see what is in front of you.

1. Attā hi attano nātho. (Dhp 160 — Dhp p. 24)

    *attā* self, oneself (m. nom. sg.) · *hi* indeed · *attano* of oneself (m. gen. sg.) · *nātho* protector (m. nom. sg.)

    "Oneself is indeed one's own protector."

    Read it aloud twice. Say which vowel is long and which consonant is doubled, and hold both when you say it again. Then give the stem, the stem class and the case of *attano*.

2. Punappunaṁ vaccho upeti mātaraṁ. (SN 7.12 — S i 174)

    *punappunaṁ* again and again · *vaccho* calf (m. nom. sg.) · *upeti* goes to, approaches · *mātaraṁ* mother (f. acc. sg.)

    "Again and again the calf goes to its mother."

    Aloud, holding the doubled *pp*. Then: what stem class is *mātaraṁ*, and what is the nominative singular of that word?

3. Te taṁ rattiṁ tikkhattuṁ paccuṭṭhāya pañjalikā aggiṁ namassanti. (AN 10.119 — A v 234)

    *te* they · *taṁ rattiṁ* that night (f. acc. sg., adverbial) · *tikkhattuṁ* three times · *paccuṭṭhāya* having risen (absol.) · *pañjalikā* with palms joined (m. nom. pl.) · *aggiṁ* fire (m. acc. sg.) · *namassanti* they worship

    "Rising three times that night, with joined palms, they worship the fire."

    Aloud. Two *i*-stems stand in this line, *rattiṁ* and *aggiṁ*, and they are both accusative singular. One is feminine and one is masculine. Say what shows you which is which, and be careful, because it is not the ending.

4. Mātā yathā niyaṁ puttam āyusā ekaputtamanurakkhe. (Snp 1.8 — Sn p. 26)

    *mātā* mother (f. nom. sg.) · *yathā* as, just as · *niyaṁ* own · *puttam* son (m. acc. sg.) · *āyusā* with her life (inst. sg.) · *ekaputtam* only son (m. acc. sg.) · *anurakkhe* would guard (opt.)

    "As a mother would guard with her life her own son, her only son."

    Aloud, slowly; this is a line worth having by heart. *Mātā* here and *mātaraṁ* in item 2 are the same word. Give the case of each. Then say why a search of the printed edition for the word *āyusā* on its own finds nothing, although the form is here.

### Vacasā paricitā — nouns

Recite each line until you can say it without looking. Then cover the Pāli and rebuild it from the gloss alone, aloud. Rebuilding is the work; recognising is not.

5. Idha pana, upavāṇa, bhikkhu cakkhunā rūpaṁ disvā rūpappaṭisaṁvedī ca hoti. (SN 35.70 — S iv 41)

    *idha* here · *pana* moreover · *upavāṇa* Upavāṇa (m. voc. sg.) · *bhikkhu* a monk (m. nom. sg.) · *cakkhunā* with the eye (n. inst. sg.) · *rūpaṁ* form, sight (n. acc. sg.) · *disvā* having seen (absol.) · *rūpappaṭisaṁvedī* one who experiences the form (m. nom. sg.) · *ca* and · *hoti* is

6. Nibbindaṁ virajjati; virāgā vimuccati. Vimuttasmiṁ vimuttamiti ñāṇaṁ hoti. (SN 22.59 — S iii 68)

    *nibbindaṁ* being disillusioned · *virajjati* desire fades · *virāgā* through the fading of desire (m. abl. sg.) · *vimuccati* he is freed · *vimuttasmiṁ* when he is freed (m. loc. sg.) · *vimuttamiti* "freed" · *ñāṇaṁ* the knowledge (n. nom. sg.) · *hoti* comes to be

    When you can say it, name the case of *virāgā* and of *vimuttasmiṁ* without looking at the gloss, and say what each contributes.

7. … pubbe ananussutesu dhammesu cakkhuṁ udapādi, ñāṇaṁ udapādi, paññā udapādi, vijjā udapādi, āloko udapādi. (SN 56.11 — S v 422)

    *pubbe* before · *ananussutesu dhammesu* regarding teachings not heard before (m. loc. pl.) · *cakkhuṁ* vision (n. nom. sg.) · *udapādi* arose · *ñāṇaṁ* knowledge (n. nom. sg.) · *paññā* wisdom (f. nom. sg.) · *vijjā* realisation (f. nom. sg.) · *āloko* light (m. nom. sg.)

    Five nouns arise in a row, and they are not all of one gender. Rebuild the line from the gloss; then say which are feminine, which neuter and which masculine, and how much of that you could have told from the endings alone.

### Manasānupekkhitā — nouns

Cover the chapter and the paradigms. These are answered from memory or not at all.

**Identify the form.** For each, give the stem and its class, the gender, and the case and number.

8. *bhikkhūnaṁ*
9. *raññā*
10. *satthāraṁ*
11. *cakkhunā*
12. *manasi*
13. *bhagavato*

Say what the ending does not tell you. Each of these is ambiguous. Give every case and number it could be, and then say what you would look for in a sentence to settle it.

14. *purisā*
15. *kaññāya*
16. *rūpāni*
17. *aggino*

### Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — nouns

**Read the line.** Give a word-by-word gloss and an idiomatic rendering, and name the stem class of every noun in it.

18. Manopubbaṅgamā dhammā, manoseṭṭhā manomayā; (Dhp 1 — Dhp p. 1)
19. Attanā hi kataṁ pāpaṁ, attanā saṅkilissati. (Dhp 165 — Dhp p. 24)
20. Ārocemi kho te, mahārāja, paṭivedemi kho te, mahārāja, adhivattati kho taṁ, mahārāja, jarāmaraṇaṁ. (SN 3.25 — S i 101)

**One stem, two cases, one sentence.** The Vinaya's procedure for relinquishing a robe opens by telling the monk what to do, step by step.

21. Tena bhikkhunā saṅghaṁ upasaṅkamitvā ekaṁsaṁ uttarāsaṅgaṁ karitvā vuḍḍhānaṁ bhikkhūnaṁ pāde vanditvā ukkuṭikaṁ nisīditvā añjaliṁ paggahetvā evamassa vacanīyo. (Bu Np 1 — Vin iii 196) — Gloss it. *Bhikkhu* appears twice in different cases and numbers: give both, and say what each one's role is. Then say what declension *pāde* belongs to and why it cannot be a locative here.

**Put it back into Pāli.** Producing a form is harder than recognising one, and it is the only way to find out which of the two you can do. Each phrase below occurs in a line already in this set. Give the Pāli from memory, with the case and number of every noun in it, and only then look back at the line it came from.

22. Four phrases, one line each:

    (a) "one's own protector" — Dhp 160 — Dhp p. 24

    (b) "with the eye" — SN 35.70 — S iv 41

    (c) "with her life" — Snp 1.8 — Sn p. 26

    (d) "at the feet of the senior monks" — Bu Np 1 — Vin iii 196

### Coming back — nouns

The set is not finished on the day it is worked. Re-attempt these, from cold, without reading the answers first.

**Day 3.** Items 1–4 aloud, and 14–17; the ambiguities are the first thing to go.

**Day 10.** Items 8–13 with the paradigms covered, and item 18.

**Day 30.** Items 21 and 22. If 22 still comes out, the paradigms have held.

## Exercises: Gender and Adjectives

An adjective has no endings of its own. Everything it does it borrows, and the whole skill this chapter asks for is reading that borrowing backwards: given an adjective, what does its ending tell you about the noun it belongs to? That is more useful than it sounds, because it is often the adjective, not the noun, that resolves an ambiguous phrase.

Agreement is easier to hear than to work out, so the first stage gives whole lines with nothing withheld. The last stage takes up comparison, which the chapter treats as the second defining property of an adjective. Every Pāli line below is canonical and cited; none has been composed. Worked answers are at the back.

### Sotānugata — gender and adjectives

Nothing is withheld. Each line comes with its gloss and its rendering, and the work is to say it and to hear one stem take different endings from the words around it.

1. Dīghā jāgarato ratti, dīghaṁ santassa yojanaṁ; Dīgho bālānaṁ saṁsāro, saddhammaṁ avijānataṁ. (Dhp 60 — Dhp p. 9)

    *dīghā* long (f. nom. sg.) · *jāgarato* for one lying awake (m. gen. sg., pres. part.) · *ratti* the night (f. nom. sg.) · *dīghaṁ* long (n. nom. sg.) · *santassa* for the weary (m. gen. sg., p.p.) · *yojanaṁ* a league (n. nom. sg.) · *dīgho* long (m. nom. sg.) · *bālānaṁ* for fools (m. gen. pl.) · *saṁsāro* wandering on (m. nom. sg.) · *saddhammaṁ* the true teaching (m. acc. sg.) · *avijānataṁ* of those not understanding (m. gen. pl., pres. part.)

    "Long is the night for one lying awake; long is the league for the weary; long is the wandering on for fools who do not understand the true teaching."

    Say it aloud, one *pāda* at a time. One adjective, three genders, three lines, and the stem never changes. Say the three forms of it in order without looking, and then say which noun gave each its ending.

2. Seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, bālo abyatto akusalo sūdo rājānaṁ vā rājamahāmattaṁ vā nānaccayehi sūpehi paccupaṭṭhito assa. (SN 47.8 — S v 149)

    Seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, paṇḍito byatto kusalo sūdo rājānaṁ vā rājamahāmattaṁ vā nānaccayehi sūpehi paccupaṭṭhito assa. (SN 47.8 — S v 151)

    *seyyathāpi* suppose · *bhikkhave* mendicants (m. voc. pl.) · *bālo* foolish · *abyatto* incompetent · *akusalo* unskilful · *paṇḍito* wise · *byatto* competent · *kusalo* skilful · *sūdo* a cook (m. nom. sg.) · *rājānaṁ* a king (m. acc. sg.) · *vā* or · *rājamahāmattaṁ* a royal minister (m. acc. sg.) · *nānaccayehi* of various kinds (m. inst. pl.) · *sūpehi* with sauces (m. inst. pl.) · *paccupaṭṭhito* waiting on, attending (m. nom. sg., p.p.) · *assa* were to be (3 sg. opt.)

    "Suppose, mendicants, a foolish, incompetent, unskilful cook were to wait on a king or a royal minister with sauces of various kinds."

    "Suppose, mendicants, a wise, competent, skilful cook were to wait on a king or a royal minister with sauces of various kinds."

    The same sentence twice, four sections of one sutta apart, with three adjectives turned round. Say both aloud. Six adjectives and one noun, and every one of the six carries the same three letters at the end. Then say what the second and the fifth have in common that the others do not.

3. Tena kho pana samayena sukkā bhikkhunī mahatiyā parisāya parivutā dhammaṁ deseti. (SN 10.9 — S i 212)

    *tena kho pana samayena* now at that time · *sukkā* Sukkā (f. nom. sg.) · *bhikkhunī* the nun (f. nom. sg.) · *mahatiyā* large (f. inst. sg.) · *parisāya* by an assembly (f. inst. sg.) · *parivutā* surrounded (f. nom. sg., p.p.) · *dhammaṁ* the Dhamma (m. acc. sg.) · *deseti* she teaches

    "Now at that time the nun Sukkā was teaching the Dhamma surrounded by a large assembly."

    Five feminines in a row, and they are not all in one case. Say it aloud, then say which two of the five are not nominative, and how you know; and say why *parivutā*, which stands at the far end of the sentence from the word it describes, is nominative and not instrumental.

4. Yo pana bhikkhu atirekacīvaraṁ dhāreyya, nissaggiyaṁ pācittiyan'ti. (Bu Np 1 — Vin iii 195)

    Yā pana bhikkhunī pattasannicayaṁ kareyya, nissaggiyaṁ pācittiyan'ti. (Bi Np 1 — Vin iv 243)

    *yo* whoever (m. nom. sg.) · *yā* whoever (f. nom. sg.) · *pana* moreover · *bhikkhu* a monk (m. nom. sg.) · *bhikkhunī* a nun (f. nom. sg.) · *atirekacīvaraṁ* a surplus robe (n. acc. sg.) · *dhāreyya* should keep (3 sg. opt.) · *pattasannicayaṁ* a stash of bowls (m. acc. sg.) · *kareyya* should make (3 sg. opt.) · *nissaggiyaṁ* entailing relinquishment (n. nom. sg.) · *pācittiyaṁ* an offence entailing confession (n. nom. sg.) · *ti* (quotation)

    "Whatever monk should keep a surplus robe: an offence entailing relinquishment and confession."

    "Whatever nun should make a stash of bowls: an offence entailing relinquishment and confession."

    The monks' code and the nuns' code, built on one frame. Say the two openings one after the other. Two words change and the rest does not. Say which two, and then say why the four words that close both rules did not have to move.

### Vacasā paricitā — gender and adjectives

Recite each line until you can say it without looking. Then cover the Pāli and rebuild it from the gloss alone, aloud. Producing the endings is the work; recognising them is not.

5. Tāvadeva chāyā metabbā, utuppamāṇaṁ ācikkhitabbaṁ, divasabhāgo ācikkhitabbo, saṅgīti ācikkhitabbā, cattāro nissayā ācikkhitabbā. (Kd 1 — Vin i 95)

    *tāvadeva* straightaway · *chāyā* the shadow (f. nom. sg.) · *metabbā* is to be measured (f. nom. sg.) · *utuppamāṇaṁ* the date (n. nom. sg.) · *ācikkhitabbaṁ* is to be told (n. nom. sg.) · *divasabhāgo* the part of the day (m. nom. sg.) · *ācikkhitabbo* (m. nom. sg.) · *saṅgīti* the recitation (f. nom. sg.) · *ācikkhitabbā* (f. nom. sg.) · *cattāro nissayā* the four supports (m. nom. pl.) · *ācikkhitabbā* (m. nom. pl.)

    "Straightaway the shadow is to be measured, the date told, the part of the day told, the recitation told, and the four supports told."

    This is what a newly ordained monk is told in the first minutes after his ordination. One word takes four different endings in one sentence, and rebuilding it from the gloss means producing all four. Do that before reading on. Then say which two of the four are spelled alike, and what in the sentence (not in the word) decides between them.

6. Sahassamapi ce vācā, anatthapadasaṁhitā; Ekaṁ atthapadaṁ seyyo, yaṁ sutvā upasammati. (Dhp 100 — Dhp p. 15)

    *sahassaṁ* a thousand (n. nom. sg.) · *api* even · *ce* if · *vācā* words (f. nom. pl.) · *anatthapadasaṁhitā* not connected with a meaningful saying (f. nom. pl.) · *ekaṁ* one (n. nom. sg.) · *atthapadaṁ* a meaningful saying (n. nom. sg.) · *seyyo* better · *yaṁ* which (n. acc. sg.) · *sutvā* having heard (absol.) · *upasammati* one is calmed

    "Though there be a thousand words unconnected with meaning, better is one meaningful saying, hearing which one is calmed."

    Recite it, then rebuild it from the gloss. This is the chapter's comparison table in a verse, and *seyyo*, alone of its forms, cannot be derived from anything: it has to be known. When the line is fluent, say what its positive degree is according to the chapter's table of irregulars.

### Manasānupekkhitā — gender and adjectives

Cover the chapter. These are answered from memory or not at all.

**Give the feminine.** For each masculine stem, form the feminine and name the rule that produced it.

7. *dāsa* 'servant'
8. *dīgha* 'long'
9. *upāsaka* 'lay follower'
10. *hatthin* 'elephant'
11. *bhikkhu* 'mendicant'
12. *mahant* 'great'

What does the adjective tell you about its noun? For each adjective, say every gender, case and number it could be carrying, and therefore what you know about the noun it agrees with.

13. *kusalā*
14. *dukkhaṁ*
15. *sīlavato*
16. *mahatiyā*

### Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — gender and adjectives

**Read the line.** Give a word-by-word gloss and an idiomatic rendering, and account for every adjective: its stem, its agreement, and whether it is being compared.

17. Maggānaṭṭhaṅgiko seṭṭho, saccānaṁ caturo padā. (Dhp 273 — Dhp p. 40)
18. Atthi kho, bhikkhu, sakko nāma devānamindo amhehi abhikkantataro ca paṇītataro ca. (DN 11 — D i 216)
19. Cakkhuṁ, bhikkhave, dukkhaṁ. … jivhā dukkhā … kāyo dukkho … mano dukkho. (SN 35.2 — S iv 2)

**Agreement across a long sentence.** A formal act of the Sangha names a thing once and then keeps agreeing with it.

20. Idaṁ cīvaraṁ itthannāmassa bhikkhuno nissaggiyaṁ āyasmantānaṁ nissaṭṭhaṁ. (Bu Np 1 — Vin iii 197) — Gloss it. Four words agree with one another and two do not. Say which are which, and what fixes the gender of the four.

**Put it back into Pāli.** Every item so far has run from a printed ending to the noun behind it. Now run it the other way, which is harder, and which is where you find out whether you can form an agreement or only recognise one. Each phrase below occurs in a line already in this set. Give the Pāli from memory, naming the gender, case and number of every word in it, and only then look back at the line it came from.

21. Four phrases, one line each:

    (a) "long is the night" — Dhp 60 — Dhp p. 9

    (b) "surrounded by a large assembly" — SN 10.9 — S i 212

    (c) "the recitation is to be told" — Kd 1 — Vin i 95

    (d) "whatever nun should make a stash of bowls" — Bi Np 1 — Vin iv 243

Where the ending is not the word you think it is.

22. Item 1 turns on *santassa*, and the chapter's table of irregular comparatives has a *sant* in it. Look up both. Say whether they are the same word, what each is built from, and what would have gone wrong in the rendering of Dhp 60 had you taken the table's *sant* to be the word in the verse. Then find the table's word somewhere else in the same verse, where it is hiding inside a compound.

### Coming back — gender and adjectives

The set is not finished on the day it is worked. Re-attempt these, from cold, without reading the answers first.

**Day 3.** Items 1 and 5 aloud, and 7–12. The feminine suffixes are a table, and a table goes first.

**Day 10.** Items 13–16 from memory, and item 20.

**Day 30.** Items 21 and 22. If 21 still comes out with the feminines intact, the table in 7–12 has become something you can use, not just recite.

## Exercises: Pronouns

The chapter's one recurring claim is that a Pāli pronoun has to be identified, not merely recognised, and this set is built to make that claim expensive to ignore. The first stage gives whole sentences with the pronouns already parsed, because a form met in a sentence is easier to identify later than a form met in a list. The third stage takes the parsing away.

Every Pāli sentence below is canonical and cited; none has been composed. Worked answers are at the back.

### Sotānugata — pronouns

Nothing is withheld. Each sentence comes with its gloss and its rendering, and the work is to say it and to hear how much of the sentence is pronoun.

1. Tvaṁ, samma medakathālike, mamaṁ rakkha, ahaṁ taṁ rakkhissāmi. (SN 47.19 — S v 168)

    *tvaṁ* you (2nd nom. sg.) · *samma* dear · *medakathālike* Medakathālikā (voc.) · *mamaṁ* me (1st acc. sg.) · *rakkha* protect! · *ahaṁ* I (1st nom. sg.) · *taṁ* you (2nd acc. sg.) · *rakkhissāmi* I shall protect

    "You protect me, dear Medakathālikā, and I shall protect you."

    An acrobat speaking to his assistant on the pole. Say it aloud until the four pronouns come out in the right order without thinking (*tvaṁ … mamaṁ … ahaṁ … taṁ*), because the sentence is a chiasmus and half the work of holding it is rhythmic. Then say which two of the four are first person and which two second, and notice that the two accusatives are the two that look least like their nominatives.

2. Evaṁ me sutaṁ. (DN 1 — D i 1)

    *evaṁ* thus · *me* by me (1st instr. sg.) · *sutaṁ* heard

    "Thus have I heard."

    Three words, and they open almost every sutta in the canon. Say it. Then say what case *me* is in here and, before looking anything up, say whether you had ever thought about that before.

3. Ayaṁ te patto, ayaṁ saṅghāṭi, ayaṁ uttarāsaṅgo, ayaṁ antaravāsako. (Kd 1 — Vin i 94)

    *ayaṁ* this (nom. sg.) · *te* your (2nd gen./dat. sg.) · *patto* bowl (m. nom. sg.) · *saṅghāṭi* outer robe (f. nom. sg.) · *uttarāsaṅgo* upper robe (m. nom. sg.) · *antaravāsako* inner robe (m. nom. sg.)

    "This is your bowl, this your outer robe, this your upper robe, this your inner robe."

    A newly ordained monk being handed his requisites, one at a time. Say it aloud. One word, four times, and it is not agreeing with four words of one gender. Say which of the four nouns is the odd one out, and then say what that shows about the form *ayaṁ*.

4. Ko āpanno, kiṁ āpanno, kismiṁ āpanno, kathaṁ āpanno, kaṁ bhaṇatha, kiṁ bhaṇathā'ti? (Bu Pc 12 — Vin iv 35)

    *ko* who (nom. sg. m.) · *āpanno* has fallen into (an offence) · *kiṁ* what (acc. sg. n.) · *kismiṁ* in what (loc. sg.) · *kathaṁ* how (interrog. adverb) · *kaṁ* whom (acc. sg. m.) · *bhaṇatha* do you speak of · *ti* (quotation)

    "Who has committed an offence? What has he committed? In what has he committed it? How has he committed it? Whom are you speaking of? What are you speaking of?"

    A monk being cross-examined, and one of the plainest displays of the interrogative paradigm anywhere in the canon. Say it aloud as a series: *ko, kiṁ, kismiṁ, kathaṁ, kaṁ, kiṁ*. Then say which one of the six is not a pronoun at all, and which one is built on the chapter's second stem.

### Vacasā paricitā — pronouns

Recite each line until you can say it without looking. Then cover the Pāli and rebuild it from the gloss alone, aloud. Producing the enclitic in the right place is the work; recognising it is not.

5. Nagaraṁ yathā paccantaṁ, guttaṁ santarabāhiraṁ; Evaṁ gopetha attānaṁ, khaṇo vo mā upaccagā. (Dhp 315 — Dhp p. 45)

    *nagaraṁ* city · *yathā* as · *paccantaṁ* frontier · *guttaṁ* guarded · *santarabāhiraṁ* within and without · *evaṁ* so · *gopetha* guard! (2nd pl. imv.) · *attānaṁ* yourselves (acc. sg.) · *khaṇo* the moment · *vo* for you (2nd enclitic) · *mā* do not · *upaccagā* let it pass by

    "As a frontier city is guarded within and without, so guard yourselves. Do not let the moment pass you by."

    Two pronouns and neither behaves as English would. When the verse is fluent, say what is odd about the number of *attānaṁ* against the number of *gopetha*, and why that is not a mistake. Then say where in the line *vo* could not have stood, and why.

6. Yo vo, ānanda, mayā dhammo ca vinayo ca desito paññatto, so vo mamaccayena satthā. (DN 16 — D ii 154)

    *yo* which (nom. sg. m.) · *vo* to you (2nd enclitic) · *ānanda* Ānanda · *mayā* by me (1st instr. sg.) · *dhammo* teaching · *ca* and · *vinayo* training · *desito* taught · *paññatto* laid down · *so* that (nom. sg. m.) · *mamaccayena* < *mama* of me (1st gen. sg.) + *accayena* with the passing · *satthā* teacher

    "Ānanda, the teaching and training I have taught and laid down will be your Teacher after I am gone."

    The Buddha's instruction shortly before his death, and there is no sentence in the canon it is more useful to have by heart. Five pronouns in one sentence, in four cases. Rebuild it from the gloss, and expect the two *vo* to be the hard part. Then say what an English translation of *vo* has to throw away.

### Manasānupekkhitā — pronouns

Cover the chapter and the tables. These are answered from memory or not at all.

**Identify the form.** For each, give the stem it belongs to, and its case, number and gender, and its person where it has one.

7. *amūsaṁ*
8. *tuyhaṁ*
9. *enaṁ*
10. *attanā*
11. *kismā*

**Resolve the ambiguity.** Each of these forms has two readings that this chapter has met. Give both, and say what in a sentence would decide between them.

12. *taṁ*
13. *no*
14. *assa*

### Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — pronouns

**Read the sentence.** Give each a word-by-word gloss and then an idiomatic rendering, and name the class of every pronoun in it.

15. Sabbe tasanti daṇḍassa, sabbesaṁ jīvitaṁ piyaṁ. (Dhp 130 — Dhp p. 19)
16. Ko imaṁ pathaviṁ vicessati, yamalokañca imaṁ sadevakaṁ. (Dhp 44 — Dhp p. 7)
17. Tumhehi kiccamātappaṁ, akkhātāro tathāgatā. (Dhp 276 — Dhp p. 40)
18. Tato naṁ dukkhamanveti, cakkaṁva vahato padaṁ. (Dhp 1 — Dhp p. 1)

A demonstrative welded to a personal pronoun. What the monk says as he hands the robe over.

19. Imāhaṁ saṅghassa nissajjāmi. (Bu Np 1 — Vin iii 196) — Resolve the first word into the two pronouns that made it, give the case and number of each, and say what each one's role is. Then say why looking the verb up in this shape will fail.

Four ways of saying "self", in four lines. This is the hardest reading in the set, and it is here because the verse is about the very thing it is difficult for: a person who talks about himself.

20. Yo attano sīlavatāni jantu, anānupuṭṭhova paresa pāva; Anariyadhammaṁ kusalā tamāhu, yo ātumānaṁ sayameva pāva. (Snp 4.3 — Sn p. 153) — Gloss it and render it. Then identify the three reflexive expressions it holds, say which of the three does not decline, and say why the fourth pronoun in the verse, the one in the second line, has an ending no noun would give it.

**Put it back into Pāli.** Every item so far has run from a printed form to the pronoun behind it. Now run it the other way, which is where an enclitic will catch you out, because producing one means knowing where in the sentence it is allowed to stand. Each phrase below occurs in a line already in this set. Give the Pāli from memory, with the case, number and person of every pronoun in it, and only then look back at the line it came from.

21. Four phrases, one line each:

    (a) "I shall protect you" — SN 47.19 — S v 168

    (b) "Thus have I heard" — DN 1 — D i 1

    (c) "This is your bowl" — Kd 1 — Vin i 94

    (d) "so guard yourselves" — Dhp 315 — Dhp p. 45

### Coming back — pronouns

The set is not finished on the day it is worked. Re-attempt these, from cold, without reading the answers first.

**Day 3.** Items 1–4 aloud, and 12–14. The ambiguities are the first thing to go, and they go unnoticed.

**Day 10.** Items 7–11 with the tables covered, and item 19.

**Day 30.** Items 6, 20 and 21. If 6 still comes out whole, with both *vo* in place, the enclitics have stopped being a list.

## Exercises: Numerals

The pronoun chapter's exercises began with isolated forms because its thesis was that a pronoun must be identified, not merely recognised. This chapter's thesis is different: the forms are the easy half, and what has to be worked out is how a numeral attaches to what it counts. So the third stage here matters most, and the first two exist to make it possible.

The first stage is arranged around the four constructions, not around the numbers, because a reader has to be able to hear them. Every Pāli line below is canonical and cited; none has been composed. Worked answers are at the back.

### Sotānugata — numerals

Nothing is withheld. Each line comes with its gloss and its rendering, and the work is to say it and to hear what the numeral is attached to.

1. Ekaṁ samayaṁ bhagavā rājagahe viharati veḷuvane kalandakanivāpe. (SN 10.9 — S i 212)

    *ekaṁ* one, a certain (m. acc. sg.) · *samayaṁ* occasion, time (m. acc. sg.) · *bhagavā* the Blessed One (m. nom. sg.) · *rājagahe* at Rājagaha (n. loc. sg.) · *viharati* dwells · *veḷuvane* in the Bamboo Grove (n. loc. sg.) · *kalandakanivāpe* at the squirrels' feeding ground (m. loc. sg.)

    "At one time the Buddha was staying near Rājagaha, in the Bamboo Grove, the squirrels' feeding ground."

    This is the numeral you have heard most often and have probably never counted with. Say it aloud. *Eka* here agrees with *samayaṁ* as an adjective does, and it is not really counting anything; it does what English does with "a". Then say which of the four constructions this is, and whether you would have called it a numeral at all had it not been printed under this heading.

2. Tena kho pana samayena aññatitthiyā paribbājakā cātuddase pannarase aṭṭhamiyā ca pakkhassa sannipatitvā dhammaṁ bhāsanti. (Kd 2 — Vin i 101)

    *tena kho pana samayena* now at that time · *aññatitthiyā* of other sects (m. nom. pl.) · *paribbājakā* wanderers (m. nom. pl.) · *cātuddase* on the fourteenth (m. loc. sg.) · *pannarase* on the fifteenth (loc. sg., used as an adverb) · *aṭṭhamiyā* on the eighth (f. loc. sg.) · *ca* and · *pakkhassa* of the fortnight (m. gen. sg.) · *sannipatitvā* having assembled (absol.) · *dhammaṁ* the teaching (m. acc. sg.) · *bhāsanti* they speak

    "Now at that time wanderers of other sects would assemble on the fourteenth, the fifteenth and the eighth of the fortnight and speak on the teaching."

    The Buddha's fortnightly observance was instituted in answer to this passage, so here the uposatha days enter the canon. Say it aloud. Three ordinals in a row, and they are not all of one gender. Say which one is the odd one out, and hold the answer until item 15 asks you for it again.

3. Abhivādanasīlissa, niccaṁ vuḍḍhāpacāyino; Cattāro dhammā vaḍḍhanti, āyu vaṇṇo sukhaṁ balaṁ. (Dhp 109 — Dhp p. 16)

    *abhivādanasīlissa* for one whose habit is to bow (m. gen. sg.) · *niccaṁ* constantly · *vuḍḍhāpacāyino* honouring elders (m. gen. sg.) · *cattāro* four (m. nom. pl.) · *dhammā* things (m. nom. pl.) · *vaḍḍhanti* increase · *āyu* long life · *vaṇṇo* beauty · *sukhaṁ* happiness · *balaṁ* strength

    "For one in the habit of bowing, always honouring the elders, four things increase: long life, beauty, happiness and strength."

    Say it aloud, and then say the four things without looking. The numeral and its noun share case, number and gender, which is the construction most readers assume is the only one. Item 4 is what happens when it is not.

4. Tasmiṁ sahassadhā loke sahassaṁ candānaṁ sahassaṁ sūriyānaṁ … cattāri mahāsamuddasahassāni … sahassaṁ brahmalokānaṁ. (AN 10.29 — A v 59)

    *tasmiṁ* in that (m. loc. sg.) · *sahassadhā* thousandfold · *loke* world (m. loc. sg.) · *sahassaṁ* a thousand (n. nom. sg.) · *candānaṁ* of moons (m. gen. pl.) · *sūriyānaṁ* of suns (m. gen. pl.) · *cattāri* four (n. nom. pl.) · *mahāsamuddasahassāni* thousands of great oceans (n. nom. pl.) · *brahmalokānaṁ* of realms of divinity (m. gen. pl.)

    "In that thousandfold world there are a thousand moons, a thousand suns … four thousand great oceans … a thousand realms of divinity."

    The full passage says *sahassaṁ* fourteen times, and it is a list built to be recited. Say it. Nothing here agrees with anything: *sahassaṁ* is a neuter noun and what it counts stands in the genitive plural beside it, so the phrase is literally "a thousand of moons". Then find the place in the line where the construction changes, and say what changed and why it had to.

### Vacasā paricitā — numerals

Recite each until it is fluent. Then cover the Pāli and rebuild it from the gloss alone, aloud. Producing a numeral compound is the work; recognising one is not.

5. Niṭṭhitacīvarasmiṁ bhikkhunā ubbhatasmiṁ kathine dasāhaparamaṁ atirekacīvaraṁ dhāretabbaṁ. Taṁ atikkāmayato nissaggiyaṁ pācittiyan'ti. (Bu Np 1 — Vin iii 196)

    *niṭṭhitacīvarasmiṁ* when the robe is finished (n. loc. sg.) · *bhikkhunā* by a monk (m. inst. sg.) · *ubbhatasmiṁ* when it has been withdrawn (n. loc. sg., p.p.) · *kathine* the kathina (n. loc. sg.) · *dasāhaparamaṁ* for ten days at most (adv., acc. sg.) · *atirekacīvaraṁ* a surplus robe (n. acc. sg.) · *dhāretabbaṁ* may be kept (n. nom. sg., gerundive) · *taṁ* that (n. acc. sg.) · *atikkāmayato* for one exceeding it (m. gen. sg., pres. part.) · *nissaggiyaṁ* entailing relinquishment · *pācittiyaṁ* an offence entailing confession · *ti* (quotation)

    "When a monk's robe is finished and the kathina has been withdrawn, a surplus robe may be kept for ten days at most. For one exceeding that: an offence entailing relinquishment and confession."

    A rule recited fortnightly by every community of monks, and the whole of it turns on one word. Rebuild it from the gloss, and expect *dasāhaparamaṁ* to be the word that will not come. Then say how many parts that word has, and what case it is in. Having answered that, say why a word in that case is being translated as "for ten days at most" and not as the object of anything.

6. Māse māse sahassena, yo yajetha sataṁ samaṁ; Ekañca bhāvitattānaṁ, muhuttamapi pūjaye; Sāyeva pūjanā seyyo, yañce vassasataṁ hutaṁ. (Dhp 106 — Dhp p. 16)

    *māse māse* month by month (m. loc. sg., repeated) · *sahassena* with a thousand (n. inst. sg.) · *yo* whoever · *yajetha* should sacrifice (opt.) · *sataṁ* a hundred (n. acc. sg.) · *samaṁ* year (f. acc. sg.) · *ekaṁ* one (m. acc. sg.) · *ca* and · *bhāvitattānaṁ* one who has developed himself (m. acc. sg.) · *muhuttaṁ* for a moment (adv., acc. sg.) · *api* even · *pūjaye* should honour (opt.) · *sā* that · *eva* just · *pūjanā* honouring (f. nom. sg.) · *seyyo* better · *yaṁ* than which · *ce* if · *vassasataṁ* a hundred years (n. acc. sg.) · *hutaṁ* offering (n. nom. sg., p.p.)

    "Though month after month for a hundred years one should sacrifice with a thousand, and another honour for a single moment one who has developed himself — that honouring is better than a hundred years of offerings."

    Six numeral expressions in six lines, and no two of them work the same way. Rebuild it from the gloss and then set them out: which is a distributive, which a plain instrumental, which an accusative of duration, which an ordinary agreeing adjective, and which two are compounds. Item 16 takes the first two lines apart in detail; this stage asks only that the whole verse be in your mouth first.

### Manasānupekkhitā — numerals

Cover the chapter. These are answered from memory or not at all.

**Identify the form.** For each, give the numeral it belongs to, what class of numeral it is, and its case and number, or say why it has none.

7. *tiṇṇannaṁ*
8. *ubhinnaṁ*
9. *catutthī*
10. *vīsatiyā*
11. *tikkhattuṁ*

Say how the numeral joins its noun. Each of these uses one of the four constructions. Name it, and say what in the phrase shows which one it is.

12. **sahassaṁ** candānaṁ (AN 10.29 — A v 59)
13. saddhiṁ **bhikkhusahassena** (SN 35.28 — S iv 19)
14. **Cattāro** dhammā vaḍḍhanti (Dhp 109 — Dhp p. 16)

### Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — numerals

**Read the verse.** Give each a word-by-word gloss and then an idiomatic rendering, and account for every numeral in it.

15. Yo sahassaṁ sahassena, saṅgāme mānuse jine; Ekañca jeyyamattānaṁ, sa ve saṅgāmajuttamo. (Dhp 103 — Dhp p. 15)
16. Māse māse sahassena, yo yajetha sataṁ samaṁ; (Dhp 106 — Dhp p. 16)
17. Sabbampi taṁ na catubhāgameti, Abhivādanā ujjugatesu seyyo. (Dhp 108 — Dhp p. 16)
18. Appamādo kho, mahārāja, eko dhammo, yo ubho atthe samadhiggayha tiṭṭhati. (SN 3.17 — S i 86)

The same three days, twice, in two genders. The chapter quotes the Bodhisatta naming the nights he chose to sit out in the forest.

19. Cātuddasī pañcadasī aṭṭhamī ca pakkhassa. (MN 4 — M i 20) — Set this beside item 2 and give the gender and case of all six day-names across the two passages. Two of the three days are named twice in two different genders. Say which two, which gender each passage uses, and what that suggests about whether these words are ordinals or names. Then say why the third day behaves differently from the other two.

**Put it back into Pāli.** Every item so far has run from a printed numeral to the construction behind it. Now run it the other way. Each phrase below occurs in a line already in this set. Give the Pāli from memory, naming the construction it uses and the case and number of every word in it, and only then look back at the line it came from.

20. Four phrases, one line each:

    (a) "at one time" — SN 10.9 — S i 212

    (b) "four things increase" — Dhp 109 — Dhp p. 16

    (c) "a thousand moons" — AN 10.29 — A v 59

    (d) "for ten days at most" — Bu Np 1 — Vin iii 196

### Coming back — numerals

The set is not finished on the day it is worked. Re-attempt these, from cold, without reading the answers first.

**Day 3.** Items 1–4 aloud, and 12–14. The four constructions are the whole chapter, and they go before the forms do.

**Day 10.** Items 7–11 from memory, and item 18.

**Day 30.** Items 5, 6 and 20. If (c) and (d) in 20 still come out, the two constructions that do not agree with anything have stopped being exceptions.

## Exercises: The Present System

The chapter's thesis is that the reader works from the present stem, not the root, and that three of Pāli's moods are built on it. So the third stage asks you to get back to the stem, and then to deal with the consequence: because the three moods share one stem, several forms belong to more than one of them, and only the sentence decides.

The first two stages come before that and are arranged around the moods, not around the verbs, because once you have heard the imperative and the optative in real lines you will not confuse them later. Every Pāli line below is canonical and cited; none has been composed. Worked answers are at the back.

### Sotānugata — the present system

Nothing is withheld. Each line comes with its gloss and its rendering, and the work is to say it and to hear which mood is speaking.

1. Pare ca na vijānanti, mayamettha yamāmase; Ye ca tattha vijānanti, tato sammanti medhagā. (Dhp 6 — Dhp p. 2)

    *pare* others (m. nom. pl.) · *ca* and · *na* not · *vijānanti* understand (3 pl. pres.) · *mayaṁ* we (nom. pl.) · *ettha* here · *yamāmase* let us restrain ourselves (1 pl. imper. middle) · *ye* those who (m. nom. pl.) · *tattha* there · *tato* thereupon · *sammanti* are appeased (3 pl. pres.) · *medhagā* quarrels (m. nom. pl.)

    "Others do not understand that here we must restrain ourselves; but those who do understand — thereupon their quarrels are appeased."

    The chapter quotes the first two lines of this from MN 128, where the Buddha says them to the quarrelling monks of Kosambī. Say the whole verse aloud. Three of its four finite verbs are ordinary present indicatives and the fourth is not, and the fourth is the only middle form most readers will ever meet. Say which it is, and say what in its ending marks it off from the other three.

2. Sabbe tasanti daṇḍassa, sabbe bhāyanti maccuno; Attānaṁ upamaṁ katvā, na haneyya na ghātaye. (Dhp 129 — Dhp p. 19)

    *sabbe* all (m. nom. pl.) · *tasanti* tremble (3 pl. pres.) · *daṇḍassa* at the rod (m. gen. sg.) · *bhāyanti* fear (3 pl. pres.) · *maccuno* death (m. gen. sg.) · *attānaṁ* oneself (m. acc. sg.) · *upamaṁ* as the comparison (f. acc. sg.) · *katvā* having made (absol.) · *na* not · *haneyya* should kill (3 sg. opt.) · *ghātaye* should have [another] kill (3 sg. opt.)

    "All tremble at the rod, all fear death. Taking oneself as the measure, one should neither kill nor have another killed."

    Say it aloud. The verse turns on its hinge: two present indicatives in the first half, two optatives in the second, and the mood carries the whole argument, because the first half states what is and the second what one ought therefore to do. Then look at the two optatives. They are the same mood, the same person and the same number, and they do not look alike at all. Say what the difference is.

3. Na paro paraṁ nikubbetha, nātimaññetha katthaci na kañci; Byārosanā paṭighasaññā, nāññamaññassa dukkhamiccheyya. (Snp 1.8 — Sn p. 26)

    *na* not · *paro* one person (m. nom. sg.) · *paraṁ* another (m. acc. sg.) · *nikubbetha* should deceive (3 sg. opt. middle) · *na·atimaññetha* should not despise (3 sg. opt. middle) · *katthaci* anywhere · *kañci* anyone (m. acc. sg.) · *byārosanā* anger (f.) · *paṭighasaññā* thought of enmity (f.) · *na·aññamaññassa* not for one another (m. gen. sg.) · *dukkham·iccheyya* < *dukkhaṁ* suffering (n. acc. sg.) + *iccheyya* should wish (3 sg. opt.)

    "Let no one deceive another, nor despise anyone anywhere; in anger or in thoughts of enmity, let none wish suffering upon another."

    From the Mettasutta, which a great many readers will already know by heart, so say it from memory if you can. Three optatives in four lines, and two of them are middle. Say which two, and say what the third does differently. Then say why a verse of this kind is where such forms are found.

4. Rūpaṁ 'netaṁ mama, nesohamasmi, na meso attā'ti samanupassati. (MN 22 — M i 136)

    *rūpaṁ* form (n. acc. sg.) · *na·etaṁ* not this (n. nom. sg.) · *mama* mine (1 sg. gen.) · *na·eso·ahaṁ·asmi* < *na* not + *eso* this (m. nom. sg.) + *ahaṁ* I (nom. sg.) + *asmi* am (1 sg. pres. of *atthi*) · *na* not · *me·eso* this of mine (m. nom. sg.) · *attā* self (m. nom. sg.) · *ti* (quotation) · *samanupassati* regards (3 sg. pres.)

    "One regards form thus: 'This is not mine, I am not this, this is not my self.'"

    Say it aloud, slowly, and count the words. One of them is four words printed as one, and one of the four is the first person singular of the commonest verb in the language. Separate them out. Then say what the chapter warns will happen if you go looking for that verb form on its own in a text.

### Vacasā paricitā — the present system

Recite each until it is fluent. Then cover the Pāli and rebuild it from the gloss alone, aloud. Producing a mood is the work; recognising one is not.

5. Handa dāni, bhikkhave, āmantayāmi vo, vayadhammā saṅkhārā appamādena sampādethā'ti. (DN 16 — D ii 156)

    *handa* come now · *dāni* now · *bhikkhave* monks (m. voc. pl.) · *āmantayāmi* I address (1 sg. pres.) · *vo* you (2 pl. acc.) · *vaya·dhammā* subject to decay (m. nom. pl.) · *saṅkhārā* conditioned things (m. nom. pl.) · *appamādena* with diligence (m. inst. sg.) · *sampādetha* strive on (2 pl. imper.) · *ti* (quotation)

    "Come now, monks, I address you: conditioned things are subject to decay; strive on with diligence."

    The last words the Buddha spoke. Recite them, then rebuild the whole from the gloss. Two finite verbs, one present indicative and one imperative, and both stems belong to the same conjugation class: the eighth, whose affix the chapter's table gives twice over. Say what the two stems are, and say which of the two forms the affix takes in each. Then say why the second verb needs no pronoun to tell the monks it is addressed to them.

6. Attanā hi kataṁ pāpaṁ, attanā saṅkilissati; Attanā akataṁ pāpaṁ, attanāva visujjhati; Suddhī asuddhi paccattaṁ, nāñño aññaṁ visodhaye. (Dhp 165 — Dhp p. 24)

    *attanā* by oneself (m. inst. sg.) · *hi* indeed · *kataṁ* done (n. nom. sg., p.p.) · *pāpaṁ* evil (n. nom. sg.) · *saṅkilissati* is defiled (3 sg. pres.) · *akataṁ* not done (n. nom. sg., p.p.) · *attanā·eva* by oneself alone · *visujjhati* is purified (3 sg. pres.) · *suddhī* purity (f. nom. sg.) · *asuddhi* impurity (f. nom. sg.) · *paccattaṁ* individually (adv.) · *na·añño* no other (m. nom. sg.) · *aññaṁ* another (m. acc. sg.) · *visodhaye* can purify (3 sg. opt., causative)

    "Evil is done by oneself; by oneself one is defiled. Evil is left undone by oneself; by oneself alone one is purified. Purity and impurity are one's own; no one purifies another."

    Rebuild it from the gloss, and expect the last word to be the one that will not come. The verse's last two verbs are the same verb twice, once plain and once causative, and the chapter's section on vowel gradation is what explains the difference between them. Say what that difference is before you look it up.

### Manasānupekkhitā — the present system

Cover the chapter. These are answered from memory or not at all.

**Identify the form.** For each, give the dictionary form of the verb, its present stem, and the person, number, tense or mood, and voice of the form as it stands.

7. *suṇāma*
8. *karonti*
9. *gacchāhi*
10. *jāneyyuṁ*
11. *bhavantu*
12. *yamāmase*

**Resolve the ambiguity.** Each of these can be parsed in more than one way, or looks like something it is not. Give every reading the form allows, and say what in a sentence would decide between them.

13. *gacchāma*
14. *desetha*
15. *siyā*
16. *vuccati*

### Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — the present system

**Read the verse.** Give each a word-by-word gloss and then an idiomatic rendering, resolving any sandhi in the gloss. Account for every finite verb: its stem, its mood, and, where the mood allows more than one sense, which sense the verse needs.

17. Manopubbaṅgamā dhammā, manoseṭṭhā manomayā; Manasā ce paduṭṭhena, bhāsati vā karoti vā; Tato naṁ dukkhamanveti, cakkaṁva vahato padaṁ. (Dhp 1 — Dhp p. 1)
18. Na hi verena verāni, sammantīdha kudācanaṁ; Averena ca sammanti, esa dhammo sanantano. (Dhp 5 — Dhp p. 2)
19. Hīnaṁ dhammaṁ na seveyya, pamādena na saṁvase; Micchādiṭṭhiṁ na seveyya, na siyā lokavaḍḍhano. (Dhp 167 — Dhp p. 25)

**One imperative, two numbers.** The chapter gives the third person singular imperative that opens a formal act. Here is the same formula when the act is done before a group instead of the whole Sangha.

20. Suṇantu me āyasmantā. (Bu Np 1 — Vin iii 197) — Gloss it and render it. Name the verb's person, number and mood, and say what *me* is. Then say what changes and what does not between this and *Suṇātu me, bhante, saṅgho*.

**Two verbs for 'is'.** The chapter says the difference between *atthi* and *hoti* "is not absolute but it is the way to bet". Here is each of them doing the job the other could not.

21. Atthi imasmiṁ kāye kesā lomā nakhā dantā taco … (MN 10 — M i 57)

    Jātassa maraṇaṁ hoti, jāto dukkhāni phussati; (SN 5.6 — S i 132)

    Say which verb each line uses and what it is asserting. Then swap them, and say what each sentence would then mean, or fail to mean. One of the two lines also has its verb disagreeing in number with what follows it; find it, and say whether that is an error.

**Put it back into Pāli.** Every item so far has run from a printed form to the mood behind it. Now run it the other way. Each phrase below occurs in a line already in this set. Give the Pāli from memory, naming the mood and the person and number of the verb, and only then look back at the line it came from.

22. Four phrases, one verb each:

    (a) "they fear death" — Dhp 129 — Dhp p. 19

    (b) "let none wish suffering upon another" — Snp 1.8 — Sn p. 26

    (c) "strive on with diligence" — DN 16 — D ii 156

    (d) "let the venerables listen to me" — Bu Np 1 — Vin iii 197

### Coming back — the present system

The set is not finished on the day it is worked. Re-attempt these, from cold, without reading the answers first.

**Day 3.** Items 1–4 aloud, and 7–12. The stem goes before everything else, because every form in the chapter is built on it.

**Day 10.** Items 13–16, and 20. If the three overlapping cells still come out as overlaps and not as errors, the paradigm has been understood, not memorised.

**Day 30.** Items 5, 6 and 22. If (b) and (c) in 22 still come out, the optative and the imperative have separated for good.

## Exercises: The Past and the Future

The chapter's four aorist types are one habit to acquire, not four rules to memorise: look for the augment, then ask what is left. So the third stage gives you six aorists cold, and the fourth gives you three of them standing together in one paragraph of narrative, as they are actually met.

Between the two lies the point the chapter argues hardest: a tense in Pāli does not always do what its name suggests. A future can state a general law, an aorist can carry a prohibition, and the conditional exists chiefly to say what did not happen. The first two stages put that in front of you before any of it is named, because these are forms a reader has already heard hundreds of times without parsing one. Every Pāli line below is canonical and cited; none has been composed. Worked answers are at the back.

### Sotānugata — the past and the future

Nothing is withheld. Each line comes with its gloss and its rendering, and the work is to say it and to hear what the tense does.

1. Idamavoca bhagavā. Attamanā te bhikkhū bhagavato bhāsitaṁ abhinandunti. (MN 10 — M i 63)

    *idam·avoca* < *idaṁ* this (n. acc. sg.) + *avoca* said (3 sg. aor.) · *bhagavā* the Blessed One (m. nom. sg.) · *attamanā* satisfied (m. nom. pl.) · *te* those (m. nom. pl.) · *bhikkhū* monks (m. nom. pl.) · *bhagavato* of the Blessed One (m. gen. sg.) · *bhāsitaṁ* what was said (n. acc. sg., p.p.) · *abhinanduṁ* they approved (3 pl. aor.) · *ti* (quotation)

    "This is what the Blessed One said. Satisfied, those monks approved what the Blessed One had said."

    The last sentence of most of the suttas in the canon, so a reader has heard it more often than any other sentence in the language and has probably never parsed a word of it. Say it aloud. Both its finite verbs are aorists and neither looks like the present of its own verb. Separate the first word into the two it is made of, and then say which two present-tense verbs the two aorists belong to.

2. Gahakāraka diṭṭhosi, puna gehaṁ na kāhasi; Sabbā te phāsukā bhaggā, gahakūṭaṁ visaṅkhataṁ; Visaṅkhāragataṁ cittaṁ, taṇhānaṁ khayamajjhagā. (Dhp 154 — Dhp p. 23)

    *gahakāraka* house-builder (m. voc. sg.) · *diṭṭho·si* < *diṭṭho* seen (m. nom. sg., p.p.) + *asi* you are (2 sg. pres.) · *puna* again · *gehaṁ* house (n. acc. sg.) · *na* not · *kāhasi* you will build (2 sg. fut.) · *sabbā* all (f. nom. pl.) · *te* your (2 sg. gen.) · *phāsukā* rafters (f. nom. pl.) · *bhaggā* broken (f. nom. pl., p.p.) · *gahakūṭaṁ* roof-peak (n. nom. sg.) · *visaṅkhataṁ* demolished (n. nom. sg., p.p.) · *visaṅkhāragataṁ* gone to the unconditioned (n. nom. sg.) · *cittaṁ* mind (n. nom. sg.) · *taṇhānaṁ* of cravings (f. gen. pl.) · *khayam·ajjhagā* < *khayaṁ* the ending (m. acc. sg.) + *ajjhagā* has reached (3 sg.)

    "House-builder, you are seen! You will not build a house again. All your rafters are broken, your roof-peak demolished. The mind has gone to the unconditioned; it has reached the ending of cravings."

    The verse tradition gives to the Buddha at the moment of awakening. Say it aloud. Three of this chapter's topics stand in six lines: a present tense hidden in a sandhi, a future that has no *-ss-* in it anywhere, and a past whose augment you can see with the naked eye. Find all three. The last of them is *ajjhagā*, and its prefix is *adhi-*: say where the augment went.

3. Māvamaññetha pāpassa, na mantaṁ āgamissati; Udabindunipātena, udakumbhopi pūrati; Bālo pūrati pāpassa, thokaṁ thokampi ācinaṁ. (Dhp 121 — Dhp p. 18)

    *mā* do not · *avamaññetha* let one think lightly (3 sg. opt. middle) · *pāpassa* of evil (n. gen. sg.) · *na* not · *man·taṁ* < *maṁ* to me (1 sg. acc.) + *taṁ* that (n. nom. sg.) · *āgamissati* will come (3 sg. fut.) · *uda·bindu·nipātena* by the falling of drops of water (m. inst. sg.) · *udakumbho* the water-pot (m. nom. sg.) · *api* even · *pūrati* is filled (3 sg. pres.) · *bālo* the fool (m. nom. sg.) · *thokaṁ thokaṁ* little by little · *api* even · *ācinaṁ* accumulating (m. nom. sg., pres. part.)

    "Do not think lightly of evil, saying 'it will not come to me'. By the falling of drops of water even a water-pot is filled; the fool is filled with evil, accumulating it little by little."

    Say it aloud. The verse has one prohibition and one future, and neither does what its name says. The prohibition is not on the aorist that the chapter's *mā* section leads you to expect; say what mood it is on instead, and what difference that makes to how hard it pushes. And the future is not the verse's own prediction. It is the content of the complacent thought being denied. Say who, in the verse, makes that prediction.

4. Etaṁ satiṁ adhiṭṭheyya, Brahmametaṁ vihāramidhamāhu. (Snp 1.8 — Sn p. 26)

    *etaṁ* this (f. acc. sg.) · *satiṁ* mindfulness (f. acc. sg.) · *adhiṭṭheyya* should undertake (3 sg. opt.) · *brahmam·etaṁ* < *brahmaṁ* divine (m. acc. sg.) + *etaṁ* this (m. acc. sg.) · *vihāram·idham·āhu* < *vihāraṁ* abiding (m. acc. sg.) + *idha* here + *āhu* they say (3 pl. perf.)

    "One should undertake this mindfulness. This, they say, is the divine abiding here."

    Two more lines of the Mettasutta, so this too is met by ear before it is met on a page. Say them aloud. One finite verb belongs to the last chapter and one to this one, and only the second is rare: *āhu* is one of a bare handful of forms surviving from a tense Pāli otherwise lost altogether. Name the tense. Then say what present-tense verb a reader would go looking for on the strength of it, and why they would not find one.

### Vacasā paricitā — the past and the future

Recite each until it is fluent. Then cover the Pāli and rebuild it from the gloss alone, aloud. Producing a past tense is the work; recognising one is not.

5. Anekajātisaṁsāraṁ, sandhāvissaṁ anibbisaṁ; Gahakāraṁ gavesanto, dukkhā jāti punappunaṁ. (Dhp 153 — Dhp p. 23)

    *aneka·jāti·saṁsāraṁ* through the wandering-on of many births (m. acc. sg.) · *sandhāvissaṁ* I ran on (1 sg. aor.) · *anibbisaṁ* not finding (m. nom. sg., pres. part.) · *gahakāraṁ* the house-builder (m. acc. sg.) · *gavesanto* seeking (m. nom. sg., pres. part.) · *dukkhā* painful (f. nom. sg.) · *jāti* birth (f. nom. sg.) · *punappunaṁ* again and again

    "Through the wandering-on of many births I have run, not finding, seeking the builder of this house — painful is birth again and again."

    The verse before item 2, and the two are one utterance. Rebuild this one from the gloss. Two of its words end in *-aṁ* and neither is what that ending would suggest, which is the whole difficulty of the line: one has the ending the chapter's conditional table gives for the first person singular and is an aorist, and the other looks like a first person singular and is not a finite verb at all. Say which is which before you look at the answer.

6. Tena kho pana samayena aññataro bhikkhu divā bhaṇḍaṁ passitvā nimittaṁ akāsi—"rattiṁ avaharissāmī"ti. So taṁ maññamāno attano bhaṇḍaṁ avahari. (Bu Pj 2 — Vin iii 57)

    *tena kho pana samayena* now at that time · *aññataro* a certain (m. nom. sg.) · *bhikkhu* monk (m. nom. sg.) · *divā* by day · *bhaṇḍaṁ* goods (n. acc. sg.) · *passitvā* having seen (absol.) · *nimittaṁ* a mental note (n. acc. sg.) · *akāsi* made (3 sg. aor.) · *rattiṁ* at night (f. acc. sg.) · *avaharissāmi* I will steal (1 sg. fut.) · *ti* (quotation) · *so* he (m. nom. sg.) · *taṁ* that (n. acc. sg.) · *maññamāno* thinking it to be (m. nom. sg., pres. part. middle) · *attano* his own (m. gen. sg.) · *bhaṇḍaṁ* goods (n. acc. sg.) · *avahari* stole (3 sg. aor.)

    "Now at that time a certain monk saw some goods by day and made a mental note: 'I'll steal them at night.' Thinking it was that, he stole his own goods."

    A case from the rule against stealing, and one of the few in the Vinaya that is funny. Rebuild it from the gloss. The same verb stands here twice, once in each tense this chapter is about. Say which two forms they are, and say what the difference between them costs the monk. Then say what *akāsi* is, and why someone who knows only *karoti* will not recognise it.

### Manasānupekkhitā — the past and the future

Cover the chapter. These are answered from memory or not at all.

**Name the type.** Each of these is an aorist. Say which of the four types it belongs to, give its person and number, and name the verb it comes from.

7. *akāsi*
8. *upasaṅkamiṁsu*
9. *agā*
10. *addasaṁ*
11. *assosi*
12. *avocumha*

**Take the tense apart.** Each of these is a future or a conditional. Say which, and show how the form is built from the stem you would find in a dictionary.

13. *gamissanti*
14. *lacchasi*
15. *abhavissa*
16. *hehiti*
17. *ñassati*

### Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — the past and the future

**Decide what the form does.** Each of these sentences turns on a point this chapter makes. Say what the marked verb is, and what it does in the sentence, which is not always what its tense would suggest.

18. Mā bhāyi, vakkali. (SN 22.87 — S iii 122)

19. Na vatimāni manussabhūtassa padāni bhavissantī'ti. (AN 4.36 — A ii 37)

20. Atthi, kassapa, maggo atthi paṭipadā, yathāpaṭipanno sāmaṁyeva ñassati sāmaṁ dakkhati. (DN 8 — D i 165)

**Read the passage.** Gloss it word by word, then render it, then answer the question that follows.

21. No cedaṁ, bhikkhave, paṇḍito sucintitacintī ca abhavissa subhāsitabhāsī ca sukatakammakārī ca, kena naṁ paṇḍitā jāneyyuṁ. (AN 3.3 — A i 102) — Two different moods are at work here, one in each clause. Which is in which, and why that way round?

22. Atha kho te bhikkhū sāvatthiyaṁ piṇḍāya caritvā pacchābhattaṁ piṇḍapātapaṭikkantā yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkamiṁsu; upasaṅkamitvā bhagavantaṁ abhivādetvā ekamantaṁ nisīdiṁsu. Ekamantaṁ nisinnā kho te bhikkhū bhagavantaṁ etadavocuṁ. (AN 10.27 — A v 49) — There are three finite verbs here and they are not all the same type of aorist. Sort them, and say what that mix teaches about how to approach an unfamiliar past tense.

**Read the case law.** The Vibhaṅga records what happened, decides it, and moves on. Its narrative is the plainest prose in the canon and it is almost entirely aorist, which makes it the best place to practise one.

23. Tena kho pana samayena aññataro bhikkhu ajjhokāse cīvaraṁ pattharitvā vihāraṁ pāvisi. (Bu Pj 2 — Vin iii 57) — There is one finite verb. Name it, say what it comes from, and find its augment. The chapter's rule about where an augment goes when a verb has a prefix will get you most of the way; the last step needs a dictionary.

24. Tena kho pana samayena sambahulā bhikkhū gijjhakūṭā pabbatā orohantā sīhavighāsaṁ passitvā pacāpetvā paribhuñjiṁsu. (Bu Pj 2 — Vin iii 58) — Gloss it, then render it. Four verb forms stand here and only one of them is finite: say what the other three are, and what fixes the time of the whole sentence. One of the three is a causative, and the monks' innocence depends on it.

**What happened next.** Item 23 leaves a robe on the ground and a monk indoors. A few cases later the Vibhaṅga runs the same situation again with a bowl, and this is how that one goes.

25. Aññataro bhikkhu—"māyaṁ patto nassī"ti paṭisāmesi. So nikkhamitvā taṁ bhikkhuṁ pucchi—"āvuso, mayhaṁ patto kena avahaṭo"ti? So evamāha—"mayā avahaṭo"ti. So taṁ ādiyi, "assamaṇosi tvan"ti. (Bu Pj 2 — Vin iii 57)

    Gloss it and render it. Four finite verbs stand here, and between them they cover most of this chapter. Three are aorists of three different shapes; the fourth is not an aorist at all and belongs to the tense item 4 introduced. Sort them. Then find the *mā* construction in the first sentence, say what verb form it governs, and say why the sense here is a wish, not a prohibition. The chapter says the two shade into each other, and here they do.

**Put it back into Pāli.** Every item so far has run from a printed form to the tense behind it. Now run it the other way. Each phrase below occurs in a line already in this set. Give the Pāli from memory, naming the tense and the person and number of the verb, and only then look back at the line it came from.

26. Four phrases, one verb each:

    (a) "you will not build again" — Dhp 154 — Dhp p. 23

    (b) "this, they say" — Snp 1.8 — Sn p. 26

    (c) "I will steal them at night" — Bu Pj 2 — Vin iii 57
