    (d) "he stole" — Bu Pj 2 — Vin iii 57

### Coming back — the past and the future

The set is not finished on the day it is worked. Re-attempt these, from cold, without reading the answers first.

**Day 3.** Items 1–4 aloud, and 7–12. The four types are the chapter, and no amount of reading about them substitutes for having six in the mouth.

**Day 10.** Items 13–17 and 25. If the four verbs in 25 sort themselves without the chapter open, the aorist has stopped being a table.

**Day 30.** Items 5, 6 and 26. If (c) and (d) in 26 still come out as two different tenses of one verb, the future and the past have separated where they are hardest to keep apart.

## Exercises: Passive, Causative and Denominative

This set is longer than the ones before it, because five formations have to be told apart and two of the five are traps. The four stages do what they have done since the first set, but the weight has moved: stage 3 is the heart of this one, since a formation you cannot name from memory is one you will not spot in a sentence, and stage 4 is unusually long because reading at length is what all the rest is for.

Stage 1 is four lines a reader has almost certainly already heard, two of them carrying the plainest passives in the canon and two of them carrying causatives nobody thinks of as causatives. Every Pāli line below is canonical and cited; none has been composed. Worked answers are at the back.

### Sotānugata — derived conjugations

Nothing is withheld. Each line comes with its gloss and its rendering, and the work is to say it and to hear which stem carries the meaning.

1. ayaṁ vuccati jarā. (SN 12.2 — S ii 2)

    *ayaṁ* this (f. nom. sg.) · *vuccati* is called (3 sg. pres. pass.) · *jarā* old age (f. nom. sg.)

    "This is called old age."

    Three words, and the middle one is a passive with an active ending. Say it aloud. This is the formula that closes every term in the analysis of dependent origination, so anyone who has been through that analysis once has heard it twelve times in a row. Say what in the form shows it is passive and, having failed to find anything, say where the information actually is.

2. Ruppatī'ti kho, bhikkhave, tasmā 'rūpa'nti vuccati. (SN 22.79 — S iii 86)

    *ruppati* is afflicted (3 sg. pres. pass.) · *ti* (quotation) · *kho* indeed · *bhikkhave* monks (m. voc. pl.) · *tasmā* therefore · *rūpaṁ* 'form' (n. nom. sg.) · *ti* (quotation) · *vuccati* it is called (3 sg. pres. pass.)

    "'It is afflicted', monks — that is why it is called 'form'."

    Say it aloud. Both finite verbs here are passives, and one of them is the word you were listening for while the other is not. Find the second. Then say why the doctrine in this sentence would not work if the verb were active.

3. Mettañca sabbalokasmi, Mānasaṁ bhāvaye aparimāṇaṁ; Uddhaṁ adho ca tiriyañca, Asambādhaṁ averamasapattaṁ. (Snp 1.8 — Sn p. 26)

    *mettaṁ* loving-kindness (f. acc. sg.) · *ca* and · *sabbalokasmi* in the whole world (m. loc. sg.) · *mānasaṁ* heart, mind (n. acc. sg.) · *bhāvaye* should develop (3 sg. opt., causative) · *aparimāṇaṁ* boundless (n. acc. sg.) · *uddhaṁ* above · *adho* below · *tiriyaṁ* across · *asambādhaṁ* unobstructed (n. acc. sg.) · *avera·masapattaṁ* < *averaṁ* without hatred (n. acc. sg.) + *asapattaṁ* without enmity (n. acc. sg.)

    "And towards the whole world one should develop a boundless heart of loving-kindness — above, below, across, unobstructed, without hatred, without enmity."

    A third helping of the Mettasutta, which by now should be coming without the page. Say it aloud. The verb is a causative and almost nobody notices, because the word it has become is one of the commonest in the vocabulary of practice. Say what verb it is built on, and then say what the causative literally does to that verb's meaning, and whether any of that literal sense survives in the line.

4. Sabbadā ve sukhaṁ seti, brāhmaṇo parinibbuto; Yo na limpati kāmesu, sītibhūto nirūpadhi. (SN 10.8 — S i 212)

    *sabbadā* always · *ve* indeed · *sukhaṁ* at ease (adv.) · *seti* sleeps (3 sg. pres.) · *brāhmaṇo* the brahmin (m. nom. sg.) · *parinibbuto* fully quenched (m. nom. sg., p.p.) · *yo* who (m. nom. sg.) · *na* not · *limpati* clings, is smeared (3 sg. pres.) · *kāmesu* in sensual pleasures (m. loc. pl.) · *sītibhūto* become cool (m. nom. sg.) · *nirūpadhi* free of attachment (m. nom. sg.)

    "A brahmin fully quenched always sleeps at ease — one who does not cling to sensual pleasures, cooled, without attachment."

    The Buddha's answer to the question that closes item 30. Read it aloud five times, slowly, holding the long vowels for two beats and sounding both halves of every doubled consonant. Then say what you notice about where the long syllables fall. Then answer: not one word in this verse is a derived stem of any of the five kinds. But one of its two finite verbs raises the chapter's question anyway. Which, and what is the question?

### Vacasā paricitā — derived conjugations

Recite each until it is fluent. Then cover the Pāli and rebuild it from the gloss alone, aloud. Both are stock formulas, which is the point: the canon teaches vocabulary by repetition, and a formula in the mouth is several words learnt at once.

5. Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhu anuppannānaṁ pāpakānaṁ akusalānaṁ dhammānaṁ anuppādāya chandaṁ janeti vāyamati vīriyaṁ ārabhati cittaṁ paggaṇhāti padahati. (AN 4.13 — A ii 15)

    *idha* here · *bhikkhave* monks (m. voc. pl.) · *bhikkhu* a monk (m. nom. sg.) · *anuppannānaṁ* unarisen (m. gen. pl.) · *pāpakānaṁ* bad (m. gen. pl.) · *akusalānaṁ* unwholesome (m. gen. pl.) · *dhammānaṁ* of states (m. gen. pl.) · *anuppādāya* for the non-arising (m. dat. sg.) · *chandaṁ* desire (m. acc. sg.) · *janeti* generates (3 sg. pres. caus.) · *vāyamati* strives · *vīriyaṁ* energy (n. acc. sg.) · *ārabhati* arouses · *cittaṁ* the mind (n. acc. sg.) · *paggaṇhāti* exerts · *padahati* endeavours

    "Here, monks, a monk generates desire for the non-arising of unarisen bad, unwholesome states; he strives, arouses energy, exerts the mind and endeavours."

    The first of the four right efforts. Five finite verbs in a row and only one of them is derived. Rebuild the sentence from the gloss, then say which one, and what it is built on.

6. Idhānanda, bhikkhu sabbasaṅkhāresu aṭṭīyati harāyati jigucchati. (AN 10.60 — A v 111)

    *idha* here · *ānanda* Ānanda (m. voc. sg.) · *bhikkhu* a monk (m. nom. sg.) · *sabbasaṅkhāresu* in all conditioned things (m. loc. pl.) · *aṭṭīyati* is troubled (3 sg. pres.) · *harāyati* is ashamed (3 sg. pres. denom.) · *jigucchati* is disgusted (3 sg. pres. desid.)

    "Here, Ānanda, a monk is troubled, ashamed and disgusted by all conditioned things."

    Rebuild it. Three verbs in a row and three different answers, and so it is here and not in stage 1: one is a denominative, one is one of the five surviving desideratives, and the third is the word the sources cannot agree how to spell. Say which is which.

### Manasānupekkhitā — derived conjugations

Cover the chapter. These are answered from memory or not at all, and this stage is the long one because this is the chapter where naming the formation *is* the skill.

**Recall.** Answer these in a phrase, before opening anything. If one will not come, that is the item to go back to.

7. Which three of the five derived stems are productive, and what does "productive" mean about a category?
8. In Pāli, what carries the difference between an active verb and a passive one: the personal endings, or the stem?
9. Name the three routes by which the passive suffix reaches a stem.
10. How many objects does a causative verb govern, compared with the verb it is built on?
11. The desiderative and the intensive are unproductive. What does that mean you should *do* on meeting one?

**Identify the formation.** For each form: which of the five formations is it, what is it built on, and what does it mean? Two of these are traps; say what the trap is.

12. *labbhati*
13. *kārāpeti*
14. *namassati*
15. *caṅkamati*
16. *sādiyati*
17. *titikkhati*
18. *dissati*
19. *deseti*

**Build the form.** Give the form, and show the step that produces it.

20. The passive of *labhati* 'gets'.
21. The passive of *pajahati* 'abandons'.
22. The causative of *bhavati* 'is'.
23. The causative of *karoti* 'does': both the simple form and the one with *-āpe-*.
24. The third person plural present passive of *pajahati*.

### Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — derived conjugations

**Read the sentence.** Gloss word by word, render it, and name the derived stems at work.

25. … Satisambojjhaṅgaṁ tasmiṁ samaye bhikkhu bhāveti, satisambojjhaṅgo tasmiṁ samaye bhikkhuno bhāvanāpāripūriṁ gacchati. (MN 118 — M iii 85)

26. Yo pana bhikkhu pathaviṁ khaṇeyya vā khaṇāpeyya vā, pācittiyan'ti. (Bu Pc 10 — Vin iv 33) — you have met this one in the chapter. Do it again without looking, and say what the rule turns on.

**Put it back into Pāli.** Each of these is the rendering of a real sentence. Reconstruct the Pāli. The words you need are given; what you have to supply is the derived stem and the ending. Do not settle for a sentence that merely works; the answer is a particular line of canon, and getting close to it is the exercise.

27. "This is called death.": *idaṁ*, the verb 'is called', *maraṇa*.

28. "Let the Blessed One teach the Dhamma; let the Holy One teach the Dhamma.": *bhagavā*, *sugata*, *dhamma*, *bhante*, and the causative of *dis-*, in the imperative.

29. "And one should not cherish anything in the world.": *na*, *ca*, *kiñci*, *lokasmiṁ*, and the denominative of *mama*, in the optative middle.

**Read at length.** This is not a drill. Read the following passage through, twice, without stopping to parse: the first time for the build of the sentences, the second for the sense. Then go back and answer the questions under it.

30. Tena kho pana samayena bhagavā rattiyā paccūsasamayaṁ paccuṭṭhāya abbhokāse caṅkamati. Addasā kho bhagavā anāthapiṇḍikaṁ gahapatiṁ dūratova āgacchantaṁ. Disvāna caṅkamā orohitvā paññatte āsane nisīdi. Nisajja kho bhagavā anāthapiṇḍikaṁ gahapatiṁ etadavoca: "ehi, sudattā"ti. Atha kho anāthapiṇḍiko gahapati, nāmena maṁ bhagavā ālapatīti, haṭṭho udaggo tattheva bhagavato pādesu sirasā nipatitvā bhagavantaṁ etadavoca: "kacci, bhante, bhagavā sukhamasayitthā"ti? (SN 10.8 — S i 212)

    - *Caṅkamati* and *caṅkamā* stand in consecutive sentences. What is the difference between them, and what does the second one being a noun tell you about the first?
    - *Orohitvā* is built on the verb that also gives the chapter's causative *voropeti*. Having read this passage, say what *voropeti* literally does to somebody.
    - *Sukhamasayittha* is a verb form Chapter 7 warned you about and Chapter 8 gave you the tense of. What is it, and why is it in this sentence instead of an ordinary *-i* aorist?
    - Nothing here is a passive. Say why not: what is it about this kind of narrative that keeps the passive out of it?

**Reaching back.** Four items reaching over earlier chapters. Each is a place where what you learnt there and what you learnt here have to be held together.

31. Chapter 7 gives *deseti* as its specimen verb of the eighth conjugation class. Chapter 9 gives *deseti* as a causative. Are both true, and if so, what is the relation between the two statements?

32. Chapter 8 taught *bhavissati* 'it will be'. Chapter 9 taught *bhāveti* 'develops'. Both are built on *bhū-*, and neither is the other. Set out how each is formed, and name the single feature that tells them apart on sight.

33. Chapter 6. The verse at SN 10.8 — S i 211, two pages before the passage in item 30, reads Kalaṁ nāgghanti soḷasiṁ. What kind of numeral is *soḷasiṁ*, what is it agreeing with, and what is the idiom saying?

34. Chapter 5. In item 30, Anāthapiṇḍika thinks nāmena maṁ bhagavā ālapati. Why *maṁ* and not the enclitic *me*?

**A causative in the case law.** One sentence from the Vinaya's account of how the rule against theft came to be laid down.

35. Atha kho bhagavā etasmiṁ nidāne etasmiṁ pakaraṇe bhikkhusaṅghaṁ sannipātāpetvā chabbaggiye bhikkhū paṭipucchi. (Bu Pj 2 — Vin iii 46) — Gloss it and render it. One of the two verb forms is a causative: name it, say what it is the causative of, and say what difference it makes to who did what.

### Coming back — derived conjugations

The set is not finished on the day it is worked. Re-attempt these, from cold, without reading the answers first.

**Day 3.** Items 7–11 and 12–19. The five formations and the two traps go before anything else, because everything after them is an application of the naming.

**Day 10.** Items 20–24 and 35. If the causative of *karoti* still comes out in both its shapes, the *-āpe-* has stopped being a spelling.

**Day 30.** Items 1–6 aloud, and 27–29. If item 29 comes out with a middle ending on it, three chapters have joined up: the *attanopada* of Chapter 7, the optative of Chapter 7, and the denominative of this one.

## Exercises: Participles

The chapter's one rule to carry into every sentence you meet from here on is that a past participle's voice depends on the verb it is built from, not on the participle itself. Everything else in this set (the past active participle, the gerundive's true name, the bare-*-a* type) is less a new rule than a new place to look.

A participle is the commonest thing in Pāli that a reader half-parses and moves past, so stage 1 gives four lines in which the participle is the whole sentence. Stage 4 tests the voice rule. Every Pāli line below is canonical and cited; none has been composed. Worked answers are at the back.

### Sotānugata — participles

Nothing is withheld. Each line comes with its gloss and its rendering, and the work is to say it and to hear which participle is carrying the sentence.

1. Idha, mahāli, bhikkhu tiṇṇaṁ saṁyojanānaṁ parikkhayā sotāpanno hoti avinipātadhammo niyato sambodhiparāyaṇo. (DN 6 — D i 156)

    *idha* here · *mahāli* Mahāli (m. voc. sg.) · *bhikkhu* a monk (m. nom. sg.) · *tiṇṇaṁ saṁyojanānaṁ* of the three fetters (n. gen. pl.) · *parikkhayā* through the complete ending (m. abl. sg.) · *sotāpanno* a stream-enterer (m. nom. sg., p.p.) · *hoti* is · *avinipātadhammo* not liable to fall to a lower realm (m. nom. sg.) · *niyato* bound, destined (m. nom. sg., p.p.) · *sambodhiparāyaṇo* headed for awakening (m. nom. sg.)

    "Here, Mahāli, through the complete ending of the three fetters a monk is a stream-enterer, not liable to fall to a lower realm, bound for and headed toward awakening."

    Say it aloud. The most consequential word in the sentence is a past participle being used as a noun. Say which one, and say what verb it is built on. Then say which of the other words in the predicate are participles and which only look like them.

2. Tiṭṭhaṁ caraṁ nisinno va, Sayāno yāvatāssa vitamiddho; (Snp 1.8 — Sn p. 26)

    *tiṭṭhaṁ* standing (m. nom. sg., pres. part.) · *caraṁ* walking (m. nom. sg., pres. part.) · *nisinno* seated (m. nom. sg., p.p.) · *va* or · *sayāno* lying down (m. nom. sg., pres. part.) · *yāvatā·assa* < *yāvatā* as long as + *assa* he is · *vitamiddho* free of drowsiness (m. nom. sg.)

    "Standing, walking, seated or lying down, as long as he is free of drowsiness …"

    Say it aloud. Four postures, and three different participle endings inside them. Set 08 took the second half of this same verse for its perfect; here the first half is wanted for something else. Name the ending on each of the four words, and say which one belongs to the older type that only a handful of verbs keep; the answer to that is also the answer to item 11.

3. The first sermon turns each of the four truths three times, and the second and third turns are this chapter's two participles set against each other.

    Taṁ kho panidaṁ dukkhaṁ ariyasaccaṁ pariññeyyan'ti. (SN 56.11 — S v 422)

    Taṁ kho panidaṁ dukkhaṁ ariyasaccaṁ pariññātan'ti. (SN 56.11 — S v 422)

    Taṁ kho panidaṁ dukkhasamudayaṁ ariyasaccaṁ pahātabban'ti. (SN 56.11 — S v 422)

    Taṁ kho panidaṁ dukkhasamudayaṁ ariyasaccaṁ pahīnan'ti. (SN 56.11 — S v 422)

    *taṁ kho panidaṁ* now this · *dukkhaṁ ariyasaccaṁ* the noble truth of suffering (n. nom. sg.) · *pariññeyyaṁ* is to be fully understood (n. nom. sg., gerundive) · *pariññātaṁ* has been fully understood (n. nom. sg., p.p.) · *dukkhasamudayaṁ ariyasaccaṁ* the noble truth of the origin of suffering (n. nom. sg.) · *pahātabbaṁ* is to be abandoned (n. nom. sg., gerundive) · *pahīnaṁ* has been abandoned (n. nom. sg., p.p.) · *ti* (quotation)

    "'Now this noble truth of suffering is to be fully understood.' … 'Now this noble truth of suffering has been fully understood.' … 'Now this noble truth of the origin of suffering is to be abandoned.' … 'Now this noble truth of the origin of suffering has been abandoned.'"

    Say all four aloud, in order. The third and fourth truths do the same thing with *sacchikātabbaṁ* against *sacchikataṁ* and *bhāvetabbaṁ* against *bhāvitaṁ*. Eight words, four verbs, two participles apiece, and between them they carry the whole doctrinal architecture of the sermon. Say what the difference between the two members of each pair is, in one sentence, without using the words "gerundive" or "past participle".

4. Yathā yathā vā panassa kāyo paṇihito hoti tathā tathā naṁ pajānāti. (MN 10 — M i 57)

    *yathā yathā vā* in whatever way · *pana·assa* now, for him · *kāyo* the body (m. nom. sg.) · *paṇihito* positioned, disposed (m. nom. sg., p.p.) · *hoti* is · *tathā tathā* in just that way · *naṁ* it (m. acc. sg.) · *pajānāti* he understands

    "And however his body is positioned, that is how he understands it."

    This follows directly on the four-postures passage the chapter quotes at 6.1, and it sums that passage up. Say it aloud. One participle, and it does what four finite verbs did in the sentence before it. Say what *paṇihito* is built on, and why a generalising sentence of this kind needs a participle, not a verb.

### Vacasā paricitā — participles

Recite each until it is fluent. Then cover the Pāli and rebuild it from the gloss alone, aloud. Both are formulas, and a formula in the mouth is several participles learnt at once.

5. 'Khīṇā jāti, vusitaṁ brahmacariyaṁ, kataṁ karaṇīyaṁ, nāparaṁ itthattāyā'ti pajānāti. (SN 22.79 — S iii 90)

    *khīṇā* exhausted (f. nom. sg., p.p.) · *jāti* birth (f. nom. sg.) · *vusitaṁ* lived (n. nom. sg., p.p.) · *brahmacariyaṁ* the spiritual life (n. nom. sg.) · *kataṁ* done (n. nom. sg., p.p.) · *karaṇīyaṁ* what had to be done (n. nom. sg., gerundive) · *na* not · *aparaṁ* further (n. nom. sg.) · *itthattāya* for this state of being (m. dat. sg.) · *ti* (quotation) · *pajānāti* he understands

    "He understands: 'Birth is exhausted, the spiritual life has been lived, what had to be done has been done, there is no further coming to any state of being.'"

    The arahant's declaration, fixed word for word across the canon. Rebuild it from the gloss. Four participles in eight words, and one of them is not a past participle at all. Find it, and say why it stands next to the past participle of the very same verb.

6. Adinnaṁ nāma yaṁ adinnaṁ anissaṭṭhaṁ apariccattaṁ rakkhitaṁ gopitaṁ mamāyitaṁ parapariggahitaṁ. Etaṁ adinnaṁ nāma. (Bu Pj 2)

    *adinnaṁ nāma* "what is not given" means · *yaṁ* which (n. nom. sg.) · *adinnaṁ* not given · *anissaṭṭhaṁ* not let go of · *apariccattaṁ* not relinquished · *rakkhitaṁ* guarded · *gopitaṁ* protected · *mamāyitaṁ* treated as one's own · *parapariggahitaṁ* possessed by another · *etaṁ* this (n. nom. sg.)

    "'What is not given' means: what is not given, not let go of, not relinquished; what is guarded, protected, treated as one's own, possessed by another. That is what 'what is not given' means."

    The Vinaya defining theft's object by piling up past participles until nothing is left out. Rebuild the list from the gloss. It is harder than it looks, and the order is not arbitrary. Every word between *yaṁ* and the full stop is a past participle. Say what each is from, sort them into the ones carrying the negative prefix and the ones not, and say what gender and number they are all in and why.

### Manasānupekkhitā — participles

Cover the chapter. These are answered from memory or not at all.

**Recall.** Answer these in a phrase, before opening anything.

7. What does a participle inherit from a verb, and what from an adjective?
8. State in one sentence which verbs give the past participle a passive sense and which give it an active sense.
9. Name the two participle types no coursebook consulted for this chapter taught on its own, and say what each is built from.
10. Why is "future participle" a misleading name for the gerundive?
11. Which verb keeps the older, bare *-āna* present-participle ending on the plain root, with no *-m-* at all?

**Identify the formation.** For each: which participle is it, what voice does it carry, and what is it built from? Two of these are traps; say what the trap is.

12. *gato*
13. *desito*
14. *kātabbaṁ*
15. *vusitavā*
16. *karonto*
17. *dukkaraṁ*
18. *asantaṁ*
19. *sotāpanno*

**Build the form.** Give the form, and show the step that produces it.

20. The past participle of *dadāti* 'gives'.
21. The gerundive, *-tabba* type, of *pajahati* 'abandons'.
22. The past active participle of the verb behind *vijitāvī*'s own root sense, 'has conquered'.
23. The present participle (*-(n)t* type, nom. sg. m.) of *jānāti* 'knows'.
24. The present participle (*-māna* type) of *vedeti* 'feels'.

### Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — participles

**Put it back into Pāli.** Each of these is the rendering of a real sentence. Reconstruct the Pāli. The words you need are given; what you have to supply is the participle. Do not settle for a sentence that merely works; the answer is a particular line of canon, and getting close to it is the exercise.

25. "House-builder, you are seen!": *gahakāraka* (vocative), the past participle of *dassati*, and the second person of *atthi*, fused onto it.

26. "A living being is not to be killed.": *pāṇa-*, *na*, the *-tabba* gerundive of *hanati* 'kills'.

**Read at length.** Read the following passage through, twice, without stopping to parse: the first time for the build of the sentences, the second for the sense. Then go back and answer the questions under it.

27. *"Bhojanaṁ, suppavāse, dentī ariyasāvikā paṭiggāhakānaṁ cattāri ṭhānāni deti. Katamāni cattāri? Āyuṁ deti, vaṇṇaṁ deti, sukhaṁ deti, balaṁ deti. Āyuṁ kho pana datvā āyussa bhāginī hoti dibbassa vā mānusassa vā. Vaṇṇaṁ datvā vaṇṇassa bhāginī hoti dibbassa vā mānusassa vā. Sukhaṁ datvā sukhassa bhāginī hoti dibbassa vā mānusassa vā. Balaṁ datvā balassa bhāginī hoti dibbassa vā mānusassa vā. Bhojanaṁ, suppavāse, dentī ariyasāvikā paṭiggāhakānaṁ imāni cattāri ṭhānāni detīti.* (AN 4.57 — A ii 63)

    - *Dentī* opens the passage and closes it, framing everything between. What kind of participle is it, and what does its feminine ending tell you about who is speaking?
    - *Datvā*, repeated four times, is not a participle this chapter has taught. What is it, and which chapter will give it its own section?
    - *Bhāginī* 'sharer' looks like it could be a participle and is not. What is it actually, and what does the resemblance tell you about how the possessive adjectives in *-in* and the past active participle in *-in* were built?
    - Nothing in this passage is a gerundive, although the whole passage is about an obligation of sorts: giving food well. Why would a writer choose four plain finite verbs (*deti*) over four gerundives here?

**Test the voice rule.** The chapter's central claim is that a past participle's voice comes from the verb underneath it. This set has now printed enough past participles to check the claim, not take it on trust.

28. Take *sotāpanno* from item 1, *pariññātaṁ* and *pahīnaṁ* from item 3, and *khīṇā*, *vusitaṁ* and *kataṁ* from item 5. Sort all six into the ones that mean "having done" and the ones that mean "having been done". Then, for each, name the verb it is built on and say whether that verb is transitive. If the rule holds, the two sortings agree. Say whether they do, and say what you would have to conclude about any that did not.

**Reaching back.** Two items reaching over earlier chapters.

29. Chapter 9 taught *kāreti* as the causative of *karoti*. Chapter 10 corrected a source's *kārayamāna*, given as the plain present middle participle of *karoti*, to *kurumāno*/*kubbamāno*, while noting that *kārayamāna* is itself a real word meaning something else. What does it actually mean, which chapter's material shows you that, and why would looking it up as *kārayamāno* have suggested it did not exist?

30. Chapter 7 gave *atthi*'s irregular present as *asmi*, *amhi*, *amha*. Chapter 10's *santo* is the very same verb's present participle. Which paradigm does it inflect by, and why is that not the surprise it first looks like?

**Say it aloud.** The verse that gives this chapter its most famous line, and which set 08 took for its tenses.

31. Gahakāraka diṭṭhosi, puna gehaṁ na kāhasi; sabbā te phāsukā bhaggā, gahakūṭaṁ visaṅkhataṁ; visaṅkhāragataṁ cittaṁ, taṇhānaṁ khayamajjhagā. (Dhp 154 — Dhp p. 23) — Read it aloud several times, holding the long vowels for two beats and the doubled consonants for the full length of both letters. Then answer: two words in the second half share a root that means 'construct, put together' — *visaṅkhataṁ* and *visaṅkhāragataṁ*. The first describes the roof-peak; the second describes the mind. What does the verse achieve by using the same root twice, once literally and once figuratively?

### Coming back — participles

The set is not finished on the day it is worked. Re-attempt these, from cold, without reading the answers first.

**Day 3.** Items 7–11 and 12–19. The voice rule and the two under-taught types go before anything else, because every item after them is an application.

**Day 10.** Items 20–24 and 28. If the two sortings in 28 still agree without the chapter open, the rule has been understood, not memorised.

**Day 30.** Items 3, 5 and 6 aloud, and 25–26. If item 6's seven participles come back in the right order, the Vinaya's way of defining a thing by exhausting it has become legible.

## Exercises: Absolutives and Infinitives

Both formations in this chapter are indeclinable, and that one fact generates most of the work below. An absolutive cannot agree with anything, so it cannot tell you whose action it names, and the reader supplies that from the main verb, except in the one construction where the default fails. An infinitive cannot decline either, so Pāli reaches for a dative noun where English would reach for "to".

The set drills two things in parallel: recognising the forms, which is mechanical, and deciding what they attach to, which is not. Stage 1 is four lines in which an absolutive or an infinitive carries the sentence, and in stage 4 the agent question gets decided, not assumed. Every Pāli line below is canonical and cited; none has been composed. Worked answers are at the back.

### Sotānugata — absolutives and infinitives

Nothing is withheld. Each line comes with its gloss and its rendering, and the work is to say it and to hear what the non-finite form is attached to.

1. Idhāvuso, bhikkhu satthāraṁ upanissāya viharati aññataraṁ vā garuṭṭhāniyaṁ sabrahmacāriṁ, yatthassa tibbaṁ hirottappaṁ paccupaṭṭhitaṁ hoti pemañca gāravo ca. (DN 34 — D iii 284)

    *idha* here · *āvuso* friend (voc.) · *bhikkhu* a monk (m. nom. sg.) · *satthāraṁ* the Teacher (m. acc. sg.) · *upanissāya* depending on, close to (absol. as postposition, + acc.) · *viharati* dwells · *aññataraṁ vā garuṭṭhāniyaṁ* or some other one standing in a teacher's place (m. acc. sg.) · *sabrahmacāriṁ* a fellow monk (m. acc. sg.) · *yattha·assa* in whom, for him · *tibbaṁ* keen (n. acc. sg.) · *hirottappaṁ* conscience and shame (n. acc. sg.) · *paccupaṭṭhitaṁ* established (n. acc. sg., p.p.) · *hoti* is · *pemañca gāravo ca* and affection and respect

    "Here, friend, a monk lives depending on the Teacher, or on some other fellow monk standing in a teacher's place, in whom keen conscience and shame are present, and affection and respect."

    Say it aloud. One word here is an absolutive that has stopped behaving like one. Find it, say what verb it comes from, and then say what it governs, and why that is not what an absolutive normally does.

2. Kicchena me adhigataṁ, halaṁ dāni pakāsituṁ; Rāgadosaparetehi, nāyaṁ dhammo susambudho. (SN 6.1 — S i 136)

    *kicchena* with difficulty (adv., inst. sg.) · *me* by me (1 sg. inst.) · *adhigataṁ* attained (n. nom. sg., p.p.) · *ha·laṁ* < *hi* indeed + *alaṁ* enough, no need · *dāni* now · *pakāsituṁ* to reveal (inf.) · *rāga·dosa·paretehi* by those overwhelmed by greed and hate (m. inst. pl.) · *na·ayaṁ* not this (m. nom. sg.) · *dhammo* teaching (m. nom. sg.) · *susambudho* easily understood (m. nom. sg.)

    "This that I have attained with difficulty — there is no need to reveal it now. This teaching is not easily understood by those overwhelmed by greed and hate."

    The Buddha's hesitation after the awakening, and the reason the Brahmā request in set 09 had to be made. Say it aloud. The second line rests on its infinitive, and the infinitive has no expressed agent at all. Say what the form is and what verb it comes from. Then say what English has to supply that the Pāli does without, and notice that *adhigataṁ* in the line above does have its agent, in a case set 09 will have taught you to expect.

3. Atha kho te bhikkhū sāvatthiyaṁ piṇḍāya caritvā pacchābhattaṁ piṇḍapātapaṭikkantā yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkamiṁsu; upasaṅkamitvā bhagavantaṁ abhivādetvā ekamantaṁ nisīdiṁsu. (AN 10.27 — A v 49)

    *atha kho* then · *te* those (m. nom. pl.) · *bhikkhū* monks (m. nom. pl.) · *sāvatthiyaṁ* in Sāvatthī (f. loc. sg.) · *piṇḍāya* for alms (m. dat. sg.) · *caritvā* having wandered (absol.) · *pacchābhattaṁ* after the meal · *piṇḍapātapaṭikkantā* returned from the almsround (m. nom. pl.) · *yena … tena* to where · *bhagavā* the Blessed One (m. nom. sg.) · *upasaṅkamiṁsu* they approached · *upasaṅkamitvā* having approached (absol.) · *bhagavantaṁ* the Blessed One (m. acc. sg.) · *abhivādetvā* having paid homage (absol.) · *ekamantaṁ* to one side · *nisīdiṁsu* they sat down

    "Then those monks, having wandered for alms in Sāvatthī and returned from the almsround after the meal, approached the Blessed One; having approached and paid homage to him, they sat down to one side."

    Set 08 took this sentence for its aorists. Here it is for the three words between them. Say it aloud. Three absolutives and two finite verbs, and all five have the same subject. That makes the sentence readable at speed, and it is the default the chapter states. Say which word gives you the subject, and how many times it is expressed.

4. Na dāni sukaraṁ amhehi lābhasakkārasiloke pariccajituṁ. (MN 76 — M i 524)

    *na* not · *dāni* now · *sukaraṁ* easy (n. nom. sg.) · *amhehi* by us (1 pl. inst.) · *lābha·sakkāra·siloke* gain, honour and renown (m. acc. pl.) · *pariccajituṁ* to give up (inf.)

    "It is not easy for us now to give up gain, honour and renown."

    Say it aloud. Name the case of the infinitive's agent, and say why it is not the nominative. The sentence has no grammatical subject at all, which is the point, and the case that steps in is the one set 09's passives use for theirs.

### Vacasā paricitā — absolutives and infinitives

Recite each until it is fluent. Then cover the Pāli and rebuild it from the gloss alone, aloud.

5. Sakkā bhoge ca bhuñjituṁ puññāni ca kātuṁ. (AN 4.122 — A ii 125)

    Yannūnāhaṁ sikkhaṁ paccakkhāya hīnāyāvattitvā bhoge ca bhuñjeyyaṁ puññāni ca kareyyanti. (AN 4.122)

    *sakkā* it is possible (ind.) · *bhoge* possessions (m. acc. pl.) · *ca* and · *bhuñjituṁ* to enjoy (inf.) · *puññāni* meritorious deeds (n. acc. pl.) · *kātuṁ* to do (inf.) · *yaṁ nūna ahaṁ* what if I · *sikkhaṁ* the training (f. acc. sg.) · *paccakkhāya* having renounced (absol.) · *hīnāya* to the lower life (m. dat. sg.) · *āvattitvā* having turned back (absol.) · *bhuñjeyyaṁ* I might enjoy (1 sg. opt.) · *kareyyaṁ* I might do (1 sg. opt.) · *ti* (quotation)

    "It is possible to enjoy possessions and to make merit. What if I were to renounce the training, return to the lower life, and enjoy possessions and make merit?"

    Two consecutive sentences, and both are one man talking himself out of the robe. Recite them in order, then rebuild them from the gloss. The same two verbs, *bhuñjati* and *karoti*, stand in both sentences and never once in the same form. Say what form each takes in each line, and then say what changes between the first sentence and the second when the verbs change: the facts do not move, and something else does.

6. Bhagavāpi, bhante, etarahi arahaṁ sammāsambuddho pañca nīvaraṇe pahāya cetaso upakkilese paññāya dubbalīkaraṇe catūsu satipaṭṭhānesu supatiṭṭhitacitto satta bojjhaṅge yathābhūtaṁ bhāvetvā anuttaraṁ sammāsambodhiṁ abhisambuddho'ti. (DN 16 — D ii 83)

    *bhagavā·pi* the Blessed One too · *bhante* venerable sir (voc.) · *etarahi* now · *arahaṁ sammāsambuddho* the worthy one, the fully awakened one (m. nom. sg.) · *pañca nīvaraṇe* the five hindrances (m. acc. pl.) · *pahāya* having abandoned (absol.) · *cetaso upakkilese* the defilements of the mind (m. acc. pl.) · *paññāya dubbalīkaraṇe* that weaken wisdom (m. acc. pl.) · *catūsu satipaṭṭhānesu* in the four applications of mindfulness (n. loc. pl.) · *supatiṭṭhitacitto* with mind well established (m. nom. sg.) · *satta bojjhaṅge* the seven awakening factors (m. acc. pl.) · *yathābhūtaṁ* as they really are · *bhāvetvā* having developed (absol.) · *anuttaraṁ sammāsambodhiṁ* unsurpassed full awakening (f. acc. sg.) · *abhisambuddho* has fully awakened (m. nom. sg., p.p.) · *ti* (quotation)

    "And the Blessed One too, venerable sir, who is at present the worthy one, the fully awakened one — having abandoned the five hindrances, the defilements of the mind that weaken wisdom, with mind well established in the four applications of mindfulness, having truly developed the seven awakening factors — has fully awakened to unsurpassed full awakening."

    One sentence of Sāriputta's declaration of faith, and item 27 is the whole of it. Rebuild it from the gloss; it is long, formulaic, and much easier the fourth time. Two absolutives, and the predicate at the far end is not a finite verb at all. Find all three, and say what holds the sentence together in the absence of a finite verb.

### Manasānupekkhitā — absolutives and infinitives

Cover the chapter. These are answered from memory or not at all.

**Recall.**

7. Absolutives take no case ending. What part of speech does this make them, grammatically, and what does it mean for how they agree (or fail to agree) with the noun they might seem to describe?

8. State the default rule for whose action an absolutive names, and then state the one construction the chapter documents where that default does not hold.

9. Name the absolutive's two main suffix types, and the sound change that regularly turns the second of them into *-cca*.

10. The gerund and the gerundive differ by one letter and mean entirely different things. What is each, and which chapter teaches the other?

11. Pāli reaches for the infinitive less often than English reaches for "to." Why?

**Identify the formation.**

12. *gantvā*
13. *paccakkhāya*
14. *jahetvā* — one of *hitvā*, *jahitvā* and *jahetvā* is not actually printed anywhere in the root texts. Which, and how would you check?
15. *desetuṁ*
16. *cassa*, in *paññāya cassa disvā āsavā parikkhīṇā honti*
17. *amhehi*, in *na dāni sukaraṁ amhehi lābhasakkārasiloke pariccajituṁ*
18. *nissāya*
19. *sakkā*

**Build the form.**

20. The *-tvā* absolutive of *bhāveti* 'develops'.
21. The infinitive of *bhāveti*.
22. The *-ya* absolutive of *pajahati* 'abandons' (already met once in this chapter; name it here from the rule, not from memory).
23. The infinitive of *suṇāti* 'hears'.
24. The *-tvā* absolutive of *dadāti* 'gives'.

### Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — absolutives and infinitives

**Put it back into Pāli.**

25. "It is not easy to teach the Dhamma to others."

26. "Having heard that teaching, he gains faith in the Realized One."

**Read at length.**

27. DN 16 — D ii 83, the *Mahāparinibbāna Sutta*'s account of Sāriputta's declaration of faith, all three sentences together:

    Ye te, bhante, ahesuṁ atītamaddhānaṁ arahanto sammāsambuddhā, sabbe te bhagavanto pañca nīvaraṇe pahāya cetaso upakkilese paññāya dubbalīkaraṇe catūsu satipaṭṭhānesu supatiṭṭhitacittā sattabojjhaṅge yathābhūtaṁ bhāvetvā anuttaraṁ sammāsambodhiṁ abhisambujjhiṁsu. Yepi te, bhante, bhavissanti anāgatamaddhānaṁ arahanto sammāsambuddhā, sabbe te bhagavanto pañca nīvaraṇe pahāya cetaso upakkilese paññāya dubbalīkaraṇe catūsu satipaṭṭhānesu supatiṭṭhitacittā satta bojjhaṅge yathābhūtaṁ bhāvetvā anuttaraṁ sammāsambodhiṁ abhisambujjhissanti. Bhagavāpi, bhante, etarahi arahaṁ sammāsambuddho pañca nīvaraṇe pahāya cetaso upakkilese paññāya dubbalīkaraṇe catūsu satipaṭṭhānesu supatiṭṭhitacitto satta bojjhaṅge yathābhūtaṁ bhāvetvā anuttaraṁ sammāsambodhiṁ abhisambuddho'ti.

    Translate the passage, then answer:
    - The same two absolutives, *pahāya* and *bhāvetvā*, appear in all three sentences, governed each time by a different finite verb. Name the three finite verbs and their tenses.
    - What single grammatical fact makes it possible to write one long formula three times with only the finite verb (and its supporting participle) changing?
    - *Supatiṭṭhitacittā* is a compound built partly on participial material of the kind Chapter 10 taught. Which element in it is that, and what does it contribute?
    - Why might a sutta choose to say this three times (past buddhas, future buddhas, this Buddha) instead of once?

**Whose action is it.** The chapter says an absolutive's agent is ordinarily the subject of the main verb, and then documents the place where that fails. Both sentences are now in front of you.

28. Set item 3 beside this:

    Paññāya cassa disvā āsavā parikkhīṇā honti. (MN 25 — M i 160)

    In item 3, whose action is *caritvā*, and how do you know? In this sentence, whose action is *disvā*? Say what in the second sentence makes the default reading impossible. The test is a question about what the nominative subject could plausibly have done, not a rule about word order. Then say which word supplies the real agent, and why its case cannot be settled from its form.

**Reaching back.**

29. Chapter 8 taught the optative as the ordinary vehicle for a conditional apodosis. *Mañhi, bhante, aññatitthiyā sāvakaṁ labhitvā kevalakappaṁ nāḷandaṁ paṭākaṁ parihareyyuṁ* (MN 56 — M i 379) is a conditional sentence with no conditional-mood verb in it at all. What serves as the protasis instead, and what carries the apodosis?

30. Chapter 10 gave the transitive/intransitive rule for the past participle's voice. *Vatvāna*, this chapter's absolutive of *vadati* 'says', and *vutta* 'said', the past participle Pāli uses for that verb, both mean 'saying' done and over, though DPD builds the first on √*vad* and the second on √*vac*. Which of the two carries a voice distinction (active vs passive) and which does not, and why does that difference follow directly from what each formation *is*?

**Two subordinations in one sentence.** The Vinaya's case law puts an absolutive and a locative absolute side by side more often than the suttas do, because a legal narrative has to keep track of who did what.

31. Tena kho pana samayena aññataro bhikkhu saṅghassa cīvare bhājīyamāne theyyacitto kusaṁ saṅkāmetvā cīvaraṁ aggahesi. (Bu Pj 2 — Vin iii 58) — Gloss it and render it. Two subordinate constructions stand here. Name each, say whose action each reports, and show that the rule of Chapter 16 decides which one the sentence had to use in each place. One of the two also happens to be a causative.

### Coming back — absolutives and infinitives

The set is not finished on the day it is worked. Re-attempt these, from cold, without reading the answers first.

**Day 3.** Items 7–11 and 12–19. The default agent rule and its one exception go first, because every reading question after them depends on it.

**Day 10.** Items 20–24 and 28. If item 28's two sentences still separate without the chapter open, the exception has been understood, not memorised.

**Day 30.** Items 5 and 6 aloud, and 25–26 and 31. If item 31's two constructions still sort themselves, the absolutive and the locative absolute have stopped being interchangeable.

## Exercises: Indeclinables and Particles

Every other exercise set in this book can drill a form by asking you to build it. This one cannot, because an indeclinable has nothing to build: it falls into the language whole, which is what *nipāta* means. So the work here is recognition, not formation: knowing which job a small word has, and noticing when a word you have met as a case form has a different one. For the same reason the chapter had to check its forms against the canon directly, and why several items below turn on whether a form is printed at all.

Stage 1 is four short sentences in which the small words carry everything, which is the fastest way to stop skimming them. Every Pāli line is canonical and cited; none has been composed. Worked answers are at the back.

### Sotānugata — indeclinables

Nothing is withheld. Each line comes with its gloss and its rendering, and the work is to say it and to hear which words join, separate, deny and fill.

1. Tesaṁ divā ca ratto ca, sadā puññaṁ pavaḍḍhati. (SN 1.47 — S i 33)

    *tesaṁ* for them (m. gen. pl.) · *divā* by day (adv.) · *ca* and · *ratto* by night (adv.) · *ca* and · *sadā* constantly · *puññaṁ* merit (n. nom. sg.) · *pavaḍḍhati* grows

    "For them, by day and by night, merit constantly grows."

    Say it aloud. Two of these words look like case forms and are not behaving as any case. Find them, name the case each was, and then say why the pair does not have to agree with each other or with anything else in the line.

2. Ruppatī'ti kho, bhikkhave, tasmā 'rūpa'nti vuccati. (SN 22.79 — S iii 86)

    *ruppati* it is deformed · *ti* (particle) · *kho* indeed · *bhikkhave* monks (m. voc. pl.) · *tasmā* therefore · *rūpaṁ* 'form' (n. nom. sg.) · *ti* (particle) · *vuccati* it is called

    "Because it is deformed, monks, it is therefore called 'form'."

    Set 09 took this sentence for its two passives. Say it aloud again and listen to the two *iti*s instead. They have different jobs, and only one of them is what a reader has been taught to call a quotation mark. Name both jobs, and say which word in the sentence answers to the first of them; if you read the first *iti* as a quotation, that word is left answering to nothing.

3. Kiṁ nu kho, āvuso gotama, taṁ jīvaṁ taṁ sarīraṁ, udāhu aññaṁ jīvaṁ aññaṁ sarīran'ti? (DN 7 — D i 159)

    *kiṁ* what · *nu* (question) · *kho* indeed · *āvuso gotama* friend Gotama (voc.) · *taṁ* that (n. nom. sg.) · *jīvaṁ* soul (n. nom. sg.) · *sarīraṁ* body (n. nom. sg.) · *udāhu* or · *aññaṁ* other (n. nom. sg.) · *ti* (quotation)

    "Is the soul the same as the body, friend Gotama, or is the soul one thing and the body another?"

    Say it aloud. Two particles between them make this a question with only two permitted answers. Name both. Then notice what the Buddha does with it. This is one of the undeclared questions, and his refusal to answer is a refusal of the frame these two small words have built.

4. Selo yathā ekaghano, vātena na samīrati; Evaṁ nindāpasaṁsāsu, na samiñjanti paṇḍitā. (Dhp 81 — Dhp p. 12)

    *selo* a rock (m. nom. sg.) · *yathā* just as · *ekaghano* of one solid mass (m. nom. sg.) · *vātena* by the wind (m. inst. sg.) · *na* not · *samīrati* is shaken · *evaṁ* so · *nindā·pasaṁsāsu* amid blame and praise (f. loc. pl.) · *na* not · *samiñjanti* are moved · *paṇḍitā* the wise (m. nom. pl.)

    "As a rock of one solid mass is not shaken by the wind, so the wise are not moved amid blame and praise."

    Say it aloud. Two indeclinables carry the whole simile and there is no verb of comparison anywhere in the verse. Name them, and say what English has to add that Pāli does not. Then say what the two *na*s contribute, and whether the second could have been left out.

### Vacasā paricitā — indeclinables

Recite each until it is fluent. Then cover the Pāli and rebuild it from the gloss alone, aloud.

5. Ayaṁ kho me, samma, sāṇabhāro dūrābhato ca susannaddho ca, alaṁ me tvaṁ pajānāhī'ti. (DN 23 — D ii 351)

    *ayaṁ* this (m. nom. sg.) · *kho* indeed · *me* my (1 sg. gen.) · *samma* friend (voc.) · *sāṇabhāro* load of hemp (m. nom. sg.) · *dūrābhato* brought from afar (m. nom. sg., p.p.) · *ca* and · *susannaddho* well tied up (m. nom. sg., p.p.) · *ca* and · *alaṁ* enough · *me* for me (1 sg. dat.) · *tvaṁ* you (nom. sg.) · *pajānāhi* know! (2 sg. imper.) · *ti* (quotation)

    "'This hemp load of mine, friend, was brought from far off and is well tied up; it is enough for me — you see to yourself.'"

    Rebuild it from the gloss. The second half is a refusal, and the refusing is done by one syllable. Say which, and say what it conveys that "enough" does not quite capture in English.

6. "Na sūpaṁ vā byañjanaṁ vā odanena paṭicchādessāmi bhiyyokamyataṁ upādāyā"ti sikkhā karaṇīyā. (Bu Sk 36 — Vin iv 192)

    *na* not · *sūpaṁ* curry (m. acc. sg.) · *vā* or · *byañjanaṁ* condiment (n. acc. sg.) · *vā* or · *odanena* with rice (m. inst. sg.) · *paṭicchādessāmi* I shall cover (1 sg. fut.) · *bhiyyokamyataṁ* wanting more (f. acc. sg.) · *upādāya* on account of (absol. as postposition) · *ti* (quotation) · *sikkhā* the training (f. nom. sg.) · *karaṇīyā* is to be done (f. nom. sg., gerundive)

    "'I shall not cover curry or condiment with rice out of wanting more' — this is a training to be done."

    One of the seventy-five *sekhiya* rules, all built to this pattern and all recited fortnightly. Rebuild it from the gloss. Three of the Saddanīti's four jobs for a particle are done here. Name each particle and the job it does, and say which job is missing and why a rule of this shape has no use for it. Then account for the *ā* at the end of *upādāyā*, which is not part of the word.

### Manasānupekkhitā — indeclinables

Cover the chapter. These are answered from memory or not at all.

**Recall.**

7. The tradition sorts the *nipātā* by the job each does, not by part of speech. Name the four jobs, in Pāli or in English, and give one specimen of each.

8. Two of the reference grammars this book relies on most have no section on indeclinables at all. Say why. The reason is about how those grammars are organised, not about the words.

9. Pāli has no adverb. What does it have instead, and what does that change about how you should expect to identify one on the page?

10. What does *mā* govern, and why must a reader know that above all else about it?

11. Duroiselle divides adverbs into three groups. Name them, and say which group a word belongs to if you cannot analyse it at all.

**Identify the formation.** Say what each word is, how it is built where it can be analysed, and which of the tradition's four jobs it does. Three of these are traps; say what the trap is.

12. *tato*
13. *sudaṁ*
14. *kira*
15. *cirassaṁ*
16. *tikkhattuṁ*
17. *iṅgha*
18. *bho*
19. *ekopi*
20. *nanu*
21. *divā*

**Build the form.**

22. The adverb of place meaning 'everywhere', built on *sabba-* with the *-ttha* suffix.
23. The adverb of time meaning 'when?', built on the interrogative stem with the *-dā* suffix.
24. The adverb meaning 'sevenfold, in seven ways', built on *satta* with the *-dhā* suffix.
25. The prohibition 'do not go', using *mā* and the aorist of *gacchati*. Then compare your answer with what the canon has before trusting it, and say what you find.
26. The ablative-sense adverb 'from here', built on the proximal stem with *-to*.

### Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — indeclinables

**Read the sentence.** Gloss it, render it, and answer the question.

27. Nanu tvaṁ sālavatikaṁ ajjhāvasasī'ti? (DN 12 — D i 227) — what answer does the questioner expect, and what in the sentence shows it?

28. Kudāssu nāma imassa dukkhassa nissaraṇaṁ paññāyissati jarāmaraṇassā'ti? (SN 12.10 — S ii 10) — one word here contains a particle that never stands alone in the root texts. Find it, separate it out, and say what the whole word would look like if the two were printed apart.

**Put it back into Pāli.** The answer is a particular line of canon, and getting close to it is the exercise.

29. "Do not speak so, friend Sāti; do not misrepresent the Blessed One." (Watch the mood.)

30. "Come now, monks, I address you." (Four words in the Pāli, three of them indeclinable.)

**Read at length.**

31. AN 3.65 — A i 189, the Kālāmas' instruction, in one passage:

    Etha tumhe, kālāmā, mā anussavena, mā paramparāya, mā itikirāya, mā piṭakasampadānena, mā takkahetu, mā nayahetu, mā ākāraparivitakkena, mā diṭṭhinijjhānakkhantiyā, mā bhabbarūpatāya, mā samaṇo no garūti.

    Read it through twice without stopping to parse. Then:
    - Count the *mā*s. This chapter taught *mā* with the aorist; how many aorists are in this passage, and what does *mā* govern here instead?
    - Give the case of *anussavena*, *paramparāya* and *ākāraparivitakkena*, and say what that case is contributing.
    - *Takkahetu* and *nayahetu* break the pattern of the others. How?
    - The last item, *mā samaṇo no garū'ti*, breaks it completely: it is a whole clause with a finite verb understood and an *iti* closing it. Translate it, and say what the *iti* marks.
    - What is gained by repeating *mā* ten times instead of writing it once and listing the rest?

**Reaching back.**

32. *Chapter 8.* The aorist is the past tense. In item 29 above it is not. State the rule that governs which of the two readings applies, and say what would happen to the sentence if the particle were removed.

33. *Chapter 5.* The pronoun chapter taught *tasmā*, *tato* and *tahiṁ* as oblique forms of *ta(d)* hardened into adverbs. This chapter taught *tato* as a *-to* adverb and *tasmā* as a frozen ablative. These are not two competing accounts. Say what each is describing, and why both can be true of the same word.

34. *Chapter 4.* The adjectives chapter taught that Pāli makes an adverb from the accusative singular. What does this chapter add to that, and which single sentence quoted in this chapter shows both an adverb-by-suffix and an adverb-by-accusative side by side?

35. *Chapter 11.* Both chapters teach *paṭicca*, *nissāya* and *uddissa*. What does each chapter contribute that the other does not?

**Say it aloud.**

36. Dhp 1 — Dhp p. 1, the opening verse of the Dhammapada:

    Manopubbaṅgamā dhammā, manoseṭṭhā manomayā;\
    Manasā ce paduṭṭhena, bhāsati vā karoti vā;\
    Tato naṁ dukkhamanveti, cakkaṁva vahato padaṁ.

    Read it five times slowly, holding the long vowels and sounding the geminates in *paduṭṭhena* and *cakkaṁva*. Four indeclinables carry the whole logic of the verse: *ce* sets the condition, *vā … vā* offers the two ways it may be met, *tato* draws the consequence, and *va* makes the simile. Notice that not one of them inflects, and that without them the six lines are a list of nouns. Set 07 read this verse for its present tenses; this is what was left when the verbs were accounted for.

### Coming back — indeclinables

The set is not finished on the day it is worked. Re-attempt these, from cold, without reading the answers first.

**Day 3.** Items 7–11 and 12–21. The four jobs and the three traps go first, because recognition is the whole skill in this chapter and there is no form to fall back on.

**Day 10.** Items 22–26 and 31. If the ten *mā*s in 31 still resolve without the chapter open, the particle's job has separated from the construction it is usually taught in.

**Day 30.** Items 1–4 aloud, and 29–30. If item 30 comes out in four words, the difference between a word that inflects and a word that does not has stopped being theoretical.

## Exercises: Compounds

In compounding, alone of the topics in this book, the drill and the reading are the same activity. Every long word in a sutta is an exercise set by the text itself, and the only way to get faster is to take a great many of them apart. So this set is longer on splitting than on anything else, and the reading passage at the end is chosen for density, not difficulty: it is a formula you will meet dozens of times, and it is worth learning thoroughly once.

Stage 1 is four passages in which the compounds are already resolved for you, because hearing where a seam falls is a different skill from finding it, and it comes first. Every Pāli line is canonical and cited; none has been composed. Five of the later items contain a trap, and they are not marked. Where you find one, say what the trap is as well as what the answer is; naming it is a different act from spotting it. Worked answers are at the back.

### Sotānugata — compounds

Nothing is withheld. Each line comes with its gloss and its rendering, and the work is to say it and to hear the joins.

1. jātipaccayā jarāmaraṇaṁ sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsā sambhavanti. (SN 12.1 — S ii 1)

    *jātipaccayā* with birth as condition · *jarāmaraṇaṁ* old age and death · *sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsā* < *soka* sorrow + *parideva* lamentation + *dukkha* pain + *domanassa* dejection + *upāyāsa* distress · *sambhavanti* come to be

    "With birth as condition, old age and death come to be, and sorrow, lamentation, pain, dejection and distress."

    Say it aloud slowly and hear where the seams fall. Four Pāli words carry thirteen English ones, and the last of them has five members written solid, yet only one ending appears in it. Say which member that ending sits on and what it settles about the whole. Then say why four of the five members, looked up in a digital text as the compound spells them, would not be found: the failure is the compound behaving correctly.

2. 'sambādho gharāvāso rajopatho, abbhokāso pabbajjā. (MN 27 — M i 179)

    *sambādho* crowded, confined · *gharāvāso* < *ghara* house + *āvāsa* dwelling · *rajopatho* < *raja* dust + *patha* path · *abbhokāso* open space, the open air · *pabbajjā* going forth

    "Life at home is confined, a road of dust; the going forth is the open air."

    Say it aloud. Five words and no verb anywhere. Two of them are compounds; a third looks like one and is not. Name the two and their classes, and say which of them stands in apposition to the other. Then take the third: say what it would divide into if the appearance were true, what makes that division so attractive, and how the question is actually settled.

3. Tirokuṭṭesu tiṭṭhanti, sandhisiṅghāṭakesu ca; Dvārabāhāsu tiṭṭhanti, āgantvāna sakaṁ gharaṁ. (Kp 7)

    *tirokuṭṭesu* < *tiro* beyond + *kuṭṭa* wall · *tiṭṭhanti* they stand · *sandhisiṅghāṭakesu* < *sandhi* junction + *siṅghāṭaka* crossroads · *ca* and · *dvārabāhāsu* < *dvāra* door + *bāhā* post · *āgantvāna* having come · *sakaṁ* their own · *gharaṁ* house

    "Outside the walls they stand, and at the junctions and crossroads; they stand beside the door posts, having come to their own house."

    Say it aloud. Three compounds, three classes, and no two of them are alike. An *avyayībhāva* is normally a frozen neuter accusative in *-aṁ* and does not decline; the first word here is one, and it is a locative plural. Say what that shows about the class. Then name the classes of the other two, which are not the same as each other.

4. Two cases from the Vinaya's case law on theft, the first and the second, in a fixed frame and one word apart:

    Tena kho pana samayena aññataro bhikkhu rajakattharaṇaṁ gantvā mahagghaṁ dussaṁ passitvā theyyacittaṁ uppādesi. (Bu Pj 2 — Vin iii 56)

    Tena kho pana samayena aññataro bhikkhu rajakattharaṇaṁ gantvā mahagghaṁ dussaṁ passitvā theyyacitto āmasi. (Bu Pj 2 — Vin iii 56)

    *tena kho pana samayena* now at that time · *aññataro* a certain (m. nom. sg.) · *bhikkhu* monk (m. nom. sg.) · *rajakattharaṇaṁ* < *rajaka* laundry worker + *attharaṇa* spreading — the place where laundry workers spread their cloth (n. acc. sg.) · *gantvā* having gone · *mahagghaṁ* costly (n. acc. sg.) · *dussaṁ* cloth (n. acc. sg.) · *passitvā* having seen · *theyyacittaṁ* a thought of theft (n. acc. sg.) · *theyyacitto* with theft in mind (m. nom. sg.) · *uppādesi* he produced (3 sg. aor.) · *āmasi* he touched (3 sg. aor.)

    "Now at that time a certain monk went to a laundry workers' spreading-ground, saw a costly cloth, and produced a thought of theft."

    "Now at that time a certain monk went to a laundry workers' spreading-ground, saw a costly cloth, and, with theft in mind, touched it."

    Read the two aloud one after the other. Nothing inside the compound changes, and nor does anything else in the frame, except one ending. Say what *theyya* + *citta* is in each sentence (what class, what gender, what it names), and say why the second sentence contains no word for a thought at all. Set 08 read four passages from this same series of cases for their aorists; here are the compounds those sentences still hold once the verbs have been accounted for.

### Vacasā paricitā — compounds

Recite each until it is fluent. Then cover the Pāli and rebuild it from the gloss alone, aloud.

5. Sabbapāpassa akaraṇaṁ, kusalassa upasampadā; Sacittapariyodapanaṁ, etaṁ buddhāna sāsanaṁ. (Dhp 183 — Dhp p. 27)

    *sabbapāpassa* < *sabba* all + *pāpa* evil, in the genitive · *akaraṇaṁ* < *a-* not + *karaṇa* doing · *kusalassa* of the wholesome · *upasampadā* undertaking · *sacittapariyodapanaṁ* < *sa* one's own + *citta* mind + *pariyodapana* cleansing · *etaṁ* this · *buddhāna* of the awakened ones · *sāsanaṁ* teaching

    "Not doing any evil, undertaking the wholesome, cleansing one's own mind — this is the teaching of the awakened ones."

    Rebuild it from the gloss, and name each compound as you rebuild it. Four compounds and three classes. One relation in the verse is written as two separate words where it could as easily have been compounded; find it, and say what having both options available suggests about the ones that were taken.

6. svākkhāto bhagavatā dhammo sandiṭṭhiko akāliko ehipassiko opaneyyiko paccattaṁ veditabbo viññūhī'ti. (AN 6.10 — A iii 285)

    *svākkhāto* well proclaimed · *bhagavatā* by the Blessed One (m. inst. sg.) · *dhammo* the teaching (m. nom. sg.) · *sandiṭṭhiko* visible here and now · *akāliko* timeless · *ehipassiko* < *ehi* come! + *passa* see! + *-ika* · *opaneyyiko* leading onwards · *paccattaṁ* individually · *veditabbo* to be known · *viññūhi* by the wise · *ti* (quotation)

    "The teaching is well proclaimed by the Blessed One — visible here and now, timeless, inviting one to come and see, leading onwards, to be known individually by the wise."

    Recite it until it is fluent; many readers will have it by heart already. Then rebuild it from the gloss. Four words in a row end in *-iko*. The chapter's last section says that suffix makes an adjective out of a finished compound: turn that claim on all four and look each one up in the dictionary. Only two of them have a compound underneath, so say what the other two are built on instead, and what that does to the chapter's statement: whether it makes it wrong, or narrower than it sounds. One of the two also shows the vowel change the chapter mentions only in a footnote. Then say why *ehipassiko* fits no class in any grammar's list.

### Manasānupekkhitā — compounds

Cover the chapter. These are answered from memory or not at all.

**Recall.**

7. The tradition names two properties that make a compound a compound. Give both, in English, and say which of the two a reader uses.

8. Name two things a compound does **not** tell you about its own members.

9. All the classes reduce to two basic kinds. What are they, and what is the difference?

10. Why is the *bahubbīhi* not a seventh class alongside the other six?

11. Aggavaṁsa divides compounds into *nicca* and *anicca*, permanent and impermanent. What practical difference does the distinction make to somebody holding a dictionary?

**Split the compound.** Give the members, in order, with any sound change named. Do not translate yet.

12. *vijjācaraṇasampanno*

13. *pubbenivāsānussatiñāṇāya*

14. *apparajakkhajātikā*

15. *mahapphalaṁ*

16. *bahulīkato*

17. *hatthigavāssavaḷavaṁ*

18. *ekamantaṁ*

**Name the class.** For each, name the class and give the relation between the members. Where the class is a *tappurisa*, say which case.

19. *dhammavinayo*

20. *bhikkhusaṅgho*

21. *sammādiṭṭhi*

22. *saḷāyatanaṁ*

23. *tirokuṭṭesu*

24. *khīṇāsavo*

25. *manasikāro*

### Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — compounds

**Build the form.** Produce the answer before checking it.

26. Resolve *dukkhasamudayo* into two separate words, supplying the case ending the first member would have had.

27. Resolve *sīladhanaṁ* on the *eva* pattern the chapter gives, and then say why 'the wealth of virtue' is not quite what the compound says.

28. *Cittaṁ* is neuter and *bhikkhu* is masculine. Build a *bahubbīhi* from *su-* + *bhāvita* + *citta* meaning 'whose mind is well developed', in the form it would take describing a monk. Then find it in the canon.

29. The grammars' standing example of a possessive compound for 'a man whose hands have been cut off' is *chinnahattho puriso*. Ask of both *chinnahattho* and *hatthacchinno* whether the canon uses them, and say which of the two Pāli actually has, and what the answer implies about a grammar's illustrations as against its rules.

**Read the sentence.** Give the three layers: the line as printed, a word-by-word gloss with every compound resolved, and your own rendering.

30. Saḷāyatanañcidaṁ, bhikkhave, kiṁnidānaṁ kiṁsamudayaṁ kiṁjātikaṁ kiṁpabhavaṁ? (MN 11 — M i 67)

31. Santi sattā apparajakkhajātikā, assavanatā dhammassa parihāyanti. (SN 6.1 — S i 137)

**Put it back into Pāli.** The answer is a particular line of canon, and getting close to it is the exercise. A well-formed sentence that is not the one in the book is a good result, but it is a different exercise.

32. 'Now this, monks, is the noble truth of the origin of suffering.' Two compounds, one nested inside the other as its attribute.

33. 'Outside the walls they stand.' Two words.

**Read at length.**

34. Read **MN 27 — M i 178** twice through without stopping to parse anything. This is not a drill and should not be treated as one; read it as you would read a paragraph in a language you know. Only then answer:

   - The passage contains at least twenty compounds. Find the one whose first member keeps a case ending, and name the case.
   - *Sadevakaṁ*, *samārakaṁ*, *sabrahmakaṁ*, *sassamaṇabrāhmaṇiṁ*, *sadevamanussaṁ* are five of a kind. What kind, and what is the *sa-* doing?
   - *Gahapati vā gahapatiputto vā* has *vā* written out. Why did the passage not compound them?
   - *Kesamassuṁ* is a collective singular. What would the other kind of *dvanda* have looked like, and would the sense have changed?

**A compound list from the canon.** The Vinaya defines a word by listing the kinds of thing it covers, and the list is almost entirely compounds.

35. Rājāno nāma pathabyārājā padesarājā maṇḍalikā antarabhogikā akkhadassā mahāmattā, ye vā pana chejjabhejjaṁ karontā anusāsanti. Ete rājāno nāma. (Bu Pj 2 — Vin iii 47) — Take the first two apart and name their class. Then say what *chejjabhejjaṁ* is, and why that one is not the same kind of compound as the two before it.

**Reaching back.**

36. *Chapter 2.* *Manopubbaṅgamā dhammā, manoseṭṭhā manomayā* (Dhp 1 — Dhp p. 1). Chapter 2 uses this line to show that very often no sandhi happens at all. Take the three compounds in it: which are *kammadhārayas* and which are *bahubbīhis*, and how do you know?

37. *Chapter 10.* *Sammāsambuddho* ends in a past participle. Is that participle doing a participle's work here, or a noun's? What in the sentence at MN 27 — M i 178 decides it?

38. *Chapter 12.* Chapter 12 treats *yathābhūtaṁ* among the indeclinables and this chapter treats it as an *avyayībhāva*. Both are correct. Say in one sentence why there is no contradiction.

**Say it aloud.**

39. Read this verse aloud five times, slowly, holding the long vowels and sounding the double consonants. Then say which compound in it no ordinary class will account for, and what it means.

    Gambhīrapaññaṁ medhāviṁ,\
    maggāmaggassa kovidaṁ;\
    Uttamatthamanuppattaṁ,\
    tamahaṁ brūmi brāhmaṇaṁ. (Dhp 403 — Dhp p. 57)

### Coming back — compounds

The set is not finished on the day it is worked. Re-attempt these, from cold, without reading the answers first.

**Day 3.** Items 12–18 and 19–25. Splitting and classing are the whole of the skill, and they are the two things that decay fastest when a reader stops reading canon daily.

**Day 10.** Items 26–29 and 34. If the twenty compounds in 34 resolve without the chapter open, the formula has stopped being an obstacle and become a foothold.

**Day 30.** Items 1–4 aloud, and 32–33. If item 4 still shows you the whole *bahubbīhi* rule in one changed ending, the class has been understood as a use, not memorised as a list.

## Exercises: Word Formation

This chapter's claim is narrow and testable: knowing how a word was built shows you where to look it up, and very little else. So the set drills recognition, not analysis. Half of it is the same operation performed forwards and backwards (given the derivative, find the base; given the base, predict the derivative), and the two halves are deliberately unequal in difficulty, because in reading you only ever do the first.

Stage 1 is five passages in which the derivatives are already resolved for you, and four of the five are the Maṅgala Sutta, which many readers of this book will have by heart before they have any grammar. That is the point of choosing them: a line already in the memory has more in it than the thing you came for. Five of the later items contain a trap. They are not marked. Where you find one, say what the trap is as well as what the answer is. Worked answers are at the back.

### Sotānugata — word formation

Nothing is withheld. Each line comes with its gloss and its rendering, and the work is to say it and to hear what the suffixes have done to the front of the word.

1. Sāmaññañca vo, bhikkhave, desessāmi, sāmaññaphalāni ca. Taṁ suṇātha. Katamañca, bhikkhave, sāmaññaṁ? (SN 45.35 — S v 25)

    *sāmaññaṁ* < *samaṇa* ascetic + *-ya* — the ascetic life (nt. acc. sg.) · *ca* and · *vo* to you · *bhikkhave* monks · *desessāmi* I will teach (1 sg. fut.) · *sāmaññaphalāni* the fruits of the ascetic life (nt. acc. pl.) · *ca* and · *taṁ* that · *suṇātha* listen (2 pl. imp.) · *katamaṁ* which, what

    "Monks, I will teach you the ascetic life and the fruits of the ascetic life. Listen to this. And what, monks, is the ascetic life?"

    Say it aloud. The same derivative stands three times in three lines, once inside a compound, and the whole of this chapter's practical claim is in its first syllable. Name the base, name the suffix, and say which letter of the dictionary the word is filed under and which letter its base is filed under. Then look *sāmañña* up and read past the first entry. It has two bases, not one, and they are different words. Say what the second is built on, and what in this sentence settles which of the two is meant.

2. Asevanā ca bālānaṁ, paṇḍitānañca sevanā; Pūjā ca pūjaneyyānaṁ, etaṁ maṅgalamuttamaṁ. (Snp 2.4 — Sn p. 46)

    *asevanā* < *a-* not + √*sev* + *-anā* — non-association (f. nom. sg.) · *ca* and · *bālānaṁ* with fools (m. gen. pl.) · *paṇḍitānaṁ* with the wise (m. gen. pl.) · *ca* and · *sevanā* association · *pūjā* < √*pūj* + *-ā* — honouring (f. nom. sg.) · *ca* and · *pūjaneyyānaṁ* of those to be honoured (m. gen. pl.) · *etaṁ* this · *maṅgalamuttamaṁ* < *maṅgalaṁ* blessing + *uttamaṁ* highest

    "Not associating with fools, and associating with the wise, and honouring those worthy of honour: this is the highest blessing."

    Say it aloud, and hear that the verse has no verb in it. Three formations in four lines, and one of them is used twice. Name the three, and say what the verse gains by making its opposition out of one noun and a particle instead of two nouns.

3. Patirūpadesavāso ca, pubbe ca katapuññatā; Attasammāpaṇidhi ca, etaṁ maṅgalamuttamaṁ. (Snp 2.4 — Sn p. 46)

    *patirūpadesavāso* < *patirūpa* suitable + *desa* region + *vāsa* dwelling — living in a suitable region (m. nom. sg.) · *ca* and · *pubbe* in the past · *ca* and · *katapuññatā* < *kata* done + *puñña* merit + *-tā* — the fact of having made merit (f. nom. sg.) · *attasammāpaṇidhi* < *atta* self + *sammā* rightly + *paṇidhi* aiming — right self-direction (m. nom. sg.) · *ca* and · *etaṁ* this · *maṅgalamuttamaṁ* < *maṅgalaṁ* blessing + *uttamaṁ* highest

    "Living in a suitable region, having made merit in the past, aiming oneself rightly: this is the highest blessing."

    Say it aloud. This is the verse between the two the chapter prints, and it carries the one suffix they do not. Say what *-tā* has been added to here (something larger than a stem), and name the part of speech of that base. Then put item 1's question to it: the front of the word has not been strengthened, and *-tā* is a secondary suffix, so why not? A later item asks the same thing of a different suffix.

4. Bāhusaccañca sippañca, vinayo ca susikkhito; (Snp 2.4 — Sn p. 47)

    *bāhusaccaṁ* < *bahu* much + *suta* heard + *-ya* — learning (nt. nom. sg.) · *ca* and · *sippaṁ* craft · *ca* and · *vinayo* discipline · *ca* and · *susikkhito* < *su-* well + *sikkhita* trained

    "Learning and a craft, and discipline well trained in."

    Say it aloud, slowly, and hold the *-cc-*. One word here is the chapter entire. Take it apart in four steps (base, suffix, vuddhi, assimilation), and then say how much of the base is still visible in the result. Item 31 will ask you to produce this line from the English, so learn it now.

5. Mātāpitu upaṭṭhānaṁ, puttadārassa saṅgaho; (Snp 2.4 — Sn p. 47)

    *mātāpitu* mother and father (dvanda) · *upaṭṭhānaṁ* < *upa-* near + √*ṭhā* + *-ana* — attending on (nt. nom. sg.) · *puttadārassa* of children and spouse (m. gen. sg.) · *saṅgaho* < *saṁ-* together + √*gah* + *-a* — support (m. nom. sg.)

    "Attending on mother and father, supporting children and spouse."

    Say it aloud. Two action nouns, two different suffixes, and both prefixes have to be undone before either word can be looked up. Say what each prefix is in its dictionary shape, and which of the two changed on the way in and why.

### Vacasā paricitā — word formation

Recite each until it is fluent. Then cover the Pāli and rebuild it from the gloss alone, aloud.

6. svākkhāto bhagavatā dhammo sandiṭṭhiko akāliko ehipassiko opaneyyiko paccattaṁ veditabbo viññūhī'ti. (AN 6.10 — A iii 285)

    *svākkhāto* well proclaimed · *bhagavatā* by the Blessed One (m. inst. sg.) · *dhammo* the teaching (m. nom. sg.) · *sandiṭṭhiko* visible here and now · *akāliko* timeless · *ehipassiko* < *ehi* come! + *passa* see! + *-ika* · *opaneyyiko* leading onwards · *paccattaṁ* individually · *veditabbo* to be known · *viññūhi* by the wise · *ti* (quotation)

    "The teaching is well proclaimed by the Blessed One — visible here and now, timeless, inviting one to come and see, leading onwards, to be known individually by the wise."

    Set 13 asked what is underneath these four words; this set asks only where to find them. Recite the line until it is fluent, rebuild it from the gloss, and then take the four words in *-iko* to a dictionary: for each, say which letter you look under, and whether that is the letter its base begins with. Only one of the four moves, and the chapter uses it to demonstrate the rule. Then say, for each of the other three, why the rule left it alone. The reasons are three, and all different.

7. 'Idaṁ dukkhaṁ ariyasaccan'ti me, bhikkhave, pubbe ananussutesu dhammesu cakkhuṁ udapādi, ñāṇaṁ udapādi, paññā udapādi, vijjā udapādi, āloko udapādi. (SN 56.11 — S v 422)

    *idaṁ* this · *dukkhaṁ ariyasaccaṁ* the noble truth of suffering · *ti* thus (quotation) · *me* to me · *bhikkhave* monks · *pubbe* formerly · *ananussutesu* < *an-* not + *anu-* along + √*su* + *-ta* — unheard (m. loc. pl.) · *dhammesu* among things · *cakkhuṁ* vision · *udapādi* < *ud-* up + augment *a-* + *pādi* — arose (3 sg. aor.) · *ñāṇaṁ* < √*ñā* + *-ana* — knowledge · *paññā* < *pa-* + √*ñā* + *-ā* — wisdom · *vijjā* clear knowledge · *āloko* < *ā-* + √*lok* + *-a* — light

    "'This is the noble truth of suffering': thus, monks, in regard to things unheard before, vision arose in me, knowledge arose, wisdom arose, clear knowledge arose, light arose."

    Rebuild it from the gloss, and name each derivative as you rebuild it. Two of the five nouns are built on one root, and they are two of the most doctrinally loaded words in the canon. Say which two, and what that pair demonstrates about deducing a meaning from a derivation. Then say where the augment sits in *udapādi*, and why it is not at the front of the word.

8. "Cattārome, bhikkhave, iddhipādā bhāvitā bahulīkatā mahapphalā honti mahānisaṁsā". (SN 51.20 — S v 276)

    *cattāro* four · *ime* these · *bhikkhave* monks · *iddhipādā* bases of spiritual power (m. nom. pl.) · *bhāvitā* developed (pp. m. nom. pl.) · *bahulīkatā* < *bahula* much + *-ī-* + *kata* done — cultivated · *mahapphalā* of great fruit · *honti* are · *mahānisaṁsā* of great benefit

    "These four bases of spiritual power, monks, when developed and cultivated, are of great fruit, of great benefit."

    Rebuild it from the gloss. This formula closes the description of every practice the Buddha teaches, so it is one to have by heart. One word in it belongs to the chapter's section on verbs made from nouns and adverbs and to no other. Name it, name the formation, and say what the *-ī-* in the middle contributes. Then say why it is neither an ordinary compound nor a denominative verb.

### Manasānupekkhitā — word formation

**Recall.** Answer from memory, in a phrase, before opening the chapter.

9. The tradition divides derivation in two. Name both divisions, and say what each kind of suffix is added to.

10. In secondary derivation, which syllable of the word gets strengthened? Give the practical consequence for somebody holding a dictionary.

11. The tradition names three things a prefix can do to the meaning of a root. Give all three in English. Which of the three does a reader most need to be told about?

12. A grammar writes a suffix as *ṇa* or *ṇvu* or *ṇika*. The *ṇ* is never pronounced. What is it doing there?

13. Give two distinct reasons why knowing a word's derivation will not tell you what the word means.

**Identify the formation.** For each, name the suffix, say whether it is primary or secondary, and give the sense the suffix contributes.

14. *vedanā*

15. *khanti*

16. *kāraka*

17. *cakkhumā*

18. *sāmaññaṁ*

19. *ācariyaka*

20. *muṇḍako*

21. *nepuññaṁ*

22. *pothujjaniko*

23. *pāragū*

24. *patti*

### Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — word formation

**Build the form.** Produce the answer before checking it.

25. From *paṇḍito* 'wise', build the neuter abstract in *-ya*. Name the two separate changes that stand between the base and the answer.

26. From *garu* 'heavy, venerable', build the masculine noun 'respect'. Which grade is the root in?

27. From *vicikicchā* 'doubt', build the possessive adjective in *-in*, nominative singular. Compare your answer with what the canon has before accepting it, and say what the result shows about the rule in item 10.

28. Warder's lesson on derivation gives *opanayika* for 'leading onwards'. Look the word up in the canon and say what is printed there instead, and what element in the formation accounts for the difference.

29. From *punabbhavo* 'renewed existence', build the adjective 'leading to renewed existence'. Then say which of the word's two vowels changed and why the other did not.

**Read the sentence.** Give the three layers: the line as printed, a word-by-word gloss with every derivative resolved, and your own rendering.

30. "Yadākhvāhaṁ, āvuso, santiṭṭhāmi tadāssu saṁsīdāmi; yadākhvāhaṁ, āvuso, āyūhāmi tadāssu nibbuyhāmi. (SN 1.1 — S i 1)

**Put it back into Pāli.** The answer is a particular line of canon, and getting close to it is the exercise. A well-formed sentence that is not the one in the book is a good result, but it is a different exercise.

31. 'Learning and a craft, and discipline well trained in.' Two of the three nouns are derivatives; one of them is an abstract in *-ya* built on a compound.

32. 'When I stood still I sank down; when I struggled I was swept away.' Four verbs, each carrying a prefix, and the two pairs are opposites.

**Read at length.**

33. Read **SN 1.1**, the whole sutta, twice through without stopping to parse anything. It is one of the shortest suttas in the canon, and the first in the Saṁyutta. This is not a drill and should not be treated as one; read it as you would read a paragraph in a language you know. Only then answer:

   - *Abhikkantāya rattiyā abhikkantavaṇṇā* has one prefix used twice, on two different words. What is the prefix contributing in each?
   - *Upasaṅkami* carries two prefixes and *upasaṅkamitvā* carries the same two. Name them, in order, and say what the root is.
   - The Buddha's explanation turns on two pairs of verbs, and his conclusion restates two of those roots in negated form. Identify the four verbs and the two negated forms, and name the prefix on each.
   - The sutta ends *tatthevantaradhāyīti*. Resolve the sandhi and identify the verb. Is *antara* a prefix?

**Reaching back.**

34. **Chapter 13.** *Ehipassiko* was taught in the compounds chapter as a syntactic compound and appears in this one as an *-ika* derivative. Both are right. Say in one sentence why there is no contradiction, and then say which of the two chapters explains the *-o* on the end.

35. **Chapter 4.** *-tara* and *-tama* are secondary suffixes and are taught with the adjectives, not here. *Uttaritaro* and *paṇītataro* both occur in a single sentence at DN 6. Which of the two is built on an adjective and which on a prefix, and does the difference show?

36. **Chapter 10.** *Diṭṭhi* and *diṭṭha* are both built on √*dis*. One is a past participle and one is an action noun in *-ti*. Given only the two forms and no context, what shows you which is which, and what does that suggest about reading a bare *-ti* form in a verse?

37. **Chapter 2.** *Nibbāna*, *nibbindati* and *nikkhamati* all begin with the same prefix. Say what the prefix is, and why the first two double a *b* while the third doubles a *kh*. Chapter 2 gives the general process; this chapter gives the reason the prefix has a consonant to contribute at all.

**Say it aloud.**

38. Read this verse aloud five times, slowly, holding the long vowels and sounding the double consonants. Then find the three derived words in it and name the suffix of each.

    Maggānaṭṭhaṅgiko seṭṭho,\
    saccānaṁ caturo padā;\
    Virāgo seṭṭho dhammānaṁ,\
    dvipadānañca cakkhumā. (Dhp 273 — Dhp p. 39)

**One root, three formations.** A single Vinaya rule uses the same root as a verb, as an action noun and as a past participle, and names the whole class of offence after one of them.

39. Nissajjitabbaṁ saṅghassa vā gaṇassa vā puggalassa vā. (Bu Np 1 — Vin iii 196) — Gloss it. Then account for the formation of *nissajjitabbaṁ*, and of *nissaggiyaṁ* and *nissaṭṭhaṁ*, which the same rule uses a few lines later. All three are built on one root; say what each suffix does and what part of speech each result is.

### Coming back — word formation
