The set is not finished on the day it is worked. Re-attempt these, from cold, without reading the answers first.

**Day 3.** Items 14–24. Recognising a suffix is the only thing in this chapter a reader does at speed, and it is the first thing to go. If the eleven come back without hesitation, the chapter has done its work.

**Day 10.** Items 25–29, and item 39. Building a form is the operation run backwards, and it is harder than it looks because nothing in reading ever asks for it.

**Day 30.** Items 1, 3 and 4 aloud, and item 33. If *sāmaññaṁ*, *katapuññatā* and *bāhusaccaṁ* still take you to the right letter of the dictionary without thinking, the front vuddhi has stopped being a rule and become a reflex, which is all this chapter wanted.

## Exercises: Case Syntax

This chapter's claim is that an ending narrows the possibilities and something else decides among them. So the set makes you decide instead of rewarding you for reciting a list of uses. Most items give you a real sentence and ask what the case *means there*, and several give you a form whose ending is truly ambiguous and ask how you would settle it.

Stage 1 is four passages with every case already named for you, because hearing what an ending means is a different skill from working it out, and it comes first. Five of the later items contain a trap. They are not marked. Where you find one, say what the trap is as well as what the answer is. Worked answers are at the back.

### Sotānugata — case syntax

Nothing is withheld. Each line comes with its gloss, its case labels and its rendering, and the work is to say it and to hear the endings doing different jobs.

1. Atha kho bhagavā kismiñcideva pakaraṇe bhikkhusaṅghaṁ paṇāmetvā pubbaṇhasamayaṁ nivāsetvā pattacīvaramādāya kapilavatthuṁ piṇḍāya pāvisi. (SN 22.80 — S iii 91)

    *atha kho* then · *bhagavā* the Blessed One (m. nom. sg.) · *kismiñcideva* for some or other (nt. loc. sg.) · *pakaraṇe* affair (nt. loc. sg.) · *bhikkhusaṅghaṁ* the Saṅgha of monks (m. acc. sg., object) · *paṇāmetvā* having dismissed (absol.) · *pubbaṇhasamayaṁ* in the morning (m. acc. sg., adverbial) · *nivāsetvā* having dressed (absol.) · *pattacīvaraṁ* bowl and robe (nt. acc. sg., object) · *ādāya* having taken (absol.) · *kapilavatthuṁ* Kapilavatthu (nt. acc. sg., goal) · *piṇḍāya* for alms (m. dat. sg., purpose) · *pāvisi* he entered (3 sg. aor.)

    "Then the Blessed One, having dismissed the Saṅgha of monks over some matter or other, dressed in the morning, took his bowl and robe, and entered Kapilavatthu for alms."

    Say it aloud slowly, and hear that four words in this sentence carry the accusative ending with different functions. Name the four, say what each one does, and then say which of the four is not a participant in the action at all. This is the sentence the chapter opens with, and items 14 and 15 will come back to two of its words cold.

2. Puttā matthi dhanaṁ matthi, iti bālo vihaññati; (Dhp 62 — Dhp p. 9)

    *puttā* sons (m. nom. pl.) · *matthi* < *me* of me (1st gen./dat. sg.) + *atthi* there is · *dhanaṁ* wealth (nt. nom. sg.) · *iti* thus · *bālo* the fool (m. nom. sg.) · *vihaññati* is tormented

    "'I have sons, I have wealth' — so the fool torments himself."

    Say it aloud. Pāli has no verb 'to have', and this line shows what it does instead. Give the literal structure of the first half, and which case carries the possessor. Then two things about *matthi*: say what has happened at the join, and say why a reader looking for the possessor at the front of the word will not find it. The case of *me* here cannot be decided, and item 37 will ask you what the tradition does about that.

3. Ajjeva rattiyā pacchime yāme tathāgatassa parinibbānaṁ bhavissati. (DN 16 — D ii 139)

    *ajja* today · *eva* just · *rattiyā* of the night (f. gen. sg., partitive) · *pacchime* last (m. loc. sg.) · *yāme* in the watch (m. loc. sg., time when) · *tathāgatassa* of the Tathāgata (m. gen. sg.) · *parinibbānaṁ* final extinguishment (nt. nom. sg.) · *bhavissati* will be

    "This very day, in the last watch of the night, the Tathāgata's final extinguishment will take place."

    Say it aloud. There is a genitive inside the locative phrase, in the most ordinary use a genitive has. Name it and say what that use is. Then say why *rattiyā*, alone in the line, has an ending that settles nothing on its own, and what settles it.

4. Ayaṁ, deva, pūraṇo kassapo saṅghī ceva gaṇī ca gaṇācariyo ca ñāto yasassī titthakaro sādhusammato bahujanassa rattaññū cirapabbajito addhagato vayoanuppatto. (DN 2 — D i 47)

    *ayaṁ* this (m. nom. sg.) · *deva* your majesty (m. voc. sg.) · *pūraṇo kassapo* Pūraṇa Kassapa (m. nom. sg.) · *saṅghī* having a community (m. nom. sg.) · *ceva … ca* both … and · *gaṇī* having a following · *gaṇācariyo* a teacher of a following · *ñāto* well known · *yasassī* renowned · *titthakaro* a founder of a sect · *sādhusammato* held in high regard · *bahujanassa* by many people (m. gen. sg., agent with a past participle) · *rattaññū* of long standing · *cirapabbajito* long gone forth · *addhagato* advanced in years · *vayoanuppatto* come to the last stage of life

    "This Pūraṇa Kassapa, your majesty, has a community and a following, is a teacher of a following, well known and renowned, a founder of a sect, held in high regard by many people, of long standing, long gone forth, advanced in years and come to the last stage of life."

    Say it aloud. Eleven words in a row in one case, and then one word that is not. Find it, and say which case would ordinarily stand there, and why this one stands there instead. Then say what holds the eleven together, given that no verb governs any of them.

### Vacasā paricitā — case syntax

Recite each until it is fluent. Then cover the Pāli and rebuild it from the gloss alone, aloud.

5. Musāvādā veramaṇīsikkhāpadaṁ samādiyāmi. (Kp 2)

    *musāvādā* from false speech (m. abl. sg.) · *veramaṇīsikkhāpadaṁ* < *veramaṇī* abstaining + *sikkhāpadaṁ* training rule — the training rule of abstaining (nt. acc. sg.) · *samādiyāmi* I undertake

    "I undertake the training rule of abstaining from false speech."

    Many readers of this book will have said this line, or one of its four fellows, more often than any other sentence in Pāli. Recite it and rebuild it from the gloss. Then answer the question that reciting it does not: the ablative is not governed by the verb. Say what does govern it, and where that word is. Then say what the answer implies about whether the phrase could survive being compounded, as it has been.

6. Etaṁ nibbidāya virāgāya nirodhāya upasamāya abhiññāya sambodhāya nibbānāya saṁvattati. (DN 9 — D i 189)

    *etaṁ* this (nt. nom. sg.) · *nibbidāya* to disenchantment (f. dat. sg.) · *virāgāya* to dispassion (m. dat. sg.) · *nirodhāya* to cessation (m. dat. sg.) · *upasamāya* to peace (m. dat. sg.) · *abhiññāya* to direct knowledge (f. dat. sg.) · *sambodhāya* to awakening (m. dat. sg.) · *nibbānāya* to extinguishment (nt. dat. sg.) · *saṁvattati* leads

    "This leads to disenchantment, to dispassion, to cessation, to peace, to direct knowledge, to awakening, to extinguishment."

    Rebuild it from the gloss. Seven datives in a row: now check the claim that all seven are equally readable as datives. Sort them by stem class, count how many carry an ending that can be nothing but a dative, and say what the remainder are. Then say what makes the remainder readable anyway, and set item 25's rule beside your answer: the two should agree, and in this set they once did not.

7. Evaṁ passaṁ, bhikkhave, sutavā ariyasāvako rūpasmimpi nibbindati, vedanāyapi nibbindati, saññāyapi nibbindati, saṅkhāresupi nibbindati, viññāṇasmimpi nibbindati. (SN 22.59 — S iii 68)

    *evaṁ* thus · *passaṁ* seeing (m. nom. sg., pres. part.) · *bhikkhave* monks (voc. pl.) · *sutavā* learned (m. nom. sg.) · *ariyasāvako* noble disciple (m. nom. sg.) · *rūpasmiṁ* regarding form (nt. loc. sg.) · *pi* also · *nibbindati* becomes disenchanted · *vedanāya* regarding feeling (f. loc. sg.) · *saññāya* regarding perception (f. loc. sg.) · *saṅkhāresu* regarding volitional formations (m. loc. pl.) · *viññāṇasmiṁ* regarding consciousness (nt. loc. sg.)

    "Seeing thus, monks, a learned noble disciple becomes disenchanted with form, with feeling, with perception, with volitional formations, with consciousness."

    Rebuild it from the gloss. The chapter says this sentence is the whole chapter in one line. Say why: how many of the five endings can only be locative, how many could be five cases each, and what the first group does for the second. Item 36 will ask you to do the same work on one of these words with the sentence taken away.

8. Tiṭṭhatu, bhante, bhagavā kappaṁ, tiṭṭhatu sugato kappaṁ bahujanahitāya bahujanasukhāya lokānukampāya atthāya hitāya sukhāya devamanussānaṁ. (DN 16 — D ii 103)

    *tiṭṭhatu* let him remain (3 sg. imper.) · *bhante* sir (voc.) · *bhagavā* the Blessed One (m. nom. sg.) · *kappaṁ* for an aeon (m. acc. sg., extent of time) · *sugato* the Fortunate One (m. nom. sg.) · *bahujanahitāya* for the welfare of many people (nt. dat. sg.) · *bahujanasukhāya* for the happiness of many people (nt. dat. sg.) · *lokānukampāya* out of compassion for the world (f. dat. sg.) · *atthāya* for the benefit (m. dat. sg.) · *hitāya* for the welfare (nt. dat. sg.) · *sukhāya* for the happiness (nt. dat. sg.) · *devamanussānaṁ* of gods and humans (m. gen. pl.)

    "Let the Blessed One remain for an aeon, sir; let the Fortunate One remain for an aeon, for the welfare of many people, for the happiness of many people, out of compassion for the world, for the benefit and welfare and happiness of gods and humans."

    Rebuild it from the gloss. One accusative among the datives, and it is not an object. Name its function. Then take the genitive at the end and say how many of the datives it reaches back over, and how you know where to stop.

### Manasānupekkhitā — case syntax

**Recall.** Answer from memory, in a phrase, before opening the chapter.

9. The tradition counts eight of one thing and six of another. Name both, name the two of the eight that are not among the six, and give the tradition's reason for leaving them out.

10. Two pairs of cases have partly merged in form. Name both pairs, say where each pair still stays apart, and give the consequence for reading.

11. You meet a word whose ending could be five different cases. Name the two things you should look at, in order.

12. When do the nominative and the agent come apart, and what case does the agent take when they do?

13. Pāli has no verb corresponding to English 'have'. What does it use instead, and what is the literal shape of the resulting sentence?

**Name the case and its job.** For each bolded word, name the case and say which use of it this is. The sentences are all in the chapter.

14. *kapilavatthuṁ **piṇḍāya** pāvisi*

15. ***kapilavatthuṁ** piṇḍāya pāvisi*

16. ***pubbaṇhasamayaṁ** nivāsetvā*

17. *svākkhāto **bhagavatā** dhammo*

18. *paccattaṁ veditabbo **viññūhi***

19. *sabbe tasanti **daṇḍassa***

20. *purisaṁ … **kesesu** gahetvā*

21. ***jātipaccayā** jarāmaraṇaṁ*

22. *aññataro kho panāyasmā subhaddo **arahataṁ** ahosi*

23. *sakkā panetaṁ, bhante, **mayā** ñātuṁ*

24. *tathāgatassa **vesāliyā** dassanaṁ*

### Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — case syntax

**Choose the case.** Produce the answer before checking it.

25. 'For the welfare of many people, for the happiness of many people.' Which case, and what is the ending on an *a*-stem? Then say why you would not be able to identify that case with the same confidence if the nouns were feminine *ā*-stems.

26. A verb of fearing or trembling takes its complement in one particular case, which is not the one an English speaker expects. Name the case, and give two such verbs.

27. Pāli has two ways of forming an ablative and one of them is unmistakable. Name it, say why Wijesekera declines to call it a proper case ending at all, and give three examples of it from this chapter.

28. A reputable grammar lists *appamaññati* 'thinks lightly of' among the verbs governing the genitive and cites Dhammapada 121 for it. Look the verse up. Say what the canon actually prints, what the verb actually is, and what has happened at the join.

29. *Itthattāya*: name the case, give the stem it is built on and the suffix, and say why the bare word is almost never found in the texts.

**Read the sentence.** Give the three layers: the line as printed, a word-by-word gloss naming the case of every inflected word, and your own rendering.

30. Seyyathāpi, bho gotama, puriso purisaṁ narakapapātaṁ patantaṁ kesesu gahetvā uddharitvā thale patiṭṭhapeyya. (DN 12 — D i 234)

**Put it back into Pāli.** The answer is a particular line of canon, and getting close to it is the exercise. A well-formed sentence that is not the one in the book is a good result and is not this exercise.

31. 'Birth is exhausted, the spiritual life has been lived out, what was to be done has been done, there is no further coming to this state of being.' The last four words are three: a negative, a comparative adverb, and one noun in one case.

32. 'All tremble at the rod, all fear death.' Two verbs, two nouns, and the nouns are not in the case an English speaker would put them in.

**Read at length.**

33. Read **Ud 1.10**, the Bāhiya sutta, twice through without stopping to parse anything. It is short, it is one of the best-known passages in the canon, and the instruction at its centre is almost entirely a matter of case syntax. Only then answer:

   - *Diṭṭhe diṭṭhamattaṁ bhavissati, sute sutamattaṁ bhavissati* … Four words in the same case open the four clauses. Name the case, and say which of the tradition's four kinds of locative you think it is, and why the question is harder than it looks.
   - Each of those four clauses has a second inflected word, and it is not in the same case as the first. Name that case and say what it does there.
   - Bāhiya asks the Buddha to teach him and is refused twice. Find the sentence in which he presses his request and name the case of every inflected word in it. One of them is open between two cases; say which, and why the chapter would not force a choice.
   - The sutta's closing verse turns on *yattha* and *tattha*. Are these case forms? Say what they are built on and where in this book each half of the answer is treated.

**Reaching back.**

34. **Chapter 12.** *Saddhiṁ* and *saha* govern the instrumental, and *aññatra* and *vinā* govern it too. Say in one sentence why 'with' and 'without' should govern the same case, using the tradition's name for that case's fundamental sense.

35. **Chapter 13.** A *tappurisa* compound is a case relation with the ending taken off. Resolve *bhikkhusaṅgho* and *kāmataṇhā* into two words each, and say why the recovered cases are different. Then say which fact from *this* chapter is what actually decides the second one.

36. **Chapter 3.** The feminine *ā*-stem singular ending *-āya* does five jobs. List them. Then take *vedanāya* from item 7 and say, in the chapter's terms, what settles which of the five it is there.

37. **Chapter 5.** *Evaṁ me sutaṁ*. Say what Buddhaghosa does with the *me*, and what this chapter recommends a reader do when the tradition itself declines to choose.

**A chain of genitives.** The chapter takes the first half of this definition. Here is the second, which differs from it in one word.

38. Aparikkhittassa gāmassa gharūpacāre ṭhitassa majjhimassa purisassa leḍḍupāto. (Bu Pj 2 — Vin iii 46) Gloss it. Then say which noun each genitive hangs on, and how you know, since the endings cannot show it.

**Say it aloud.**

39. Read these five verses aloud, slowly, twice through. They are consecutive in the Dhammapada and they are the same sentence five times. Then say which two of the five bolded words carry an ending that could be more than one case, and what makes them readable anyway.

   **Piyato** jāyatī soko, piyato jāyatī bhayaṁ; (Dhp 212 — Dhp p. 32)\
   **Pemato** jāyatī soko, pemato jāyatī bhayaṁ; (Dhp 213 — Dhp p. 32)\
   **Ratiyā** jāyatī soko, ratiyā jāyatī bhayaṁ; (Dhp 214 — Dhp p. 32)\
   **Kāmato** jāyatī soko, kāmato jāyatī bhayaṁ; (Dhp 215 — Dhp p. 32)\
   **Taṇhāya** jāyatī soko, taṇhāya jāyatī bhayaṁ. (Dhp 216 — Dhp p. 32)

### Coming back — case syntax

The set is not finished on the day it is worked. Re-attempt these, from cold, without reading the answers first.

**Day 3.** Items 14–24. Naming a case is the operation this chapter exists to make automatic, and eleven bolded words are the whole of it in miniature. The five that carry a trap are the five to check yourself hardest on.

**Day 10.** Items 25–29, and item 30. Choosing a case is the harder direction, and repeat item 28 even when the rest come back: a reader is least prepared to find a source wrong.

**Day 30.** Items 1 and 7 aloud, and item 39. If *vedanāya* still settles itself out of the company it keeps, the chapter's claim has been understood, not merely agreed with: the ending narrows and the sentence decides.

## Exercises: Clause and Sentence Syntax

This chapter's claim is that Pāli subordinates by case and by participle, not by conjunction, and that the reader has to supply the relation the language leaves unstated. So the set is arranged around the one decision that governs everything else, whose action this is, and only then asks what the relation is.

Every sentence below is real canon, and cited. Stage 1 is four passages in which the subordination is already named for you, and the first three are chosen so that one thing changes at a time. Three of the later items contain a trap. They are not marked. Where you find one, say what the trap is as well as what the answer is. Worked answers are at the back.

### Sotānugata — clause and sentence syntax

Nothing is withheld. Each passage comes with its gloss and its rendering, and the work is to say it and to hear where one clause stops and the next begins.

1. Sīho, bhikkhave, migarājā sāyanhasamayaṁ āsayā nikkhamati. Āsayā nikkhamitvā vijambhati. Vijambhitvā samantā catuddisā anuviloketi. Samantā catuddisā anuviloketvā tikkhattuṁ sīhanādaṁ nadati. (AN 4.33 — A ii 33)

    *sīho* the lion · *bhikkhave* monks · *migarājā* king of beasts · *sāyanhasamayaṁ* in the evening (m. acc. sg., adverbial) · *āsayā* from his lair (m. abl. sg.) · *nikkhamati* comes out · *nikkhamitvā* having come out · *vijambhati* he stretches · *vijambhitvā* having stretched · *samantā catuddisā* in all four directions · *anuviloketi* he surveys · *anuviloketvā* having surveyed · *tikkhattuṁ* three times · *sīhanādaṁ* a lion's roar (m. acc. sg.) · *nadati* he roars

    "The lion, monks, the king of beasts, comes out from his lair in the evening. Having come out from his lair, he stretches. Having stretched, he surveys the four directions all around. Having surveyed the four directions all around, he roars a lion's roar three times."

    Say it aloud, all four sentences, without stopping between them. Each one opens by repeating in the absolutive what the sentence before ended with as a finite verb. Say what single condition licenses the absolutive throughout, and what would have had to change in the Pāli if the lion had not been the one doing all four things. Then say what the pattern is *for*. It has a purpose, and the purpose has more to do with how a passage was carried than with how it was parsed.

2. Parinibbute bhagavati saha parinibbānā mahābhūmicālo ahosi bhiṁsanako salomahaṁso. (DN 16 — D ii 156)

    *parinibbute* fully extinguished (m. loc. sg., past participle) · *bhagavati* the Blessed One (m. loc. sg.) · *saha parinibbānā* together with the full extinguishment · *mahābhūmicālo* a great earthquake (m. nom. sg.) · *ahosi* there was (3 sg. aor.) · *bhiṁsanako* terrifying · *salomahaṁso* hair-raising

    "When the Blessed One had attained final Nibbāna, there was, together with the final Nibbāna, a great earthquake, terrifying and hair-raising."

    Say it aloud. No word in this sentence means 'when'. Say what carries it. Then say what the *tense* of the participle settles about the order of the two events, and what would have been settled differently had the participle been a present one. The next item is that sentence.

3. Imasmiñca pana veyyākaraṇasmiṁ bhaññamāne āyasmato koṇḍaññassa virajaṁ vītamalaṁ dhammacakkhuṁ udapādi. (SN 56.11 — S v 423)

    *imasmiṁ* this (nt. loc. sg.) · *veyyākaraṇasmiṁ* exposition (nt. loc. sg.) · *bhaññamāne* being spoken (nt. loc. sg., present participle of the passive *bhaññati*, from *bhaṇati*) · *āyasmato koṇḍaññassa* of the venerable Koṇḍañña (m. gen. sg.) · *virajaṁ* dust-free · *vītamalaṁ* stainless · *dhammacakkhuṁ* the eye of the Dhamma (nt. nom. sg.) · *udapādi* arose (3 sg. aor.)

    "And while this exposition was being spoken, the dust-free, stainless eye of the Dhamma arose in the venerable Koṇḍañña."

    Say it aloud. This is item 2's construction with one thing changed. Name the thing, and say what it changes about the timing of the two events. Then say what makes the pair worth learning together, not separately: between them they carry a rule the chapter states in one line and never has to repeat.

4. Pavattite ca pana bhagavatā dhammacakke bhummā devā saddamanussāvesuṁ. (SN 56.11 — S v 423)

    *pavattite* set rolling (nt. loc. sg., past part.) · *bhagavatā* by the Blessed One (m. ins. sg.) · *dhammacakke* the wheel of Dhamma (nt. loc. sg.) · *bhummā devā* the earth-dwelling gods (m. nom. pl.) · *saddaṁ* a cry (m. acc. sg.) · *anussāvesuṁ* raised (3 pl. aor.)

    "And when the wheel of Dhamma had been set rolling by the Blessed One, the earth-dwelling gods raised a cry."

    Say it aloud, immediately after item 3; the two stand one sentence apart in the same sutta. This is item 2's construction again, with one word added. Name the word and its case. Then say why the grammatical subject of the subordinate clause and its logical subject are standing in two different cases, and which of the two a reader should follow to find out who did the thing.

### Vacasā paricitā — clause and sentence syntax

Recite each until it is fluent. Then cover the Pāli and rebuild it from the gloss alone, aloud.

5. Yā pana bhikkhunī lasuṇaṁ khādeyya, pācittiyaṁ. (Bi Pc 1 — Vin iv 259)

    *yā* whatever (f. nom. sg.) · *pana* (filler) · *bhikkhunī* nun (f. nom. sg.) · *lasuṇaṁ* garlic (nt. acc. sg.) · *khādeyya* should eat (3 sg. opt.) · *pācittiyaṁ* an offence requiring confession (nt. nom. sg.)

    "Whatever nun should eat garlic: an offence requiring confession."

    Six words, and most of the Pātimokkha is built on this frame. Recite it and rebuild it from the gloss. Then: there is no *sace*, no *ce* and no *yadi*, and the second half has no verb in it. Say what is carrying the 'if' and what is carrying the 'then'. Then say what *yā pana bhikkhunī* gives the rule that a name or a plural would not, and which chapter of this book that word belongs to.

6. **No cetaṁ**, bhikkhave, **abhavissa** ajātaṁ abhūtaṁ akataṁ asaṅkhataṁ, nayidha jātassa bhūtassa katassa saṅkhatassa nissaraṇaṁ **paññāyetha**. (Ud 8.3 — Ud p. 80)

    *no ce* if not · *etaṁ* this (nt. nom. sg.) · *abhavissa* were to be (3 sg. cond.) · *ajātaṁ* unborn · *abhūtaṁ* unbecome · *akataṁ* unmade · *asaṅkhataṁ* unconditioned (all nt. nom. sg.) · *na idha* not here · *jātassa* of the born (nt. gen. sg.) · *nissaraṇaṁ* escape (nt. nom. sg.) · *paññāyetha* would be discerned (3 sg. opt. mid. of the passive *paññāyati*)

    "If there were no unborn, unbecome, unmade, unconditioned, no escape from the born, become, made and conditioned would be discerned here."

    Recite it until it is fluent, then rebuild it from the gloss. Name the type of conditional and give the mood of each verb. Here, and almost nowhere else in the language, a reader regularly meets the conditional tense, and the reason has to be stated: say what the sentence would be asserting if the moods were indicative instead, and why that would be the opposite of the doctrine.

7. The sentence above is followed immediately by another. Say what that second sentence does to the first, and what construction it uses to do it. (Ud 8.3 — Ud p. 81)

### Manasānupekkhitā — clause and sentence syntax

**Recall.** Answer from memory, in a phrase, before opening the chapter.

8. You meet a chain of actions with only one finite verb at the end. What form are the other verbs in, and what one condition has to hold for the language to be allowed to do that?

9. Give the rule that decides between an absolutive and a locative absolute. One sentence.

10. Which two participles can stand as the predicate of an absolute construction, and which two cannot? Name the exception the chapter records to that rule.

11. The tradition has no word for 'absolute construction'. What does it have instead, and which two case-endings does its rule mention?

12. Name the three types of conditional sentence by the moods they use, and say which of the three is the only regular home of the conditional tense.

13. A sentence has no verb in it. Name the two verbs that are being left out, and say what happens to gender concord when a noun stands where the verb would be.

**Whose action is it?** For each sentence, say whether the bolded form belongs to the subject of the main verb or to somebody else, and name the construction accordingly.

14. Atha kho bhagavā pubbaṇhasamayaṁ **nivāsetvā** pattacīvaramādāya sāvatthiṁ piṇḍāya pāvisi. (MN 26 — M i 160)

15. Atha kho māro pāpimā **acirapakkante āyasmante ānande** yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkami. (DN 16 — D ii 104)

16. So kho ahaṁ … **akāmakānaṁ mātāpitūnaṁ assumukhānaṁ rudantānaṁ** kesamassuṁ ohāretvā … agārasmā anagāriyaṁ pabbajiṁ. (MN 26 — M i 163)

17. Addasā kho nigrodho paribbājako sandhānaṁ gahapatiṁ **dūratova āgacchantaṁ**. (DN 25 — D iii 37)

18. So kho ahaṁ, bhikkhave, aparena samayena **daharova samāno** susukāḷakeso … pabbajiṁ. (MN 26 — M i 163)

19. Te jātisaṁvattanikepi saṅkhāre **abhisaṅkharitvā** … jātipapātampi papatanti. (SN 56.42 — S v 449)

**Name the construction and its sense.** Say what the construction is, and which of its senses is at work here: time, attendant circumstance, cause, condition, hypothesis or concession.

20. **Jātiyā sati** jarāmaraṇaṁ hotī ti. (SN 12.49 — S ii 78)

21. **Deve vassante deve gaḷagaḷāyante vijjullatāsu niccharantīsu asaniyā phalantiyā** neva passeyya, na pana saddaṁ suṇeyya. (DN 16 — D ii 131)

22. Atha kho, bhikkhave, rājā khattiyo muddhābhisitto **dibbe cakkaratane antarahite** anattamano ahosi. (DN 26)

23. Atha kho brāhmaṇo pokkharasāti … yāne āropetvā **ukkāsu dhāriyamānāsu** ukkaṭṭhāya niyyāsi. (DN 3 — D i 108)

24. **Dassane, bhagavā, sati** kathaṁ paṭipajjitabban ti? (DN 16 — D ii 141)

25. **Āturakāyassa me sato** cittaṁ anāturaṁ bhavissatī ti. (SN 22.1 — S iii 1)

26. Seyyathāpi, ānanda, gahapatissa vā gahapatiputtassa vā manuññaṁ bhojanaṁ **bhuttāvissa** bhattasammado hoti. (DN 17 — D ii 195)

27. **Hatthesu**, bhikkhave, **sati** ādānanikkhepanaṁ paññāyati; **pādesu sati** abhikkamapaṭikkamo paññāyati. (SN 35.236 — S iv 171)

### Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — clause and sentence syntax

**The sentence types.**

28. **Sace** maṁ bhagavā **anujāneyya**, **āgaccheyyāhaṁ** imaṁ ambavanaṁ padhānāya. (AN 9.3 — A iv 355) — which type, and how do you know?

29. **Āditte**, bhikkhave, **cele vā sīse vā** kimassa karaṇīyan ti? (SN 56.34 — S v 440) — there is no *sace* and no *ce* here. What is carrying the condition, and would you translate it 'when' or 'if'?

30. Appamādo amatapadaṁ, pamādo maccuno padaṁ. (Dhp 21 — Dhp p. 4) — what is missing, and what has happened to the concord?

31. 'Pasaṁsā me āgacchatū' ti sīlaṁ rakkheyya paṇḍito. (Iti 76 — It p. 67) — the sentence ends a quotation with *ti* and nobody is speaking. What is the *ti* for?

32. Seyyathāpi, bhante, purisassa agāraṁ ekādasadvāraṁ, so tasmiṁ agāre āditte ekamekenapi dvārena sakkuṇeyya attānaṁ sotthiṁ kātuṁ. (MN 52 — M i 353) — name the three constructions in this one sentence and say why the verb is optative.

33. Atthi nu kho, bhante, añño koci mayā upaṭṭhākataro ti? (MN 81 — M ii 51) — two things mark this as a question. Name both, and say which of them a reader should trust.

**Adjudicate.**

34. Santaṁ yeva kho pana paraṁ lokaṁ 'natthi paro loko' ti saṅkappeti. (MN 60 — M i 402) — name the construction, and say what evidence the chapter offers that the tradition recognised it.

35. Santaṁ vā ajjhattaṁ byāpādaṁ 'atthi me ajjhattaṁ byāpādo' ti pajānāti. (MN 10 — M i 60) — this looks like the item above and two good grammars read it differently. Give both readings, and say what would settle it.

36. The Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana and the Buddha Jayanti print *Saṁvaṭṭamāne loke yebhuyyena sattā ābhassarasaṁvattanikā honti* at DN 1 — D i 17; the Pali Text Society prints *Saṁvaṭṭamāno loko* at the same place. Three grammars build a grammatical category on the second reading. What is the category, and what should a reader conclude?

37. Kaccāyana's *vutti* on the *anādara* rule gives two sentences side by side: *Rudato dārakassa pabbaji, rudantasmiṁ dārake pabbaji*. Say what each is, why they are printed together, and one reason a reader should not take either as an example of canonical Pāli.

**Read the sentence.** Translate, then say what each construction is contributing.

38. Yatra hi nāma tathāgato atīte buddhe parinibbute … jātitopi anussarissati, nāmatopi anussarissati, gottatopi anussarissati. (DN 14 — D ii 8)

**The case law.** The Vibhaṅga's case reports are short, and each packs several of this chapter's constructions into one sentence because it has to say who did what and in what order before it can decide anything.

39. Tena kho pana samayena aññataro bhikkhu saṅghassa odane bhājīyamāne—"aparassa bhāgaṁ dehī"ti amūlakaṁ aggahesi. (Bu Pj 2 — Vin iii 59) — Gloss it and render it. Three of this chapter's constructions are here. Name them, and say which of the three tells you that the monk was not the one distributing the rice.

**The reading passage.** Ānanda asks the Buddha, shortly before the *parinibbāna*, how monks are to conduct themselves towards women. The exchange is short and turns entirely on this chapter's constructions.

40. Read DN 16 — D ii 141 and translate it. Then answer three questions about it in prose, not in a list: which construction carries the second and third questions and what its sense is there; why *sati* has the ending it has, given the case of the word it goes with; and what the whole exchange demonstrates about how much of a Pāli sentence can be left unsaid.

### Coming back — clause and sentence syntax

The set is not finished on the day it is worked. Re-attempt these, from cold, without reading the answers first.

**Day 3.** Items 14–19. **Whose action is it** is the decision this chapter exists to make automatic, and it is prior to every other question in the set: get it wrong and the sense of the construction cannot be right by accident. Two of the six carry a trap.

**Day 10.** Items 20–27, and item 39. In naming the sense of an absolute a reader stops reading grammar and starts reading the passage. Nothing in the words says *cause* or *concession*, and in item 39 getting it right decides a case at law.

**Day 30.** Items 1 to 4 aloud, and item 40. If the three locative absolutes still sort themselves by the tense of the participle without your going back to the rule, the chapter's central claim has stopped being a rule and become the way you read.

## Exercises: Relative Clauses

This chapter's claim is that Pāli subordinates by pairing, not by embedding, and that the relative clause therefore arrives before there is anything to attach it to. So the set is arranged around the reader's actual difficulty (what is the answering word, and what does each half of the pair agree with?), and only then asks what kind of clause it is.

Every sentence below is real canon, and cited. Stage 1 gives you four pairs with both halves already named, and the group that follows in stage 3 is the same exercise on four sentences where the pairing goes wrong in some way. Six of the later items contain a trap. They are not marked. Where you find one, say what the trap is as well as what the answer is. Worked answers are at the back.

### Sotānugata — relative clauses

Nothing is withheld. Each line comes with its gloss and its rendering, and the work is to say it and to hear the two halves answer each other.

1. Atha kho bhagavā yena dvārena nikkhami, taṁ gotamadvāraṁ nāma ahosi. (DN 16 — D ii 89)

    *atha kho* then · *bhagavā* the Blessed One · *yena dvārena* by which gate (n. instr. sg.) · *nikkhami* went out (aor.) · *taṁ* that (n. nom. sg.) · *gotamadvāraṁ* the Gotama Gate (n. nom. sg.) · *nāma* by name · *ahosi* became (aor.)

    "The gate by which the Blessed One went out came to be called the Gotama Gate."

    Say it aloud. This is the construction at its plainest: one word opens, one word closes, and they are the same word twice. Name the two, and say what is identical about them and what is not. Then say which half of the concord rule accounts for the sameness and which half for the difference. The rule has two halves and this sentence needs both.

2. Yo, bhikkhave, rāgakkhayo dosakkhayo mohakkhayo — idaṁ vuccati, bhikkhave, asaṅkhataṁ. (SN 43.12 — S iv 362)

    *yo* which (m. nom. sg.) · *bhikkhave* monks · *rāgakkhayo* the ending of greed (m. nom. sg.) · *dosakkhayo* the ending of hate · *mohakkhayo* the ending of delusion · *idaṁ* this (n. nom. sg.) · *vuccati* is called · *asaṅkhataṁ* the unconditioned (n. nom. sg.)

    "The ending of greed, the ending of hate, the ending of delusion: this, monks, is called the unconditioned."

    Say it aloud. The answering word is not from the same stem as the relative, and it is not even the same gender. Name it, and say what each of the two takes its gender from. If you have learnt *yo … so* as a fixed pair you will look past this one entirely, so say also what the general rule is that *yo … idaṁ* obeys.

3. Yāni kānici, bhikkhave, bhayāni uppajjanti sabbāni tāni bālato uppajjanti, no paṇḍitato. (AN 3.1 — A i 101)

    *yāni kānici* whatever (n. nom. pl.) · *bhayāni* dangers (n. nom. pl.) · *uppajjanti* arise · *sabbāni tāni* all those (n. nom. pl.) · *bālato* from the fool (m. abl. sg.) · *no paṇḍitato* not from the astute one

    "Whatever dangers arise, monks, all of them arise from the fool, not from the astute one."

    Say it aloud. The relative has been generalised and so has the word that answers it, and each has taken a second word to do it. Name both additions, and say what each contributes. Then say why a generalised relative needs a generalised correlative at all, when item 1 managed with a bare pair.

4. **Yā** kho, bhikkhave, imesaṁ tiṇṇaṁ dhammānaṁ saṅgati sannipāto samavāyo, **ayaṁ** vuccati cakkhusamphasso. (SN 35.93 — S iv 68)

    *yā* which (f. nom. sg.) · *imesaṁ tiṇṇaṁ dhammānaṁ* of these three things (m. gen. pl.) · *saṅgati* the meeting (f. nom. sg.) · *sannipāto* the coming together (m. nom. sg.) · *samavāyo* the concurrence (m. nom. sg.) · *ayaṁ* this (m. nom. sg.) · *cakkhusamphasso* eye-contact (m. nom. sg.)

    "The meeting, the coming together, the concurrence of these three things is called eye-contact."

    Say it aloud. The two pronouns point at the same thing and are in different genders, and neither is wrong. Say what each takes its gender from. There are three nouns in the relative clause and they are not all the same gender, so name which one *yā* has chosen and why. This is the sentence to keep in mind for the four items of stage 3 that ask the same question and get a different answer.

### Vacasā paricitā — relative clauses

Recite each until it is fluent. Then cover the Pāli and rebuild it from the gloss alone, aloud.

5. Yaṁ kiñci samudayadhammaṁ sabbaṁ taṁ nirodhadhammaṁ. (SN 56.11 — S v 423)

    *yaṁ kiñci* whatever (n. nom. sg.) · *samudayadhammaṁ* is of the nature to arise (n. nom. sg., adj. compound *samudaya* + *dhamma*) · *sabbaṁ taṁ* all that (n. nom. sg.) · *nirodhadhammaṁ* is of the nature to cease (n. nom. sg., *nirodha* + *dhamma*)

    "Whatever is of the nature to arise, all that is of the nature to cease."

    This is what Koṇḍañña saw, and it is probably the best-known relative sentence in the canon; most readers will have it already. Recite it and rebuild it from the gloss. Then: there is no verb in it anywhere. Say what holds the sentence together in place of one, and how the two halves bear on each other. Then compare it with item 3 and say what the two constructions have in common.

6. Yathā yathā vā panassa kāyo paṇihito hoti tathā tathā naṁ pajānāti. (MN 10 — M i 57)

    *yathā yathā* in whatever way · *vā pana* or moreover · *assa* his (m. gen. sg.) · *kāyo* body (m. nom. sg.) · *paṇihito* is disposed (pp.) · *hoti* is · *tathā tathā* in just that way · *naṁ* it (m. acc. sg.) · *pajānāti* he understands

    "Or however his body is disposed, he understands it accordingly."

    Recite it and rebuild it from the gloss. Both halves of the pair are doubled, and the doubling is not emphasis. Say what it contributes, and why the instruction would be a weaker one if either word stood singly. Then name the kind of clause the pair opens.

7. Yasmā ca kho, ānanda, suññaṁ attena vā attaniyena vā tasmā suñño lokoti vuccati. (SN 35.85 — S iv 54)

    *yasmā* because · *ca kho* (filler) · *ānanda* Ānanda · *suññaṁ* empty (n. nom. sg.) · *attena* of a self (m. instr. sg.) · *vā … vā* either … or · *attaniyena* of what belongs to a self (n. instr. sg.) · *tasmā* therefore · *suñño* empty (m. nom. sg.) · *loko* the world (m. nom. sg.) · *ti* end of quotation · *vuccati* is called

    "Because it is empty of a self or of what belongs to a self, Ānanda, it is said that the world is empty."

    Recite it and rebuild it from the gloss. Name the kind of clause the pair opens. Then note the **order**: the reason stands first and the conclusion second. Say whether that is the ordinary Pāli order or the exceptional one, and what an English speaker's habit would have done with the same two clauses. This sentence is the Buddha's reply to a question, and item 27 is that question.

### Manasānupekkhitā — relative clauses

**Recall.** Answer from memory, in a phrase, before opening the chapter.

8. State the rule that decides the number, gender and case of a relative pronoun. It has two halves; give both, and say which half identifies what the pronoun points at.

9. The tradition calls the relative pronoun *aniyamattha*. Translate the term, and say in one sentence why it is a better name for the class than "relative".

10. *Yad-* is in the paradigm and occurs nowhere in the canon as a word. Explain both facts at once.

11. Warder ranks the relative words by how much each of them means. Name the two he puts at the top of that ranking, and say why the ranking is also a difficulty ranking.

12. Name Buddhaghosa's four uses of *yaṁ* by the cases they correspond to. What single claim about the empty relative do the four of them together amount to?

13. Two words mean "because" and open a causal clause. Name both, and say which of the two belongs more to the canon and which to the commentaries.

14. Perniola says a relative clause or phrase can be expressed five ways. Name three of the four that are not the correlative sentence.

**Find the answering word.** For each sentence, name the word in the main clause that answers the relative, or say that there is none. Where there is none, say what is standing in for it. These are the three sentences of that group in which something is missing; items 1 to 3 are the three in which nothing is.

15. Ayaṁ vuccati, bhikkhave, bhikkhu phegguṁ aggahesi brahmacariyassa. (MN 29 — M i 196)

16. Yāvakīvañca, ānanda, vajjī abhiṇhaṁ sannipātā sannipātabahulā bhavissanti, vuddhiyeva, ānanda, vajjīnaṁ pāṭikaṅkhā, no parihāni. (DN 16 — D ii 73)

17. Ko nu kho, bhante, hetu ko paccayo, yena mātugāmo neva sabhāyaṁ nisīdati …? (AN 4.80 — A ii 82)

**What agrees with what.** For each bolded pronoun, say what it takes its gender and number from, and what it takes its case from. Name both sources separately.

18. Tatra, bhikkhave, **yo so** satto paṭhamaṁ upapanno **tassa** evaṁ hoti. (DN 1 — D i 18)

19. Yāvatakaṁ, bhante, sekhena ñāṇena sekhāya vijjāya pattabbaṁ, anuppattaṁ **taṁ** mayā. (MN 73 — M i 494)

20. Vinicchayā **yāni** pakappitāni, (iti māgaṇḍiyo) **Te** ve munī brūsi anuggahāya. (Snp 4.9 — Sn p. 164)

21. Yāvakīvañca, ānanda, vajjī **yā tā** kulitthiyo kulakumāriyo, **tā** na okkassa pasayha vāsessanti, vuddhiyeva, ānanda, vajjīnaṁ pāṭikaṅkhā, no parihāni. (DN 16 — D ii 74)

Supply the case of the empty relative. Each of these carries a *yaṁ* that does not obviously belong to any noun. Following Buddhaghosa's method, resolve it: say which case it stands for, and rewrite the phrase with that case made explicit, as he rewrites *yaṁ vipassī* as *yasmiṁ kappe vipassī*.

22. Ito so, bhikkhave, ekanavutikappe yaṁ vipassī bhagavā arahaṁ sammāsambuddho loke udapādi. (DN 14 — D ii 2)

23. Aṭṭhānametaṁ anavakāso yaṁ ekissā lokadhātuyā dve arahanto sammāsambuddhā apubbaṁ acarimaṁ uppajjeyyuṁ, netaṁ ṭhānaṁ vijjati. (DN 28 — D iii 114)

24. Tasmātihānanda, tuyhevetaṁ dukkaṭaṁ … yaṁ tvaṁ … na tathāgataṁ yāci. (DN 16 — D ii 115)

25. So, ānanda, samayo yaṁ mahāvātā vāyanti; mahāvātā vāyantā udakaṁ kampenti. (AN 8.70 — A iv 312)

### Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — relative clauses

**Name the clause and its sense.** For each, say what kind of subordinate clause the relative word opens (time, cause, place, manner, generalisation), and give the sense of the pair.

26. Yato ca kho, bhikkhave, tathāgato loke uppajjati arahaṁ sammāsambuddho, atha mahato ālokassa pātubhāvo hoti mahato obhāsassa. (SN 56.38 — S v 443)

27. Kittāvatā nu kho, bhante, suñño lokoti vuccatī ti? (SN 35.85 — S iv 54)

**What the counts license.** Three items that hand you a count and ask what may be concluded from it. Every figure is a count over the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana, given for the root texts, the commentaries, the sub-commentaries, and the literature outside all three.

28. Warder says *yasmā* is "a rarely used synonym of *yato*". In the root texts *yato* stands 1,370 times against *yasmā*'s 374. In the commentaries the order reverses, *yasmā* 3,317 against *yato*'s 590, and in the sub-commentaries it reverses again the same way, 1,605 against 327. State what is right about his claim, and what the counts add to it.

29. The chapter gives *yāvataka* and *yattaka* as relative adjectives of the same meaning. In the root texts *yāvatak-* occurs 117 times and its correlative *tāvatak-* 78; *yattak-* occurs 12 times and *tattak-* 15, of which four are *tatta-* "heated" inside a compound and not the pronoun at all. Say what you would tell a beginner to learn and what you would tell them to skip. Then say why those counts had to be taken on the stem *yāvatak-*, not on the dictionary form *yāvataka*.

30. *Yesānaṁ* occurs seven times in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana, and not once in the root texts, the commentaries or the sub-commentaries. Six of the seven are declension runs in Kaccāyana, the *Padarūpasiddhi* and the *Saddanīti*, grammars printing a paradigm, and the seventh is a verse in the *Thūpavaṁsa*, a mediaeval chronicle. What does that license you to say about the form, and what does it not license you to say?

**Adjudicate.**

31. Grammars differ on Buddhaghosa's third use of *yaṁ*, the one he calls *karaṇavacane*. Set out the two readings and the evidence for each, and say which you would follow and why. Do not settle it by counting.

32. A textbook prints the formula as *Yad 'aniccaṁ, taṁ dukkhaṁ*, with an apostrophe, on a quotation line. The canon has it solid, as *yadaniccaṁ*. Say what is wrong with the printed form, why the impulse behind it is nonetheless right, and where in this book's three-layer example format the resolution belongs.

33. Perniola illustrates the *kammadhāraya* alternative to a relative clause with *jātapuriso kālaṁ karissati*. Look for it in the canon, as a whole sentence and word by word. Then say what the result does and does not tell you about the construction he is illustrating.

**Read the sentence.** Give a literal rendering and then an idiomatic one, and say in a phrase what the relative construction does in each.

34. Sukarāni asādhūni, attano ahitāni ca; Yaṁ ve hitañca sādhuñca, taṁ ve paramadukkaraṁ. (Dhp 163 — Dhp p. 24)

35. Yassa accantadussīlyaṁ, māluvā sālamivotthataṁ; Karoti so tathattānaṁ, yathā naṁ icchatī diso. (Dhp 162 — Dhp p. 24)

The relative with nothing to hold on to. The Khandhaka's minor rules are the shortest sentences in the canon that are still whole sentences. Two of them, differing in one word.

36. Yo ugghaṁseyya, āpatti dukkaṭassa. (Kd 15 — Vin ii 105) — Render it. Then say what *yo* agrees with, what answers it, and how a reader knows who is meant.

37. Yo kārāpeyya, āpatti dukkaṭassa. (Kd 15 — Vin ii 133) — The same sentence with one word changed. Render it, and say what the change does to the person the rule catches.

**The reading passage.** **DN 16 — D ii 74**: three of the seven conditions for the non-decline of the Vajjians, in the form the Buddha states them to Ānanda.

The passage is chosen because it repeats one construction three times and changes only the gender: *ye te … te* of the elders, *yā tā … tā* of the women and girls, *yāni tāni … tāni* of the shrines. No other passage in the canon lets you watch the concord rule work three times over on the same sentence frame.

38. Set the three conditions out side by side and mark, in each, the outer relative, the inner relative, and every word that answers either. Two of the three carry an answering word in a case the inner relative is not in; find it, and say why it stands there.

39. The Buddha's statement of each condition is in the future tense, and Ānanda's report of the same fact a sentence earlier is in the present. Account for the difference using the tense rule in the chapter, and say what the future conveys that a translation into English "as long as they honour" leaves out.

40. *Yāvakīvañca* is answered by nothing in any of the three. Say what closes the *yāva* clause instead, and why the construction is not defective.

### Coming back — relative clauses

The set is not finished on the day it is worked. Re-attempt these, from cold, without reading the answers first.

**Day 3.** Items 15–21. Finding the answering word and saying what each half agrees with is the whole of the reading skill, and five of these seven are the sentences where the ordinary expectation fails. If you can say *why* each fails, the rule is understood, not memorised.

**Day 10.** Items 22–25, and items 36 and 37. The empty relative is the hardest thing in the chapter because there is nothing on the page to work from, and the two Khandhaka rules are the same difficulty stripped to six words apiece.

**Day 30.** Items 1 to 7 aloud, and item 38. If items 5 and 7 still come back whole (the sentence Koṇḍañña saw and the sentence about the empty world), then the two commonest shapes in the canon are in the memory, and the reading passage is only those shapes repeated with the gender changed.

## Exercises: Metre

Scansion is the only part of Pāli grammar that can be done wholly by rule, and the fastest way to learn it is to do a great deal of it. So this set is weighted differently from the rest of the workbook: the recall group is short, and most of the items ask you to mark up a line and say what you find. The four stages still order it, but here the first stage hands you the marks instead of the gloss, because for this chapter the marks *are* the gloss.

Write the marks above the line, syllable by syllable, before reading any further. **⏑** for a light syllable, **—** for a heavy one, **×** for the last syllable of a line, and **¦** where the line divides.

Every line below is real canon, cited, and every one has been checked. Seven of the items contain a trap. They are not marked. Where you find one, say what the trap is as well as what the answer is. Worked answers are at the back.

### Sotānugata — metre

Nothing is withheld. The four lines of this stage are the Buddha's first utterance after the awakening, standing at the head of the Vinaya, and they are given here already scanned. Recite each aloud against its marks until the marks stop being a diagram and start being the sound you are making.

1. Yadā have pātubhavanti dhammā, (Kd 1 — Vin i 2)

    *⏑—⏑—¦—⏑⏑¦—⏑—×*

    Say it aloud, slowly, letting each heavy syllable take its time. Then: five of the first ten syllables are heavy, and three different things have made them so. Sort the five into the three, and name the thing in each case. The one that catches people is the fourth.

2. Ātāpino jhāyato brāhmaṇassa; (Kd 1 — Vin i 2)

    *——⏑—¦—⏑—¦—⏑—×*

    Say it aloud. Every heavy syllable in this line but one is heavy for the same reason. Name that reason, name the exception and the reason for it, and say which two syllables would have caught you out if you had been marking from the shape of the letters. Then compare the marks with item 1's: they differ in two places. Find both, and say which of the two matters and which does not.

3. Athassa kaṅkhā vapayanti sabbā, (Kd 1 — Vin i 2)

    *⏑—⏑—¦—⏑⏑¦—⏑—×*

    Say it aloud, then say item 1 aloud after it. The two lines have the same eleven marks and not one word in common. Say what that identity is evidence of, and (the harder half) what it is *not* evidence of. Line four of the verse has the same eleven marks again.

4. Yato pajānāti sahetudhamman”ti. (Kd 1 — Vin i 2)

    *⏑—⏑—¦—⏑⏑¦—⏑—×*

    Say it aloud. There are eleven marks and the line as printed has twelve syllables. Say which syllable has gone unmarked and why. Then, from the four lines together: how many syllables has each, are they all the same, and what does that settle about which of the four families this verse belongs to?

### Vacasā paricitā — metre

Recite each line until it is fluent: four or five times, aloud, not silently. Then cover the letters and mark it from the sound alone, and only afterwards check your marks against the spelling. Say how many syllables you get.

5. Manopubbaṅgamā dhammā (Dhp 1 — Dhp p. 1)

6. manoseṭṭhā manomayā (Dhp 1 — Dhp p. 1)

7. Na hi verena verāni (Dhp 5 — Dhp p. 2)

8. esa dhammo sanantano (Dhp 5 — Dhp p. 2)

### Manasānupekkhitā — metre

**Recall.** Answer from memory, in a phrase, before opening the chapter.

9. Four Pāli terms are in play and it is fatal to collapse them. Give the two that describe a **vowel** and the two that describe a **syllable**, and say which pair is about length and which about weight.

10. State the three conditions that make a syllable heavy. Then state, in one clause, what makes a syllable light.

11. *E* and *o* are the awkward vowels. Say what is variable about them, and say whether a syllable containing one is ever light by rule.

12. Copy out the Vuttodaya's two words for the two marks, and say why a Pāli grammar printed in Devanāgarī may look as though it has scanned every line backwards.

13. What is a *yati*? Give the tradition's gloss on the term, and say what it is not.

14. The canon names a metre only once. Give the sutta, the word, and the sense the commentary assigns it.

15. Name the four families of metre in Pāli, with the Pāli term for each, and say in a phrase what fixes the length of a line in each.

16. What is the *pādantagaru* rule, and in which family of metres does it change the arithmetic?

**Scan the line.** Mark each by the four rules alone, text uncovered but no other aid to hand. Do not apply any exception yet. Say how many syllables you get.

17. Kālena dhammassavanaṁ (Kp 5)

18. Sabbattha sotthiṁ gacchanti (Kp 5)

19. Appaṁ vata jīvitaṁ idaṁ (Snp 4.6 — Sn p. 158)

20. Satto guhāyaṁ bahunābhichanno (Snp 4.2 — Sn p. 151)

### Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — metre

**Now say what disturbed the count.** Each of these gives the wrong number of syllables, or the wrong shape, when the four rules are applied as written. Say which accommodation is needed, and scan it again.

21. Dānañca dhammacariyā ca (Kp 5)

22. Nibbānasacchikiriyā ca (Kp 5)

23. Tapo ca brahmacariyañca (Kp 5)

24. ariyasaccāna dassanaṁ (Kp 5)

25. Patirūpadesavāso ca (Kp 5)

26. Taṁ tesaṁ maṅgalamuttamanti (Kp 5). Compare it with the refrain *etaṁ maṅgalamuttamaṁ*, which stands ten times in the same sutta.

27. Tiṭṭhaṁ naro mohanasmiṁ pagāḷho (Snp 4.2 — Sn p. 151). This one scans to the right number. Something else about it is irregular; say what, and say whether it is a fault.

**Metre as the cause.**

28. Dhp 183 — Dhp p. 27 reads *etaṁ buddhāna sāsanaṁ*. Write out the line as it would stand with the genitive plural in full, scan both versions, and say what the missing niggahīta buys.

29. Ānandajoti gives two examples of a form chosen "according to the needs of the metre": *daṭṭhu* for *disvā*, and *ghātvā* for *ghāyitvā*. One of the two should not be taught. Say which, and which check settles it.

30. A poet needs the final syllable of a word to be heavy and it is light. List the three kinds of change available, and say which of the three is the commonest in the texts and why it is nevertheless the hardest to demonstrate.

31. Explain, in two sentences, why *upekhā* and *upekkhā* scan alike although the *e* is pronounced differently in each.

**Name the pattern.**

32. Here is the profile of the Siloka's two lines:

    | | Pattern |
    |---|---|
    | Odd (prior) line | [⏑/—/⏑⏑] ⏓ ⏓ ⏓ ¦ ⏑ — — × |
    | Even (posterior) line | [⏑/—/⏑⏑] ⏓ ⏓ ⏓ ¦ ⏑ — ⏑ × |

    One syllable distinguishes them. Say which, and say what would be lost if the two lines were identical.

33. Scan Kp 5, *Ākaṅkhamānā sotthānaṁ*, and Kp 5, *Sabbattha sotthiṁ gacchanti*. Both depart from the *pathyā* in the same way. Name the variation, and give the further feature both lines have that the chapter says this variation normally takes.

34. Scan Kp 5, *Kālena dhammassavanaṁ*. Name the variation. Then say why someone who had learnt only the *pathyā* would have concluded that three lines of the Maṅgalasutta were corrupt.

35. These two lines stand next to each other in the same verse (Kp 6 and 6:8.3):

    Yathindakhīlo pathavissito siyā\
    Tathūpamaṁ sappurisaṁ vadāmi

    Scan both. They are in different metres. Name each, say what the difference between them amounts to, and say whether the change is a fault.

36. A line runs ⏓—⏑—⏑⏑⏑—×. Two different analyses fit the marks. Name both, and say what single piece of evidence, not in the marks, decides between them.

**What the evidence licenses.** Four items that hand you the evidence and ask what follows from it. Each answer is a judgement, not a lookup.

37. Ānandajoti scans Kp 6 with *abhabbŏ*, the final *-o* read light against its nature. The Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana prints *Chaccābhiṭhānāni **abhabba** kātuṁ*, and its apparatus records *abhabbo* against the siglum *sī.*, the Sinhalese edition; the Buddha Jayanti and the Pali Text Society both print *abhabbo*. Say what happens to the licence, and then what the case shows about claims of metrical licence in general.

38. The metre this book calls the Siloka has another name. In the Vuttodaya *vatta* stands six times, and the treatise's section on the metre is headed *Vattappabhedo*, "the varieties of *Vatta*"; the two apparent occurrences of *siloka* are both *Visiloka*, which is a different metre. Say which name the tradition uses. Then say why this book uses the other one anyway.

39. *Tuṭṭhubha* does not occur anywhere in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana, not even inside a longer word, so its absence is not an artefact of the form it was looked up in. The Vuttodaya, meanwhile, heads a section ***Tiṭṭhubhā*** (v. 74). Explain the two findings together, and say what they mean about the name in general use.

40. You scanned *esa dhammo sanantano* at item 8, from the Dhammapada. The same line stands in the Vinaya, at the close of the Kosambī quarrel, and there it is printed as one word fewer:

    Averena ca sammanti, esadhammo sanantano. (Kd 10 — Vin i 349)

    Scan the second half of it as the Vinaya prints it, and compare your marks with item 8's. Then answer the question the comparison raises: can the metre decide between the two divisions? Say what it can settle and what it cannot, and name the one feature of a line where a word division does become metrical evidence. Then say which way that feature falls here.

**Put it back into Pāli.**

41. This is the reverse of everything above: instead of taking a line apart, make one. The second line of the Kosambī verse is a Siloka even line of eight syllables, and you met the whole verse in the present-system set, where it was read for its verbs.

    Take the two words *sammanti*, "they are appeased", and *idha*, "here", and put them before *kudācanaṁ*. Written out plainly that is nine syllables and will not scan. Say what single operation reduces it to eight, write out the line as it stands in the canon, and check yourself against Kd 10 — Vin i 349. Then decide: is the operation one of the three kinds of metrical change you listed at item 30, or is it something else? Answer that, and say what the answer implies about how much of a verse's form the metre is responsible for.

**The reading passage.**

42. Scan the whole of Dhp 1 (six lines), marking the caesura where you hear one.

    Manopubbaṅgamā dhammā,\
    manoseṭṭhā manomayā;\
    Manasā ce paduṭṭhena,\
    bhāsati vā karoti vā;\
    Tato naṁ dukkhamanveti,\
    cakkaṁva vahato padaṁ. (Dhp 1 — Dhp p. 1)

    Then answer three questions about it. How many syllables has each line, and what does that tell you about the metre? Which lines are odd lines and which even, and how do you know from the marks alone, not from the position? And a Siloka verse normally has four lines — say what has happened here, and whether the chapter licenses it.

### Coming back — metre

The set is not finished on the day it is worked. Re-attempt these, from cold, without reading the answers first.

**Day 3.** Items 5–8 and 17–20. Eight lines is enough scanning to make the four rules automatic, and until they are automatic nothing in stage 4 can be attempted. Four of the eight carry a trap, so a clean second pass means the rules have displaced the rules of thumb.

**Day 10.** Items 21–27, and item 34. This is the whole of what disturbs a count, and the reason to come back to it as a block is that the accommodations are only learnable by contrast: sarabhatti, resolution, a lost niggahīta and a quotation marker outside the metre all look identical from the syllable count alone.

**Day 30.** Items 1–4 aloud, then items 40 and 41. If the first udāna still comes back whole, with its marks, then a Tuṭṭhubha is in the ear and not merely on the page, and the two Vinaya items test whether you can now use the instrument on a question it does not settle as well as on one it does.

## Review: Sounds and Script

A review is more than a recap. The exercises after each chapter test that chapter. This set tests what only becomes visible once several chapters are in place. Nothing here can be answered from a single chapter.

It covers **Script and Pronunciation** and **Sandhi**, the whole of Part I. The two chapters have one idea in common, and this review is built around it: the length of a syllable is a quantity that the language is constantly balancing, and a great deal of what looks like arbitrary spelling turns out to be that balancing. Almost every item below joins a fact about sounds to a fact about junctions.

The four stages run as they do in the chapter sets. What is different is that every line in stages 1 and 2 has already been worked in an earlier set, and is here to be asked a question that set could not ask.

### Sotānugata — sounds and script

Nothing is withheld. Each line is given as it stands, divided, and glossed. Say it aloud first and answer afterwards.

1. Suṇātu me, bhante, saṅgho. (Kd 1 — Vin i 95)

    su·**ṇā**·tu **me** **bhan**·**te** **saṅ**·**gho**

    *suṇātu* let it listen (3 sg. imper.) · *me* to me (dat./gen. sg.) · *bhante* venerable sirs (voc.) · *saṅgho* the Sangha (m. nom. sg.)

    "Let the Sangha listen to me, venerable sirs."

    You met this line in the first set, where the question was about its weights. Here it is a sandhi question. There are three word boundaries in it and nothing has happened at any of them. Name what each boundary consists of (what ends the word before, what begins the word after), and then say, for each, what would have had to be different for a rule to fire. Item 10 asks for the name of this situation; here you are being asked to recognise it before you have the name.

2. Sabbapāpassa akaraṇaṁ, kusalassa upasampadā; (Dhp 183 — Dhp p. 27)

    **sab**·ba·**pā**·**pas**·sa a·ka·ra·**ṇaṁ**, ku·sa·**las**·sa u·pa·**sam**·pa·**dā**

    *sabbapāpassa* of all evil (n. gen. sg.) · *akaraṇaṁ* the not-doing (n. nom. sg.) · *kusalassa* of the wholesome (n. gen. sg.) · *upasampadā* the taking up (f. nom. sg.)

    "Not doing any evil; taking up what is wholesome."

    Say it aloud. Twice in these two lines a vowel meets a vowel across a word boundary, and twice nothing happens. Find both. Then do the arithmetic the review is built on: for each of the two, count the *mattā* of the two syllables as they stand, and count what they would have come to had the two vowels contracted in the ordinary way. Say what the difference is, in syllables and in *mattā*, and say which of the two spellings a reciter would find easier to keep in time.

3. …yena brahmāyu brāhmaṇo tenupasaṅkami; upasaṅkamitvā brahmāyuṁ brāhmaṇaṁ abhivādetvā ekamantaṁ nisīdi. (MN 91 — M ii 135)

    *yena* by which (m. inst. sg.) · *brahmāyu* Brahmāyu · *brāhmaṇo* the brahmin (m. nom. sg.) · *tenupasaṅkami* he approached by that way · *upasaṅkamitvā* having approached · *brahmāyuṁ* Brahmāyu (acc.) · *brāhmaṇaṁ* the brahmin (acc.) · *abhivādetvā* having bowed · *ekamantaṁ* to one side · *nisīdi* he sat down

    "…he went to the brahmin Brahmāyu; and having approached, he bowed to the brahmin Brahmāyu and sat down to one side."

    Say it aloud twice; you will meet this formula hundreds of times. The same verb stands twice in it, once inside a junction and once free. Find both, take the first apart, and then say what it is about the position of the second that meant no junction could form there. Then answer the harder half: *ekamantaṁ* is also a junction, and unlike the first it has been frozen into a word the dictionaries list. Say what makes the difference between a junction a reader is expected to undo and one a reader is expected to look up.

### Vacasā paricitā — sounds and script

Recite until the line is fluent, then rebuild it with the Pāli covered. Both of these are formulas, and so they are the ones to have by heart.

4. Itipi so bhagavā arahaṁ sammāsambuddho vijjācaraṇasampanno sugato lokavidū anuttaro purisadammasārathi satthā devamanussānaṁ buddho bhagavā. (AN 6.10 — A iii 285)

    "Such is the Blessed One: perfected, fully awakened, accomplished in knowledge and conduct, holy, knower of the world, supreme guide for those fit to be trained, teacher of gods and humans, awakened, blessed."

    Recite it, then write it out from memory and check it letter by letter. Then the review's question: thirteen word boundaries, and at two of them a vowel meets a vowel. Find the two. Say what has happened at each, and then say what has happened at the other eleven. The answer is one word, the same for all eleven. If you have just learnt the vowel-junction rules you will expect this formula to be full of them, and it is one of the least contracted stretches of Pāli there is.

5. Pātimokkhanti ādimetaṁ mukhametaṁ pamukhametaṁ kusalānaṁ dhammānaṁ. (Kd 2 — Vin i 103)

    *pātimokkhanti* the Pātimokkha, so called · *ādimetaṁ* this is the beginning · *mukhametaṁ* this is the face · *pamukhametaṁ* this is the fore · *kusalānaṁ dhammānaṁ* of wholesome states (m. gen. pl.)

    "'Pātimokkha': this is the beginning, this is the face, this is the fore of wholesome states."

    Rebuild it from the gloss alone, aloud, junctions and all. You were asked in the sandhi set which of these junctions behaves differently from the others. Here the question is about weight. Take *ādimetaṁ* and *mukhametaṁ*: write out the two words each was made from, divide both the pieces and the joined form into syllables, and say what the junction has done to the weight of the syllable at the join. Then say whether the same thing happens in all four, and what that suggests about why a reciter feels a junction before a reader sees one.

### Manasānupekkhitā — sounds and script

**Recall.** Answer from memory, in a phrase. If a question needs the book open, that is the answer to it.

6. State the two-*mattā* ceiling in one sentence, and then say what a long vowel does when a cluster of consonants arrives after it.
7. Vowel length and syllable weight are two different measurements. Give a single syllable that has a short vowel and is nonetheless heavy, and say what makes it so.
8. Name the two accounts of what the niggahīta sounded like, and then name the one fact about it that neither account disputes.
9. Why are there four kinds of junction between two Pāli words, and no more? Derive the number; do not list them.
10. The tradition has a name for the case where two sounds meet and nothing happens. Give the name, and say why a chapter of sandhi rules makes a reader worse at recognising it.
11. *E* and *o* are long by nature. Give the condition under which they shorten, and name the one class of following cluster before which the grammars disagree about whether they do.
12. A consonant appears between two vowels at a junction. Name the two quite different things it may be, and say what test, if any, tells them apart.
13. How many letters are in the Pāli alphabet? Give every defensible answer and say what each one is counting.

Say what the form is, and what decides it. Each of these belongs to both chapters at once. Give the analysis, and name what in the form itself decides it; that is the point of the group.

14. *natthi*
15. *ekamantaṁ*
16. *svākkhāto*
17. *diyaḍḍha*
18. *yaṁnūna*
19. *upekkhā*

### Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — sounds and script

**Read the line.** Give a word-by-word gloss and an idiomatic rendering; divide every syllable, mark the heavy ones, and account for every junction, including any place where two sounds meet and nothing has happened.

20. Namo tassa bhagavato arahato sammāsambuddhassāti. (MN 91 — M ii 140)
21. Manopubbaṅgamā dhammā, manoseṭṭhā manomayā; (Dhp 1 — Dhp p. 1)
22. Esevanto dukkhassā. (SN 12.51 — S ii 84)

**Put it back into Pāli.**

23. The quotative is the junction this book will ask you to undo more often than any other, so make five of them instead. Here are five pairs, every one of which stands joined somewhere in the canon:

    *bhikkhu* + *iti* · *bhikkhako* + *iti* · *nivāsessāmi* + *iti* · *pātimokkhaṁ* + *iti* · *pana* + *iti*

    Write the joined form of each. Then divide the last two syllables of each joined form and mark their weights, and say what the junction has done in every case. Three of the five lengthen a vowel; one has nothing to lengthen; the fifth has no vowel there at all and changes a nasal instead. Sort them, and say what decided each.

    Then the reason this matters more than any other junction: say what happens when a reader meets one of these joined forms and looks it up in a dictionary, and what they must do first.

### Coming back — sounds and script

The set is not finished on the day it is worked. Re-attempt these, from cold, without reading the answers first.

**Day 3.** Items 1 to 5 aloud. All five are lines you will meet again (three of them are formulas recited daily somewhere in the Theravāda world), and the reason to come back to them first is that the junctions in them are the ones you will be undoing for the rest of the book.

**Day 10.** Items 14 to 19. Six forms, each of which needs a fact from both chapters at once, and the second visit is where you find out whether you were reciting the analysis or making it.

**Day 30.** Items 20 to 23. The three reading lines and the production item together are the whole of Part I in about forty words of Pāli: if the junctions come apart cleanly, the weights fall out right, and you can build the five quotatives, not merely recognise them, then Part I has done its work and the noun chapters can start.

## Review: The Noun Phrase

A review is not a recap. The exercises after each chapter test that chapter, and they are the right instrument for that. This tests what only becomes visible once several chapters are in place: where they overlap, where a form belongs to both, and where the habit one chapter builds is the habit the next one breaks. Nothing here can be answered from a single chapter.

It covers only chapters that have been verified in full. It now covers the whole of Part II: **Nouns**, **Gender and Adjectives**, **Pronouns** and **Numerals**. It was first written when only the last two had been through the verification passes, and it has been extended, not rewritten, as the first two caught up, as the format was designed to allow.

The four stages run as they do in the chapter sets, with the review's own constraint on the first two: every line in stages 1 and 2 has already been worked in an earlier set, and is here to be asked a question that set could not ask.

### Sotānugata — the noun phrase

Nothing is withheld. Each line is given with its gloss and its rendering. Say it aloud, then answer.

1. Dīghā jāgarato ratti, dīghaṁ santassa yojanaṁ; Dīgho bālānaṁ saṁsāro, saddhammaṁ avijānataṁ. (Dhp 60 — Dhp p. 9)

    *dīghā* long (f. nom. sg.) · *jāgarato* for one lying awake (m. gen. sg., pres. part.) · *ratti* the night (f. nom. sg.) · *dīghaṁ* long (n. nom. sg.) · *santassa* for the weary (m. gen. sg., p.p.) · *yojanaṁ* a league (n. nom. sg.) · *dīgho* long (m. nom. sg.) · *bālānaṁ* for fools (m. gen. pl.) · *saṁsāro* wandering on (m. nom. sg.) · *saddhammaṁ* the true teaching (m. acc. sg.) · *avijānataṁ* of those not understanding (m. gen. pl., pres. part.)

    "Long is the night for one lying awake; long is the league for the weary; long is the wandering on for fools who do not understand the true teaching."

    You met this verse in the adjectives set, where the question was how one stem takes three genders. Here the question is what it takes them from. Name the three nouns that hand *dīgha* its three endings, give the stem class of each, and say for each whether the adjective's ending is the same ending the noun is carrying or merely the same case. One of the three is not a match, and the item turns on it.

2. Ayaṁ te patto, ayaṁ saṅghāṭi, ayaṁ uttarāsaṅgo, ayaṁ antaravāsako. (Kd 1 — Vin i 94)

    *ayaṁ* this (nom. sg.) · *te* your (2nd gen./dat. sg.) · *patto* bowl (m. nom. sg.) · *saṅghāṭi* outer robe (f. nom. sg.) · *uttarāsaṅgo* upper robe (m. nom. sg.) · *antaravāsako* inner robe (m. nom. sg.)

    "This is your bowl, this your outer robe, this your upper robe, this your inner robe."

    A newly ordained monk being handed his requisites. The pronouns set asked which noun is the odd one out. Here the question is what the nouns show and what they do not. Take the four nouns one at a time and say whether the ending alone fixes the gender; then say the same of *ayaṁ*, four times over. To learn the gender of *saṅghāṭi* you have two words in front of you, and neither will say.

    Then the general point, which is the review's: name the only place in this sentence where gender is visible at all, and say what a reader must do when it is visible nowhere.

3. Tasmiṁ sahassadhā loke sahassaṁ candānaṁ sahassaṁ sūriyānaṁ … cattāri mahāsamuddasahassāni … sahassaṁ brahmalokānaṁ. (AN 10.29 — A v 59)

    *tasmiṁ* in that (m. loc. sg.) · *sahassadhā* thousandfold · *loke* world (m. loc. sg.) · *sahassaṁ* a thousand (n. nom. sg.) · *candānaṁ* of moons (m. gen. pl.) · *sūriyānaṁ* of suns (m. gen. pl.) · *cattāri* four (n. nom. pl.) · *mahāsamuddasahassāni* thousands of great oceans (n. nom. pl.) · *brahmalokānaṁ* of realms of divinity (m. gen. pl.)

    "In that thousandfold world there are a thousand moons, a thousand suns … four thousand great oceans … a thousand realms of divinity."

    The numerals set asked you to find where the construction changes. Here the question is about one word wearing two hats. *Sahassa* stands four times in this line and is not the same kind of thing each time. Say what it is in *sahassaṁ candānaṁ*, what it is in *mahāsamuddasahassāni*, and what *cattāri* is agreeing with in the second (it is not the oceans). Then say what the two constructions together show about how Pāli builds a number larger than eighteen, and why the noun being counted keeps ending up in the genitive.

### Vacasā paricitā — the noun phrase

Recite each until it is fluent, then cover the Pāli and rebuild it from the gloss alone, aloud.

4. Tvaṁ, samma medakathālike, mamaṁ rakkha, ahaṁ taṁ rakkhissāmi. (SN 47.19 — S v 168)

    *tvaṁ* you (2nd nom. sg.) · *samma* dear · *medakathālike* Medakathālikā (voc.) · *mamaṁ* me (1st acc. sg.) · *rakkha* protect! · *ahaṁ* I (1st nom. sg.) · *taṁ* you (2nd acc. sg.) · *rakkhissāmi* I shall protect

    "You protect me, dear Medakathālikā, and I shall protect you."

    An acrobat speaking to his assistant on the pole. Rebuild it from the gloss, aloud, until the four pronouns come in the right order without thinking.

    Then the review question, which is item 6's rule turned on a real sentence. Both verbs already carry their person, so *tvaṁ* and *ahaṁ* add nothing you did not already know from *rakkha* and *rakkhissāmi*. Say why they are there. Then take them out, say the sentence again without them, and say what has been lost: the answer is a single word, and the sentence is a chiasmus built to make it audible.

5. Abhivādanasīlissa, niccaṁ vuḍḍhāpacāyino; Cattāro dhammā vaḍḍhanti, āyu vaṇṇo sukhaṁ balaṁ. (Dhp 109 — Dhp p. 16)

    *abhivādanasīlissa* for one whose habit is to bow (m. gen. sg.) · *niccaṁ* constantly · *vuḍḍhāpacāyino* honouring elders (m. gen. sg.) · *cattāro* four (m. nom. pl.) · *dhammā* things (m. nom. pl.) · *vaḍḍhanti* increase · *āyu* long life · *vaṇṇo* beauty · *sukhaṁ* happiness · *balaṁ* strength

    "For one in the habit of bowing, always honouring the elders, four things increase: long life, beauty, happiness and strength."

    Rebuild it from the gloss, then say the four things without looking. The numeral agrees with *dhammā* and the four things it counts are not all of *dhammā*'s gender. Take the four in turn, give the gender of each, and say what the numeral would have had to be had it agreed with the list instead of with the word that heads it. Then say what a reader should do, in general, when the members of a list disagree in gender with the word that names them.

    One of the four is not an *a*-stem at all, and the class it belongs to is the subject of the recall group's last question. Find it, and say what the two classes have in common.

### Manasānupekkhitā — the noun phrase

**Recall.** Answer from memory, in a phrase. If a question needs the book open, that is the answer to it.

6. A Pāli verb already carries its person. What follows for pronoun subjects, and what does it predict about where you will actually meet *ahaṁ* and *tvaṁ*?
7. Numerals from one to eighteen behave one way and those from nineteen upwards another. Name the difference in one sentence, and say what changes at nineteen, not at twenty.
8. Which numeral declines on the pronoun pattern instead of as an ordinary adjective, and which one form of it goes further and follows the *ima-* type?
9. Name the four ways a numeral can be joined to what it counts. Which of the four is a reader most likely to assume is the only one?
10. One word in the language still inflects as a dual. Name it, say what its *-o* is not, and say why 'two' has no proper paradigm of its own.
11. Masculine and neuter nouns are declined identically except in three cases. Name them, and say what follows when you meet an oblique ending and want to know the gender.
12. A noun in *-a* and an adjective in *-a* form their feminines with different suffixes. Give both, and say why the difference is a grammatical fact, not a phonetic one.
13. Some grammars call the *-as* stems neuter and others call them masculine. Say what is actually happening to those words, and name the one sense-base in the standard list whose adjective gives the game away.

Say what the form is, and what decides it. Each of these is either ambiguous or belongs to more than one chapter at once. Give the analysis, and (the point of the group) name what in a sentence would settle it.

14. *ekissā*
15. *tissannaṁ*
16. *paṭhamaṁ*
17. *dvīhi*
18. *tāya*
19. *manasā*

### Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — the noun phrase

**Read the sentence.** Give a word-by-word gloss and an idiomatic rendering, and account for every pronoun, numeral, noun and adjective in it. Each of these needs at least two chapters.

20. Ime kho, bhikkhave, tayo niruttipathā adhivacanapathā paññattipathā asaṅkiṇṇā … (SN 22.62 — S iii 72)
21. Yā kho, bhikkhave, imesaṁ tiṇṇaṁ dhammānaṁ saṅgati sannipāto samavāyo, ayaṁ vuccati cakkhusamphasso. (SN 35.93 — S iv 68)
22. Mano tesaṁ dhammānaṁ paṭhamaṁ uppajjati, anvadeva akusalā dhammā. (AN 1.56 — A i 11)
23. Tīṇimāni, bhikkhave, titthāyatanāni yāni paṇḍitehi samanuyuñjiyamānāni … parampi gantvā akiriyāya saṇṭhahanti. (AN 3.61 — A i 173)
24. Sabbapāpassa akaraṇaṁ, kusalassa upasampadā; Sacittapariyodapanaṁ, etaṁ buddhāna sāsanaṁ. (Dhp 183 — Dhp p. 27)

**Put it back into Pāli.**

25. Three English phrases, every one of which stands somewhere above with a citation beside it. Write the Pāli for each **from memory**, then find the item it came from and check yourself letter by letter.

    "of the Buddhas" · "of these three things" · "a thousand of moons"

    Then, for each, name the chapter or chapters whose rules fixed the endings you wrote. The three do not draw on the same chapters, and one of them draws on three at once.

    Finally, the one that is not quite what it should be. One of the three is short of a niggahīta, and the reason has nothing to do with the noun phrase. Say what the prose form would be, and say what you would have to know to be sure the shortened form is not an error.

### Coming back — the noun phrase

The set is not finished on the day it is worked. Re-attempt these, from cold, without reading the answers first.

**Day 3.** Items 1 to 5 aloud. Five lines, and between them the whole of Part II: three genders on one adjective, one pronoun over four nouns, a numeral that counts by taking a genitive, four pronouns in a chiasmus and a numeral agreeing with the head of a list, not its members. If these five come back, the recall group will mostly answer itself.

**Day 10.** Items 14 to 19. Six forms, each ambiguous or belonging to two chapters at once, and in this group a second attempt shows what a first cannot: the first time you are working out what a form *could* be, and the second you find out whether you remember what settles it.

**Day 30.** Items 20 to 25. The five reading sentences and the production item. If *paṭhamaṁ* at item 22 still refuses to resolve and you can say why it refuses instead of reaching for one reading, the review has done its work: some questions close and some do not, and knowing which is which is most of the skill.

## Review: The Verb

A review is more than a recap. The exercises after each chapter test that chapter, and they are the right instrument for that.
