This set tests what only becomes visible once several chapters are in place: where they overlap, where a form belongs to both, and where the habit one chapter builds is the habit the next one breaks. Nothing here can be answered from a single chapter.

It covers the whole of Part III: **The Present System**, The Past and the Future, and **Passive, Causative and Denominative**. All three have been through the verification passes, which is the condition for a chapter appearing in a review at all.

Much of what follows is drawn from one sutta, the *Brahmāyācana* at SN 6.1, because it is the natural text for this part of the book. The whole discourse turns on mood, not tense: what the Buddha *would* teach and what others *might* fail to understand, Brahmā's *let him teach*, the flat future *there will be those who understand*. If you can follow the moods you can follow the argument; if you cannot, you will get the events right and the sense wrong.

The four stages run as they do in the chapter sets, with the review's own constraint on the first two: every line in stages 1 and 2 has already been worked in an earlier set, and is here to be asked a question that set could not ask.

### Sotānugata — the verb

Nothing is withheld. Each line is given with its gloss. Say it aloud, then answer.

1. Sabbe tasanti daṇḍassa, sabbe bhāyanti maccuno; Attānaṁ upamaṁ katvā, na haneyya na ghātaye. (Dhp 129 — Dhp p. 19)

    *sabbe* all (m. nom. pl.) · *tasanti* tremble (3 pl. pres.) · *daṇḍassa* at the rod (m. gen. sg.) · *bhāyanti* fear (3 pl. pres.) · *maccuno* death (m. gen. sg.) · *attānaṁ* oneself (m. acc. sg.) · *upamaṁ* as the comparison (f. acc. sg.) · *katvā* having made (absol.) · *na* not · *haneyya* should kill (3 sg. opt.) · *ghātaye* should have [another] kill (3 sg. opt.)

    "All tremble at the rod, all fear death; making oneself the comparison, one should not kill nor have another kill."

    Say it aloud. Four finite verbs, and between them the whole of this part. Name the chapter each belongs to. Then the item: *haneyya* and *ghātaye* are the same mood, the same person and the same number, and they are not the same verb. Say what separates them, in one morpheme, and say what that morpheme does to what is being forbidden.

    Then say why taking the two as elegant variation misreads a precept and not merely a line.

2. Gahakāraka diṭṭhosi, puna gehaṁ na kāhasi; Sabbā te phāsukā bhaggā, gahakūṭaṁ visaṅkhataṁ; Visaṅkhāragataṁ cittaṁ, taṇhānaṁ khayamajjhagā. (Dhp 154 — Dhp p. 23)

    *gahakāraka* house-builder (m. voc. sg.) · *diṭṭho·si* < *diṭṭho* seen (m. nom. sg., p.p.) + *asi* you are (2 sg. pres.) · *puna* again · *gehaṁ* house (n. acc. sg.) · *na* not · *kāhasi* you will build (2 sg. fut.) · *sabbā* all (f. nom. pl.) · *te* your (2 sg. gen.) · *phāsukā* rafters (f. nom. pl.) · *bhaggā* broken (f. nom. pl., p.p.) · *gahakūṭaṁ* roof-peak (n. nom. sg.) · *visaṅkhataṁ* demolished (n. nom. sg.) · *visaṅkhāragataṁ* gone to the unconditioned (n. nom. sg.) · *cittaṁ* mind (n. nom. sg.) · *taṇhānaṁ* of cravings (f. gen. pl.) · *khayam·ajjhagā* < *khayaṁ* the ending (m. acc. sg.) + *ajjhagā* has reached (3 sg.)

    "House-builder, you are seen! You will not build a house again. All your rafters are broken, the roof-peak demolished. The mind has gone to the unconditioned, it has reached the ending of cravings."

    Say it aloud. Three of the four verbal ideas here are not carried by a finite verb at all, and the past-and-future set asked you only about the two that are. Find the three, say what is carrying each, and say what supplies the tense in each case. Something does, and it is not an ending.

    Then *kāhasi*, which is the trap. It is a future, and it is not built the way the chapter's rule builds one. Say what is missing from it, and say what a reader should do on meeting a future they cannot cut into a stem and an affix.

3. Idhānanda, bhikkhu sabbasaṅkhāresu aṭṭīyati harāyati jigucchati. (AN 10.60 — A v 111)

    *idha* here · *ānanda* Ānanda (m. voc. sg.) · *bhikkhu* a monk (m. nom. sg.) · *sabbasaṅkhāresu* in all conditioned things (m. loc. pl.) · *aṭṭīyati* is troubled (3 sg. pres.) · *harāyati* is ashamed (3 sg. pres. denom.) · *jigucchati* is disgusted (3 sg. pres. desid.)

    "Here, Ānanda, a monk is troubled, ashamed and disgusted by all conditioned things."

    Say it aloud. Three verbs in a row, three different formations, and one ending. Say which formation each is. Then the question this review exists for, and the one to carry out of this part: say which chapter supplied the ending and which supplied the stem, and state in a sentence what a reader can read off a Pāli verb's ending and what they can never read off it.

### Vacasā paricitā — the verb

Recite each until it is fluent, then rebuild it with the Pāli covered.

4. Pare ca na vijānanti, mayamettha yamāmase; Ye ca tattha vijānanti, tato sammanti medhagā. (Dhp 6 — Dhp p. 2)

    *pare* others (m. nom. pl.) · *ca* and · *na* not · *vijānanti* understand (3 pl. pres.) · *mayaṁ* we (nom. pl.) · *ettha* here · *yamāmase* let us restrain ourselves (1 pl. imper. middle) · *ye* those who (m. nom. pl.) · *tattha* there · *tato* thereupon · *sammanti* are appeased (3 pl. pres.) · *medhagā* quarrels (m. nom. pl.)

    "Others do not understand that here we must restrain ourselves; but those who do understand — thereupon their quarrels are appeased."

    Rebuild it from the gloss, aloud. Three of the four finite verbs take one set of endings and the fourth takes another. Name both sets, say which verb is the odd one, and say what the ending does there that the ordinary set could not. This depends on the distinction of item 8, which costs sentences when it is run together with the other thing "voice" names.

5. Apārutā tesaṁ amatassa dvārā, / ye sotavanto pamuñcantu saddhaṁ; / vihiṁsasaññī paguṇaṁ na bhāsiṁ, / dhammaṁ paṇītaṁ manujesu brahme. (SN 6.1 — S i 138) — Read it aloud five times, slowly, holding the long vowels for two beats and sounding both halves of every doubled consonant. Then answer: *pamuñcantu* and *na bhāsiṁ* stand in consecutive lines and belong to different chapters of this part. Parse both. One of them is missing something that Chapter 8 says its type "generally" has — say what, and say what decides it here.

### Manasānupekkhitā — the verb

**Recall.** Answer from memory, in a phrase. If a question needs the book open, that is the answer to it.

6. What is the present stem, how do you get it from a dictionary, and which three forms of the verb are built on it? What are the rest built on, and why does that one division decide which chapter a form is treated in?

7. Pāli has a set of primary personal endings and a set of secondary ones. Say which tense or mood takes which, across the whole of Part III. One of them takes both: name it.

8. The word "voice" names two different distinctions in this part, and running them together will cost you sentences. Name both, say what carries each, and say which of the two the personal endings can never tell you.

9. *Mā* and *na* both come out as "don't" in English. Name the construction each of them takes, and say what the difference is in what they forbid.

10. There are four aorists and one of them is still alive. Which, what makes that fact useful and not merely historical, and what does it turn the other three into?

11. Of the five derived stems, three are productive and two are not. Name them either way, and say what productivity licenses you to do with a verb no grammar lists, and what it forbids you to do.

Say what the form is, and what would settle it. Each of these is either ambiguous or belongs to more than one chapter at once. Give the analysis, and name what in a sentence would decide it; that is the point of the group.

12. *desesi*

13. *assa*

14. *khomhi*

15. *avoca*

16. *dissati*

17. *sādiyati*

18. *labbhetha*

**Build the form.** Give the form, and show the steps that produce it.

19. The third person singular conditional of *bhavati*, and then the third person plural. Name the three separate things that go into the form before the ending does.

20. The third person singular optative of *atthi*: both of the forms the canon actually uses, and the two different endings they come from.

21. The second person singular imperative of *gacchati*, in both of its shapes. Then say which stems can take the shorter shape and why the others cannot.

22. The third person singular present passive of *vadati* 'says'. Then say why the aorist of this verb gives you no help at all in getting there.

23. The causative of *bhavati*, third person plural present; then the future of that same causative, third person singular. The second one is where the trap is, so show the stem you are attaching *-ssa-* to.

24. "Monks, do not fear": second person plural, in the construction Chapter 8 requires, not the one Chapter 7 would offer.

### Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — the verb

**Read the sentence.** Give a word-by-word gloss and an idiomatic rendering, and account for every finite verb in it. Each of these needs at least two of the three chapters.

25. Rūpañca hidaṁ, bhikkhave, attā abhavissa, nayidaṁ rūpaṁ ābādhāya saṁvatteyya, labbhetha ca rūpe … (SN 22.59 — S iii 66)

    - Three finite verbs, three different things going on. Take them in order.
    - Which clause is the protasis and which the apodosis, and does this sentence follow the normal division of labour between the two moods?

26. No cetaṁ padaheyya, nayidaṁ saccamanupāpuṇeyya. (MN 95 — M ii 174)

    - This is a conditional sentence with no conditional verb in it. What is marking the condition, what mood are both verbs in, and what does the pair 25–26 together tell you about how often you will meet the form you built in item 19?

27. Atha kho bhagavā bhikkhū āmantesi: "handa dāni, bhikkhave, āmantayāmi vo, vayadhammā saṅkhārā, appamādena sampādetha." (DN 16 — D ii 120)

    - *Āmantesi* and *āmantayāmi* are two lines apart and are one stem. What is the relation between the two shapes, and which chapter's table has both of them in a single row?
    - *Sampādetha* closes the Buddha's last words. What formation is it, what is it built on, and what is the mood?

28. Evameva kho, bhikkhave, bhavissanti bhikkhū anāgatamaddhānaṁ, ye te suttantā tathāgatabhāsitā gambhīrā gambhīratthā lokuttarā suññatappaṭisaṁyuttā, tesu bhaññamānesu na sussūsissanti na sotaṁ odahissanti na aññā cittaṁ upaṭṭhāpessanti na ca te dhamme uggahetabbaṁ pariyāpuṇitabbaṁ maññissanti. (SN 20.7 — S ii 267)

    - Five of the verbs here are in the same tense. What is it, and how is it built?
    - Now look at what it is built *on*. Four different kinds of stem are carrying that one tense: name the kind in each case. This sentence is Chapter 9's whole argument demonstrated in Chapter 8's tense.
    - *Maññissanti* has the doubled consonant Chapter 9 warns about. Is it a passive?

29. Uṭṭhehi vīra vijitasaṅgāma, satthavāha anaṇa vicara loke; desassu bhagavā dhammaṁ, aññātāro bhavissantī"ti. (SN 6.1 — S i 137)

    - *Uṭṭhehi* and *vicara* are the same mood in the same person and number. Why do they not look alike?
    - *Desassu* is the form to stop on. Parse it fully: formation of the stem, mood, person, number and voice.
    - The Syāmese, Cambodian and PTS editions print *desetu* here where the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana prints *desassu*. Parse that one too, and say what changes about who is being addressed.

30. ekaṁ samayaṁ bhagavā uruvelāyaṁ viharati najjā nerañjarāya tīre ajapālanigrodhamūle paṭhamābhisambuddho. Atha kho bhagavato rahogatassa paṭisallīnassa evaṁ cetaso parivitakko udapādi … (SN 6.1 — S i 136)

    - Two consecutive sentences about the same afternoon, and the verbs are in two different tenses. Name them, and say why this is not an inconsistency.

**Put it back into Pāli.** Each of these renders a real sentence. Reconstruct the Pāli. The words you need are given; what you have to supply is the stem and the ending. Do not settle for a sentence that merely works; the answer is a particular line of canon, and getting close to it is the exercise.

31. "And if I were to teach this Dhamma, others would not understand me.": *ahaṁ*, *ca*, *eva*, *kho pana*, *dhamma*, *para*, *na*, the enclitic for 'me', and the verbs 'teach' and 'understand' (*ā-jān-*), both in the optative.

32. "Monks, do not fear merit.": *bhikkhave*, *puñña* in the genitive plural, and the verb 'fear' in the construction of item 24.

33. "Monks, I will teach you the burden and the bearer of the burden and the taking up of the burden and the laying down of the burden.": *bhāra*, *bhārahāra*, *bhārādāna*, *bhāranikkhepana*, *ca*, *bhikkhave*, the enclitic for 'to you' (plural), and the verb in the future.

**Read at length.** This is not a drill. Read the following passage through, twice, without stopping to parse: the first time for the build of the sentences, the second for the sense. Then go back and answer the questions under it.

34. Atha kho brahmuno sahampatissa bhagavato cetasā cetoparivitakkamaññāya etadahosi: "nassati vata bho loko, vinassati vata bho loko, yatra hi nāma tathāgatassa arahato sammāsambuddhassa appossukkatāya cittaṁ namati, no dhammadesanāyā"ti. Atha kho brahmā sahampati—seyyathāpi nāma balavā puriso samiñjitaṁ vā bāhaṁ pasāreyya, pasāritaṁ vā bāhaṁ samiñjeyya; evameva—brahmaloke antarahito bhagavato purato pāturahosi. Atha kho brahmā sahampati ekaṁsaṁ uttarāsaṅgaṁ karitvā dakkhiṇajāṇumaṇḍalaṁ pathaviyaṁ nihantvā yena bhagavā tenañjaliṁ paṇāmetvā bhagavantaṁ etadavoca: "desetu, bhante, bhagavā dhammaṁ, desetu sugato dhammaṁ. Santi sattā apparajakkhajātikā, assavanatā dhammassa parihāyanti. Bhavissanti dhammassa aññātāro"ti. Idamavoca brahmā sahampati, idaṁ vatvā athāparaṁ etadavoca: (SN 6.1 — S i 137)

    - Brahmā's speech runs present, imperative, future in three consecutive sentences: *parihāyanti*, *desetu*, *bhavissanti*. Set out what each tense or mood contributes to his argument. He is trying to change someone's mind, and the sequence is how he does it.
    - The simile of the outstretched arm has two optatives in it, *pasāreyya* and *samiñjeyya*. Nobody is wishing for anything and no condition is being stated. What is the optative doing here?
    - *Nassati* and *vinassati* stand together, and Bodhi renders them "the world is lost!" and "the world is to perish", neither of them a plain present. Which use of the present tense is that, and which chapter treats it?
    - Five of the finite verbs in this passage are aorists, and there are only two verbs between them. Find them, and say why a narrative in Pāli should be so heavily built out of two.

**Reaching back.** Four items reaching into Parts I and II. Each is a place where what you learnt there and what you learnt here have to be held together.

35. Chapter 2. Resolve the junctions in *etadavoca*, *idamavoca*, *tenañjaliṁ* and *khomhi*. One of the four is the reason a perfectly ordinary verb form cannot be found as a word at all. Say which, and say why looking for it inside a longer word does not rescue it either.

36. Chapter 5. In item 27 the Buddha says *āmantayāmi vo*, and in item 31 he says *pare ca me na ājāneyyuṁ*. Neither *vo* nor *me* is the form a table would give you first. Say what they are, why they are preferred here, and what one rule of position they both obey.

37. Chapter 6. At DN 16 — D ii 102 the Buddha says: Yassa kassaci, ānanda, cattāro iddhipādā bhāvitā bahulīkatā … so ākaṅkhamāno kappaṁ vā tiṭṭheyya kappāvasesaṁ vā. Which of the four constructions of Chapter 6 joins *cattāro* to what it counts? What mood is *tiṭṭheyya*, and what does that mood contribute to a claim about the Buddha's lifespan that an indicative would not?

38. Chapters 4 and 9. At DN 16 — D ii 120 the Buddha says: Ime kho te, bhikkhave, dhammā mayā abhiññā desitā. There is no finite verb in it. Say how the sentence works without one, what *mayā* contributes and why it is in the case it is in, and what *desitā* has to agree with.

### Coming back — the verb

The set is not finished on the day it is worked. Re-attempt these, from cold, without reading the answers first.

**Day 3.** Items 1 to 5 aloud. Between them they hold the whole of the part: three chapters in one verse of Dhammapada, three participles doing a finite verb's work, three formations under one ending, a middle where you expect an active, and the verse the Brahmāyācana was built towards. If these five come back, most of the recall group will answer itself.

**Day 10.** Items 12 to 24. The ambiguous forms and the built ones together, and the reason to take them as one block is that they are the same skill in the two directions: item 13 asks what *assa* could be and item 20 asks you to make it. A form you can build and cannot recognise is a form you have not learnt, and so is the reverse.

**Day 30.** Items 31 to 33, then 34, then 38. The three production items first, because they are the only place in the set where a wrong answer is unmistakable; then the long passage, read straight through; and then the last of the reaching-back items, which is a sentence with no finite verb in it at all. It is the right thing to close on, because by then you have spent a month on finite verbs and the canon keeps writing sentences without them.

## Review: The Sentence and the Verse

A review is more than a recap. The exercises after each chapter test that chapter, and they are the right instrument for that. This set tests what only becomes visible once several chapters are in place: where they overlap, where a form belongs to both, and where the habit one chapter builds is the habit the next one breaks. Nothing here can be answered from a single chapter.

It covers the whole of Parts IV, V, VI and VII: **Participles**, **Absolutives and Infinitives**, **Indeclinables and Particles**, **Compounds**, **Word Formation**, **Case Syntax**, **Clause and Sentence Syntax**, **Relative Clauses**, and **Metre**. That is nine chapters, all of them now through the verification passes, and the largest stretch any review in this book has had to cover. R1–R3 each answered to one Part; this one answers to four, because Part VII is a single chapter and does not support a review of its own, and because the nine chapters share one idea worth holding together, not four smaller ones held apart.

The idea is this: each of these chapters is a way of building something larger than a word without adding many words to do it. A participle stands in for a clause. A compound stands in for a phrase, sometimes a whole sentence's worth of one. A case ending stands in for what a preposition would carry in English. A pair of pronouns, one *y-* and one *t-*, stands in for a conjunction English cannot do without. A count of syllables stands in for the punctuation a manuscript never had. Nine chapters, one method: say less, on the understanding that the reader will do the arithmetic.

The four stages run as they do in the chapter sets, with one constraint on the first two that belongs to the review: every line in stages 1 and 2 has already been worked in an earlier set, and is here to be asked a question that set could not ask. Two of them are halves of passages the workbook has so far only ever printed in halves.

### Sotānugata — the sentence and the verse

Nothing is withheld. Each line is given with its gloss. Say it aloud, then answer.

1. Tiṭṭhaṁ caraṁ nisinno va, Sayāno yāvatāssa vitamiddho; (Snp 1.8 — Sn p. 26)

    *tiṭṭhaṁ* standing (m. nom. sg., pres. part.) · *caraṁ* walking (m. nom. sg., pres. part.) · *nisinno* seated (m. nom. sg., p.p.) · *va* or · *sayāno* lying down (m. nom. sg., pres. part.) · *yāvatā·assa* < *yāvatā* as long as + *assa* he is · *vitamiddho* free of drowsiness (m. nom. sg.)

    "Standing, walking, seated or lying down, for as long as he is free of drowsiness —"

    Say it aloud. You met this half of the verse in the participles set, and the other half in the tenses set: *Etaṁ satiṁ adhiṭṭheyya*, "he should undertake this mindfulness". Put the two halves back together and answer three things.

    Four participles stand here and one of them is not the same kind as the other three. Say which, and say what that difference of kind does to the sense. Then say what all four have in common syntactically: they are not the subject of anything, and there is no verb in this half of the verse at all. Then, with the other half in front of you: say what these four are attached to, and say why a language with participles can put four alternatives into a single clause where English needs a subordinate one.

2. Tirokuṭṭesu tiṭṭhanti, sandhisiṅghāṭakesu ca; Dvārabāhāsu tiṭṭhanti, āgantvāna sakaṁ gharaṁ. (Kp 7)

    *tirokuṭṭesu* < *tiro* beyond + *kuṭṭa* wall · *tiṭṭhanti* they stand · *sandhisiṅghāṭakesu* < *sandhi* junction + *siṅghāṭaka* crossroads · *ca* and · *dvārabāhāsu* < *dvāra* door + *bāhā* post · *āgantvāna* having come · *sakaṁ* their own · *gharaṁ* house

    "They stand beyond the walls, and at the junctions and crossroads; they stand at the doorposts, having come to their own house."

    Say it aloud. Four lines, and three chapters of this stretch are in them. Name the class of each of the three compounds. Then name the case they all stand in and its job here; it is the same job three times, and the plainest one the case has. Then *āgantvāna*, which is an absolutive with a longer ending than the one you were taught first: say what the ordinary form would be, and say why the longer one stands in a line of verse. Two chapters have to be held together to answer that last one.

3. Ayaṁ, deva, pūraṇo kassapo saṅghī ceva gaṇī ca gaṇācariyo ca ñāto yasassī titthakaro sādhusammato bahujanassa rattaññū cirapabbajito addhagato vayoanuppatto. (DN 2 — D i 47)

    *ayaṁ* this (m. nom. sg.) · *deva* your majesty (m. voc. sg.) · *pūraṇo kassapo* Pūraṇa Kassapa (m. nom. sg.) · *saṅghī* having a community (m. nom. sg.) · *ceva … ca* both … and · *gaṇī* having a following · *gaṇācariyo* a teacher of a following · *ñāto* well known · *yasassī* renowned · *titthakaro* a founder of a sect · *sādhusammato* held in high regard · *bahujanassa* by many people (m. gen. sg., agent with a past participle) · *rattaññū* of long standing · *cirapabbajito* long gone forth · *addhagato* advanced in years · *vayoanuppatto* come to the last stage of life

    "Your majesty, this Pūraṇa Kassapa has a community and a following, teaches a following, is well known and renowned, a founder of a sect, held in high regard by many people, of long standing, long gone forth, advanced in years, come to the last stage of life."

    Say it aloud. It is a courtier flattering a king, and it is meant to sound like a list that will not stop. This one sentence needs four chapters of this stretch, and it has no verb in it.

    Say first what is holding it together: every word after *kassapo* is in one case, and you should say which and what the relation is called. Then sort the epithets into their kinds (the ones that are past participles, the ones built with a possessive suffix, and the ones that are compounds), and name the chapter that accounts for each kind. Then *bahujanassa*, which a reader is likely to get wrong: say what case it is, what it is attached to, and why it is not what it looks like.

### Vacasā paricitā — the sentence and the verse

Recite each until it is fluent, then rebuild it with the Pāli covered.

4. jātipaccayā jarāmaraṇaṁ sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsā sambhavanti. (SN 12.1 — S ii 1)

    *jātipaccayā* with birth as condition · *jarāmaraṇaṁ* old age and death · *sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsā* < *soka* sorrow + *parideva* lamentation + *dukkha* pain + *domanassa* dejection + *upāyāsa* distress · *sambhavanti* come to be

    "With birth as condition, old age and death come to be, and sorrow, lamentation, pain, dejection and distress."

    Rebuild it from the gloss, aloud. It is the last link of dependent origination and you will meet it more often than almost any other sentence in the canon, so it is worth having whole.

    Then: four words, and two of them are compounds of different classes. Name both classes and say what each does that a string of separate words would not. Then *jātipaccayā*: say what case it is and what job that case has here, and say what the same two words would look like if the relation were spelt out instead of compounded.

5. Sabbapāpassa akaraṇaṁ, kusalassa upasampadā; Sacittapariyodapanaṁ, etaṁ buddhāna' sāsanaṁ. (Dhp 183 — Dhp p. 27) — Read it aloud, holding every long vowel to its full two beats. Then answer: name the suffix and the negative prefix in *akaraṇaṁ*, the class of the compound and the action-noun suffix on its last member in *sacittapariyodapanaṁ*, and say what the last word's genitive plural would have had to keep, and what it had to lose, for the line to be a line of verse at all.

### Manasānupekkhitā — the sentence and the verse

**Recall.** Answer from memory, in a phrase. If a question needs the book open, that is the answer to it.

6. A transitive verb's past participle and an intransitive verb's point in opposite directions. State the rule, and say what English does with one shape that hides the difference.
7. An absolutive's unstated agent is ordinarily the main verb's subject. Say what has to be true of the main clause before that stops holding, and give the tradition's name for the construction that results.
8. Name the tradition's four jobs for a *nipāta*, and say which one changes how a page is actually read.
9. Give *ekapadatta* and *ekavibhattitā* in a sentence each, and say which of the two catches a beginner out.
10. A *taddhita* suffix strengthens a vowel that is not next to it. Say where, and say why that single fact decides where a word is filed in the dictionary.
11. The tradition counts eight *vibhatti* and six *kāraka*. Name the two endings left over, and say why the tradition puts them outside the system, as opposed to merely finding them hard to classify.
12. State the rule governing the whole absolutive chain in one sentence, and say what has to change about the sentence before the chain stops working.
13. Give the rule that decides every relative pronoun, and say which half of it is agreement and which is function.
14. Give the rule for *e* and *o*, and say what makes them different from every other vowel in the language for this purpose.
15. Participles, absolutives and infinitives are three chapters built from one class of suffix. Name the tradition's word for that class, and say what single job unites all three formations under it.
16. *Bahubbīhi* is not a seventh class of compound. Say what it actually is, and name the suffix that does structurally the same thing (turns a finished compound into an adjective), and say which chapter treats it, which is not the one you would guess.
17. The genitive and the vocative sit outside the *kāraka* system, each for a stated reason. An absolute construction is named after its case for a related reason. Give both.
18. Two light syllables equal one heavy. Name the two devices this equivalence licenses in either direction, and say which is common and which is rare.

**Say what the form is, and what decides it.** Each of these is either ambiguous or belongs to more than one chapter at once. Give the analysis, and name what in the sentence decides it; that is the point of the group.

19. *disvā*, in **Paññāya cassa disvā āsavā parikkhīṇā honti.** (MN 25 — M i 160)
20. *sati*, in **Pādesu sati abhikkamapaṭikkamo paññāyati.** (SN 35.236 — S iv 171)
21. *santaṁ*, in **Santaṁ yeva kho pana paraṁ lokaṁ 'natthi paro loko' ti saṅkappeti.** (MN 60 — M i 402)
22. *taṇhāya*, in **Taṇhāya jāyatī soko, taṇhāya jāyatī bhayaṁ.** (Dhp 216 — Dhp p. 32)
23. *bhuttāvissa*, in **… manuññaṁ bhojanaṁ bhuttāvissa bhattasammado hoti.** (DN 17 — D ii 195)
24. *yena*, twice: **… yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkami.** (SN 1.1 — S i 1) against **… yena mātugāmo … na kammantaṁ payojeti …** (AN 4.80 — A ii 82)
25. *yato* against *yasmā*. This time there is no sentence, only a question about two words the grammars call synonyms and one source does not.
26. *ye te*, in **Yāvakīvañca, ānanda, vajjī ye te vajjīnaṁ vajjimahallakā, te sakkarissanti …** (DN 16 — D ii 74) — two relative clauses, one inside the other. Say what answers each.
27. the two *ti*s in **Ruppatī'ti kho, bhikkhave, tasmā 'rūpa'nti vuccati.** (SN 22.79 — S iii 86)

**Take the compound or the word apart.** Resolve, name the class or the suffix, and say which chapter supplies which layer.

28. **sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsā**, in *Jātipaccayā jarāmaraṇaṁ sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsā sambhavanti.* (SN 12.1 — S ii 1)
29. **pubbenivāsānussatiñāṇāya**, in *… pubbenivāsānussatiñāṇāya cittaṁ abhinīharati abhininnāmeti.* (DN 2 — D i 81)
30. ***vusitavā*** against ***sīlavā*** — same ending, same paradigm, two different formations. Say what in the base decides which is which.
31. ***ehipassiko*** against ***dhammiko*** — both end in *-ika*, and the suffix has a different job in each. Say what the difference is, and where each job is taught.
32. **bāhusaccaṁ**, in *Bāhusaccañca sippañca, vinayo ca susikkhito;* (Snp 2.4 — Sn p. 47) — four operations in one word. Name them in order.

### Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — the sentence and the verse

**Read the sentence.** Give a word-by-word gloss and an idiomatic rendering, and account for every non-finite form, every case, and every relative pair in it. Each of these needs at least two chapters from this stretch.

33. So kho ahaṁ … akāmakānaṁ mātāpitūnaṁ assumukhānaṁ rudantānaṁ kesamassuṁ ohāretvā kāsāyāni vatthāni acchādetvā agārasmā anagāriyaṁ pabbajiṁ. (MN 26 — M i 163)

    - Two absolutives share a subject with the main verb; one whole phrase does not. Say whose action each construction reports, and say why the second could not have been an absolutive.

34. Evaṁ passaṁ, bhikkhave, sutavā ariyasāvako rūpasmimpi nibbindati, vedanāyapi nibbindati, saññāyapi nibbindati, saṅkhāresupi nibbindati, viññāṇasmimpi nibbindati. (SN 22.59 — S iii 68)

    - Three of the five case-marked words are unambiguous. Two are not. Say what decides the two, and name the two different things that decide them.

35. Seyyathāpi, bhante, purisassa agāraṁ ekādasadvāraṁ, so tasmiṁ agāre āditte ekamekenapi dvārena sakkuṇeyya attānaṁ sotthiṁ kātuṁ. (MN 52 — M i 353)

    - Three constructions from this stretch are in this one sentence. Name all three and say what each contributes.

36. No cetaṁ, bhikkhave, abhavissa ajātaṁ abhūtaṁ akataṁ asaṅkhataṁ, nayidha jātassa bhūtassa katassa saṅkhatassa nissaraṇaṁ paññāyetha. Yasmā ca kho, bhikkhave, atthi ajātaṁ abhūtaṁ akataṁ asaṅkhataṁ, tasmā jātassa bhūtassa katassa saṅkhatassa nissaraṇaṁ paññāyati. (Ud 8.3 — Ud p. 80)

    - Two sentences, two moods in the first and one mood repeated in the second. Name the moods and say what the shift between the two sentences does to the meaning of *yasmā*.

37. Ito so, bhikkhave, ekanavutikappe yaṁ vipassī bhagavā arahaṁ sammāsambuddho loke udapādi. (DN 14 — D ii 2)

    - Buddhaghosa resolves this *yaṁ* instead of leaving it unanalysed. Give his resolution, name the case he assigns it, and say what would have to be true of an empty relative for a reader to accept his method generally, not only here.

38. Yaṁ kiñci samudayadhammaṁ sabbaṁ taṁ nirodhadhammaṁ. (SN 56.11 — S v 423)

    - No finite verb, and no *yo … so*. Name the two devices holding the sentence together, and say what kind of clause this is on the reckoning of the chapter on clause syntax.

**Scan the line.** Each of these has already been scanned in the metre chapter. Do not merely repeat the grid; say what the departure is, and connect it to something taught earlier in the book.

39. **Sabbapāpassa akaraṇaṁ, kusalassa upasampadā; Sacittapariyodapanaṁ, etaṁ buddhāna' sāsanaṁ.** (Dhp 183 — Dhp p. 27) — in the last line, what case and number does the full, un-elided form of the last word carry, and where in the book was that ending first taught?
40. **Bahū devā manussā ca, maṅgalāni acintayuṁ; Ākaṅkhamānā sotthānaṁ, brūhi maṅgalamuttamaṁ.** (Kp 5) — name the variation the third *pāda* is in, and say what its cadence is in place of the *pathyā*'s.
41. **Tapo ca brahmacariyañca, ariyasaccāna' dassanaṁ; Nibbānasacchikiriyā ca, etaṁ maṅgalamuttamaṁ.** (Kp 5) — the first *pāda* needs two separate accommodations and the second needs two more. Name all four, and say why none of them can do another's work.
42. **Catūhapāyehi ca vippamutto, Chaccābhiṭhānāni abhabba kātuṁ;** (Kp 6) — this line and its counterpart *abhabbŏ* raise a question this book has raised twice before, in two different chapters. Name both.
43. **Satto guhāyaṁ bahunābhichanno, Tiṭṭhaṁ naro mohanasmiṁ pagāḷho;** (Snp 4.2 — Sn p. 151) — the second line departs from the first inside the metre's break. Say how.
44. **Appaṁ vata jīvitaṁ idaṁ, Oraṁ vassasatāpi miyyati;** (Snp 4.6 — Sn p. 158) — count the measures in both lines. They do not come out the same. Say what the metre is fixing, then, if it is not the length of one line against the next.

**Read at length.** Three consecutive passages, all of them met before, none of them together. Read all three through before answering.

45. Imasmiñca pana veyyākaraṇasmiṁ bhaññamāne āyasmato koṇḍaññassa virajaṁ vītamalaṁ dhammacakkhuṁ udapādi. (SN 56.11 — S v 423)

    Yaṁ kiñci samudayadhammaṁ sabbaṁ taṁ nirodhadhammaṁ. (SN 56.11 — S v 423)

    Pavattite ca pana bhagavatā dhammacakke bhummā devā saddamanussāvesuṁ. (SN 56.11 — S v 423)

    - The first sentence names its construction by its tense. Which chapter's construction is it, and what does the tense say about when Koṇḍañña's insight arrives relative to the discourse still being spoken?
    - The second sentence has no finite verb and no correlative pronoun of the ordinary shape. Name what generalises it, and name the chapter that treats it.
    - The third sentence's case would be an ordinary locative of place if the sentence stopped one word earlier. What turns it into something else, and whose action does the instrumental name?

**Reaching back.** Five items reaching into Parts I–III. Each is a place where what you learnt there and what you learnt in this stretch have to be held together.

46. Chapter 2. *Duccaritaṁ* keeps its *c*; *duttaro* keeps its *t*; *uggacchati* keeps its *g*. State the rule from the compounds chapter that explains why an ordinary rule of sandhi does not flatten any of them, and say whose problem the rule is solving.
47. Chapter 3. *Jātitopi anussarati, nāmatopi anussarati, gottatopi anussarati.* (DN 14) Name the suffix, say which earlier chapter introduces it, and say what the *pi* on each word has done to a reader's chances of finding the bare form by search.
48. Chapter 5. The relative stem *ya(d)-* inflects as one demonstrative stem does, on one source's authority. Name the stem and the source, then follow the chain back to the earlier chapter that actually prints a paradigm in full. It is one step further back than you will expect.
49. Chapter 4. The chapter on gender and adjectives promised the adverbial accusative would return, and it did. Give one example from each of the two chapters, and name the larger table the second chapter built on top of the promise.
50. Chapter 8. The conditional tense was called the rarest finite form in the language, in the review that covered it. Where does it finally get the one job it does regularly, and what is the standing form of that job?

**Put it back into Pāli.** The nine chapters of this stretch have one method between them, and every item so far has run it in the direction of taking things apart. This one runs it the other way.

51. Here are the members of three compounds this review has already printed, given uncompounded. Build each, put the first into the locative plural as its line has it, and name the class of each.

    *tiro* + *kuṭṭa* · *ghara* + *āvāsa* · *raja* + *patha*

    Then say which of the three you could not have built from its members alone. The answer is a fact about one word, not a rule about compounding, and the form you would otherwise have written does not occur anywhere in the canon. Check the first against item 2, where it stands in its line, and the other two against the compounds set, where they stand in theirs.

    Finally, the general question this stretch has been building towards. Say in two sentences what a compound buys a reciter that a string of separate words does not, and what it costs a reader.

### Coming back — the sentence and the verse

The set is not finished on the day it is worked. Re-attempt these, from cold, without reading the answers first.

**Day 3.** Items 1 to 5 aloud. Five passages, and between them the whole method of this stretch: four participles standing in for a clause, three compounds standing in for three phrases, a courtier's list with no verb in it, the last link of dependent origination in four words, and a verse whose final genitive plural had to lose a syllable to be a verse at all. Say them, do not read them.

**Day 10.** Items 39 to 44. The six scanned lines, together, and from the marks up, not from the answer down. Scansion alone in the book can be done wholly by rule, so a second attempt either comes out identical to the first or shows you which rule you have not got.

**Day 30.** Items 46 to 51. The five reaching-back items and the production item. These are the only places in the book where a claim about the book itself is being asked for, and they are the last things to be sure of: if you can say where each of these nine chapters reaches back to, then the book has been read as one argument, not as nineteen.

## Answers: Script and Pronunciation

**Sotānugata — met by ear.**

1. Namo tassa bhagavato arahato sammāsambuddhassa. (MN 91 — M ii 140)

    na·**mo** **tas**·sa bha·ga·va·**to** a·ra·ha·**to** **sam·mā·sam·bud·dhas**·sa

    *Mo*, *to* and *to* are heavy because *o* is long and their syllables are open. *Tas*, *sam*, *sam*, *bud* and *dhas* are heavy because each is closed by a consonant. *Mā* is heavy because the vowel is long. Every light syllable in the line is a short vowel standing open: *na*, *sa*, *bha*, *ga*, *va*, *a*, *ra*, *ha*, *sa*.

    The longest run of lights is *bha·ga·va*, three of them, and then *a·ra·ha*, three more; the longest run of heavies is the last word, five in a row. Three unhurried words of mostly light syllables, and then the homage lands on its object and stays there. It is built that way.

2. Suṇātu me, bhante, saṅgho. (Kd 1 — Vin i 95)

    su·**ṇā**·tu **me** **bhan**·**te** **saṅ**·**gho**

    The two light syllables are *su* and *tu*, and everything else in the line is heavy. Six heavy syllables out of eight is unusually dense for prose, which is part of why the formula sounds the way it does.

    The *e* of *bhante* is heavy because *e* is long by nature and the syllable is open: the reasoning of item 11, met before it is asked for. And *saṅgho* is written with *ṅ* because a group nasal attaches to members of its own group, so a nasal standing before *g* or *gh* can only be the guttural nasal. The line carries the other half of that rule as well: *bhante* has a plain *n*, because *t* is a dental. One rule, two spellings, and nobody chose either.

    This is the opening of every formal act of the Sangha (every ordination, every recitation of the *pātimokkha*, every settlement of a dispute), and a monastic will say or hear it more often than any other sentence in the canon.

3. Sabbapāpassa akaraṇaṁ, kusalassa upasampadā; (Dhp 183 — Dhp p. 27)

    **sab**·ba·**pā**·**pas**·sa a·ka·ra·**ṇaṁ**, ku·sa·**las**·sa u·pa·**sam**·pa·**dā**

    Seven heavy syllables, and five of them are heavy for reasons the eye catches at once: *pā* and *dā* have long vowels, and *sab*, *pas* and *las* are each closed by the first of a doubled consonant.

    The question is after ***ṇaṁ*** and ***sam***. *Ṇaṁ* is closed by the niggahīta, which is neither a doubled consonant nor a long vowel but closes a syllable as either would. *Sam*, in *upasampadā*, is closed by the *m* of the cluster *mp*: a single consonant, not a doubled one, and it still closes the syllable before it because the break falls between two adjacent consonants. A syllable is heavy if it ends in anything other than a short vowel, and the four ways of ending otherwise are all present in this one line.

**Vacasā paricitā — rehearsed aloud.**

4. Itipi so bhagavā arahaṁ … buddho bhagavā. (AN 6.10 — A iii 285)

    The chapter prints this line divided and weighed, and comparing your recitation against it is the check. What the writing tests is something else.

    Of the errors a first attempt produces, two kinds are audible and one is not. A missing macron (*bhagava* for *bhagavā*, *sattha* for *satthā*) is audible, and so is a missing doubling: *anutaro* for *anuttaro*, *devamanusānaṁ* for *devamanussānaṁ*. The second of those is the more instructive, because the mistake does not produce a non-word: *sattha* is 'a knife' and 'a caravan' and 'a science', and only the macron makes it the Teacher. If you were reciting properly, your ear had the information and your hand lost it. The failure is the hand's.

    The retroflex of *vijjācaraṇasampanno* is the other case. Most reciters do not distinguish *ṇ* from *n* audibly, and anyone never taught to make the retroflex sounds cannot hear the difference to write it down; that one has to be learned as spelling. Do not conclude from this that the distinction is unimportant. It separates real words, and the chapter's variant box is about what happens when editors disagree over these letters.

5. Ayaṁ itthannāmo itthannāmassa āyasmato upasampadāpekkho. (Kd 1 — Vin i 95)

    Six doubled consonants and four long vowels, in a line of five words. The doubled ones are *tth* and *nn* in *itthannāmo*, *tth*, *nn* and *ss* in *itthannāmassa*, and *kkh* in *upasampadāpekkho*; the long vowels are the *ā* of each *-nāma-*, the *ā* of *āyasmato*, and the *ā* of *-padā-*.

    Three of the six doublings are aspirates doubled the Pāli way (*tth* is *t* + *th* and *kkh* is *k* + *kh*), item 6's rule twice over. And the line shows well why writing is a different skill from reading: nothing in it is hard to say, and almost every word of it is easy to write down wrongly.

    *Itthannāma* is 'so-and-so', the name-shaped hole in a formula, and it is itself a junction: *itthaṁ* + *nāma*, with the niggahīta assimilating to the *n* that follows it. *Upasampadāpekkho* is *upasampadā* + *apekkha*, where the long *ā* and the short *a* meet and come out as one long *ā*. Both are sandhi, and both are the next chapter's business; they are pointed out here only because they make the line hard to spell.

**Manasānupekkhitā — gone over in the mind.**

6. *dukkha* — **five letters**: *d*, *u*, *k*, *kh*, *a*. Six characters are printed, because *kh* is a digraph and takes two of them. The doubled sound here is *k* followed by *kh*, not *kh* twice, the ordinary way Pāli doubles an aspirate: the first member loses its aspiration. So the spelling is *dukkha* and never *dukhkha*.

7. *amhi* — **four letters**: *a*, *m*, *h*, *i*. Four characters too, and hold this pair beside 6. *Mh* is **not** a digraph, because *m* is not one of the sounds that takes an aspirate partner; it is an *m* followed by an *h*. The chapter's test is exact: every combination with *h* that appears in the alphabet list is a digraph, and every other combination with *h* is two letters. Item 18 is the same test on a different nasal.

8. *saṅgha* — **five letters**: *s*, *a*, *ṅ*, *gh*, *a*. Six characters, *gh* being the digraph. Some works, the PTS dictionary among them, write this word *sangha* with a plain *n*, because *ṅ* occurs only before *k*, *kh*, *g* and *gh* and can therefore be inferred. Nothing is lost either way; this book writes *ṅ*.

9. *daḷha* — four letters, or five, and the disagreement is real. *D*, *a*, *ḷh*, *a* if *ḷh* is one letter; *d*, *a*, *ḷ*, *h*, *a* if it is two. It is written with two characters even in the Asian scripts, and it stands for a single sound. Perniola counts it among his liquids and so gets forty-two letters in the alphabet; the PTS dictionary's list and Kaccāyana's both omit it and get forty-one. Either answer is defensible provided you can say which count you are using. Note that the syllable division does not wait on the answer: *daḷha* divides *da·ḷha* on either view, because *ḷh* is one *sound* whatever it is called.

10. *sammāsambuddho* — **thirteen letters**: *s*, *a*, *m*, *m*, *ā*, *s*, *a*, *m*, *b*, *u*, *d*, *dh*, *o*. Fourteen characters, the digraph being *dh*. The two doubled consonants behave differently on the page and identically in principle: *mm* is a plain geminate, and *ddh* is *d* + *dh*, an aspirate doubled by an unaspirated copy of itself, as in 6.

11. *bhante* — **long**. The *e* is at the end of the word, so its syllable is open and unclosed. *E* and *o* are long by nature and stay long unless something takes the length away.

12. *mettā* — **short**. Divide it *met·tā*: the *e* is closed by the first of the two *t*s, and a closed syllable holding a long vowel would come to three *mattā*, which Pāli does not allow. The vowel gives up its length so the syllable can keep its consonant. This is the two-*mattā* ceiling in its ordinary operation, and *bhante* beside *mettā* is the cleanest minimal pair for it.

13. *soṇḍo* — the first *o* is the disputed case, and the second is not. The final *o* is word-final, so it is long by the same reasoning as 11. The first *o* is followed by the conjunct *ṇḍ*, which by the general rule of 12 should shorten it. Buddhappiya's exception says otherwise: *e* and *o* shorten before *geminate* consonants but not before conjuncts formed with a group nasal, so *soṇḍo* 'drunkard' and *meṇḍo* 'ram' keep a long first vowel where *bhāseyya* does not. Kaccāyana holds that *e* and *o* are simply always long; Moggallāna holds that a short *e* must be a different letter from a long one, and counts forty-three letters in consequence. Say which authority you follow and the answer is settled; assert one flatly and you are taking a side without knowing it.

14. *arahaṁ* — **short**, and the question is a trap worth falling into. *A* is a short vowel and there is no rule that lengthens it here. What is easy to slip into is confusing the vowel with the syllable: the syllable *-haṁ* at the end of the word is **heavy**, because the niggahīta closes it, but the vowel inside it is still short. Vowel length and syllable weight are two different measurements, and almost every error in reading a verse line comes from running them together. Item 17 is the same distinction met where it costs something.

**Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — penetrated by view.**

15. Manopubbaṅgamā dhammā, manoseṭṭhā manomayā; (Dhp 1 — Dhp p. 1)

    ma·**no**·**pub**·**baṅ**·ga·**mā** **dham**·**mā**, ma·**no**·**seṭ**·**ṭhā** ma·**no**·ma·**yā**

    Three points here. *No* is heavy three times over, and each time for the same reason as *bhante*'s *e*: the syllable is open and *o* is long by nature. *Baṅ* is heavy because the niggahīta's own consonant, *ṅ*, closes it, with the weight a written niggahīta would carry. And *pub* is heavy because the *b* of *pubbaṅgama* is doubled, so the first of the two closes the preceding syllable.

    This is also the line where nothing joins: *mano* keeps its *-o* unchanged in front of all three following consonants. The next chapter has a name for that, and sets this same line again to make the point.

16. …poriyā vācāya samannāgato vissaṭṭhāya anelagalāya atthassa viññāpaniyā… (DN 4 — D i 114)

    **po**·ri·**yā** **vā**·**cā**·ya sa·**man**·**nā**·ga·**to** **vis**·**saṭ**·**ṭhā**·ya a·**ne**·la·ga·**lā**·ya **at**·**thas**·sa **viñ**·**ñā**·pa·ni·**yā**

    Check yourself on *ne* in *anelagalāya*. Only a single *l* follows it, so the syllable is open, and an open syllable with *e* in it is heavy. Take *e* as short by default and you will make this light and find the line does not scan.

    That is the same trap as *de-* in *devamanussānaṁ*, and it is the commonest weight error there is. The rule to hold: *e* and *o* are long unless something closes their syllable.

17. Yassa nakkhamati, so bhāseyya. (Kd 1 — Vin i 95)

    **yas**·sa **nak**·kha·ma·ti, **so** **bhā**·**sey**·ya

    "Whoever does not consent should speak."

    *Nakkhamati* is *na* + *khamati*, 'it is not acceptable'. The negative particle joins, and the aspirate doubles the only way a Pāli aspirate can, by putting an unaspirated copy of itself in front. So *na* + *kh* gives *kkh* and not *khkh*: item 6's rule in a live junction, not a dictionary word.

    *Bhāseyya* is the harder half. The chapter's rule, in item 13, is that *e* shortens before a geminate, and *yy* is a geminate, so the *e* here is short. The weight does not change. The syllable *sey* is closed by the first *y* whatever the vowel inside it, so it is heavy either way, and a scansion of this line cannot show you the shortening at all. Item 14 made that point in the abstract: length and weight are two measurements, and here the first one moves while the second stands still. The shortening is real, it is audible, and it is metrically invisible.

    Every monastic in the tradition has heard this sentence at their own ordination, and it is the moment at which a formal act can be stopped.

18. Yassāyasmato khamati … so tuṇhassa; (Kd 1 — Vin i 95)

    tuṇ·has·sa: **eight letters**: *t*, *u*, *ṇ*, *h*, *a*, *s*, *s*, *a*.

    *Ṇh* is two letters and not a digraph, by the same test that settled *amhi* in item 7: the aspirated nasals do not stand in the alphabet, so any nasal followed by *h* is a nasal followed by an *h*. Nothing about the retroflex changes that.

    The division is the interesting part. By the rule as the chapter states it, a break falls between two adjacent consonants, which gives *tuṇ·has·sa*: heavy, heavy, light. But the chapter's box lists *nh* among the clusters that may be divided either way, and if *ṇh* goes with its dental counterpart there, then *tu·ṇhas·sa* is equally allowed and the first syllable is **light**. The chapter does not say whether *ṇh* belongs on that list, and no source read for it says either.

    The consequence is safe. This is prose, and prose does not force the choice; in a verse line the metre would force it, and the division exists for the metre. To want a rule where there is none is to misunderstand what the rules are for.

    The word itself is a junction too: *tuṇhī* 'silent' + *assa* 'would be', 'let him be silent'. The silence is consent, and so item 17 is the alternative to it.

**Put the diacritics back.**

19. Three words, and only one of them cannot be decided.

    (a) *sammāsambuddhassa*: the long *ā* is **audible**, and if you have said the homage aloud at chanting pace you have the information already. Nothing here needs a book.

    (b) *saṅgho*: recoverable **by rule**, without hearing anything at all. A nasal before *k*, *kh*, *g* or *gh* is always *ṅ*, so the dot cannot be anywhere else, and so the PTS dictionary is content to print *sangha* and lose nothing, as item 8 says.

    (c) *anelagalāya*: not recoverable, and this is the undecidable one. The final *-lāya* has an audible long *ā* like (a), but the two *l*s are another matter: the word has two of them, each may be *l* or *ḷ*, and all four resulting spellings are printed in real editions. The Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana prints *anelagalāya* in its running text and records *aneḷagalāya* for the Sinhalese and PTS editions in a note at that line, with *anelagaḷāya* in a third. So the question "which is correct" has no answer in the abstract; it has an answer for the edition in front of you, and a different one for the edition next to it.

    That is what separates this item from the other two. Vowel length is a fact about the language and you can hear it. The *l*/*ḷ* of this word is a fact about a manuscript tradition, and no amount of careful listening will supply it. Knowing which of the two kinds of question you are facing is most of what this chapter is for.

## Answers: Sandhi

**Sotānugata — met by ear.**

1. Yo panāti yo yādiso … Eso vuccati "yo panā"ti. (Bu Pj 1 — Vin iii 23)

    The long *ā* comes from the meeting, and it belongs to neither word. *Pana* ends in a short *a* and *iti* begins with a short *i*; the *i* is lost, and what is left lengthens to pay for it. Said at speed the two syllables simply will not stay apart, which is the whole of the explanation.

    This line comes first because here the canon does the exercise itself. Each rule of the Pātimokkha is followed by a word-analysis that quotes the rule's word as the rule prints it, joined, and then gives it back with the join marked instead of written: *Eso vuccati "yo panā"ti*. And the division does not restore the words. The *ā* has to be left standing somewhere, and the editor leaves it on *panā*, where it never was.

2. Bhikkhūti bhikkhakoti bhikkhu, (Bu Pj 1 — Vin iii 24)

    *Bhikkhūti* is *bhikkhu* + *iti*, and the short *u* lengthens to *ū*. *Bhikkhakoti* is *bhikkhako* + *iti*, and the *o* stays as it is, because *o* is already long. The rule is that a *short* vowel before the quotative lengthens, and there was nothing here to lengthen.

    So the same junction has two outcomes, and neither is a special case: one vowel was short and the other was not. This uses the fact of set 01's item 11, and so the script chapter comes first.

3. "Parimaṇḍalaṁ nivāsessāmīti sikkhā karaṇīyā"ti. (Bu Sk 1 — Vin iv 185)

    *Nivāsessāmi* + *iti* gives *nivāsessāmīti*: the *i* of *iti* is lost and the *i* before it lengthens. Set beside the first two items, the three cover *a* → *ā*, *u* → *ū* and *i* → *ī*, with *o* unchanged. One rule, four vowels, and only the short ones move.

    There is a practical consequence, and it is why these three items are here and not in a footnote. The word's own last vowel has changed, so the form a dictionary files it under is **not even contained** in the printed text. *Nivāsessāmi* has 0 root-text occurrences. *Nivāsessāmīti* has 2 root-text occurrences. Every *sekhiya* rule is built this way, so the whole class answers a whole-word search with silence, and to trust the silence is to conclude that a common form does not exist.

4. Esevanto dukkhassā. (SN 12.51 — S ii 84)

    Three words inside one, and both junctions are the plainest kind: the final vowel of *eso* drops, then the final vowel of *eva* drops, and *anto* keeps its own. Two vowels lost, one surviving. DPD lists the whole string as a sandhi entry with that construction, and prints it *es'ev'anto*, the apostrophes marking the two elisions.

    Take two points from it. Unless you divide this correctly you cannot look up any of the three words, because *esevanto* is in no dictionary as a headword except as a sandhi form. And the resolved sequence *eso eva anto* is a string no manuscript contains, so this book puts the resolution on the gloss line and never inside the quotation.

**Vacasā paricitā — rehearsed aloud.**

5. …yena mithilā yena brahmāyu brāhmaṇo tenupasaṅkami; upasaṅkamitvā brahmāyuṁ brāhmaṇaṁ abhivādetvā ekamantaṁ nisīdi. (MN 91 — M ii 135)

    "…he went to Mithilā, to the brahmin Brahmāyu; and having approached, he bowed to the brahmin Brahmāyu and sat down to one side."

    Four junctions, of three different kinds.

    *Tenupasaṅkami* is *tena* + *upasaṅkami*, the final *a* elided before the *u*. It is the commonest single sandhi in the canon, because it sits inside this formula, and if you do not recognise it you will look up a verb that does not exist. *Brahmāyu*, the man's name, is itself a compound, *brahma* + *āyu*, with two *a*-sounds contracting to *ā*; that is internal sandhi, and so the name is not *Brahmaāyu*. *Ekamantaṁ* is *ekaṁ* + *anta* with the accusative ending, so the *m* in the middle is a niggahīta that met a vowel, as in item 8, and so this stock adverb, one of the most frequent phrases in the canon, is a frozen sandhi.

    And the exercise was fishing for the fourth: *abhivādetvā ekamantaṁ*. A long *ā* stands immediately before an *e*, the two words are said in sequence, and **nothing happens**. That is *pakatisandhi* across a word boundary, and it is the ordinary state of a Pāli sentence. Perniola's point stands behind it: in Pāli, unlike Sanskrit, words in a sentence are kept separate unless habit has run them together.

6. Pātimokkhanti ādimetaṁ mukhametaṁ pamukhametaṁ kusalānaṁ dhammānaṁ. Tena vuccati pātimokkhanti. (Kd 2 — Vin i 103)

    The odd one out is ***pātimokkhanti***, and what decided it is what stood in front of the niggahīta.

    In *ādimetaṁ*, *mukhametaṁ* and *pamukhametaṁ*, the niggahīta of *ādiṁ*, *mukhaṁ* and *pamukhaṁ* meets the vowel of *etaṁ* and becomes the labial *m*. In *pātimokkhanti* it meets *iti*, and *iti* loses its initial *i* first, which brings the niggahīta up against a *t*, where it becomes the dental *n*. Same niggahīta, two outcomes, and the vowel that decided it is no longer there to be seen. It is the two-step of item 8 in a different order, and it explains why the fourth of the chapter's four junction types is kept separate from the third.

    There is the sense of it as well. *Ādi*, *mukha*, *pamukha*, beginning, face, fore: the Pātimokkha is being described as the front of the wholesome qualities, which is the traditional etymology the word is given.

**Manasānupekkhitā — gone over in the mind.**

7. *natthi* — **vowel + vowel**. It is *na* + *atthi*, and DPD lists it under that construction. Two short *a*s meet and one survives, and the survivor is **short**. Two identical vowels contracting ought to give a long one, but the resulting syllable *nat-* is closed by the *t*, and a closed syllable cannot hold a long vowel. The two-*mattā* ceiling from the previous chapter decides it, and so the form is *natthi* and not *nātthi*.

8. *ahampi* — **niggahīta + vowel**, though the *m* on the page makes it look like a consonant junction. It is *ahaṁ* + *api*: the initial vowel of *api* drops, which brings the niggahīta up against the *p*, and the niggahīta then takes the nasal of the labial group. Two changes, in that order, and the second is only possible because the first happened.

9. *tañca* — **niggahīta + consonant**. *Taṁ* + *ca*, with the niggahīta taking the palatal nasal before the palatal *c*. The plainest instance of the rule there is.

10. *nappajānāti* — **vowel + consonant**. *Na* + *pajānāti*, with the *p* doubling. The doubling is historical, not decorative: the *p* had a cluster behind it in Old Indic, the cluster simplified at the beginning of a word, and putting a word in front restores the middle-of-word environment in which it survives. Compare *na* + *khamati* → *nakkhamati*.

11. *tenupasaṅkami* = ***tena*** + ***upasaṅkami***, 'he approached by that [way]'. The final *a* of *tena* is elided before the *u*. This is the commonest single sandhi in the canon, because it sits inside the stock narrative formula *yena … tena upasaṅkami*, and if you do not recognise it you will look up a verb that does not exist.

12. *sveva* = ***so*** + ***eva***, 'that very one'. Two steps. The diphthong *o* is resolved into its parts *a* + *u*; then the sonant *u*, now standing before a vowel, takes its consonantal form *v*, and the *a* drops. That is the chapter's point about *ādeso*: no consonant has appeared out of nowhere; a vowel has changed role.

13. *etadavoca* = ***etaṁ*** + ***avoca***, 'said this'. The *d* is not inserted. *Etad* is what this pronoun looked like in Old Indic, and before a vowel the old final consonant surfaces again; DPD gives the Sanskrit as *etad*. The same *d* appears in *yadeva*, *sammadeva*, *bahudeva* and *sakideva*.

14. *appekacce* = ***api*** + ***ekacce***, 'some'. Two steps again, and DPD spells them out: *api* > *apy* > *app*. The *i* of *api* becomes its semivowel *y* before the following vowel, and the resulting *py* then assimilates: *p* and *y* are of unequal strength, a mute against a semivowel, so by the rule the weaker gives way to the stronger. This is the single most instructive form in the set, because it needs the *ādeso* rule and the strength hierarchy in sequence.

15. *yāvadeva* = ***yāva*** + ***eva***, 'just as far as'. As with 13, the *d* is a relic and not a bridge: Old Indic *yāvad*. This is the item the chapter warns against getting confidently wrong. Dividing it as *yāva* + *d* + *eva*, with *d* as an inserted glide, gives two real words and one invented rule; dividing it as *yāvad* + *eva* explains where the *d* came from and predicts *punareti* from *punar* as well.

16. *evañhi* = ***evaṁ*** + ***hi***, 'for thus'. The niggahīta takes the palatal nasal before *h*, which belongs to no group of its own. The same thing happens in *bhavañhi*, which opens the Soṇadaṇḍa passage quoted in the chapter on script.

**Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — penetrated by view.**

17. Manopubbaṅgamā dhammā, manoseṭṭhā manomayā; (Dhp 1 — Dhp p. 1)

    *manopubbaṅgamā* < *mano* mind + *pubbaṅgama* going before (m. nom. pl.) · *dhammā* mental states (m. nom. pl.) · *manoseṭṭhā* < *mano* + *seṭṭha* chief · *manomayā* < *mano* + *maya* made of

    "Mind precedes mental states; mind is their chief, they are made by mind."

    Every junction in the line is *pakatisandhi*, and that is the point of setting it. *Mano* meets *p*, then *s*, then *m*, and its final *-o* stands unchanged in all three. Nothing has happened, nothing was going to, and a reader hunting for a rule here will invent one.

    The one change is invisible and belongs to the noun chapter, not this one: *mano* is the *-as* stem *manas*, and the *-o* is what *-as* became at the end of a word. That change happened long before the compound was formed.

18. *coro* + *asi* · *bālo* + *asi* · *mūḷho* + *asi* · *theno* + *asi* + *iti*

    "'You are a robber, you are a fool, you are deluded, you are a thief.'"

    Each of the four is a nominative noun or adjective followed by *asi*, the second person singular of *atthi*, 'you are'. The junction is the elision this set's third stage drills: the final *-o* of the noun stands, the initial *a-* of *asi* is lost, and the two words are written as one. *Coro asi* becomes *corosi*, and so for *bālo*, *mūḷho* and *theno*. There is no other change: no vowel lengthens and no consonant assimilates.

    The fourth is longer because it carries a second junction on top of the first. It closes the quotation, so *iti* follows it, and the quotative lengthens the vowel in front of it: *thenosi* + *iti* gives *thenosīti*. Two joins in one word, of different kinds: an elision at the first and a lengthening at the second.

    That second join leaves a mark. *Thenosi* has 0 root-text occurrences as a word standing alone. The edition has *thenosī*, with 2 root-text occurrences, because the form never occurs without the quotative behind it.

    The fully joined *thenosīti* has 1 root-text occurrence, which is the line quoted here. Looked for as it would be spelt in isolation, the form is not found. The word is there and the lookup was wrong, which is what the edition does to any word whose last syllable a following particle has altered.

    *Theno* is 'thief', from √*then*; *coro* is the ordinary word for a robber. The Vinaya prints both because the rule is defining what kind of taking would have a king treat a man as a criminal, and it wants the whole vocabulary of the accusation.

19. The four junctions made, not undone.

    (a) *tenupasaṅkami* (MN 91 — M ii 135): the final *a* of *tena* is elided before the *u*, and no other change occurs.

    (b) *esevanto* (SN 12.51 — S ii 84): two elisions in sequence: the *o* of *eso* goes, then the *a* of *eva*, and *anto* is left with its own vowel intact.

    (c) *corosi* (Bu Pj 2 — Vin iii 47): the initial *a-* of *asi* is elided and the *-o* of *coro* stands. Note that this is the mirror image of (a): there the first word lost its vowel, here the second word loses its own.

    (d) *nivāsessāmīti* (Bu Sk 1 — Vin iv 185): the *i* of *iti* is lost and the short *i* before it lengthens.

    If you produced (a) and (c) and hesitated over (b) and (d), that is the expected shape of the result. A single elision is a rule you can hold; two in sequence, and a change that alters a vowel you thought you had finished with, are what actually has to be practised. Producing a junction and recognising one are different skills, and this book has drilled the second nine times before asking for the first.

20. *Panā* occurs elsewhere, and it is still not a word.

    The test returns a positive: *panā* has 6 root-text occurrences. So the division *yo panā* + *ti* passes the check that the chapter's phantom-glide test applies, and it should not.

    Look at what the six are. Every one of them is in the Mahāniddesa and every one is in a single verse line, *Saddhammapūjāpi panā tatheva*; DPD carries no headword for *panā* at all. The test has found a string in the edition, not a word the language has.

    So the test rules a division out; it does not rule one in. That qualifies it without making an exception to it, because the test only ever had one purpose: a division whose pieces occur nowhere is certainly wrong, and a division whose pieces occur somewhere is merely not yet refuted. Item 15 is the same point from the other side — *yāva* and *eva* both occur, and the division that uses them is still the wrong one.

    Hold the two together and the chapter's real position appears. A division is a hypothesis about what a speaker said, and it is tested by whether it explains the form, not by whether its pieces can be found in an index.

## Answers: Nouns

**Sotānugata — met by ear.**

1. *Attā hi attano nātho.* — The long vowel is the *ā* of *attā*, and again of *nātho*; the doubled consonant is the *tt*, which belongs to both *attā* and *attano* and has to be held in each. Said properly the line has a shape: two heavy words, a short particle, and a landing.

    *Attano* is the genitive singular of *attan* 'self', a masculine *-an* stem, the same class as *rājan* in item 9. Four words teach two points. The genitive and dative of this class fall together in *-ano*, so *attano* is equally 'for oneself'; and the line puts one stem in two cases, *attā* nominative and *attano* genitive, which is the trick item 21 turns at greater length.

2. *Punappunaṁ vaccho upeti mātaraṁ.* — The doubled *pp* is the whole point of *punappunaṁ*, 'again and again', which is *puna* doubled with the consonant assimilating at the join.

    *Mātaraṁ* is the accusative singular of *mātar* 'mother', a feminine *-ar* stem, and its nominative singular is *mātā*. Note the **short** *a* of *mātaraṁ* against the long *ā* of *satthāraṁ* in item 10. That is no spelling accident: it is the division between the relationship nouns (*mātar*, *pitar*, *bhātar*), which keep the short vowel here, and the agent nouns formed from verbs, which lengthen it. One vowel separates a family word from a job description.

3. *Te taṁ rattiṁ tikkhattuṁ paccuṭṭhāya pañjalikā aggiṁ namassanti.* *Rattiṁ* is feminine and *aggiṁ* masculine, and the line does not show it. The *i*-stem accusative singular is *-iṁ* in both genders, so the ending cannot help; and the only word that might have helped does not, because *taṁ* is the accusative singular of *ta(d)* in all three genders alike.

    Only knowledge of the words decides it. *Ratti* is feminine and *aggi* masculine, as the dictionary and the paradigms have it, and a reader learns them one at a time. A sentence *can* supply, when it happens to, an adjective or a participle agreeing with the noun in a case where the genders differ, and this line supplies none. It shows fairly how much of Pāli gender is vocabulary, not grammar.

4. *Mātā yathā niyaṁ puttam āyusā ekaputtamanurakkhe.* — *Mātā* is the nominative singular and *mātaraṁ* in item 2 the accusative singular, of one word.

    A search of the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana for the word *āyusā* on its own finds nothing, and the form is here all the same: the edition has *puttam* and *āyusā* run together as *puttamāyusā*, so a search for the word looks straight past it. This is the caution the chapter draws out of this very line, and it is one to keep. A failed search is a question, not a verdict — look for the string inside longer words before believing it.

    *Āyusā* is an instrumental singular, and the form is the older one: *āyu* 'life' is historically a *-us* stem, and *-usā* is what such a stem does in the instrumental. Its ordinary canonical instrumental is *āyunā*, on the *u*-stem pattern the word has otherwise gone over to. The old form survives in verse, which is where this line is.

**Vacasā paricitā — rehearsed aloud.**

5. *Idha pana, upavāṇa, bhikkhu cakkhunā rūpaṁ disvā rūpappaṭisaṁvedī ca hoti.* — "Here, Upavāṇa, a monk, having seen a form with the eye, experiences the form."

    *Cakkhunā* is the form of item 11, met in its own sentence: this line and its fellows are why the instrumental is the reading to reach for first. Look again at *rūpaṁ* while you have the line in your mouth, because *-aṁ* is the neuter nominative **and** accusative singular, and only *disvā* decides between them: one does not see with a form.

6. *Nibbindaṁ virajjati; virāgā vimuccati. Vimuttasmiṁ vimuttamiti ñāṇaṁ hoti.* — *Virāgā* is an ablative singular of *virāga* 'the fading of desire', a masculine *a*-stem: an ablative of cause, 'through the fading of desire'. *Vimuttasmiṁ* is a locative singular, marking the circumstance — 'when he is freed'.

    Two *a*-stem obliques in consecutive clauses, and they show the two forms the declension offers. *Virāgā* takes the bare *-ā*, which is the ablative that stands beside *-asmā*, *-amhā* and *-ato*; *vimuttasmiṁ* takes the full *-asmiṁ*. Both are ordinary canonical forms, not paradigm fillers, and not every cell in the table can say as much.

7. *… pubbe ananussutesu dhammesu cakkhuṁ udapādi, ñāṇaṁ udapādi, paññā udapādi, vijjā udapādi, āloko udapādi.* — *Paññā* and *vijjā* are feminine, *cakkhuṁ* and *ñāṇaṁ* neuter, *āloko* masculine; all five are nominative singular, and *ananussutesu dhammesu* is a masculine locative plural.

    The ending narrows the field in every case and settles it in none, and the set you are holding supplies the counterexamples. *-ā* here marks *paññā* and *vijjā* as feminine *ā*-stems, but *attā* in item 1 and *mātā* in item 4 are also nominative singulars in *-ā*, from consonant stems, and one of those is masculine. *-o* marks *āloko* as a masculine *a*-stem, but *mano*, whose locative is item 12, has the same nominative and is the word whose gender the sources cannot agree about. *-aṁ* is the least informative of the three: neuter nominative and accusative singular, and masculine accusative singular besides.

    So the ending gives you a short list and the sentence decides among them, which is the chapter's whole argument in five words of one line. Here it is the verb that decides: *udapādi*, 'arose', takes no object, so every noun beside it is a subject.

**Manasānupekkhitā — gone over in the mind.**

8. *bhikkhūnaṁ* — *bhikkhu*, masculine *u*-stem. Genitive/dative plural. The lengthened stem vowel is the plural marker: *bhikkhu-naṁ* becomes *bhikkhūnaṁ* by the same lengthening that gives *bhikkhūhi* and *bhikkhūsu*. One row, two cases, because the genitive and dative have fallen together everywhere except the *a*-stem singular.

9. *raññā* — *rājan* 'king', masculine *-an* stem. Instrumental or ablative singular. Know this form cold, because nothing about it announces that it belongs to *rājā*: the *-jan-* of the stem has assimilated to *-ññ-*. The alternative *rājinā* exists and is very rare — one occurrence in the root texts against seventy-one for *raññā*.

10. *satthāraṁ* — *satthar* 'teacher', masculine *-ar* stem. Accusative singular. The **long** stem vowel matters here: it marks *satthar* as an **agent noun**, one of the group formed from verbs (*dātar* 'giver', *netar* 'leader'), as against the relationship nouns *pitar*, *mātar*, *bhātar*, which have a short vowel in this case and in the nominative, vocative and accusative plural. *Satthāraṁ* against *pitaraṁ* is the whole distinction in two words, and item 2's *mātaraṁ* shows it in a real sentence.

11. *cakkhunā* — *cakkhu* 'eye', neuter *u*-stem. Instrumental or ablative singular, and the form does not choose between them. In practice the instrumental is overwhelmingly the more likely, because *cakkhunā* is a standing phrase: *cakkhunā rūpaṁ disvā*, 'having seen a form with the eye', which is item 5.

12. *manasi* — *manas* 'mind', an *-as* stem. Locative singular. The *-asi* ending is one of the four that mark this class as still declining the old way, not as an ordinary *a*-stem, alongside *mano*, *manasā* and *manaso*. Note that the same word also has a complete *a*-stem declension running beside this one (*manaṁ*, *manena*, *manasmiṁ*), which is the migration described in the chapter, and the same migration that has taken *āyusā* to *āyunā* in item 4.

13. *bhagavato* — *bhagavant*, masculine *-(n)t* stem. Genitive/dative singular. It is built on the **weak** stem *bhagavat-*, not the strong *bhagavant-*, which is the class's defining behaviour: strong stem in the accusative singular and throughout the plural (*bhagavantaṁ*, *bhagavanto*), weak stem in the other singular cases (*bhagavatā*, *bhagavato*, *bhagavati*).

14. *purisā* — three possibilities. **Nominative plural** masculine (*purisa-as* > *purisā*); **vocative plural**, which is the same form; and **ablative singular**, from the bare *-ā* ending that stands beside *-asmā*, *-amhā* and *-ato* — the ending item 6's *virāgā* carries. To settle it, look at the verb: a plural verb makes it a nominative plural. If the verb is singular, look for a verb of motion, separation, origin or fear, which is what an ablative attaches to — and for the possibility that a following word governs it.

15. *kaññāya* — **five**: instrumental, ablative, genitive, dative and locative singular. This is the sharpest ambiguity in the nominal system, and so the feminine *ā*-stems deserve attention out of proportion to their difficulty. The form itself carries no information beyond "feminine, singular, oblique". Everything is decided by the sentence: the verb, any preposition-like indeclinable, and whether there is another noun for it to depend on: a genitive needs something to possess, a dative someone to benefit.

16. *rūpāni* — neuter nominative, vocative or accusative plural. The three are always identical in the neuter, in every stem class, which saves effort instead of adding difficulty. Settle it by looking for another nominative in the clause and by the verb: a transitive verb with its own subject makes *rūpāni* the object. The singular *rūpaṁ* has the same ambiguity in a shorter form, which is item 5.

17. *aggino* — **genitive or dative singular** of *aggi* 'fire', built on the *-ino* ending this class shares with the *-in* stems. It is also listed in the paradigms as a **nominative plural**, an alternative to *aggayo*. Here the texts settle what the grammar leaves open: as a nominative plural *aggino* occurs nowhere in the root texts, only in the commentaries and the grammarians' works. So the ambiguity is real on paper and, in the canon, almost always resolves to the genitive.

**Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — penetrated by view.**

18. Manopubbaṅgamā dhammā, manoseṭṭhā manomayā; (Dhp 1 — Dhp p. 1)

    *mano-pubbaṅgamā* having mind as forerunner (m. nom. pl.) · *dhammā* states, things (m. nom. pl.) · *mano-seṭṭhā* having mind as chief (m. nom. pl.) · *mano-mayā* made of mind (m. nom. pl.)

    "Mind goes before all states; mind is their chief; they are made of mind."

    Two stem classes, and one of them is disguised. *Dhamma* is an ordinary masculine *a*-stem, nominative plural. The three long words are compound adjectives agreeing with it, each built on *manas*, and *manas* appears in composition as ***mano-***, its old nominative form pressed into service as a stem. So a reader meets *mano-* dozens of times in compounds and *manas-* almost never: the compounding form and the paradigm have parted company.

    *Manoseṭṭhā* also contains a superlative, *seṭṭha* 'best, chief', which is one of the irregular comparatives set out in the next chapter, not formed from any adjective it resembles.

19. Attanā hi kataṁ pāpaṁ, attanā saṅkilissati. (Dhp 165 — Dhp p. 24)

    *attanā* by oneself (m. inst. sg.) · *hi* indeed · *kataṁ* done (past participle, n. nom. sg.) · *pāpaṁ* evil (n. nom. sg.) · *attanā* by oneself (m. inst. sg.) · *saṅkilissati* one is defiled

    "Evil is done by oneself; by oneself one is defiled."

    *Attan* is an *-an* stem, and *attanā* is its instrumental singular: the agent of a passive construction in the first clause and the means in the second. *Pāpa* is a neuter *a*-stem, nominative singular, the subject of the participle *kataṁ*, which agrees with it. Two stem classes, and the whole verse rests on repeating one word in one case. Item 1 is the same stem in two other cases, four verses earlier in the same collection.

    The Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana's own apparatus records a variant here: the Sinhalese, Syāmese and Pali Text Society editions read *attanāva*, not *attanā hi*, and the printed PTS text has it so. Nothing in the grammar changes, and it is a fair illustration of the kind of variation the chapter on the alphabet described — real, recorded, and of no consequence for reading.

20. Ārocemi kho te, mahārāja, paṭivedemi kho te, mahārāja, adhivattati kho taṁ, mahārāja, jarāmaraṇaṁ. (SN 3.25 — S i 101)

    *ārocemi* I announce · *kho* indeed · *te* to you (dat./gen. sg.) · *mahārāja* great king (m. voc. sg.) · *paṭivedemi* I inform · *adhivattati* bears down, rolls in · *taṁ* upon you (acc. sg.) · *jarāmaraṇaṁ* old age and death (n. nom. sg.)

    "I announce it to you, great king; I inform you, great king: old age and death are bearing down upon you, great king."

    Three stem classes. *Mahārāja* is the *-an* stem *rājan* as the last member of a compound, in the vocative singular, and the vocative is *-a*, as in the simple word *rāja*, while the nominative of the compound is *mahārājā*. *Jarāmaraṇaṁ* is an *a*-stem, neuter nominative singular; it is a *dvanda* compound of two nouns, 'ageing and death', and like several such compounds it is treated as a single neuter singular, not as a plural. *Te* and *taṁ* are pronouns and belong to a later chapter; *te* here is the enclitic dative, 'to you'.

    The rhetorical shape matters, since this book's premise is that the grammar serves the reading. The vocative is repeated three times in one sentence, which ordinary Pāli does not do. This is the Pabbatūpama Sutta, the simile of the mountain: a trusted man arrives from the eastern direction reporting a mountain as high as the clouds advancing and crushing every living thing, and then another from each of the remaining directions. Old age and death are the fifth report. The grammar of address carries the insistence.
