21. *tena bhikkhunā* by that monk (m. instr. sg.) · *saṅghaṁ* the Sangha (m. acc. sg.) · *upasaṅkamitvā* having approached (absol.) · *ekaṁsaṁ* over one shoulder (m. acc. sg., adverbial) · *uttarāsaṅgaṁ* the upper robe (m. acc. sg.) · *karitvā* having arranged (absol.) · *vuḍḍhānaṁ bhikkhūnaṁ* of the senior monks (m. gen. pl.) · *pāde* the feet (m. acc. pl.) · *vanditvā* having paid homage (absol.) · *ukkuṭikaṁ* squatting (adverbial) · *nisīditvā* having sat (absol.) · *añjaliṁ* joined palms (m. acc. sg.) · *paggahetvā* having raised (absol.) · *evaṁ* thus · *assa* by him (m. gen./dat. sg.) · *vacanīyo* is to be said (m. nom. sg., fpp.)

    "That monk should approach the Sangha, arrange his upper robe over one shoulder, pay homage at the feet of the senior monks, sit down squatting, raise his joined palms, and say this."

    *Bhikkhunā* is the instrumental singular and *bhikkhūnaṁ* the genitive plural, both of the *u*-stem *bhikkhu*. The first is the agent of the whole sentence: a gerundive construction puts its agent in the instrumental, so the monk is nowhere in the nominative. The second is possessive: the feet belong to the senior monks. The stem vowel lengthens before the plural ending and not before the singular one, the difference to hold on to, since *bhikkhunā* and *bhikkhūnaṁ* are otherwise easy to confuse by eye. Said aloud they are different words, and that is the reason for saying them.

    *Pāde* is the **accusative plural** of the *a*-stem *pāda* 'foot', the object of *vanditvā*. It cannot be a locative singular, though the form is identical, because *vandati* takes an accusative: one pays homage *to* the feet, and Pāli treats that as a direct object, not as a place. The verb decides it, not the ending. That is the chapter's general rule in a real sentence: an ending narrows the possibilities and the context decides among them.

22. The four phrases, with the line each came from:

    (a) *attano nātho* (Dhp 160 — Dhp p. 24): *attano*, genitive singular of the *-an* stem *attan*; *nātho*, nominative singular of the masculine *a*-stem *nātha*. English needs three words and a possessive; Pāli needs two.

    (b) *cakkhunā* (SN 35.70 — S iv 41): instrumental singular of the neuter *u*-stem *cakkhu*. One word for "with the eye", and no preposition anywhere: the case *is* the preposition, the hardest habit to acquire in producing Pāli.

    (c) *āyusā* (Snp 1.8 — Sn p. 26): instrumental singular of *āyu*, in the older *-us* form. If you produced *āyunā*, you produced the canonical instrumental of the word and the wrong one for this line; the exercise is set to show that both exist.

    (d) *vuḍḍhānaṁ bhikkhūnaṁ pāde* (Bu Np 1 — Vin iii 196): genitive plural, genitive plural, accusative plural. The adjective *vuḍḍhānaṁ* agrees with its noun in case and number, so it looks like a second genitive plural and not like an adjective; and the whole phrase, three words, is what English needs six for.

    If (a) and (b) came out and (c) and (d) did not, that is the expected result, not a failure: a phrase of one word can be produced long before a phrase with agreement in it. Come back to (d) on day 30.

## Answers: Gender and Adjectives

**Sotānugata — met by ear.**

1. Dīghā jāgarato ratti, dīghaṁ santassa yojanaṁ; Dīgho bālānaṁ saṁsāro, saddhammaṁ avijānataṁ. (Dhp 60 — Dhp p. 9)

    The three forms in order are ***dīghā*, *dīghaṁ*, *dīgho***: feminine with *ratti*, neuter with *yojanaṁ*, masculine with *saṁsāro*. The stem *dīgha* is the same in all three, and only the ending moves. This is the chapter's whole claim in one verse, and a good line to have by heart for that reason as much as for its sense.

    Two smaller features of the verse. *Jāgarato* and *avijānataṁ* are **present participles** in *-(n)t*, genitive singular and genitive plural, the row of the feminine table whose strong stem gives *gacchantī*. Here they are masculine and so decline like *bhagavant*, on the weak stem: *jāgarat-*, *avijānat-*. And each of the three long things is long **for** somebody, in the genitive: *jāgarato*, *santassa*, *bālānaṁ*. The pattern is exact, and stage 1 asks mainly that you hear it.

    The three printed editions read *bālānaṁ* here, and the Mahāsaṅgīti prints *bālāna*. The Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana, the Buddha Jayanti and the Pali Text Society all keep the niggahīta, so it is what stands above. The short form is this same genitive plural with its final nasal dropped for the metre, a form altered to fit the line, which the metre chapter accounts for under metre as the cause and keeps apart from the three cases where the spelling and the sound have merely come apart. It is not a different case and the sense does not depend on it. But a search for the genitive plural under its usual spelling will miss it.

2. Seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, bālo abyatto akusalo sūdo … (SN 47.8 — S v 149)

    Seyyathāpi, bhikkhave, paṇḍito byatto kusalo sūdo … (SN 47.8 — S v 151)

    Six adjectives, one noun, and all six masculine nominative singular in *-o*, because *sūdo* is masculine nominative singular. Two of the three pairs are made with the privative *a-* (*byatto* / *abyatto*, *kusalo* / *akusalo*), and the third, *paṇḍito* / *bālo*, is two separate words. The sentence teaches both facts at once: Pāli negates a quality freely with a prefix, and then keeps a lexical opposite anyway where it has one.

    The second and the fifth are past participles. DPD parses *byatta* as the past participle of *byañjayati*, literally 'distinguished', and *abyatta* as the same with the negative in front. *Bāla*, *paṇḍita* and *kusala* are plain adjectives with no verb behind them. The surface does not show it (all six end in *-o* and all six agree the same way), and that is the point: a participle declines like an adjective and agrees like an adjective, and its history shows only in the dictionary. The same observation returns at item 20, on *nissaggiya* and *nissaṭṭha*, and at item 18 on *abhikkantataro*.

    Two points about the sentence itself. The two versions stand four sections apart in one sutta, which is how a great deal of the canon is built: a simile stated in the negative, then the same words in the positive. And *nānaccayehi sūpehi* is an ordinary adjective-plus-noun agreement in the instrumental plural, in the middle of a sentence about nominatives.

3. Tena kho pana samayena sukkā bhikkhunī mahatiyā parisāya parivutā dhammaṁ deseti. (SN 10.9 — S i 212)

    *Mahatiyā* and *parisāya* are the two that are not nominative. They are instrumental singular, as shown by their ending *-āya*, which the *ā*-declension and the feminine *ī*-declension use for the oblique cases and never for the nominative. A feminine nominative singular would be *parisā*. The other three (*sukkā*, *bhikkhunī*, *parivutā*) are nominative, and between them they carry the two feminine declensions the chapter's table produces: *sukkā* an *ā*-stem, *bhikkhunī* an *ī*-stem from the *-u* row.

    *Parivutā* is nominative and not instrumental because it describes the nun, not the assembly: she is surrounded. The instrumental *mahatiyā parisāya* is what she is surrounded *by*. The two stand next to each other and the agreement reaches past them, the point item 20 makes at greater length: agreement in Pāli is not a matter of adjacency, and pairing each word with its neighbour gets this sentence backwards.

    *Mahatiyā* is the feminine of *mahant*, the last row of the feminine table, and it is the same form as item 16 below. Meeting it in a sentence first and parsing it cold afterwards is the order this workbook is arranged in.

4. Yo pana bhikkhu … (Bu Np 1 — Vin iii 195) and Yā pana bhikkhunī … (Bi Np 1 — Vin iv 243)

    The two words that change are *yo* → *yā* and *bhikkhu* → *bhikkhunī*. The first is the relative pronoun taking the feminine; the second is the *-u* row of the feminine table, *bhikkhu* → *bhikkhunī*, in the sentence that put it into the language.

    The four words that close both rules did not have to move because they do not agree with the monk or the nun. *Nissaggiyaṁ pācittiyaṁ* is a separate verbless clause stating the penalty, and it agrees within itself: *nissaggiya* is a gerundive, 'to be relinquished', and it takes the neuter of *pācittiyaṁ*. Whose offence it is has nothing to do with what gender the offence is. This is item 20's lesson from the other side: there, four words that had to agree; here, two that did not.

    The offences themselves differ, on purpose: a surplus robe for the monks, a stash of bowls for the nuns. The two codes are two codes on one frame, not the same rules with the genders switched, and so the frame shows clearly when they are read side by side.

**Vacasā paricitā — rehearsed aloud.**

5. Tāvadeva chāyā metabbā, utuppamāṇaṁ ācikkhitabbaṁ, divasabhāgo ācikkhitabbo, saṅgīti ācikkhitabbā, cattāro nissayā ācikkhitabbā. (Kd 1 — Vin i 95)

    The four shapes of *ācikkhitabba* are **neuter singular** *ācikkhitabbaṁ* with *utuppamāṇaṁ*, **masculine singular** *ācikkhitabbo* with *divasabhāgo*, **feminine singular** *ācikkhitabbā* with *saṅgīti*, and **masculine plural** *ācikkhitabbā* with *cattāro nissayā*. If rebuilding from the gloss produced all four, the chapter has done its work; if it produced *ācikkhitabbaṁ* four times, the exercise has found what it was built to find.

    The two spelled alike are the third and the fourth. *Ācikkhitabbā* itself cannot decide between feminine nominative singular and masculine nominative plural; the two endings are simply the same. *Saṅgīti* on one side and *cattāro nissayā* on the other decide it, and both of those are unambiguous: *saṅgīti* is a feminine *i*-stem, and *cattāro* is a masculine plural numeral. So the noun settles the adjective here, having been settled *by* the adjective two clauses earlier: the chapter's remark that agreement runs in both directions, visible inside a single sentence.

    *Metabbā* at the front is the same construction on a different verb, feminine singular with *chāyā*. Five gerundives, five nouns, and not one of the gerundives could have been guessed from the verb alone.

6. Sahassamapi ce vācā, anatthapadasaṁhitā; Ekaṁ atthapadaṁ seyyo, yaṁ sutvā upasammati. (Dhp 100 — Dhp p. 15)

    According to the chapter's table of irregulars, the positive of *seyyo* is *sant* 'good', and its superlative is *seṭṭha*. Item 22 goes on to complicate that table; recite it in this form first.

    Two features of the verse to notice while it is being rebuilt. *Anatthapadasaṁhitā* agrees with *vācā*, feminine nominative plural: a whole compound serving as one adjective, the chapter on compounds arriving early. DPD carries *atthapada* 'a beneficial saying' as a compound of *attha* and *pada*, and the verse puts *saṁhitā* 'connected with' on the end of it and the privative *an-* on the front. And *seyyo* stands beside *ekaṁ atthapadaṁ*, neuter nominative singular.

    What *seyyo* itself is remains open, and the verse does not settle it. DPD registers it as an indeclinable, 'better, it is better', and the chapter's footnote records that these comparatives were historically *-yas*-stems, which would make *seyyo* a neuter nominative singular of the same form as *mano*, agreeing with *atthapadaṁ*. The two accounts give the same rendering, and the second explains why the form looks as it does. No choice is forced here, and it is better to know that than to have picked one.

**Manasānupekkhitā — gone over in the mind.**

**Give the feminine.**

7. *dāsa* → ***dāsī*** 'female servant'. A **noun** in *-a* takes *-ī*, which replaces the stem vowel. The result declines as a feminine *ī*-stem: *dāsiyā*, *dāsiyo*.

8. *dīgha* → ***dīghā*** 'long'. An **adjective** in *-a* takes *-ā*, and the result declines as an ordinary feminine *ā*-stem like *kaññā*. This row is the most easily missed, and the contrast with 7 explains it: the same stem ending, two different suffixes, sorted by whether the word is a noun or an adjective. Item 1 is this row in a verse — *dīghā ratti*.

9. *upāsaka* → ***upāsikā*** 'laywoman'. Stems in *-aka* change *-aka* to *-ikā*. Compare *sāvaka* → *sāvikā*, *kumāraka* → *kumārikā*. In practice this is the *-a* → *-ā* rule with a vowel change in front of it, but hold it separately, because *-aka* is such a productive suffix.

10. *hatthin* → ***hatthinī*** 'she-elephant'. Stems in *-in* add *-ī*, giving *-inī*. Compare *musāvādin* → *musāvādinī* 'a woman who lies'.

11. *bhikkhu* → ***bhikkhunī*** 'nun'. Stems in *-u* and *-ū* add *-nī*. Compare *abhijjhālū* → *abhijjhālunī* 'covetous'. Item 4 is this row carrying an entire second code of monastic law.

12. *mahant* → ***mahatī*** 'great'. An **adjective** in *-(n)t* adds *-ī* to the **weak** stem, the one without the nasal: *mahat-* gives *mahatī*, *sīlavant* gives *sīlavatī*. A **present participle** in *-(n)t* adds *-ī* to the **strong** stem instead: *gacchant-* gives *gacchantī* 'going'. The same ending attaches to different stems according to the word's function, the only complication in this table, and it is a real distinction, not a spelling variant.

What does the adjective tell you about its noun?

13. *kusalā* — three readings, in two genders. **Masculine nominative plural** (the noun is masculine, plural, and the subject); **feminine nominative singular** (the noun is feminine and singular); or neuter nominative or accusative plural, since the *a*-stem neuter has a rare plural in *-ā* beside *-āni*. So the ending narrows the noun to "not masculine singular, not any oblique case" and no further. The verb usually settles the number, and the noun itself the gender.

14. *dukkhaṁ* — neuter nominative or accusative singular, or **masculine accusative singular**. Note what this excludes: it cannot be feminine, and it cannot be plural or oblique. If the sentence has *hoti* or no verb at all, a neuter nominative is likeliest and the phrase is a predication — 'X is suffering'. If there is a transitive verb with another subject, it is an accusative.

15. *sīlavato* — masculine or neuter, genitive or dative singular, built on the weak stem *sīlavat-*. Two points follow. The noun is singular and is being possessed, or benefited, or is the person about whom something is said. And it is *not* feminine: a feminine would be *sīlavatiyā*. Item 1 has two forms of this kind, *jāgarato* and *santassa*, with the second of those functions.

16. *mahatiyā* — **feminine singular**, and then the same five-way ambiguity every feminine *ī*-stem oblique has: instrumental, ablative, genitive, dative or locative. The adjective has given the gender and the number but not the case. Compare 15, where the *-(n)t* stem in the masculine narrows the case to two: the feminine of this class, having gone over to the *ī*-declension, inherits that declension's ambiguity along with its endings. In item 3 it is the noun *parisāya* and the verb that settle it as instrumental.

**Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — penetrated by view.**

**Read the line.**

17. Maggānaṭṭhaṅgiko seṭṭho, saccānaṁ caturo padā. (Dhp 273 — Dhp p. 40)

    *maggānaṁ* of paths (m. gen. pl.) · *aṭṭhaṅgiko* the eightfold (m. nom. sg.) · *seṭṭho* best (m. nom. sg.) · *saccānaṁ* of truths (n. gen. pl.) · *caturo* four · *padā* sayings, statements (n. nom. pl.)

    "Of paths the eightfold is best; of truths, the four sayings."

    Learn *seṭṭho* as vocabulary; do not derive it: it is irregular, paired with the comparative *seyya*, and it is not formed from any adjective it resembles. It agrees with *aṭṭhaṅgiko*, masculine nominative singular, which is itself an adjective ('having eight factors') standing as a noun: the chapter's point about the thin line between the two.

    The construction of the comparison is not what a reader schooled on English would expect. There is no word for 'than'. The field within which something is best goes into the **genitive plural** (*maggānaṁ*, 'among paths'), and the superlative simply agrees with its own noun. The full verse does it four times over: *dhammānaṁ*, *dvipadānaṁ*.

    Two smaller points. *Padā* is a **neuter** nominative plural in *-ā*, the alternative to *padāni* that Appendix A.1 records for this class; DPD cites this very line under *pada* in the sense 'word, phrase, saying'. And *caturo* is the form the numeral chapter gives as **masculine** nominative and accusative, standing here with a neuter noun. Verse does not always hold strictly to gender agreement, and this looks like an instance; it is hard to be certain, and the sense does not depend on it.

18. Atthi kho, bhikkhu, sakko nāma devānamindo amhehi abhikkantataro ca paṇītataro ca. (DN 11 — D i 216)

    *atthi* there is · *kho* indeed · *bhikkhu* mendicant (m. voc. sg.) · *sakko* Sakka (m. nom. sg.) · *nāma* by name · *devānaṁ* of the gods (m. gen. pl.) · *indo* lord (m. nom. sg.) · *amhehi* than us (inst./abl. pl.) · *abhikkantataro* more excellent (m. nom. sg.) · *ca* and · *paṇītataro* more sublime (m. nom. sg.) · *ca* and

    "There is, mendicant, Sakka, lord of gods by name, more excellent and more sublime than we are."

    Two comparatives in *-tara*, both masculine nominative singular, agreeing with *sakko*. Both are formed the ordinary productive way, and *abhikkantataro* is the type the chapter noted as built on a **past participle**: *abhikkanta* 'surpassing, excellent' is itself the past participle of *abhikkamati*. It is the observation item 2 made of *byatto*, one step further along: there a participle standing as an adjective, here a participle standing as an adjective and then being compared.

    Here the standard of comparison is expressed, by *amhehi*, and that is the case-syntax question the chapter deferred. The form is ambiguous: instrumental and ablative plural fell together in Pāli and share one ending, so *amhehi* is either. On the ablative reading it is 'from us', the ordinary Indo-European construction for comparison, and that is the likelier account; but the form itself does not decide, and this is a good instance of a place where morphology hands the question to syntax and stops.

19. Cakkhuṁ, bhikkhave, dukkhaṁ. … jivhā dukkhā … kāyo dukkho … mano dukkho. (SN 35.2 — S iv 2)

    *cakkhuṁ* the eye (n. nom. sg.) · *bhikkhave* mendicants (m. voc. pl.) · *dukkhaṁ* suffering (n. nom. sg.) · *jivhā* the tongue (f. nom. sg.) · *dukkhā* (f. nom. sg.) · *kāyo* the body (m. nom. sg.) · *dukkho* (m. nom. sg.) · *mano* the mind (nom. sg.) · *dukkho* (m. nom. sg.)

    "The eye, mendicants, is suffering … the tongue is suffering … the body is suffering … the mind is suffering."

    One adjective, *dukkha*, taking three genders from three nouns without any change to its stem. This is agreement at its plainest, and the sentence proves the chapter's claim that the adjective has no gender of its own: the same four letters are neuter, feminine and masculine in one paragraph. Item 1 does the same thing in verse and in one line; this does it in prose and across a whole sutta opening.

    The last clause is the interesting one. *Mano* is an *-as* stem, historically neuter, and a neuter would take *dukkhaṁ*. It takes *dukkho*, masculine. That is the migration described in the chapter on nouns, in progress: a word whose nominative singular looks like a masculine *a*-stem nominative has attracted masculine agreement, and the canon itself supplies the evidence. Told flatly that the *-as* stems are neuter, you would have to call this an error. It is the language moving, not an error.

**Agreement across a long sentence.**

20. *idaṁ* this (nt. nom. sg.) · *cīvaraṁ* robe-cloth (nt. nom. sg.) · *itthannāmassa* of such-and-such a name (m. gen. sg.) · *bhikkhuno* of a monk (m. gen. sg.) · *nissaggiyaṁ* to be relinquished (nt. nom. sg.) · *āyasmantānaṁ* to the venerables (m. dat. pl.) · *nissaṭṭhaṁ* has been relinquished (nt. nom. sg., p.p.)

    "This robe-cloth, to be relinquished by the monk so-and-so, has been relinquished to the venerables."

    The four that agree are *idaṁ*, *cīvaraṁ*, *nissaggiyaṁ* and *nissaṭṭhaṁ*, all neuter nominative singular. Two of them are not adjectives to begin with: *nissaggiya* is a gerundive and *nissaṭṭha* a past participle, and both decline like adjectives and agree like adjectives, so they can sit at either end of the sentence and still be found.

    The two that do not agree are *itthannāmassa bhikkhuno* and *āyasmantānaṁ*, because they do not describe the cloth. The first is a genitive naming whose the offence was, the second a dative naming who now receives; both are masculine, and one is plural. They stand between the cloth and the participle that describes it without disturbing the agreement, and that is the point to notice: agreement in Pāli is not a matter of adjacency.

    *Cīvara*, which is neuter, fixes the gender of the four. No other word in the sentence could: *bhikkhu* and *āyasmant* are both masculine, and if agreement worked by proximity the last word would have followed them.

**Put it back into Pāli.**

21. (a) ***Dīghā … ratti*** (Dhp 60 — Dhp p. 9): *dīghā* feminine nominative singular, agreeing with *ratti*, a feminine *i*-stem nominative singular. The trap is *dīghaṁ*, which is the form the verse uses one *pāda* later and with a different noun.

    (b) ***mahatiyā parisāya parivutā*** (SN 10.9 — S i 212): *mahatiyā* and *parisāya* feminine instrumental singular, *parivutā* feminine nominative singular. Three feminines and two cases, and the case boundary falls where the sense does.

    (c) ***saṅgīti ācikkhitabbā*** (Kd 1 — Vin i 95): both feminine nominative singular. If this came out as *ācikkhitabbaṁ*, the neuter has been taken as a default, which it is not.

    (d) ***Yā pana bhikkhunī pattasannicayaṁ kareyya*** (Bi Np 1 — Vin iv 243): *yā* and *bhikkhunī* feminine nominative singular, *pattasannicayaṁ* masculine accusative singular, *kareyya* third person singular optative. Note that the object is masculine and agrees with no other word; only the two feminines are in step.

    Producing these is a different skill from parsing them, and the failure it exposes is a particular one: the masculine and the neuter come to hand and the feminine does not. If (c) and (d) were the hard ones, the table in 7–12 is recitable and not yet usable, and day 30 is where that gets fixed.

Where the ending is not the word you think it is.

22. They are two different words that have fallen together in this case, and the table's *sant* is not the word in the verse.

    DPD carries both. The *sant* of the irregular table is the present participle of *atthi* 'to be' (*sa* + *nta*, from √*as*), which as a masculine noun means 'a good person, a virtuous man' and as an adjective 'true, real'. The *santa* of *dīghaṁ santassa yojanaṁ* is the past participle of *sammati*, √*sam* + *ta*, literally 'calmed' and in this sense 'tired, wearied'. DPD gives Dhp 60 itself as its illustration of that sense. Two roots, two formations, one genitive singular.

    And the other word is in the same verse. DPD builds *saddhamma* 'the true teaching' out of *sat* and *dhamma*, and that *sat* is the participle of *atthi*: the table's word, in a compound, one *pāda* below the *santassa* that is not it. Dhp 60 carries both, three words apart, and prints them so differently that nobody would look twice.

    Had the table's word been read into the verse, the line would have come out as "long is the league for the good man", which is grammatical, scans, and is wrong. That is the nature of the error: a form parsed correctly and assigned to the wrong word, not a form the reader cannot parse. The ending will never warn you. Only the dictionary does, and only the sense not quite fitting sends you to the dictionary.

    A note for anyone who looks *seyya* up while doing this. The chapter's table pairs *sant* → *seyya* → *seṭṭha*, which is the traditional grammarians' arrangement and records which positive the comparative does duty for. DPD's entry instead derives *seyya* from *sirī* 'glory, fortune', literally 'luckier', matching Sanskrit *śreyas*. These answer different questions. The table is about a suppletion in the living language (as English pairs *good* with *better*, which is not built from it either), and the dictionary is about where the form came from. Both can be held at once, and noticing the difference means reading the table correctly, not doubting it.

## Answers: Pronouns

**Sotānugata — met by ear.**

1. Tvaṁ, samma medakathālike, mamaṁ rakkha, ahaṁ taṁ rakkhissāmi. (SN 47.19 — S v 168)

    First person: *mamaṁ* and *ahaṁ*. Second person: *tvaṁ* and *taṁ*. They stand in the sentence as *you, me, I, you*, so the line is easier to hold as a rhythm than as a list.

    The two accusatives look least like their own nominatives, and that is a fact about both paradigms, not an accident of this sentence. *Ahaṁ* gives *maṁ* or *mamaṁ*; *tvaṁ* gives *taṁ*, *tvaṁ* or *tuvaṁ*. Read the two tables down their columns and the pattern is plain: the nominative singular shares nothing with the obliques beside it, the singular obliques run on *ma-* and *ta-*, and the plural runs on *amha-* and *tumha-* throughout. Three stems to a paradigm, and only the nominative cannot be predicted from the rest.

    The acrobat is speaking to his assistant on the pole, and *taṁ* here is the difficulty the chapter opens with. It is the second person accusative singular, "you"; it is also the third person and demonstrative accusative singular, "him, her, it". Nothing in the form separates them. *Ahaṁ* settles it: a first-person speaker addressing someone directly, in a sentence whose other half is *tvaṁ … mamaṁ*, is not switching to a third party in the last two words.

2. Evaṁ me sutaṁ. (DN 1 — D i 1)

    ***Me* is instrumental**, first person singular, and the sentence is literally "thus [it was] heard by me". There is no finite verb in it at all: DPD parses *sutaṁ* as the past participle of *suṇāti*, and gives this very sentence as its illustration.

    The commentary declines to settle it. Buddhaghosa separates three senses in which *me* is met (instrumental, dative and genitive) and then says of this place that both "heard by me" and "my hearing" fit. So the standard English rendering takes a side the tradition does not. The enclitics' second property thus appears in the first sentence a reader ever meets: *me* has spread beyond the case range it had in Sanskrit, and an enclitic is often ambiguous by nature, not by accident.

    That the three commonest words in the canon contain an unsettled parse, and that most readers pass over them for years without noticing, is the reason this item is in stage 1 and not stage 4.

3. Ayaṁ te patto, ayaṁ saṅghāṭi, ayaṁ uttarāsaṅgo, ayaṁ antaravāsako. (Kd 1 — Vin i 94)

    ***Saṅghāṭi* is the odd one out: it is feminine**, and the other three are masculine. DPD gives *saṅghāṭi* as a feminine *i*-stem from *saṅghaṭeti*, 'joined together', and *patta*, *uttarāsaṅga* and *antaravāsaka* as masculine.

    The item is set for what this shows about *ayaṁ*. The nominative singular of *ima-* is *ayaṁ* in the masculine and *ayaṁ* in the feminine alike; only the neuter differs, as *idaṁ*. So this sentence agrees a single form with two genders four times over, and if you have learnt *ayaṁ* as "the masculine one" you will misgender the robe. The chapter's table and Appendix A.2 both print the two cells identically, which is easy to read past and hard to forget once a sentence has made use of it.

    *Te* is the second person enclitic, genitive or dative singular. It stands second in its clause, which is where an enclitic has to stand, and it is not repeated in the three clauses that follow: Pāli says "your" once and lets it govern the list.

4. Ko āpanno, kiṁ āpanno, kismiṁ āpanno, kathaṁ āpanno, kaṁ bhaṇatha, kiṁ bhaṇathā'ti? (Bu Pc 12 — Vin iv 35)

    *Kathaṁ* is not a pronoun. It is an interrogative adverb, 'how', the only word in the series that does not decline and does not stand for a noun. The chapter footnotes it for this reason: in a run of five interrogatives it is invisible unless someone points at it.

    *Kismiṁ* is built on the second stem. Beside *ka-* the interrogative has a stem *ki-*, and three oblique cases admit forms built on it: ablative *kismā*, genitive *kissa*, locative *kismiṁ* and *kimhi*. These are the same pronoun in its other stem, not a separate set to be learnt, and so item 11 below can be worked out instead of memorised.

    The passage is a monk under cross-examination, and the six questions run through subject, object, location, manner, and then object twice more. Three cases are represented: *ko* nominative, *kismiṁ* locative, and *kiṁ* and *kaṁ* accusative. Look at the two accusatives at the end: *kaṁ* is masculine and asks about a person, *kiṁ* is neuter and asks about a thing, and one letter makes the whole difference.

**Vacasā paricitā — rehearsed aloud.**

5. Nagaraṁ yathā paccantaṁ, guttaṁ santarabāhiraṁ; Evaṁ gopetha attānaṁ, khaṇo vo mā upaccagā. (Dhp 315 — Dhp p. 45)

    *Attānaṁ* is singular and *gopetha* is second person plural. This is neither a mismatch nor a licence of the verse, for the reflexive has no number of its own to contribute. *Attā* serves all three persons and both numbers, taking both from the verb, so English must render three words of Pāli, *evaṁ gopetha attānaṁ*, with a "yourselves" that Pāli never says. To make *attānaṁ* agree with *gopetha* is to look for a plural that does not exist.

    *Vo* could not have stood at the head of its clause. An enclitic is never the first word of its sentence, without exception, and that positional rule makes several of the ambiguities in this set decidable at a glance. Here *vo* is second, leaning on *khaṇo*, where an enclitic belongs.

    The verse is also the chapter's cleanest instance of the reflexive used plainly as a reflexive (the object of *gopetha* is the subject of *gopetha*), against the emphatic instrumental *attanā* of item 10, which is used differently.

6. Yo vo, ānanda, mayā dhammo ca vinayo ca desito paññatto, so vo mamaccayena satthā. (DN 16 — D ii 154)

    Five pronouns, four cases: ***yo*** and ***so*** nominative singular masculine, the relative and its correlative; ***mayā*** first person instrumental singular; ***mama*** first person genitive singular, inside the compound *mamaccayena*; and ***vo*** twice, the second person enclitic.

    What an English translation of *vo* throws away is the case. It is a dative, and Wijesekera's name for it is the *sympathetic dative*: the person with a stake in the action, not the person who owns a thing. So *so vo … satthā* means "the Teacher for you, in your interest, now that I am going", not "the Teacher belonging to you". English has only "your", which flattens a dative into a possessive and loses the whole sense of the case.

    And the sentence has both halves of the rule in one clause. The division Wijesekera supplies is that the dative is used with the first and second person pronouns and the genitive with everything else: here *vo* dative for the second person, *mama* genitive inside *mamaccayena*, ten words apart. The chapter records that neither source states both halves and that the two were put together for this book, so treat it as a well-attested tendency, not a rule without exceptions.

    If the sentence was rebuilt with one *vo* instead of two, that is the ordinary failure; repeat the sentence until it is not. The first *vo* belongs to the relative clause and the second to the main clause, and Pāli does not carry it across.

**Manasānupekkhitā — gone over in the mind.**

**Identify the form.**

7. *amūsaṁ* — stem *asu*/*amu-*, the remote demonstrative "that one yonder". Genitive/dative plural, and the same form serves all three genders. Note that the singular of this pronoun keeps traces of an older stem in the *ad-* forms, *aduṁ* and *adussa*, while the plural is built on *amu-* throughout, so the plural looks more regular than the singular.

8. *tuyhaṁ* — stem *tumha-*, the second person. Genitive or dative singular, "your, to you"; the second person does not inflect for gender. Its enclitic counterpart is *te*, which stands in the same two cases and several more besides — as item 3 above shows it doing.

9. *enaṁ* — stem *ena-*, the anaphoric. Accusative singular, all three genders, and the anaphoric has no other singular case and no nominative at all. Distinguish it from *eta-*, the proximal demonstrative it was built to: *enaṁ* points back at what has already been named, *etaṁ* points out at what is present.

10. *attanā* — the noun *attā* "self", serving as the reflexive. Instrumental singular, and also ablative singular; the person and number come from the verb, not from the pronoun, so this one form can mean "by myself", "by yourself", "by himself" or "by themselves". In the instrumental it very often carries the emphatic sense "personally, of one's own doing".

11. *kismā* — stem *ka-*/*ki-*, the interrogative. Ablative singular, masculine and neuter, "from what?". It is built on the second stem *ki-*, not *ka-*, the same alternation that gives *kissa* in the genitive and the *kismiṁ* heard in item 4.

**Resolve the ambiguity.**

12. *taṁ* — either the second person accusative singular, "you", of *tumha-*; or the third person and demonstrative accusative singular, "him, her, it, that", of *ta(d)*. Both are common and the form does not separate them. The person of the verb and of the other pronouns in the clause decides: a first-person verb with an *ahaṁ* beside it points to a second-person object, as in item 1.

13. *no* — either the first person plural enclitic, "us, our, by us"; or the negative particle *no*, "not". Position settles it, because an enclitic is never the first word of its sentence. A sentence-initial *no* is the negative; anywhere else, weigh the sense.

14. *assa* — either the genitive or dative singular of *ima-*/*ayaṁ*, "of this, to this"; or the third person singular optative of *atthi*, "it would be". Here the test is syntactic, not positional: as a pronoun it will have a noun or participle depending on it, and as a verb it will be the only finite verb in its clause.

**Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — penetrated by view.**

**Read the sentence.**

15. Sabbe tasanti daṇḍassa, sabbesaṁ jīvitaṁ piyaṁ. (Dhp 130 — Dhp p. 19)

    *sabbe* all (nom. pl. m.) · *tasanti* they fear · *daṇḍassa* the rod (gen.) · *sabbesaṁ* to all (gen./dat. pl. m.) · *jīvitaṁ* life · *piyaṁ* dear

    "All tremble at the rod; to all, life is dear."

    Both pronouns are the pronominal adjective *sabba*, and the verse displays two of the three diagnostics in one line: *sabbe* in the nominative plural masculine, where a noun would give *-ā*, and *sabbesaṁ* in the genitive plural, where a noun would give *-ānaṁ*. There is no personal or demonstrative pronoun in the verse at all, and none is needed: the verb carries its own subject.

16. Ko imaṁ pathaviṁ vicessati, yamalokañca imaṁ sadevakaṁ. (Dhp 44 — Dhp p. 7)

    *ko* who (nom. sg. m.) · *imaṁ* this (acc. sg. f.) · *pathaviṁ* earth · *vicessati* will discern · *yamalokañca* < *yamalokaṁ* the world of Yama + *ca* and · *imaṁ* this (acc. sg. m.) · *sadevakaṁ* with its gods

    "Who will discern this earth, and this world of Yama with its gods?"

    Two classes here. *Ko* is the interrogative, nominative singular masculine of *ka-*. *Imaṁ* is the proximal demonstrative, and it occurs twice in different genders, feminine with *pathaviṁ* and masculine with *yamalokaṁ*. The accusative singular of *ima-* is *imaṁ* in the masculine and the feminine alike, and only the noun it agrees with tells them apart. That is the same collapse item 3 found in the nominative, one case further along.

17. Tumhehi kiccamātappaṁ, akkhātāro tathāgatā. (Dhp 276 — Dhp p. 40)

    *tumhehi* by you (2nd instr. pl.) · *kiccamātappaṁ* < *kiccaṁ* to be done + *ātappaṁ* the effort · *akkhātāro* those who tell, proclaimers · *tathāgatā* the Realised Ones

    "The effort is to be made by you; the Realised Ones are those who tell of it."

    *Tumhehi* is the second person instrumental plural, the full form and not the enclitic *vo*. That matters, because the enclitic could not stand at the head of the sentence and the full form can. The instrumental names the agent with a gerundive, *kiccaṁ*: the effort is a thing to be done, and *tumhehi* names who must do it. The line places the pronoun first for that emphasis.

18. Tato naṁ dukkhamanveti, cakkaṁva vahato padaṁ. (Dhp 1 — Dhp p. 1)

    *tato* thereupon · *naṁ* him (acc. sg., any gender) · *dukkhamanveti* < *dukkhaṁ* suffering + *anveti* follows · *cakkaṁva* < *cakkaṁ* wheel + *va* like · *vahato* of the draught ox (gen. sg.) · *padaṁ* foot

    "Then suffering follows him, as the wheel follows the foot of the ox that draws it."

    *Naṁ* is the anaphoric, accusative singular; *tato* is the ablative singular of *ta(d)* used adverbially, "from that, thereupon", one of a whole family of adverbial uses the oblique cases of *ta(d)* have: *tasmā* "therefore", *tahiṁ* "there", *yena … tena* "where … there". The verse was quoted in the chapter, and it is set again here on purpose: it is the first verse of the Dhammapada, and most readers will have known it by heart for years without ever having parsed the two pronouns in it.

A demonstrative welded to a personal pronoun.

19. *imāhaṁ* < *imaṁ* this (nt. acc. sg.) + *ahaṁ* I (nom. sg.) · *saṅghassa* to the Sangha (m. dat. sg.) · *nissajjāmi* I relinquish (1 sg. pres.)

    "I relinquish this to the Sangha."

    Two pronouns of different kinds, in different cases, doing different jobs, printed as one word. *Imaṁ* is the proximal demonstrative in the neuter accusative, agreeing with the *cīvaraṁ* named in the sentence before; it is the object. *Ahaṁ* is the first person nominative and it is the subject. The junction is the ordinary elision: the niggahīta of *imaṁ* goes and the *a* of *ahaṁ* is absorbed into the preceding vowel, which lengthens.

    The surface does not show that the word contains a subject and an object. The clue is that the sentence otherwise has neither, and a first person verb with no nominative in sight is the signal to look inside the long word at the front.

    The verb will not be found in the shape given here. *Nissajjāmi* has zero root-text occurrences as a word standing alone. The edition has *nissajjāmīti*, with 26 root-text occurrences: the quotative lengthens the final vowel, and this verb happens never to be used except inside something a monk is quoted as saying.

    The half-resolved *nissajjāmī* has 22 root-text occurrences of its own, the *ti* separated off by an editor, the lengthening left standing. A form can be common and still be absent from every search that spells it correctly.

Four ways of saying "self", in four lines.

20. Yo attano sīlavatāni jantu, anānupuṭṭhova paresa pāva; Anariyadhammaṁ kusalā tamāhu, yo ātumānaṁ sayameva pāva. (Snp 4.3 — Sn p. 153)

    *yo* who (nom. sg. m.) · *attano* his own (gen. sg.) · *sīlavatāni* precepts and practices · *jantu* person · *anānupuṭṭhova* < *anānupuṭṭho* unasked · *va* just · *paresa* to others (dat. pl. of *para*) · *pāva* told · *anariyadhammaṁ* an ignoble nature · *kusalā* the skilled · *tamāhu* < *taṁ* that (acc. sg.) + *āhu* they call · *ātumānaṁ* himself (acc. sg.) · *sayameva* < *sayaṁ* of his own accord · *eva* just

    "The person who tells others of his own precepts and practices unasked — the skilled call that an ignoble nature, in one who speaks of himself unprompted."

    **The three reflexive expressions are *attano*, *ātumānaṁ* and *sayaṁ***, and they stand within four lines of one another in a verse about the vice of talking about oneself. *Attano* is the genitive of *attā*, the ordinary reflexive. *Ātumānaṁ* is the accusative of *ātuma*, the same Sanskrit *ātman* arrived by a different route, and rare enough that Duroiselle calls it "very rarely used".

    *Sayaṁ* alone does not decline. It is an indeclinable, 'oneself, of one's own accord', and it agrees with nothing and carries no case; it simply intensifies. DPD derives it from *sa*, which puts it with the possessive *saka-* and not with *attā*. So the verse holds two reflexives from one family and one from another, and only the dictionary shows which is which.

    The fourth pronoun is *paresa*, and its ending is a pronominal one. *Para* is a pronominal adjective, so its genitive and dative plural is *paresaṁ*, where a noun of the same stem class would give *-ānaṁ*: the second of the three diagnostics, and the same ending as *sabbesaṁ* in item 15. The verse has *paresa*, the same form with its niggahīta dropped, one of the standard metrical accommodations. So one short word carries a pronominal ending and a verse-final shortening on top of it. The editions do not all agree here, and the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana's own apparatus records *parassa* against the siglum *ka.*, 'in some manuscripts'.

    This is the hardest reading in the set because nothing in it is irregular. Every form is what the chapter's tables predict. The difficulty is density, four pronouns of three kinds in four lines, two of them rare, and no table can prepare you for density.

**Put it back into Pāli.**

21. (a) ***ahaṁ taṁ rakkhissāmi*** (SN 47.19 — S v 168): *ahaṁ* first person nominative singular, *taṁ* second person accusative singular. If this came out as *maṁ* or *mamaṁ*, the two persons have been crossed; if the pronouns were left out altogether on the grounds that the verb carries its person, the emphasis of the line has been lost with them.

    (b) ***Evaṁ me sutaṁ*** (DN 1 — D i 1): *me* first person singular, instrumental here and, on the commentary's reading, possibly genitive. Three words, and only the one nobody looks at can go wrong.

    (c) ***Ayaṁ te patto*** (Kd 1 — Vin i 94): *ayaṁ* nominative singular of *ima-*, *te* second person enclitic in the genitive or dative singular. The trap is word order: *te* is second and cannot be first.

    (d) ***Evaṁ gopetha attānaṁ*** (Dhp 315 — Dhp p. 45): *attānaṁ* accusative **singular**, against a plural imperative. A plural here is the natural mistake, and this item exists to provoke it.

    The failure this stage exposes is a particular one. Enclitics are recognised effortlessly and produced badly, because recognising one asks only what it means and producing one asks where it may stand. If (c) came out with *te* at the head, the positional rule is not yet yours, and the error is more than a slip of memory.

## Answers: Numerals

**Sotānugata — met by ear.**

1. Ekaṁ samayaṁ bhagavā rājagahe viharati veḷuvane kalandakanivāpe. (SN 10.9 — S i 212)

    This is the agreement construction, and it is the plainest instance of it in the language. *Eka* is a numeral from one to eighteen, so it may behave as an ordinary adjective; *ekaṁ* and *samayaṁ* are both masculine accusative singular, and they agree in case, number and gender.

    Whether it counts anything is another question, and here it barely does. English says "at one time" and means "once, on an occasion", and Pāli does the same. A numeral in the agreement construction is an adjective, and an adjective meaning 'one' shades into an article without any change of form. The chapter's four constructions are about syntax, not arithmetic, and this item makes the difference visible: nothing in the sentence shows *ekaṁ* used numerically, because it is not.

    That is also why it is here. Almost every sutta a reader opens begins with this phrase, so the first numeral construction anyone meets is met several hundred times before it is ever named.

2. Tena kho pana samayena aññatitthiyā paribbājakā cātuddase pannarase aṭṭhamiyā ca pakkhassa sannipatitvā dhammaṁ bhāsanti. (Kd 2 — Vin i 101)

    *Aṭṭhamiyā* is the odd one out. It is a feminine *ī*-stem in an oblique case, the same *-iyā* ending item 10 meets on *vīsatiyā*, while *cātuddase* and *pannarase* are locatives of a stem that is not feminine. DPD carries *cātuddasa* as a masculine (with an adjective and a neuter beside it) and *pannarasa* as a masculine, and registers *pannarase* separately as an adverb, "on the fifteenth day of the lunar fortnight", locative singular.

    So three consecutive days of one fortnight are named by two different declensions in a single sentence, and no rule lies behind it: these are three words with three histories that happen to have converged on one use. Item 19 sets this passage beside the one the chapter quotes and asks what the pair together settle.

    The sentence matters beyond its grammar. The wanderers of other sects were already gathering on these three days and drawing crowds; the Buddha's fortnightly observance was instituted in answer to it, and so the *uposatha* enters the canon here, in a sentence about someone else's calendar.

3. Abhivādanasīlissa, niccaṁ vuḍḍhāpacāyino; Cattāro dhammā vaḍḍhanti, āyu vaṇṇo sukhaṁ balaṁ. (Dhp 109 — Dhp p. 16)

    The four are *āyu*, *vaṇṇo*, *sukhaṁ*, *balaṁ*: long life, beauty, happiness, strength. Notice that they are not all of one gender and that this does not matter at all: the numeral agrees with *dhammā*, the word for "things", and the four items are then simply listed after it in apposition.

    ***Cattāro dhammā* is agreement**, masculine nominative plural on both words. Compare item 1, which is the same construction in the singular and in a phrase nobody thinks of as counting; and compare item 4, where the numeral is high and the construction collapses. Between the two of them they mark the boundary the chapter draws at eighteen.

    The two genitives in the first two lines agree with each other and with no other word in the verse: *abhivādanasīlissa* and *vuḍḍhāpacāyino*, both masculine genitive singular, naming the person for whom the four things increase. DPD gives Dhp 109 itself as its illustration for both words.

4. Tasmiṁ sahassadhā loke sahassaṁ candānaṁ sahassaṁ sūriyānaṁ … cattāri mahāsamuddasahassāni … sahassaṁ brahmalokānaṁ. (AN 10.29 — A v 59)

    The construction changes at *cattāri mahāsamuddasahassāni*, and it had to. Everywhere else in the passage *sahassaṁ* is a neuter noun in the nominative singular with what it counts standing beside it in the genitive plural ("a thousand of moons", "a thousand of suns"), and nothing agrees with anything. But *four* thousand cannot be said that way. *Sahassa* itself now has to be counted, so it goes into the plural, *sahassāni*; *cattāri* agrees with it, neuter nominative plural; and the thing counted, having lost its place, moves inside the word as the first member of a compound, *mahāsamudda-sahassāni*.

    Three of the four constructions in one sentence, then: the genitive fourteen times, then a compound and an agreement working together to say a number the genitive cannot reach. It shows well that the four are solutions to different problems, not four styles a writer may choose between.

    *Sahassadhā* at the front is a fifth thing and belongs to none of the four: it is a distributive adverb in *-dhā*, indeclinable, "in a thousand ways, thousandfold". It describes the world and counts nothing in it.

**Vacasā paricitā — rehearsed aloud.**

5. Niṭṭhitacīvarasmiṁ bhikkhunā ubbhatasmiṁ kathine dasāhaparamaṁ atirekacīvaraṁ dhāretabbaṁ. Taṁ atikkāmayato nissaggiyaṁ pācittiyan'ti. (Bu Np 1 — Vin iii 196)

    *Dasāhaparamaṁ* has three parts and is in the accusative singular. DPD resolves it as *dasa* + *aha* + *parama* + *aṁ*: ten, day, at-most, and then the ending. So the word is a compound of a numeral with a noun and an adjective, declined as one word: the compound construction, as in item 13's *bhikkhusahassena*, but with an extra member on the end.

    The interesting half is why an accusative is translated "for ten days at most". It is the object of nothing: *dhāretabbaṁ* already has its object in *atirekacīvaraṁ*. This is the accusative used adverbially, the device the chapter on adjectives set out (*dīghaṁ* 'at length', *niccaṁ* 'constantly', *sādhukaṁ* 'well'), and DPD registers *dasāhaparamaṁ* as an indeclinable adverb for that reason. The case is frozen and no longer functions as a case. Look for what the accusative is the object of and you will not find it, and the chapter on indeclinables explains why.

    Two other words deserve a moment. *Ubbhatasmiṁ kathine* is a locative absolute, "the kathina having been withdrawn", and *atikkāmayato* is a present participle in the genitive, "for one exceeding [it]", which is the genitive of the person to whom a rule applies. Neither is a numeral, and both make this rule harder to rebuild than its length suggests.

6. Māse māse sahassena, yo yajetha sataṁ samaṁ; Ekañca bhāvitattānaṁ, muhuttamapi pūjaye; Sāyeva pūjanā seyyo, yañce vassasataṁ hutaṁ. (Dhp 106 — Dhp p. 16)

    Six numeral expressions, and the six are:

    - ***māse māse***: the **distributive**, formed by saying the word twice. "Month by month, every month."
    - ***sahassena***: a higher numeral as a noun, in the instrumental: the means by which the sacrifice is made.
    - ***sataṁ samaṁ***: the higher numeral again, and an accusative of duration. *Samaṁ* is a **singular** feminine accusative, *samā* 'year': a hundred years, with the noun in the singular, because from nineteen upwards what is counted takes no plural.
    - ***ekaṁ***: the low numeral as an ordinary adjective, agreeing with *bhāvitattānaṁ*, masculine accusative singular. The agreement construction, and the only one in the verse.
    - ***muhuttaṁ***: not a numeral at all, included because the whole comparison rests on it: the shortest span the language has a word for, set against a hundred years. DPD registers it as it registers *dasāhaparamaṁ* in item 5, an indeclinable adverb, accusative singular of *muhutta*, so the two words the verse and the Vinaya rule are each built around are the same construction, one measuring ten days and the other a moment.
    - ***vassasataṁ***: a **compound**, *vassa* + *sata*, declined as one neuter word. The same hundred years as in line 2, said the other way, in the same verse.

    The item's real lesson is that the same hundred years is said twice by two different constructions, forty words apart. *Sataṁ samaṁ* and *vassasataṁ* differ in neither quantity nor register: two of the four constructions with an identical function, chosen by what the metre and the line needed. Taught that each construction means something distinct, you will look for a difference here, and there is none to find.

**Manasānupekkhitā — gone over in the mind.**

**Identify the form.**

7. *tiṇṇannaṁ* — the cardinal *ti* "three". Genitive/dative plural, masculine and neuter. It is asked about here because it carries the ending twice over: the regular form is *tiṇṇaṁ*, and *tiṇṇannaṁ* has had the *-annaṁ* of the *pañca* class added on top of a genitive that was already complete. It is an analogy, not an inheritance: the numeral has been reanalysed as though it belonged with *pañcannaṁ*, *channaṁ*, *sattannaṁ*, one class up.

8. *ubhinnaṁ* — *ubha* "both". Genitive/dative, and the number is where the interest lies: it is neither singular nor plural but the last surviving **dual** in the language. All genders share the one form. A reader who files it as an irregular plural of "two" will have the right translation and the wrong grammar, and will then be puzzled by *ubho*, whose *-o* is a dual ending and not a masculine nominative singular.

9. *catutthī* — the ordinal *catuttha* "fourth". Feminine nominative singular. This is the ordinary feminine of an ordinal from the fourth upwards; the first three are in *-ā* instead, *paṭhamā*, *dutiyā*, *tatiyā*. Three of the *-ī* forms (*aṭṭhamī*, *cātuddasī*, *pañcadasī*) have also hardened into names for days of the lunar fortnight, but that is a lexical fact about those three words and not a rule about the class, and *catutthī* has no such second life. Items 2 and 19 show those three words in both uses.

10. *vīsatiyā* — the cardinal *vīsati* "twenty". Instrumental, ablative, genitive, dative or locative singular: a feminine *i*-stem in *-ti* declines like *jāti*, and the *-iyā* ending is as ambiguous here as it is there. The form alone shows two things. It is **singular**, because from nineteen upwards a numeral is a noun, not an adjective; and so it can tell you nothing about the gender or number of what it counts, which has to be found elsewhere in the sentence.

11. *tikkhattuṁ* — a multiplicative adverb built on *ti* "three", "three times". It has no case and no number, because it is not declined at all: *-kkhattuṁ* added to a numeral stem yields an indeclinable. The accusative *-ṁ* is historical and frozen — the same freezing item 5 found in *dasāhaparamaṁ*. Compare *dvikkhattuṁ* and *dasakkhattuṁ*, formed the same way, and *ekadhā*, *dvidhā*, *sattadhā*, formed differently but equally indeclinable.

Say how the numeral joins its noun.

12. **sahassaṁ** candānaṁ (AN 10.29 — A v 59): the **genitive** construction. *Sahassaṁ* is a neuter noun in the nominative singular and *candānaṁ* is a masculine genitive plural, so the two do not agree in case, number or gender, and cannot be read as a unit. Literally "a thousand of moons". This construction catches anyone told that numerals agree with their nouns: nothing here agrees with anything.

13. saddhiṁ **bhikkhusahassena** (SN 35.28 — S iv 19): the **compound**. The numeral *sahassa* is the last member of a determinative compound whose first member is what is counted, and the whole is then declined as one word, instrumental singular, governed by *saddhiṁ* "together with". The gender is *sahassa*'s, neuter, not *bhikkhu*'s. The canon reaches for it most often when a round number qualifies a noun.

14. **Cattāro** dhammā vaḍḍhanti (Dhp 109 — Dhp p. 16): **agreement**, the construction most readers assume is the only one. *Cattāro* is masculine nominative plural and so is *dhammā*; the numeral behaves as an ordinary adjective, as every numeral from one to eighteen may. Note that the test is shared case, number and gender, not the look of the two words, and 12 fails it.

**Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — penetrated by view.**

**Read the verse.**

15. Yo sahassaṁ sahassena, saṅgāme mānuse jine; Ekañca jeyyamattānaṁ, sa ve saṅgāmajuttamo. (Dhp 103 — Dhp p. 15)

   *yo* whoever (m. nom. sg.) · *sahassaṁ* a thousand (n. acc. sg.) · *sahassena* by a thousand (n. inst. sg.) · *saṅgāme* in battle (m. loc. sg.) · *mānuse* men (m. acc. pl.) · *jine* should conquer (opt.) · *ekaṁ* one (m. acc. sg.) · *ca* and · *jeyya* should conquer (opt.) · *attānaṁ* himself (m. acc. sg.) · *sa* he · *ve* indeed · *saṅgāmaji·uttamo* supreme victor in battle (m. nom. sg.)

   "Though one should conquer a thousand times a thousand men in battle, and another conquer one — himself — it is he who is the supreme victor."

   Three numerals, three different functions. *Sahassaṁ* is the higher numeral as a noun, accusative, with *mānuse* standing in apposition to it, not agreeing. *Sahassena* is an instrumental of multiplication, "by a thousand", giving a million: Pāli has no word for a million here, but builds one out of the syntax. Then *ekaṁ*, the low numeral, behaves as an ordinary adjective agreeing with *attānaṁ*. The verse sets the largest number the language can conveniently say against the smallest, and the grammar tracks the argument: the big number needs apparatus, the small one simply agrees.

16. Māse māse sahassena, yo yajetha sataṁ samaṁ; (Dhp 106 — Dhp p. 16)

    *māse māse* month by month (m. loc. sg., repeated) · *sahassena* with a thousand (n. inst. sg.) · *yo* whoever (m. nom. sg.) · *yajetha* should sacrifice (opt. mid.) · *sataṁ* a hundred (n. acc. sg.) · *samaṁ* year (f. acc. sg.)

    "Though month after month, for a hundred years, one should offer sacrifice with a thousand —"

    The repetition *māse māse* is the **distributive**, and it is the plainest form the distributive takes: say the word twice and it means "each" or "every". The chapter met the same device with *aṭṭha aṭṭha* "eight each". Then *sataṁ samaṁ* is the higher numeral again, and it repays a second look — *sataṁ* is the neuter accusative of duration and *samaṁ* is a **singular** feminine accusative, *samā* "year". A hundred years, with the noun in the singular. DPD cites this very line under *samā*, which is a useful reminder that the dictionary is often the fastest way to settle a form that the paradigms leave ambiguous.

    Item 6 has the whole verse, and the pair to hold beside *sataṁ samaṁ* is *vassasataṁ* in its last line: the same hundred years, said as a compound instead.

17. Sabbampi taṁ na catubhāgameti, Abhivādanā ujjugatesu seyyo. (Dhp 108 — Dhp p. 16)

    *sabbaṁ* all (n. nom. sg.) · *pi* even · *taṁ* that (n. nom. sg.) · *na* not · *catubhāgaṁ* a quarter (m. acc. sg.) · *eti* comes to · *abhivādanā* salutation (f. nom. sg.) · *ujjugatesu* to the upright (m. loc. pl.) · *seyyo* better (n. nom. sg.)

    "All that does not come to a quarter of it; better is salutation offered to the upright."

    *Catubhāgaṁ* is a **fraction**, and it is built as the chapter said fractions usually are: a numeral plus a word for "part", here *bhāga*. It is a compound and declines as one word, masculine accusative singular, governed by *eti*. Resolve the sandhi carefully: *catubhāgameti* is *catubhāgaṁ* + *eti*, and if you take the *-m-* as part of the verb you will look for a root that does not exist.

18. Appamādo kho, mahārāja, eko dhammo, yo ubho atthe samadhiggayha tiṭṭhati. (SN 3.17 — S i 86)

    *appamādo* diligence (m. nom. sg.) · *kho* indeed · *mahārāja* great king (m. voc. sg.) · *eko* one (m. nom. sg.) · *dhammo* thing (m. nom. sg.) · *yo* which (m. nom. sg.) · *ubho* both (m. acc.) · *atthe* benefits (m. acc. pl.) · *samadhiggayha* having secured (absol.) · *tiṭṭhati* stands

    "Diligence, great king, is the one thing that secures both benefits."

    This is the chapter's example, set again on purpose. Meeting it in the exposition, where the surrounding prose names the dual before the sentence arrives, is different work from parsing it cold. Two numerals: *eko*, agreeing with *dhammo* in case, number and gender, the ordinary adjectival use; and *ubho*, which agrees with nothing, because it is a dual and *atthe* is a plural. The mismatch is neither an error nor a licence: it is the residue of a lost grammatical number, and *ubho* alone still shows it. The two benefits are named in the next sentence: the one belonging to this visible life and the one belonging to what comes after.

The same three days, twice, in two genders.

19. Cātuddasī pañcadasī aṭṭhamī ca pakkhassa. (MN 4 — M i 20)

    Set beside item 2, the six day-names are:

    | | MN 4 — M i 20 | Kd 2 — Vin i 101 |
    |---|---|---|
    | fourteenth | *cātuddasī* f. nom. sg. | *cātuddase* loc. sg., not feminine |
    | fifteenth | *pañcadasī* f. nom. sg. | *pannarase* loc. sg., not feminine |
    | eighth | *aṭṭhamī* f. nom. sg. | *aṭṭhamiyā* f. loc. sg. |

    The fourteenth and the fifteenth are the two named in two genders. DPD carries *cātuddasī* and *pañcadasī* as feminines and *cātuddasa* and *pannarasa* as masculines, all four glossed as the day of the lunar fortnight, not as the plain ordinal. So the same day has two words for it, in two genders, and the canon uses both.

    The eighth behaves differently because it has only ever been feminine. DPD gives *aṭṭhama* as the ordinal, 'eighth', and *aṭṭhamī* separately as the feminine, twice over, once for the number and once for the day. There is no masculine day-name for the eighth beside it. The other two have one because they are built on the teens, where the underlying cardinal is itself a masculine-shaped compound.

    What the pair settles is that these three are names, not ordinals. An ordinal agrees with what it qualifies and takes its gender from it. These do not: they carry their own gender, they differ from each other in it, and the fifteenth even appears in two different stems: *pañcadasa* in the Sanskrit-like form and *pannarasa* in the Middle Indic one the chapter's note on the teens calls the genuine development. Words that behave like that are lexical items with histories, not cells in a paradigm. The chapter says as much ("a lexical fact about three words rather than a grammatical one about a whole class"), and here the claim has its evidence set out.

**Put it back into Pāli.**

20. (a) ***Ekaṁ samayaṁ*** (SN 10.9 — S i 212): **agreement**; both masculine accusative singular. The easiest phrase in the set to produce and the easiest to produce without noticing it is a numeral construction at all.

    (b) ***Cattāro dhammā vaḍḍhanti*** (Dhp 109 — Dhp p. 16): **agreement**; *cattāro* and *dhammā* masculine nominative plural, *vaḍḍhanti* third person plural present. The trap is *cattāri*, which is the neuter and is what item 4 uses.

    (c) ***sahassaṁ candānaṁ*** (AN 10.29 — A v 59): the **genitive** construction; *sahassaṁ* neuter nominative singular, *candānaṁ* masculine genitive plural. If this came out with the two agreeing, the commonest error about Pāli numerals has been made in the open, better than making it unnoticed in a translation.

    (d) ***dasāhaparamaṁ*** (Bu Np 1 — Vin iii 196): a **compound**, three members and an ending, frozen in the accusative and functioning as an adverb.

    This stage exposes which construction comes to hand by default, and for nearly every reader it is agreement, because an English speaker expects a numeral to agree and because (a) and (b) are the phrases most often heard. If (c) and (d) were the hard ones, the chapter has been understood and not yet absorbed, and day 30 settles the difference.

## Answers: The Present System

**Sotānugata — met by ear.**

1. Pare ca na vijānanti, mayamettha yamāmase; Ye ca tattha vijānanti, tato sammanti medhagā. (Dhp 6 — Dhp p. 2)

    *Yamāmase* is the odd one out, and its ending marks it: *-āmase*, which the chapter's imperative table gives as a first person plural in the middle voice. The other three verbs, *vijānanti* twice and *sammanti*, are third person plural present indicatives in the ordinary active *-nti*. So the verse holds one form from the column of the table that most readers never need and three from the column they will use every day.

    Look again at two of them. *Vijānanti* shows the chapter's stem rule at work. The verb is *vijānāti*, stem *vijānā-* with the long *-ā-* of the fifth class, and in the third plural that long vowel is absorbed into the ending and comes out short. *Jānāti* but *jānanti*, and here *vijānāti* but *vijānanti*: the same alternation the first-person forms show in the other direction.

    And *sammanti* is the verb of item 18, which is the verse immediately before this one. DPD gives *medhaga* 'argument, dispute' as *√medh* + *aka* > *aga*, and cites this verse for it.

    The chapter quotes the first half of this from MN 128, where the Buddha addresses it to the monks of Kosambī who will not stop quarrelling. Dhammapada 6 is the same two lines with two more after them, and the two more supply the consequence: the quarrels subside. Whoever has met the line in either place has met the *attanopada*, named or not, and so the middle gets half a page here and not a chapter.

2. Sabbe tasanti daṇḍassa, sabbe bhāyanti maccuno; Attānaṁ upamaṁ katvā, na haneyya na ghātaye. (Dhp 129 — Dhp p. 19)

    The two optatives differ only in their endings. *Haneyya* is the full form, the *-eyya* of the chapter's third person singular; *ghātaye* is the contracted *-e*, the alternative the same cell of the same table gives. Same mood, same person, same number; if you do not know that *-e* is an optative ending you will take the second for a present and lose the sentence's whole force.

    Their stems differ too, and that is the second thing to see. *Haneyya* is built on *hana-*, the plain stem of *hanati* 'strikes, kills'. *Ghātaye* is built on *ghātaya-*, the eighth class in its *-aya-* form, not its *-e-* form, the class that gives *deseti*. Whether it is also a **causative** is a question the sources answer differently. The Dhammapada commentary takes it that way, glossing the line *neva paraṁ pahareyya na paharāpeyya*, 'one should neither strike another nor have another struck', where *paharāpeyya* can only be a causative. DPD gives the headword *ghātayati* simply as 'kills, slaughters', from *√ghaṭ*, and cites this line for it. The parsing does not depend on it. *-aya-* is the eighth class's affix and the causative's affix alike, so Chapter 9 has to separate them and this chapter does not.

    The gradation is visible in the first half as well. *Tasanti* is from *√tas*, and *a* is already at the *guṇa* grade, so the stem *tasa-* shows no change at all; *bhāyanti* is from *√bhī*, and its stem *bhāya-* is that root raised. So two verbs of nearly the same meaning, side by side in one couplet, look nothing like each other.

    The verse turns at its middle. Two present indicatives state what is the case; two optatives state what one ought therefore to do. Dhammapada 130 is the same four lines with the second replaced (*sabbesaṁ jīvitaṁ piyaṁ*, "life is dear to all"), so the frame is used twice with two different reasons hung on it, and the mood has the same function both times.

3. Na paro paraṁ nikubbetha, nātimaññetha katthaci na kañci; Byārosanā paṭighasaññā, nāññamaññassa dukkhamiccheyya. (Snp 1.8 — Sn p. 26)

    *Nikubbetha* and *nātimaññetha* are the middle pair, both in *-etha*, which the chapter's optative table gives as the third person singular of the *attanopada*. DPD parses *nikubbetha* so, "opt, reflx 3rd sg", and *nātimaññetha* is *na* + *atimaññetha*, the negative absorbed into the verb by the sandhi of Chapter 2. The third optative, *iccheyya*, is the ordinary active *-eyya* on the stem *iccha-*.

    So all three are the same mood and the same person, and the difference between them is voice alone. The verse is here to show that the middle is a different column, not a different grammar: see *-etha* against *-eyya* and you have learnt the whole practical content of the *attanopada*.

    A verse is where such forms are found because, as Duroiselle says in the passage the chapter quotes, the choice between the two voices "is mostly determined now by metrical exigencies". *Nikubbeyya* would not scan. There is a second reason here, more interesting than the metre: DPD builds *nikubbati* on *√kar*, the root of *karoti*, *ni* + *kubba* + *ti*, literally 'makes down'. One root, and the chapter's table gives it the stem *karo-* while this verse gives it *kubba-*. The stem is the working unit and the root is not: the chapter's opening claim, in a line of the Mettasutta.

    The two feminine nouns of the third *pāda* are glossed above without a case label, on purpose. The ending *-ā* on a feminine *ā*-stem is the nominative, and the sense every rendering wants is "out of anger, out of thoughts of enmity", which is an ablative. This item does not depend on it and the workbook does not adjudicate it; the item is about the verbs.

4. Rūpaṁ 'netaṁ mama, nesohamasmi, na meso attā'ti samanupassati. (MN 22 — M i 136)

    *Nesohamasmi* is four words: *na* + *eso* + *ahaṁ* + *asmi*, "not, this, I, am". DPD registers the whole string as a sandhi compound and resolves it into those four, calling it *ind* + *pron* + *pron* + *pr*. Two of its neighbours in the same quotation show the same on a smaller scale: *netaṁ* is *na* + *etaṁ*, and *meso* is *me* + *eso*.

    The verb form buried at the end of it is *asmi*, the first person singular of *atthi*, and the chapter's warning concerns this. Standing alone, *asmi* occurs once in the whole of the root texts. Joined, it is everywhere: this formula alone accounts for hundreds of occurrences, since *esohamasmi* and *nesohamasmi* are how the canon says "I am this" and "I am not this". Search a text for *asmi*, find nothing, and you have learnt a fact about how the editions set sandhi and nothing about the language.

    *Samanupassati* takes the last word. It is an ordinary third person singular present indicative on the stem *passa-*, and DPD gives its root as *√dis*. The stem does not announce the root, and need not: remove *-ti* and you have the stem, and the endings go on the stem. That is the chapter's thesis in one word, a word that occurs several hundred times in the Nikāyas.

**Vacasā paricitā — rehearsed aloud.**

5. Handa dāni, bhikkhave, āmantayāmi vo, vayadhammā saṅkhārā appamādena sampādethā'ti. (DN 16 — D ii 156)

    The two stems are *āmantaya-* and *sampāde-*, and they are the same class in its two shapes. The chapter's table gives the eighth class the affix *-e-*, *-aya-*, with *deseti* as its example; DPD's constructions are *ā* + *mantaya* + *ti* and *saṁ* + *pāde* + *ti*, so the Buddha's last sentence happens to carry one of each. Taught the class by *deseti* alone, you will not recognise the first of them as belonging to it.

    *Āmantayāmi* also shows the lengthening rule twice over if you look: the stem ends in a short *-a*, and before *-mi* it lengthens, as *gaccha-* gives *gacchāmi*. *Sampādetha* is the second person plural imperative, and its ending *-tha* is one of the three the chapter flags as shared with the present indicative, so the form is, on its own, equally "you strive on" and "strive on!", and only the setting decides. Here the setting is a deathbed and there is no doubt at all.

    No pronoun is needed because *-tha* has already said 'you'. *Bhikkhave* is a vocative and is not the subject of anything; it names the people addressed, and the *-tha* marks the subject. The *vo* two words earlier is the object of *āmantayāmi*, not the subject of *sampādetha*, and if you take it for one, the second clause loses its verb's agreement.

6. Attanā hi kataṁ pāpaṁ, attanā saṅkilissati; Attanā akataṁ pāpaṁ, attanāva visujjhati; Suddhī asuddhi paccattaṁ, nāñño aññaṁ visodhaye. (Dhp 165 — Dhp p. 24)

    ***Visujjhati* and *visodhaye* are one verb twice.** DPD gives *visujjhati* as *vi* + *sujjha* + *ti* and *visodhayati* as *vi* + *sodhaya* + *ti*, both from *√sudh*, and calls the second the causative of the first. So the verse says "one is purified" and then "no one purifies another", and the difference between being purified and purifying is carried by one vowel.

    That vowel is the chapter's gradation table. The root vowel is *u*; the *guṇa* grade of *u* is *o*; so *sujjh-* is the root untouched and *sodh-* is the root raised, the same operation that turns *bhū-* into *bhavati* and *jut-* into *jotati*. The doubled *-jjh-* of the first is a second mechanism on top of it, the assimilated remains of the *-ya-* class affix, so the plain verb belongs to the third class and its causative to the eighth.

    *Visodhaye* is then the contracted optative in *-e*, the same ending as *ghātaye* in item 2 and on the same kind of stem. Two verses in this set end on that form, and in both of them it is the form a reader is likeliest to misparse.

    Two smaller things. *Saṅkilissati* is a passive-looking present of the kind item 16 is about, and the editions do not spell it alike: the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana, the Buddha Jayanti and the Pali Text Society all print *saṁkilissati* with the niggahīta, where this book writes *saṅkilissati* with the velar nasal, as Chapter 2 teaches a niggahīta becomes before a velar. That is the ṅ/ṁ variation of Chapter 1's box, in a key word of the verse, and the book's spelling is a convention, not a report of any one edition. And *paccattaṁ* is an indeclinable adverb, not a case form, which DPD registers as the frozen accusative singular of *paccatta*: the same freezing the numerals chapter found in *dasāhaparamaṁ*.

**Manasānupekkhitā — gone over in the mind.**

**Identify the form.**

7. *suṇāma* — from *suṇāti* 'hears', stem *suṇā-*. First person plural, present indicative, active. The stem belongs to the class that inserts *-ṇā-* between root and ending (*su-* + *-ṇā-*), so the stem ends in a long vowel, not in *-a*. Note that the stem vowel is already long, so the lengthening before *-ma* has nothing to do; compare *gacchāma*, where it does.

8. *karonti* — from *karoti* 'does, makes', stem *karo-*. Third person plural, present indicative, active. This is the small *-o-* class, and *karoti* is its most irregular member as well as much its most frequent. Item 3 has the same root wearing a different stem.

9. *gacchāhi* — from *gacchati* 'goes', stem *gaccha-*. Second person singular, **imperative**, active: 'go!'. The ending is *-hi*, and the stem vowel has lengthened before it by the same rule that gives *gacchāmi*. The bare stem *gaccha* is an equally ordinary form of the same command.

10. *jāneyyuṁ* — from *jānāti* 'knows', stem *jānā-*. Third person plural, **optative**, active: 'they might know', 'they should know'. The *-eyy-* is the mark of the mood, and *-uṁ* the third plural. Any form carrying *-eyy-* is an optative; the mood has no other disguise, though it does have the contraction of items 2 and 6.

11. *bhavantu* — from *bhavati* 'is, becomes', stem *bhava-*. Third person plural, **imperative**, active: 'let them be', 'may they be'. Nothing about the form says whether it is a command, a wish or a blessing; at Snp 1.8 — Sn p. 25 it is a blessing, and only the setting says so.

12. *yamāmase* — dictionary form *yamati*, stem *yama-*. First person plural, imperative, **middle** (*attanopada*). No other item in the group is not active, and the ending *-āmase* gives it away; it is also, characteristically, a verse form, and item 1 sets the whole verse in front of you. Beyond the parsing, two things about it matter. Its *sense* is disputed (Ñāṇamoli and Bodhi take it as restraining oneself, the commentaries and DPD as dying), and the chapter's footnote sets out who says what. And the dictionary form *yamati* does not itself occur in the root texts: the canon has this verb only as *yamāmase* and in the compound *saṁyamāmase*. A headword does not promise that you will meet the word in that form.

**Resolve the ambiguity.**

13. *gacchāma* is **either** first person plural present indicative, 'we go', **or** first person plural imperative, 'let us go'. The two are identical, and they are identical in every verb, because the imperative's first-person endings are simply the present's. Only the sentence decides, and the usual cues are a following particle of exhortation, an imperative in a neighbouring clause, or a context in which a statement of present fact would make no sense. This overlap covers three cells of the paradigm (first singular, first plural and second plural) and is no defect in the tables.

14. *desetha* is likewise **either** second person plural present indicative, 'you teach', **or** second person plural imperative, 'teach!'. Item 5's *sampādetha* is the third cell of the same overlap on a stem of the same class, resolved by a deathbed. The added difficulty here is the stem: *dese-* is the *-e-* class, and if you take the *-e-* for the mark of an optative you will look for a mood that is not there. An optative of this verb carries the mood-marker visibly (*deseyya* 'he might teach', *deseyyaṁ* 'I might teach'), and neither could be mistaken for the form above.

15. *siyā* is third person singular **optative** of *atthi* 'is', 'it would be', 'it might be', not a present. It belongs to the *-yā* type of optative, not the *-eyya* type, so it does not look like one, and it is among the commonest verb forms in the canon because so much of the Buddha's conditional and hypothetical speech runs through it. Its rival *assa* is the same mood of the same verb, formed the ordinary way. Only knowledge of the paradigm decides the reading: *atthi* has no present in *si-* at all.

16. *vuccati* looks like an ordinary third person singular active present, and it is 'is called', a **passive**. The form makes the point the chapter's voice section exists for: the active-looking ending settles nothing, because active against passive is carried by the **stem**, not by the endings. The stem here is a passive one built with *-ya-* on the root *vac-* 'say', assimilated to *-cc-*. Only knowledge of the verb, or an agent in the instrumental case, shows which voice is in front of you. *Saṅkilissati* and *visujjhati* in item 6 are the same shape and the same problem. The passive stem is Chapter 9's subject.

**Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — penetrated by view.**

**Read the verse.**

17. **Dhp 1 — Dhp p. 1.** *mano·pubbaṅgamā* preceded by mind (m. nom. pl.) · *dhammā* things, mental states (m. nom. pl.) · *mano·seṭṭhā* having mind as chief (m. nom. pl.) · *mano·mayā* made of mind (m. nom. pl.) · *manasā* with a mind (n. inst. sg.) · *ce* if · *paduṭṭhena* corrupted (n. inst. sg.) · *bhāsati* speaks (3 sg. pres.) · *vā* or · *karoti* acts (3 sg. pres.) · *tato* thereafter · *naṁ* him (acc. sg.) · *dukkham·anveti* < *dukkhaṁ* suffering (n. nom. sg.) + *anveti* follows (3 sg. pres.) · *cakkaṁ·va* < *cakkaṁ* the wheel (n. nom. sg.) + *iva* like · *vahato* of the one drawing, of the draught animal (m. gen. sg., present participle) · *padaṁ* the foot (n. acc. sg.)

    "Mind precedes all things; mind is their chief, they are made of mind. If with a corrupted mind one speaks or acts, suffering follows one from that — as the wheel follows the foot of the ox that draws it."

    All three finite verbs are present indicative and all three are timeless: this is the general-truth use, not a report of anything happening. *Bhāsati* and *karoti* sit in a conditional clause marked by *ce* and take the indicative, not the optative. *Ce* does not require the optative, and a conditional whose condition is stated as a fact keeps the indicative. Look at *anveti*: its stem is *e-*, from the root *i-* 'go' with the prefix *anu-* (*anu* > *anv* + *e* + *ti*), so it belongs to the long-vowel conjugation and not to any class with a visible affix. Two sandhis are resolved in the gloss and neither is optional in the printed line.

18. **Dhp 5 — Dhp p. 1.** *na* not · *hi* indeed, for · *verena* by hatred (n. inst. sg.) · *verāni* hatreds (n. nom. pl.) · *sammant·īdha* < *sammanti* are appeased (3 pl. pres.) + *idha* here · *kudācanaṁ* ever · *averena* by non-hatred (n. inst. sg.) · *ca* and · *sammanti* are appeased (3 pl. pres.) · *esa* this (m. nom. sg.) · *dhammo* law, teaching (m. nom. sg.) · *sanantano* eternal, ancient (m. nom. sg.)

    "For never here are hatreds appeased by hatred; by non-hatred are they appeased. This is an eternal law."

    Both finite verbs are the same form, third person plural present indicative of *sammati* 'is appeased', and both are the general-truth use: the verse states what always holds, as *sanantano* then says outright. Item 1 has the third occurrence of the same form one verse later, with *medhagā*, not *verāni*, as its subject. Look again at *sammati* for its stem: DPD gives the construction as *samma-* + *-ti*, from the root *sam-*, the doubled consonant being the assimilated remains of a *-ya-* class affix. The verb is intransitive and is not a passive, though English needs a passive to render it.

19. **Dhp 167 — Dhp p. 25.** *hīnaṁ* low, inferior (m. acc. sg.) · *dhammaṁ* thing, practice (m. acc. sg.) · *na* not · *seveyya* should resort to (3 sg. opt.) · *pamādena* in negligence (m. inst. sg.) · *na* not · *saṁvase* should dwell (3 sg. opt.) · *micchā·diṭṭhiṁ* wrong view (f. acc. sg.) · *na siyā* should not be (3 sg. opt. of *atthi*) · *loka·vaḍḍhano* one who increases the world, one attached to the world (m. nom. sg.)

    "One should not resort to what is low; one should not dwell in negligence. One should not resort to wrong view; one should not be a perpetuator of the world."
