    Three optatives, and for this chapter the verse matters because they take three different forms. *Seveyya* is the full *-eyya* ending. *Saṁvase* is the contracted *-e*, the same mood and the same person, from *saṁvasati* 'lives with', a form you will parse as a present indicative unless you know that the contraction exists. And *siyā* is the *-yā* type belonging to *atthi*. One verse, one mood, three endings, and no other mood in it.

    The sense is the prescriptive one: 'should', not 'might'. All four clauses are negated by *na*, and that is a different thing from the prohibition with *mā* in the next chapter: *na* + optative states a rule about what one ought not do, whereas *mā* forms a direct prohibition and takes the aorist. Both come out as 'don't' in English and they are not the same construction.

**One imperative, two numbers.**

20. *suṇantu* let them listen (3 pl. imper. of *suṇāti*) · *me* to me (1st sg. gen./dat., enclitic) · *āyasmantā* the venerables (m. nom. pl.)

    "Let the venerables listen to me."

    *Suṇantu* is the third person plural imperative, on the present stem *suṇā-* of *suṇāti* 'hears', with the ending *-antu*. Its subject is *āyasmantā*, nominative plural: the venerables are told to listen, and they are the grammatical subject of the verb even though in English the speaker seems to be the one making the request.

    *Me* is the enclitic of the first person, and its form does not show whether it is genitive or dative. Here the sense is dative, 'listen **to me**', but Chapter 5 records that the two have merged in the enclitics and that the ending will not decide it. *Suṇāti* decides it, which takes the person heard in the genitive or the dative and the thing heard in the accusative.

    Between this and *Suṇātu me, bhante, saṅgho* only the number changes. *Suṇātu* is third person singular with *saṅgho* as its subject, because the Sangha is one body; *suṇantu* is third person plural with *āyasmantā*, because a group of venerables is several people. The formula is otherwise identical, down to the enclitic. Which one a monk says depends on how many are in front of him, and the Vinaya prints both because the same act has to be performable before a Sangha, before a group, and before one person.

**Two verbs for 'is'.**

21. MN 10 — M i 57 uses *atthi* and asserts that something is there. "There are in this body head-hairs, body-hairs, nails, teeth, skin …" — the sentence introduces the parts of the body to a meditator who is about to review them, and what it claims is their presence. SN 5.6 — S i 132 uses *hoti* and asserts that one thing is another. "For one who is born there is death" — nothing is being said to exist; *maraṇaṁ* is being predicated of *jātassa*.

    Swap them and the first sentence becomes a claim that the body *is* its head-hairs and nails, which is not what the meditation says and is a doctrinal error besides; the second becomes a claim that death exists, which is true and is not the line's point. The chapter's rule holds: *hoti* says that A is B, *atthi* says that there is an A. The chapter is careful to add that the distinction is not absolute, and it is right to; these two lines are why it is nonetheless the way to bet.

    The number disagreement is in the first, and it is no error. *Atthi* is third person singular; *kesā lomā nakhā dantā taco* is a string of nominatives, four of them plural. The existential *atthi* standing at the head of a sentence regularly does not agree with what follows it: *atthi devā tāvatiṁsā*, 'there are the gods of the Thirty-Three' (DN 23 — D ii 327), is the same construction with a plural subject and a singular verb, and the negative *natthi* behaves identically in the stock list of wrong views. The chapter's paradigm does give a third plural, *santi*, and it is used, but where the verb is existential and stands first, the singular is ordinary and passes without remark. To correct it is to correct the canon.

**Put it back into Pāli.**

22. Four phrases, and the mood is the whole of the exercise:

    (a) ***bhāyanti maccuno*** (Dhp 129 — Dhp p. 19): third person plural, present indicative, active. The thing feared stands in the genitive, which English does not lead you to expect; *maccuno* is genitive singular of *maccu* 'death'.

    (b) ***nāññamaññassa dukkhamiccheyya*** (Snp 1.8 — Sn p. 26): third person **singular**, optative, active. The English has to say "none" or "no one", because Pāli leaves the subject unexpressed and the *-eyya* has already said which person it is. A plural here means you have heard the English, not the Pāli.

    (c) ***appamādena sampādetha*** (DN 16 — D ii 156): second person plural, imperative, active. Identical in form to the present indicative, and the only thing that makes it an imperative is that the Buddha is dying and is not describing anyone's habits.

    (d) ***suṇantu me āyasmantā*** (Bu Np 1 — Vin iii 197): third person plural, imperative, active, with its subject expressed because the third person needs one. Compare (c), where the second person does not.

    If (b) and (c) both came out, the two moods have separated. They are the two the chapter says are "syntactically often exchangeable", and the exercise is to produce the right form for the person you mean, not to tell their senses apart, which often nobody can.

## Answers: The Past and the Future

**Sotānugata — met by ear.**

1. Idamavoca bhagavā. Attamanā te bhikkhū bhagavato bhāsitaṁ abhinandunti. (MN 10 — M i 63)

    The first word is two words. DPD registers *idamavoca* as a sandhi compound and resolves it *idaṁ* + *avoca*, pronoun plus aorist: the junction item 22 meets in *etadavocuṁ*, where the older stem's *-d-* surfaces before a vowel. Here the pronoun is *idaṁ* and the *-m* is simply its own.

    The two present-tense verbs are *vadati* 'says' and *abhinandati* 'delights in, approves'. Neither aorist looks like its present, and they fail to for different reasons. *Avoca* is the *a*-aorist of *vadati*, which has three unrelated-looking stems and has to be learnt whole; it belongs in the chapter's irregular table. *Abhinanduṁ* is the ordinary aorist of an ordinary verb, and DPD gives the construction as *abhi* + √*nand* + *i*, which yields the singular *abhinandi*; the plural here takes *-uṁ* straight onto the root instead. That is the ending the chapter's tables give for types 1 and 2, not the *-iṁsu* of the type the singular belongs to. Whether that makes it a different type or one paradigm with mixed endings is the sort of question the chapter's section on the vanished tenses says matters little.

    This is the last sentence of most suttas in the canon, so it comes first here. Anyone who has listened to much Pāli has heard it hundreds of times, and it consists of two past tenses neither of which is formed the regular way.

2. Gahakāraka diṭṭhosi, puna gehaṁ na kāhasi; Sabbā te phāsukā bhaggā, gahakūṭaṁ visaṅkhataṁ; Visaṅkhāragataṁ cittaṁ, taṇhānaṁ khayamajjhagā. (Dhp 154 — Dhp p. 23)

    The present is *diṭṭhosi*, and it is hidden because it is joined on: DPD resolves it *diṭṭho* + *asi*, past participle plus the second person singular of *atthi*. Chapter 7's warning about the first person applies to the second as well: these forms are printed joined, and a search for *asi* on its own will badly under-count it.

    The future is *kāhasi*, and it has no *-ss-* in it at all. DPD gives it as the second person singular of *kāhati*, the future of *karoti*, from √*kar*; the chapter's footnote traces Perniola's chain at §80e, *karssati* > *kāssati* > *kāsati* > *kāhati*, the intervocalic sibilant weakening to *-h-*; only the endpoint is a form in the root texts. It is a verse form and a syllable shorter than *karissasi*, which largely explains why it exists.

    The past is *ajjhagā*, and you can see its augment, if nothing else of it. DPD's construction is *adhi* > *adhy* > *ajjh* + *a* + √*gam* + *ā*: the prefix *adhi-* palatalises to *ajjh-* before the vowel, and then the augment *a* stands between prefix and root where the chapter's rule puts it, followed by the bare *-ā* ending on the root. Compare *pāvisi* in item 23, where the same rule applies and the augment has vanished into a long vowel. One rule, two outcomes, and you can point at the augment in only one of them.

    DPD labels *ajjhagā* an **imperfect**, not an aorist. The chapter says of the imperfect that where one grammar labels a form so and another calls the same form an aorist, "nothing hangs on which is right". This is that, in the most famous verse in the Dhammapada.

3. Māvamaññetha pāpassa, na mantaṁ āgamissati; Udabindunipātena, udakumbhopi pūrati; Bālo pūrati pāpassa, thokaṁ thokampi ācinaṁ. (Dhp 121 — Dhp p. 18)

    The prohibition is on an optative, not an aorist. *Māvamaññetha* is *mā* + *avamaññetha*, and *-etha* is the third person singular middle optative of Chapter 7's table; DPD gives the verb as *avamaññati* 'thinks lightly of, belittles', from √*man*, *ava* + *mañña* + *ti*. The chapter names this construction and says what it does: with the aorist, *mā* forbids a particular act (*mā bhāyi*, 'don't be afraid'); with the optative it softens towards advice. So the verse is a counsel, not an order, and a whole mood marks the difference.

    The same verb turned up in set 07 with a different prefix. *Ati-maññati* in the Mettasutta is 'to think in excess of', to despise; *ava-maññati* here is 'to think down on', to make light of. One root, two directions, and the prefix is the whole of the difference.

    The future is not the verse's own prediction. *Na mantaṁ āgamissati* is *na* + *maṁ* + *taṁ* + *āgamissati* (DPD registers *mantaṁ* as a sandhi of *maṁ* + *taṁ* and cites this line for it), "it will not come to me", and the edition's own apparatus corroborates the resolution, since the Sinhalese and Pī manuscripts print the two words unjoined. The *pāda* is the content of the thought being warned against, and that makes sense of the *mā* in the line before: the person addressed is the one making the prediction, and the verse denies it. A future stating what will not happen, quoted in order to be contradicted.

    *Ācinaṁ* at the end is a present participle of *ācināti*, not a finite verb, 'accumulates', masculine nominative singular, agreeing with *bālo*. Item 5 has two more of these, and they are the commonest thing in Pāli verse to mistake for a first person singular.

4. Etaṁ satiṁ adhiṭṭheyya, Brahmametaṁ vihāramidhamāhu. (Snp 1.8 — Sn p. 26)

    The tense is the perfect, and *āhu* is one of a handful of forms left of it. DPD gives *āhu* as the third person plural of *āha*, from √*ah*, construction *a* + *aha* + *u*, and cites this very line. Perniola's judgement, which the chapter reports, is that in the canonical books the perfect amounts to *āha* in the second and third singular and *āhu*, *āhaṁsu* in the third plural, and to no other form that can be named.

    A reader will go looking for a present *āhati*, and there is none. That is the practical content of the whole section: *āha* is among the most frequent verb forms in the literature, it is built on no present stem, and an attempt to work back from it to a dictionary headword will fail. It has to be recognised, not derived. Item 25 has the singular *āha* doing ordinary work in a Vinaya narrative, printed joined to the word before it.

    *Adhiṭṭheyya* belongs to the previous chapter: DPD parses it as the third person singular optative of *adhiṭṭhāti*, *adhi* + √*ṭhā* + *eyya*. So the couplet holds a mood that is everywhere and a tense that has nearly gone, side by side, and the common one carries the instruction.

**Vacasā paricitā — rehearsed aloud.**

5. Anekajātisaṁsāraṁ, sandhāvissaṁ anibbisaṁ; Gahakāraṁ gavesanto, dukkhā jāti punappunaṁ. (Dhp 153 — Dhp p. 23)

    *Sandhāvissaṁ* is the aorist, with a conditional's ending. The chapter's conditional table gives *-(i)ssaṁ* for the first person singular, and there it is. But DPD parses the word as "aor 1st sg of *sandhāvi*, irreg form of *sandhāviṁ*", from √*dhāv*, and the regular first singular of this verb would be *sandhāviṁ*. So the form is a past, not a counterfactual, and its ending is the one most likely to suggest otherwise.

    *Anibbisaṁ* is not a finite verb at all. DPD gives it as the present participle of *na nibbisati*, 'not finding': masculine nominative singular, agreeing with the unexpressed 'I' of the sentence. It looks like a first person singular and is a participle; the word beside it looks like a conditional and is a first person singular. Two adjacent words, each looking like what the other is.

    *Gavesanto* in the next line settles it, because it is the same kind of word in its other form: the same present participle, masculine nominative singular, agreeing with the same unexpressed subject, and nobody could mistake *-nto* for a finite ending. One verse, one participle ending twice, spelled two ways.

    This is the verse before item 2 and the two are one utterance: the search, then the finding. Having rebuilt both, say them together.

6. Tena kho pana samayena aññataro bhikkhu divā bhaṇḍaṁ passitvā nimittaṁ akāsi—"rattiṁ avaharissāmī"ti. So taṁ maññamāno attano bhaṇḍaṁ avahari. (Bu Pj 2 — Vin iii 57)

    The verb that stands twice is *avaharati* 'steals', once as a future and once as an aorist. *Avaharissāmi* is the first person singular future, formed the wholly regular way (root, *-issā-*, primary ending), and it is what the monk says he will do. *Avahari* is the third person singular aorist, DPD's *ava* + √*har* + *i*, type 4, and it is what he did. The intention and the act, in the two tenses this chapter is about, forty words apart.

    What the difference costs him is the whole case. He marked goods by day meaning to take them at night; in the dark he took his own. The future says *taṁ*, 'that'; the aorist says *attano bhaṇḍaṁ*, 'his own goods'. There is no theft, because there is no owner to steal from, and the Vibhaṅga's rulings on the neighbouring cases depend on this: what the monk perceived against what was in fact there.

    *Akāsi* is the *s*-aorist of *karoti* and is item 7 of this set. Knowing only *karoti*, no rule will get you to it: the *-si* is the type 3 marker, the *kā-* is what is left of the root, and there is no present in the language it resembles. It is also, in this sentence, the ordinary word for making a mental note: *nimittaṁ akāsi*, 'made a sign'.

**Manasānupekkhitā — gone over in the mind.**

**Name the type.**

7. *akāsi* — **type 3, the *s*-aorist**; third person singular; from *karoti* 'does, makes'. The *-si* is the mark, and it is also why this form is met early: *akāsi* is the ordinary way of saying 'he did' or 'he made' and it looks nothing like its present. Note that *karoti* also has a root aorist, *akā*, so the same verb appears in two of the four types. Item 6 has it in a sentence.

8. *upasaṅkamiṁsu* — **type 4, the *is*-aorist**; third person plural; from *upasaṅkamati* 'approaches', stem *upasaṅkama-*. The *-iṁsu* is the ending on the present stem with its final vowel gone, and this is the type to guess at when no other suggests itself. It is also the commonest single word in the canon's narrative formula for someone going to see the Buddha.

9. *agā* — **type 1, the root aorist**; third person singular; from *gacchati* 'goes', root *gā-*. Everything here is old: the augment, which the root aorist always takes, and the bare *-ā* ending straight on the root. The verb has an *s*-aorist too, *agamāsi*, and the two coexist. *Ajjhagā* in item 2 is the same ending on the same root behind a prefix.

10. *addasaṁ* — **type 2, the *a*-aorist**; first person singular; from *dassati* 'sees'. The root is *dṛś-*, and the form it has arrived at (*a-dras-s-at* with everything assimilated) leaves almost nothing of it visible. This is the type to expect surprises from.

11. *assosi* — **type 3, the *s*-aorist**; third person singular; from *suṇāti* 'hears'. Reading it needs a moment: the double *-ss-* is the augment *a-* meeting the root *su-* with the aorist *-s-*, not a stem in *-ss-*.

12. *avocumha* — **type 2, the *a*-aorist**; first person plural; from *vadati* 'says'. The verb has three unrelated-looking stems, one for each voice and tense system: present *vadati*, aorist *avoca*, passive *vuccati*. They must simply be learnt together, and in item 1 a reader has already met the third singular of the middle one several hundred times.

**Take the tense apart.**

13. *gamissanti* — **future**, third person plural, 'they will go'. Stem *gaccha-* from *gacchati*, but the future is built on the root *gam-*, not on the present stem: *gam* + *issa* + *nti*. Both *gamissati* and *gacchissati* occur; the first is much the commoner.

14. *lacchasi* — **future**, second person singular, 'you will get', from *labhati* 'gets'. The *-ss-* has met the *-bh* of the root and the pair have collapsed: *labh-ssa-ti* > *lacchati*. Nothing on the surface of the word says future; it has to be recognised.

15. *abhavissa* — **conditional**, third person singular, from *bhavati* 'is, becomes'. Three elements are stacked: the augment *a-*, the future stem *bhavissa-*, and a secondary ending. Future stem plus past marking is the form of a counterfactual: something that was going to be and was not. Item 5 is the form this one has to be told apart from.

16. *hehiti* — **future**, third person singular, 'it will be', from *bhavati*. The chain, which is Perniola's at §80e and not the canon's, runs *bhavissati* > *havissati* > *hessati* > *hehiti*, the intervocalic sibilant weakening to *-h-*; only the last two of the four occur in the canon. It is a verse form, and a syllable shorter than the form it replaces, which largely explains why it exists. *Kāhasi* in item 2 is the same weakening on the other of the two commonest verbs in the language.

17. *ñassati* — **future**, third person singular, 'he will know', from *jānāti* 'knows'. This one has a different base, not assimilation: the future is built straight on the root *ñā-*, not on the present stem *jānā-*. The regular *jānissati* also exists and means the same thing.

**Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — penetrated by view.**

**Decide what the form means.**

18. *bhāyi* is a **second person singular aorist** of *bhāyati* 'fears', and it is not a past tense. After *mā* the aorist forms a prohibition: 'do not fear'. The past sense is gone entirely, and the reason is historical: this is a survival of the Old Indic injunctive, a form that was never past and merely coincides with the aorist in Pāli. The line also shades towards a wish, 'may you not be afraid', and only the setting decides. Vakkali is dying when it is said to him. Item 3 is the same particle on the optative instead, and item 25 is the same particle on the aorist with the wish, not the prohibition, uppermost.

19. *bhavissanti* is a **third person plural future**, and no future is meant. The sense is inferential: 'these cannot be the footprints of a human being'. Doṇa is looking at the marks on the road and reasoning from them, and Pāli puts that reasoning in the future the way English puts it in 'must'. The negated *na … bhavissati* is the frozen idiom for 'it cannot be'.

20. *ñassati* and *dakkhati* are both **third person singular futures**, of *jānāti* and *dassati*, and they are irregular in two different ways, the first built on the root, the second by assimilation. They express neither prediction nor inference but the ordinary future of a consequence: practise in this way, and this is what follows. Notice also that neither has an *-ss-* anywhere in it, and that is the whole difficulty of the section they come from.

    The editions disagree here. The Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana prints *dakkhati* and records *dakkhiti* as the Sinhalese reading in its own apparatus, and the PTS has *dākkhīti*. The variation is in the ending, not the stem, and it is the same *-ati*/*-iti* alternation that gives *hessati* beside *hehiti*.

**Read the passage.**

21. *no* not · *ce* if · *idaṁ* this (n. nom. sg.) · *bhikkhave* monks (m. voc. pl.) · *paṇḍito* the astute person (m. nom. sg.) · *sucintitacintī* one who thinks well-thought thoughts (m. nom. sg.) · *ca* and · *abhavissa* were to be (3 sg. cond.) · *subhāsitabhāsī* one who speaks well-spoken speech (m. nom. sg.) · *ca* and · *sukatakammakārī* one who does well-done deeds (m. nom. sg.) · *ca* and · *kena* by what (n. inst. sg.) · *naṁ* him (m. acc. sg.) · *paṇḍitā* the astute (m. nom. pl.) · *jāneyyuṁ* would know (3 pl. opt.)

    "If, monks, an astute person were not one who thinks well, speaks well and acts well, by what would the astute know him?"

    The **conditional** *abhavissa* stands in the protasis, the clause carrying the condition; the **optative** *jāneyyuṁ* stands in the apodosis, the clause carrying the consequence. That is the normal division of labour, and the forms themselves explain it. The conditional is a future seen from the past, something that was going to happen and did not, which is what a counterfactual condition asserts. The optative states what would follow, the mood's ordinary use. Either can appear on either side, and the conditional is rare enough that the optative usually fills both; this passage is kept because it shows the construction in its full form.

    The three long compounds are *bahubbīhi*s, treated in Chapter 13: each describes the person instead of naming a thing, 'one whose thinking is well thought'.

22. *atha kho* then · *te* those (m. nom. pl.) · *bhikkhū* monks (m. nom. pl.) · *sāvatthiyaṁ* in Sāvatthī (f. loc. sg.) · *piṇḍāya* for alms (m. dat. sg.) · *caritvā* having wandered (absol.) · *pacchābhattaṁ* after the meal · *piṇḍapātapaṭikkantā* returned from the almsround (m. nom. pl.) · *yena … tena* to where · *bhagavā* the Blessed One (m. nom. sg.) · *upasaṅkamiṁsu* they approached (3 pl. aor.) · *upasaṅkamitvā* having approached (absol.) · *bhagavantaṁ* the Blessed One (m. acc. sg.) · *abhivādetvā* having paid homage (absol.) · *ekamantaṁ* to one side · *nisīdiṁsu* they sat down (3 pl. aor.) · *nisinnā* seated (m. nom. pl.) · *kho* indeed · *etadavocuṁ* < *etaṁ* this (n. acc. sg.) + *avocuṁ* they said (3 pl. aor.)

    "Then those monks, having wandered for alms in Sāvatthī and returned from the almsround after the meal, approached the Blessed One; having approached and paid homage to him, they sat down to one side. Seated to one side, those monks said this to the Blessed One."

    Two of the three are *is*-aorists (*upasaṅkamiṁsu* and *nisīdiṁsu*, both formed on the present stem with *-iṁsu*), and the third, *avocuṁ*, is an *a*-aorist of *vadati*. The mix is the lesson. The productive type accounts for the two ordinary verbs, and the one verb that does not take it is a high-frequency irregular that has kept its inherited form. So the working method for an unfamiliar past tense is: assume the *is*-aorist and take the present stem off, and if that fails, the verb is probably one of the two dozen commonest words in the language and has to be learnt whole.

    Three of the other verb forms here (*caritvā*, *upasaṅkamitvā*, *abhivādetvā*) are **absolutives**, which are not finite and carry no tense of their own; they belong to Chapter 11. They take their time from the finite verb they hang on, so the whole sentence reads as past.

    *Etadavocuṁ* is one printed word and two: *etaṁ* + *avocuṁ*, with the *-d-* of the old stem *etad* surfacing before the vowel. Unless you resolve that junction you will search for a verb *davocuṁ* and not find one. Item 1 closes the sutta with the singular of the same junction.

**Read the case law.**

23. *tena kho pana samayena* now at that time · *aññataro* a certain (m. nom. sg.) · *bhikkhu* monk (m. nom. sg.) · *ajjhokāse* in the open air (m. loc. sg.) · *cīvaraṁ* robe (nt. acc. sg.) · *pattharitvā* having spread out (absol.) · *vihāraṁ* the dwelling (m. acc. sg.) · *pāvisi* entered (3 sg. aor.)

    "Now at that time a certain monk spread a robe out in the open air and went into his dwelling."

    The finite verb is *pāvisi*, an aorist of *pavisati* 'enters'. The augment is inside the long *ā*, and only that remains of it. The chapter's rule puts the augment between prefix and root, which would give *pa* + *a* + *vis*; and *a* + *a* is *ā*, so the augment and the prefix's own vowel have run together into a single long syllable at the front of the word. DPD's construction field says so (*pa* + *a* + √*vis* + *i*), and that is the last step a reader cannot take by eye, because a lengthened *pa-* and a *pa-* with an augment behind it look identical. *Ajjhagā* in item 2 is the same rule with the augment left standing where you can see it.

    Set it beside the chapter's other note, that the augment is *often dropped* after a prefix ending in *-a*, so *pahāsi* stands without one. The same environment has two outcomes, and the surface of the word does not distinguish them. A derivation is a second claim, and it is settled in a dictionary, not by looking.

24. *tena kho pana samayena* now at that time · *sambahulā* several (m. nom. pl.) · *bhikkhū* monks (m. nom. pl.) · *gijjhakūṭā pabbatā* from the Vulture Peak mountain (m. abl. sg.) · *orohantā* coming down (m. nom. pl. pres. part.) · *sīhavighāsaṁ* a lion's leavings (m. acc. sg., *sīha* + *vighāsa*) · *passitvā* having seen (absol.) · *pacāpetvā* having had it cooked (absol. of the causative *pacāpeti*) · *paribhuñjiṁsu* they ate (3 pl. aor.)

    "Now at that time several monks, coming down from the Vulture Peak mountain, saw a lion's leavings, had them cooked, and ate them."

    Only *paribhuñjiṁsu* is finite: a type 4 *is*-aorist, third person plural, on the present stem of *paribhuñjati*. The other three are not: *orohantā* is a present participle agreeing with *bhikkhū*, and *passitvā* and *pacāpetvā* are absolutives. None of the three carries a tense of its own, and all three take their time from the one finite verb at the end. That is the ordinary pattern of Pāli narrative, and so the last word of a sentence is often the first one to look for.

    *Pacāpetvā* is the absolutive of *pacāpeti*, which DPD gives as the causative of *pacati* 'cooks': 'makes cook, causes to cook'. The monks did not cook; they had the meat cooked. Chapter 9 sets out the *-āpe-* causative and cites the rule against digging, where *khaṇati* stands beside *khaṇāpeti* in the same pair. The distinction matters: what a monk may do himself and what he may have done for him are different questions in the Vinaya, and here the whole of it rests on four letters in the middle of an absolutive.

**What happened next.**

25. *aññataro* a certain (m. nom. sg.) · *bhikkhu* monk (m. nom. sg.) · *mā* let not · *ayaṁ* this (m. nom. sg.) · *patto* bowl (m. nom. sg.) · *nassi* be lost (3 sg. aor.) · *ti* (quotation) · *paṭisāmesi* put it away (3 sg. aor.) · *so* he (m. nom. sg.) · *nikkhamitvā* having come out (absol.) · *taṁ bhikkhuṁ* that monk (m. acc. sg.) · *pucchi* asked (3 sg. aor.) · *āvuso* friend (m. voc. sg.) · *mayhaṁ* my (1 sg. gen.) · *patto* bowl (m. nom. sg.) · *kena* by whom (m. inst. sg.) · *avahaṭo* taken away (m. nom. sg., p.p.) · *evam·āha* < *evaṁ* thus + *āha* said (3 sg. perf.) · *mayā* by me (1 sg. inst.) · *taṁ* him (m. acc. sg.) · *ādiyi* seized (3 sg. aor.) · *a·samaṇo·si* < *a-* not + *samaṇo* an ascetic (m. nom. sg.) + *asi* you are (2 sg. pres.) · *tvaṁ* you (nom. sg.)

    "A certain monk, thinking 'may this bowl not be lost', put it away. The other came out and asked that monk: 'Friend, by whom was my bowl taken?' He said: 'It was taken by me.' He seized him: 'You are no ascetic!'"

    Three aorists of three types, and one form that is not an aorist at all. *Paṭisāmesi* is type 3, an *s*-aorist on an *-e-* stem (DPD gives *paṭisāmeti* as the causative of *paṭisamati*, *pati* + *sāme* + *ti*), so it is formed like the *uppādesi* and *cāvesi* of the chapter's four-case frame, and shares their ambiguity with a second person singular present. *Pucchi* and *ādiyi* are type 4, *is*-aorists in *-i*, the productive type; DPD gives *ādiyi* from *ādiyati*, √*ādā*, and glosses it both 'took, grabbed' and 'stole', and the sentence depends on that.

    The fourth is *āha*, the perfect of item 4, printed joined as *evamāha*: DPD's sandhi entry gives *evaṁ* + *āha*, indeclinable plus perfect. Here the form has its ordinary use, the Vibhaṅga narrative's 'he said', and *evamāha* has 423 root-text occurrences. Item 4 had the plural in a verse; this is the singular in the plainest prose in the canon.

    ***Mā* here governs an aorist, and the sense is a wish. *Nassi* is the third person singular aorist of *nassati* 'perishes, is lost' — DPD's *nassa* + *i*, from √*nas* — so the construction is exactly item 18's, *mā* plus the aorist with its past sense gone. But nobody is being forbidden anything: the monk is not addressing the bowl, and there is no second person in the clause at all. What he is doing is wishing, and the chapter says the construction shades that way — "*mā bhāyi* is as much 'may you not be afraid' as 'don't be afraid'". Here there is no second reading available**, which makes this the cleaner instance of the two.

    The case ends a little later in an acquittal: *anāpatti, bhikkhu, niruttipathe*, "there is no offence, monk, in a manner of speaking" (Bu Pj 2 — Vin iii 57). The monk who said "it was taken by me" meant that he had picked it up, and *avaharati* is the Vinaya's word for theft; he used the word and not the intention, and the Buddha declines to convict on a word. The Vibhaṅga then runs the same situation once more with a nun in place of the monk (Bu Pj 2 — Vin iii 57), which is how the case law usually proceeds.

**Put it back into Pāli.**

26. Four phrases, and the tense is the whole of the exercise:

    (a) ***na kāhasi*** (Dhp 154 — Dhp p. 23): second person singular future of *karoti*, in its contracted *-h-* form. *Na karissasi* is correct Pāli and loses the verse's metre, which is all that is wrong with it.

    (b) ***idhamāhu*** (Snp 1.8 — Sn p. 26): third person plural perfect of *āha*, with *idha* and the junction consonant in front of it. If it came out at all, the rarest tense in the language is in your mouth.

    (c) ***rattiṁ avaharissāmi*** (Bu Pj 2 — Vin iii 57): first person singular future of *avaharati*, regular in every part: root, *-issā-*, primary ending. Of the four, only this form can be built instead of remembered.

    (d) ***avahari*** (Bu Pj 2 — Vin iii 57): third person singular aorist of the same verb, type 4. Set (c) and (d) side by side and the whole chapter is in the two of them: same root, same prefix, one syllable of difference, and between them the difference between a plan and an offence.

## Answers: Passive, Causative and Denominative

**Sotānugata — met by ear.**

1. *ayaṁ* this (f. nom. sg.) · *vuccati* is called (3 sg. pres. pass.) · *jarā* old age (f. nom. sg.)

    "This is called old age."

    Nothing in the form tells you it is passive. *Vuccati* ends in *-ti*, the ordinary third person singular active ending, as *pacati* 'cooks' does. The information is in the **stem**: *vucca-* is a passive stem, built with *-ya-* on the root *vac-* 'say' and assimilated to *-cc-*. That is item 8's answer arriving before item 8 asks for it, and the chapter puts it first in its section on the passive.

    Note the gender of *ayaṁ*: it is feminine because *jarā* is, and to assume the masculine *ayaṁ* would be to read the pronoun of Chapter 5 without its agreement. Item 27 is the same sentence with a neuter subject, a little later in the same analysis.

2. *ruppati* is afflicted (3 sg. pres. pass.) · *ti* (quotation) · *kho* indeed · *bhikkhave* monks (m. voc. pl.) · *tasmā* therefore · *rūpaṁ* 'form' (n. nom. sg.) · *ti* (quotation) · *vuccati* it is called (3 sg. pres. pass.)

    "'It is afflicted', monks — that is why it is called 'form'."

    Both finite verbs are passives, and the sentence is set for that reason. You were listening for *vuccati*, not for *ruppati*, but DPD analyses *ruppati* as a passive too. The sutta is explaining the word *rūpa* by the verb it sounds like, and the explanation only works because the thing being defined is something that *has something done to it*. Make *ruppati* active, "it afflicts", and the definition says that form does the damage instead of taking it, the opposite of the teaching the sentence belongs to. Here the grammar carries the doctrine.

3. *mettaṁ* loving-kindness (f. acc. sg.) · *ca* and · *sabbalokasmi* in the whole world (m. loc. sg.) · *mānasaṁ* heart, mind (n. acc. sg.) · *bhāvaye* should develop (3 sg. opt., causative) · *aparimāṇaṁ* boundless (n. acc. sg.) · *uddhaṁ* above · *adho* below · *tiriyaṁ* across · *asambādhaṁ* unobstructed (n. acc. sg.) · *avera·masapattaṁ* < *averaṁ* without hatred (n. acc. sg.) + *asapattaṁ* without enmity (n. acc. sg.)

    "And towards the whole world one should develop a boundless heart of loving-kindness — above, below, across, unobstructed, without hatred, without enmity."

    *Bhāvaye* is built on *bhavati* 'is, becomes'. DPD gives the headword as *bhāvayati*, "caus of *bhavati*", from √*bhū*, and glosses it literally as 'causes to become'; the form here is its optative, contracted to *-e*. So the word behind the whole vocabulary of meditative development (*bhāvanā*, *bhāveti*, *bhāvita*) is the verb 'to be' with a causative on it, and it means to bring something into being.

    Some of that literal sense survives. Nobody hearing *bhāvaye* thinks 'causes to become'; the word means 'develops, cultivates', and a translator who reached for the literal would be writing about grammar instead of the mind. But the line goes beyond cultivation. It says *mānasaṁ bhāvaye aparimāṇaṁ*, and what is boundless is a state **made to exist** where it did not, more than a plant being tended. Item 22 asks you to build this form cold, and item 32 sets it against the future of the same root.

    DPD's headword is *bhāvayati*, in *-aya-*, where the chapter and this answer say *bhāveti*, in *-e-*. Both are current and both are the eighth class; Chapter 7's table gives the affix in those two forms.

    Two smaller things. *Sabbalokasmi* is a locative singular with its niggahīta dropped (DPD registers it as an irregular form of *sabbalokasmiṁ*), as verse often treats a syllable it has no room for. And *averamasapattaṁ* is two words, not a compound, *averaṁ* and *asapattaṁ*, joined by Chapter 2's rule that a final niggahīta may become *m* before a vowel.

4. "A brahmin fully quenched always sleeps at ease — one who does not cling to sensual pleasures, cooled, without attachment."

    Count the syllables and each of the four lines has **eight**: *sab-ba-dā ve su-khaṁ se-ti*, and so for the rest. That regularity is the *siloka*, the commonest metre in the canon and Chapter 18's subject, and hearing it before being told the rule is the right order to meet it in. Reading aloud here is *sajjhāya*, the technique the tradition has used to hold this text for two and a half thousand years, and it is spaced retrieval under another name.

    The verb that raises the chapter's question is ***limpati***. On the surface it has a consonant cluster before the *-ati*, the clue the chapter warns you not to trust, and it could be an ordinary present or a passive. DPD carries the headword twice, and analyses the two senses differently: *limpati 1* is `√lip + ṁa + ti`, an active with a nasal infix, 'smears, stains'; *limpati 2* is `√lip + ya + ti`, a true *-ya-* passive, 'is smeared, is stained, is stuck to'. The second entry cites this verse.

    So the question is whether *na limpati kāmesu* says that the brahmin does not smear himself with sensual pleasures or that he is not smeared by them. The form cannot decide, because two different formations have fallen together in it. A stem carries voice, as item 8 says; but where a stem and its own passive have converged, the voice has nowhere left to sit and the sense has to be read off the sentence. There the chapter's rule ends, and that limit is better met in a verse you have just said five times than in a footnote.

**Vacasā paricitā — rehearsed aloud.**

5. *idha* here · *bhikkhave* monks (m. voc. pl.) · *bhikkhu* a monk (m. nom. sg.) · *anuppannānaṁ* unarisen (m. gen. pl.) · *pāpakānaṁ* bad (m. gen. pl.) · *akusalānaṁ* unwholesome (m. gen. pl.) · *dhammānaṁ* of states (m. gen. pl.) · *anuppādāya* for the non-arising (m. dat. sg.) · *chandaṁ* desire (m. acc. sg.) · *janeti* generates (3 sg. pres. caus.) · *vāyamati* strives · *vīriyaṁ* energy (n. acc. sg.) · *ārabhati* arouses · *cittaṁ* the mind (n. acc. sg.) · *paggaṇhāti* exerts · *padahati* endeavours

    "Here, monks, a monk generates desire for the non-arising of unarisen bad, unwholesome states; he strives, arouses energy, exerts the mind and endeavours."

    One **causative**, *janeti*, from *jan-* 'be born'; the other four verbs are plain actives. The literal 'causes to be born' is still faintly visible, for one brings the intention into being, but the word means 'generates'. The relation is item 3's, and by now it should look like a pattern and not a coincidence: the vocabulary of practice is full of causatives that have stopped announcing themselves.

    This is the right-effort formula and you will meet it many times, and so it is worth having by heart, not merely parsed.

6. *idha* here · *ānanda* Ānanda (m. voc. sg.) · *bhikkhu* a monk (m. nom. sg.) · *sabbasaṅkhāresu* in all conditioned things (m. loc. pl.) · *aṭṭīyati* is troubled (3 sg. pres.) · *harāyati* is ashamed (3 sg. pres. denom.) · *jigucchati* is disgusted (3 sg. pres. desid.)

    "Here, Ānanda, a monk is troubled, ashamed and disgusted by all conditioned things."

    Three derived stems in a row, of two different kinds: *harāyati* is a denominative built on *hiri* 'moral shame', and *jigucchati* is one of the five surviving desideratives. The sources disagree about *aṭṭīyati*: Perniola and Oberlies both print it *attīyati*, a spelling that occurs nowhere in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana as a free-standing word. The formula is the one the chapter quotes from AN 3.39 — A i 145, set here in a different frame on purpose: it is a stock phrase, and stock phrases teach vocabulary for nothing.

**Manasānupekkhitā — gone over in the mind.**

**Recall.**

7. The **passive**, the **causative** and the **denominative**. Productive means the category can be extended to a verb no grammar lists: meet a causative you have never seen, and you can work it out and be right. The other two, desiderative and intensive, are unproductive, so the reverse is true of them, and the difference is practical as well as historical.

8. The **stem**. Say this one cold. A passive verb takes the ordinary active endings, so *vuccati* 'is called' and *pacati* 'cooks' are identical in their last three letters and opposite in voice. Warder and Oberlies state it in almost the same words, which is unusual agreement between a coursebook and a historical grammar. Item 1 is the shortest possible demonstration, and in item 4 the rule runs out.

9. *-ya-* straight onto a consonant-final root, where it assimilates and leaves a doubled consonant (*labbhati*); *-ī-* before *-ya-* on a vowel-final root (*pahīyati*, *dīyati*); and *-iya-* added to the present stem (*karīyati*, *desiyati*).

10. **One more.** An intransitive verb takes no object and its causative takes one; a verb with one object gives a causative with two. Warder works the arithmetic up to three and adds that in practice the three-object case is avoided.

11. Look it up; do not derive it. There are perhaps a dozen of them and each has gone its own way, so the derivation will not give you the meaning: *sikkhati* means 'trains', not 'wishes to be able', and *jigucchati* 'is disgusted', not 'wishes to keep clear of'. This is the opposite of the advice for the other three formations.

**Identify the formation.**

12. *labbhati* — **passive** of *labhati* 'gets', so 'is got, is obtained'. Route one: *labh* + *ya*, with the *y* swallowed by the *bh*. The tell is the doubled consonant in a verb whose root has a single one.

13. *kārāpeti* — **causative** of *karoti*, with the *-āpe-* suffix on a stem that is already causative (*kāreti*). 'Has something made', 'causes to be made'. Note that the second *-āp-* does not reliably add a second causation; Oberlies calls several of these blends.

14. *namassati* — **denominative** from *namas-* 'homage', so 'pays homage to', and it governs an accusative like any ordinary verb. DPD gives it a root √*namass* of its own instead, which the chapter footnotes: a denominative that has been a plain verb throughout the canon's history has, from a dictionary's point of view, become one.

15. *caṅkamati* — **intensive** of *kamati* 'walks', with the root reduplicated. One of only two intensives the canon uses as finite verbs, and the one whose intensive sense is still alive: it means walking to and fro. Item 30 has it in a sentence, beside its own noun.

16. *sādiyati* — **the first trap.** It has the form of a passive in *-iya-* and the sense of an active verb, 'consents to, accepts'. Oberlies's account is that this small class inherited part of the dead middle voice's work. Two hundred occurrences in the root texts, so it is not a curiosity.

17. *titikkhati* — **desiderative**, built on *tij-*, literally 'desires to endure' and meaning simply 'endures, is patient'. Not a trap in the same sense as 16 and 18, and the difference matters: those two mislead about the *form*, while this one is transparent in form and has merely drifted in meaning.

18. *dissati* — **the second trap.** It is the passive of *dassati* 'sees', so 'is seen', and its surface does not show it; it reads as an ordinary present of some root *diss-*. The doubled consonant is the only signal, and it is the same signal as in *labbhati*. Chapter 8's footnote on the aorists of *dassati* says why the active verb, and not this passive, heads that table.

19. *deseti* — **causative** of *dis-* 'point out', meaning 'teaches'. Like 17, transparent in form and drifted in sense: nobody hearing *deseti* thinks 'causes to point out'. Items 3 and 5 are two more of the same kind, so the drift is named, not noted once.

**Build the form.**

20. *labbhati*. *labh* + *ya* → the *y* assimilates to the preceding *bh*, giving *-bbh-*.

21. *pahīyati*. The root is *hā-* under the prefix *pa-*; being vowel-final it takes route two, *pa* + *h* + *ī* + *ya*, and the root's own vowel goes.

22. *bhāveti*. Root *bhū-*, strengthened to *bhāv-*, plus the causative *-e-*. Literally 'causes to be', and in use the ordinary word for developing or cultivating. Item 3 is the same stem in the optative, in a line most readers know by heart.

23. *kāreti* and *kārāpeti*. The root vowel is strengthened to *kār-* in both; the second adds *-āpe-* to the first. *Kāreti* also means, of a king, simply 'rules' — SN 11.12 — S i 229 has *rajjaṁ kāreti*.

24. *pahīyanti*. The passive stem *pahīya-* plus the ordinary third person plural *-nti*, which is the whole point of item 8. It has 135 occurrences in the root texts.

**Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — penetrated by view.**

**Read the sentence.**

25. *satisambojjhaṅgaṁ* the awakening factor of mindfulness (m. acc. sg.) · *tasmiṁ samaye* at that time (m. loc. sg.) · *bhikkhu* the monk (m. nom. sg.) · *bhāveti* develops (3 sg. pres. caus.) · *satisambojjhaṅgo* the awakening factor of mindfulness (m. nom. sg.) · *tasmiṁ samaye* at that time · *bhikkhuno* for the monk (m. gen. sg.) · *bhāvanāpāripūriṁ* fulfilment of development (f. acc. sg.) · *gacchati* goes (3 sg. pres.)

    "At that time the monk develops the awakening factor of mindfulness; at that time the awakening factor of mindfulness goes to fulfilment of development in him."

    The **causative** *bhāveti*, and beside it the abstract noun *bhāvanā* built from the same stem. Both are 'causing to be'. Seeing the verb and the noun in one sentence is the cheapest possible demonstration that a derived stem is a stem like any other and feeds the whole word-formation machinery.

26. *yo pana* whatever · *bhikkhu* monk (m. nom. sg.) · *pathaviṁ* the earth (f. acc. sg.) · *khaṇeyya* should dig (3 sg. opt.) · *vā* or · *khaṇāpeyya* should have dug (3 sg. opt. caus.) · *vā* or · *pācittiyaṁ* an offence entailing confession (n. nom. sg.) · *ti* (quotation)

    "Whatever monk should dig the earth or should have it dug: an offence entailing confession."

    Repeated from the chapter on purpose, for a different use here. The rule depends on the syllable *-āp-* alone: the same optative ending on two stems, one plain and one causative. The Vinaya needs the distinction between doing a thing and getting a thing done, and Pāli supplies it morphologically where English needs a separate construction.

**Put it back into Pāli.**

27. idaṁ vuccati maraṇaṁ. (SN 12.2 — S ii 3)

    The neuter *idaṁ* here where item 1 had the feminine *ayaṁ*, because *maraṇaṁ* is neuter and *jarā* feminine. The two sentences stand a little apart in the same analysis of dependent origination and differ only in that. If you produced *ayaṁ vuccati maraṇaṁ* you have made a real mistake and a useful one.

28. desetu, bhante, bhagavā dhammaṁ, desetu sugato dhammaṁ. (SN 6.1 — S i 137)

    Brahmā Sahampati's request, and the reason there is a dispensation at all. The verb is the **causative** *deseti* in the third person singular imperative, *desetu*, 'let him teach'. The easiest thing to get wrong is *bhagavā*, which is **nominative** and not vocative: it is the subject of the imperative. And the clause is a small lesson in deferential Pāli, because the vocative *bhante* is also here, addressed to the same person. Brahmā speaks *to* the Buddha and *about* him in one breath, saying in effect "Sir, let the Blessed One teach." The third person imperative makes that possible, and English has to reach for *let* to do the same thing.

29. Na ca mamāyetha kiñci lokasmiṁ. (Snp 4.14 — Sn p. 180)

    *Mamāyetha* is the **denominative** *mamāyati*, built on *mama*, the genitive of the first person pronoun, so literally 'to me-ify', in the optative middle. Seven occurrences in the root texts. If you produced an active *mamāyeyya* the grammar is sound and the line is not: this is verse, and the middle is where verse lives, which is Chapter 7's point about the *attanopada* being chosen for its sound.

**Read at length.**

30. "Now at that time the Blessed One, having risen at the time before dawn, was walking up and down in the open air. The Blessed One saw the householder Anāthapiṇḍika coming from a distance. Seeing him, he came down from the walking path and sat on the seat made ready. Seated, the Blessed One said this to the householder Anāthapiṇḍika: 'Come, Sudatta.' Then the householder Anāthapiṇḍika, thinking 'the Blessed One addresses me by name', delighted and elated, fell at the Blessed One's feet with his head right there and said this to the Blessed One: 'I trust, venerable sir, that the Blessed One slept well?'"

    - *Caṅkamati* is the **intensive verb**, 'walks up and down'; *caṅkamā* is the **ablative singular of the noun** *caṅkama*, the walking path itself, so 'having come down from the walking path'. The noun is a physical object with a step down from it, so the verb means a located, constructed activity, the walking meditation the monasteries built paths for, and not vague pacing. Take *caṅkamā* for another verb form and the step is lost, and the seat with it.
    - *Orohitvā* is 'having come down from', an absolutive of *orohati*. *Voropeti* is its causative: it **causes someone to come down from** something, and the thing they are brought down from stands in the ablative. The canon uses it for killing: *jīvitā voropeti*, 'brings down from life'. You can now see how the euphemism is built.
    - *Sukhamasayittha* is *sukhaṁ* + *asayittha*, and *asayittha* is a **middle aorist**, third person singular, Chapter 7's *attanopada* endings on Chapter 8's aorist. It is used here, and not an ordinary *-i* aorist, because this is a formal, deferential enquiry: the middle in Pāli is largely a register and not a reflexive, and Warder's word for what it adds is "dignity". Note how it has to be looked up: *sukhamasayittha* has one occurrence in the root texts and the bare *asayittha* has **none**, because the edition never separates the two words. Look for the verb alone and you will conclude it does not exist.
    - There is no passive here because every action in the passage has a named agent acting on purpose. The Buddha walks, sees, descends, sits, speaks; Anāthapiṇḍika thinks, falls, speaks. Pāli reaches for the passive when the agent is unknown, unimportant, or suppressed on purpose: *pahīyanti*, 'they are abandoned', with nobody abandoning them. Narrative with characters in it is the wrong environment for the construction, and noticing that matters more than being able to form it.

**Reaching back.**

31. Both are true, and they are statements at two different levels. The eighth class (*curādigaṇa*) is a synchronic filing category: it collects the verbs whose present stem ends in *-e-* or *-aya-*, and it does not ask where the stem came from. The causative is a derivational statement: it says *deseti* was built from *dis-*. The class is large *because* the causatives and many denominatives ended up in it, and so Warder says the causative "formally coincides with the seventh conjugation" in his numbering and why he adds that grammarians disagree about how to file individual words. If you can conjugate it, the class is what you need; if you want to know what it means, the derivation is.

32. *Bhavissati* is the future: the ordinary stem, the tense sign *-issa-*, and the ordinary ending, *bhav* + *issa* + *ti*, with no change to the root vowel. *Bhāveti* is the causative present: the root vowel strengthened to its *vuddhi* grade, *bhū* → *bhāv-*, and then *-e-* plus the ending. They can be told apart on sight by where each is marked. The future marks itself in the *suffix* and leaves the root alone; the causative marks itself in the *root* and its suffix is only a vowel. Whenever a verb of *bhū-* has a long *ā* in the root, it is not a future.

33. *Soḷasī* is a **fractional numeral**, 'a sixteenth', formed from the cardinal *soḷasa* 'sixteen' with a feminine *-ī*; *soḷasiṁ* is its accusative singular, agreeing with *kalaṁ*, the accusative singular of *kalā* 'a part, a fraction'. So *kalaṁ nāgghanti soḷasiṁ* is 'they are not worth a sixteenth part', with the thing compared understood from the preceding lines: a hundred elephants, a hundred horses, a hundred chariots and a thousand maidens are not worth a sixteenth of one step towards the Buddha. The same idiom closes Dhammapada 70. Chapter 6 treats the fractions; what this adds is that the construction is an accusative of measure with a negated *agghati*, and that the idiom is fixed at *sixteen* and not at any round number.

34. Not because *me* would be the wrong case. That is the tempting answer and Chapter 5 refutes it in advance: the Pāli enclitics have spread well beyond their Sanskrit case range, and Wijesekera gives *te* doing duty as an accusative outright. The reason is the other property Chapter 5 gives them: an enclitic is unstressed, leaning on the word before it, and the whole content of Anāthapiṇḍika's thought is in the stress. He is not reporting that he was addressed; he is astonished that the Blessed One addressed *him*, by name, having never met him. An enclitic cannot carry that, and *maṁ* can. The position rule does not settle it here, since *maṁ* is not sentence-initial and *me* would have been positionally legal.

**A causative in the case law.**

35. *atha kho* then · *bhagavā* the Blessed One (m. nom. sg.) · *etasmiṁ nidāne* on this occasion (nt. loc. sg.) · *etasmiṁ pakaraṇe* in this connection (nt. loc. sg.) · *bhikkhusaṅghaṁ* the Sangha of monks (m. acc. sg.) · *sannipātāpetvā* having had assembled (absol. of the causative *sannipātāpeti*) · *chabbaggiye bhikkhū* the monks of the group of six (m. acc. pl.) · *paṭipucchi* questioned (3 sg. aor.)

    "Then the Blessed One, on this occasion and in this connection, had the Sangha of monks assembled and questioned the monks of the group of six."

    The causative is ***sannipātāpetvā***, the absolutive of *sannipātāpeti*, which DPD gives as the causative of *sannipatati* 'assembles, comes together', literally 'causes to fall down together'. The formation is the *-āpe-* of this chapter, laid on a verb that already carries two prefixes: *saṁ* + *ni* + *pātāpe* + *ti*.

    The word is there for the difference it makes. A Sangha assembles, *sannipatati*; the Buddha does not. Without the causative the sentence would say that he assembled, as one of those coming together, and the passage would lose its subject. With it, he has the assembling done, and the monks assemble. A causative does not add a doer. It moves the one already there up a step and puts a new one underneath.

    Note also that the second verb, *paṭipucchi*, is not a causative at all despite the length of the word. The *paṭi-* is a prefix and the *-i* is an aorist ending; and the check comes down to this: there is no *-e-* or *-āpe-* between the stem and the ending.

## Answers: Participles

**Sotānugata — met by ear.**

1. Idha, mahāli, bhikkhu tiṇṇaṁ saṁyojanānaṁ parikkhayā sotāpanno hoti avinipātadhammo niyato sambodhiparāyaṇo. (DN 6 — D i 156)

    *Sotāpanno* is the word, a past participle used as a noun. DPD resolves it *sota* + *āpanna*, and *āpanna* is the past participle of *āpajjati*, from √*pad*: 'entered into, fallen into'. So "stream-enterer" is a description, one who has entered the stream, more than a title. The chapter's "as nouns" section describes this pattern, and *Buddho* is its other famous instance.

    *Niyato* is the second real participle, 'bound, fixed', from *niyameti*. The other two are not participles, though they are built out of participial material: *avinipātadhammo* is "having the property of not falling away" and *sambodhiparāyaṇo* "having awakening as destination", both possessive compounds of Chapter 13's kind. A sentence describing a spiritual attainment leans on this chapter's whole apparatus, and three of its four long words are formed from verbs.

2. Tiṭṭhaṁ caraṁ nisinno va, Sayāno yāvatāssa vitamiddho; (Snp 1.8 — Sn p. 26)

    Three endings across four words. *Tiṭṭhaṁ* and *caraṁ* are present participles in *-aṁ*. DPD parses both as the masculine nominative singular of *tiṭṭhanta* and *caranta*, *tiṭṭha* + *nta* + *aṁ* and *cara* + *nta* + *aṁ*, so the *-nt* stem is there and the nominative has simply worn down to a niggahīta. *Nisinno* is a past participle of *nisīdati*, read actively, which is the chapter's point about verbs of posture. And *sayāno* is the present participle in **-āna**, DPD's *saya* + *āna* from √*sī*.

    *Sayāno* is the answer to item 11 as well. It is the older ending, kept by a handful of verbs, with no *-m-* in it at all, and it stands here beside two participles of the commoner type so that the difference can be heard as well as described. *Karoti*'s *kurumāno*/*kubbamāno* is the other place a reader will meet it.

    So the line puts a past participle between two present participles and reads all three the same way, actively, as descriptions of what the man is doing. None of them is passive, or could be, because standing, walking, sitting and lying down have no object for a passive to act on.

    *Yāvatāssa* is *yāvatā* + *assa*. DPD gives *yāvatā* as an indeclinable, the ablative singular of *yāva* used adverbially, 'as long as, to the extent that'. And *vitamiddho* is *vīta* + *middha*, 'with drowsiness gone'. Neither is a participle; both are here because the line does not divide where a reader expects.

3. The four pairs, of which two are printed above. (SN 56.11 — S v 422)

    The difference is that one has happened and the other has not yet. *Pariññeyyaṁ* says the truth of suffering is something a person is *to* fully understand; *pariññātaṁ* says it has been fully understood. Same verb, same neuter singular, agreeing with the same *ariyasaccaṁ*, and the whole distance between a task and its completion lies in the suffix.

    Formally: *pariññeyya* is the **gerundive**, which DPD parses *pari* + √*ñā* + *eyya*, ptp of *parijānāti*; *pariññāta* is the **past participle** of the same verb. *Pahātabbaṁ* and *pahīnaṁ* are the same pair on *pajahati*, and *pahātabbaṁ* is item 21's build target. The third and fourth truths run *sacchikātabbaṁ* against *sacchikataṁ* and *bhāvetabbaṁ* against *bhāvitaṁ*; and *sacchikātabba*, DPD says, is *sacchi* + *kātabba*, which is item 14's word with a noun welded to the front of it.

    Two things come out of this beyond the parsing. The first is that the sermon's architecture is grammatical. The turnings are twelve, not four, because each truth is stated, then given a task, then reported done, and the second and third of those are one participle each. The chapter calls the gerundive's name misleading because it is about **obligation** and not futurity; here is the whole doctrinal weight of that distinction in one passage.

    The second is that *bhāvetabbaṁ* and *bhāvitaṁ* are built on the causative set 09 spent an item on. *Bhāveti* is 'causes to become', so what the fourth truth says is that the path is *to be brought into being* and then that it has been. The causative has not stopped meaning what it means; it has only stopped announcing it.

4. Yathā yathā vā panassa kāyo paṇihito hoti tathā tathā naṁ pajānāti. (MN 10 — M i 57)

    *Paṇihito* is a past participle of *paṇidahati* 'directs, disposes', DPD's *pa* + *ni* + √*dhā* + *ita*, literally 'placed down forward'. The verb is transitive and the participle is accordingly passive: the body is what has been positioned.

    A generalising sentence of this kind needs a participle, and the sentence before it shows why. At 6.1 the sutta lists four postures with a finite verb apiece (going, standing, sitting, lying down), and then this sentence replaces the whole list with *yathā yathā … paṇihito hoti*, "however it is positioned". A finite verb has to name a particular action; a participle names a **state**, and a state can be left indefinite. So the summary of a list of verbs is a participle and not a fifth verb.

**Vacasā paricitā — rehearsed aloud.**

5. 'Khīṇā jāti, vusitaṁ brahmacariyaṁ, kataṁ karaṇīyaṁ, nāparaṁ itthattāyā'ti pajānāti. (SN 22.79 — S iii 90)

    *Karaṇīyaṁ* alone is not a past participle. It is the gerundive of *karoti*, 'what is to be done', and it stands immediately beside *kataṁ*, the past participle of the very same verb. So *kataṁ karaṇīyaṁ* is item 3's construction in miniature: the task and its completion, one verb, two participles, two words. Every one of the four pairs in the first sermon is built the same way, and a reader meets the construction most often in this formula.

    The other three are past participles: *khīṇā* from *khīyati* (DPD: √*khī* + *na*), *vusitaṁ* from *vivasati* (*vi* + √*vas* > *us* + *ita*), *kataṁ* from *karoti*. Item 28 asks what voice each carries.

    There is no finite verb in the declaration at all until *pajānāti* arrives to report it. Four participles carry what may be the most important sentence in the canon, and the reporting verb is outside the quotation. That is the chapter's closing argument, and it is best met by having said the sentence forty times.

6. Adinnaṁ nāma yaṁ adinnaṁ anissaṭṭhaṁ apariccattaṁ rakkhitaṁ gopitaṁ mamāyitaṁ parapariggahitaṁ. Etaṁ adinnaṁ nāma. (Bu Pj 2)

    All seven are past participles and all seven stand in the neuter nominative singular, agreeing with *yaṁ* and through it with *adinnaṁ*, the word being defined. They are from *dadāti* 'gives', *nissajjati* 'lets go', *pariccajati* 'relinquishes', *rakkhati* 'guards', *gopeti* 'protects', *mamāyati* 'treats as one's own', and *pariggaṇhāti* 'takes possession of' with *para-* 'another' in front.

    The first three carry the negative prefix and the last four do not, and the list is built on that division. The definition says first what has *not* happened to the object (it has not been given, let go of, or relinquished) and then what *has*: someone is guarding it and treats it as theirs. Both halves have to hold, so the order is not arbitrary, and the list is hard to rebuild. *An-* appears before the vowel of *anissaṭṭhaṁ* and *a-* before the consonants of *adinnaṁ* and *apariccattaṁ*, which is the rule this chapter gives for the negated participle.

    The neuter needs a moment. Nothing in the sentence names a thing that is neuter; the gender comes from *adinnaṁ* itself, a past participle used as a noun, 'the not-given', and everything downstream agrees with that. A participle can be the head as well as the modifier, and when it is, it fixes the agreement for whatever follows. Item 1's *sotāpanno* is the same move in the masculine.

**Manasānupekkhitā — gone over in the mind.**

**Recall.**

7. A participle carries **tense from the verb** and gender, case and number agreement from the adjective. It is a verb form that behaves grammatically like an adjective.

8. A transitive verb's past participle is passive; an intransitive verb's is active. *Desita* 'taught' describes what was done to something; *gata* 'gone' describes what the subject itself did. Item 28 tests the rule and finds where it needs sharpening.

9. **The past active participle** (*-tavant/-āvin*, as in *vusitavā*, *bhuttāvī*) and **the bare-*-a* gerundive** (*dukkara*, *sulabha*). The first is built by adding *-vant-* or *-in-* to an ordinary past participle; the second is a direct, unproductive survival of the Sanskrit *sukara-/duṣkara-* pattern, with no *-tabba* or *-(a)nīya* affix at all.

10. Because it names a shape most sources also call the "future" participle even though it is built on the **base**, not the future stem, and means **obligation** ('to be done'), not futurity. A true active participle on the future stem exists (Oberlies's *marissaṁ* 'about to die') and is not what the common label refers to. Item 3 shows the distinction carrying doctrine.

11. ***Atthi.*** *Sayāno* 'lying down' and *santo* 'being' both belong to this pattern. *Sayāno* is *saya-*, the present stem of *sayati* (√*sī*), + *-āna*, and *atthi*'s own present participle is one of the handful of verbs that keeps the older ending. *Karoti* keeps it too, in *kurumāno*/*kubbamāno*. Item 2 has *sayāno* in a line.

**Identify the formation.**

12. *gato* — **past participle, active voice**, of *gacchati* 'goes'. Not a trap by itself, but it is the chapter's central example: an intransitive verb's participle read actively, 'having gone', not 'having been gone'.

13. *desito* — **past participle, passive voice**, of *deseti* 'teaches'. *Deseti* is transitive, so the ordinary rule applies without complication.

14. *kātabbaṁ* — **gerundive**, *-tabba* type, of *karoti* 'does'. 'To be done.' Item 3's *sacchikātabbaṁ* is this word with *sacchi* welded to the front.

15. *vusitavā* — **past active participle**, *-vant* type, of *vivasati* (via its past participle *vusita* 'lived', *vi* + √*vas*, as item 5 gives it). 'One who has lived (the spiritual life).' **Trap:** this looks like it might be a present participle in *-vant/-mant* on first sight, because the possessive-adjective paradigm shares that suffix — but it is built on a *past* participle, not on a present stem, and its sense is unambiguously past and active.

16. *karonto* — **present participle, active voice**, of *karoti* 'does'. Ordinary *-nt* type on the irregular present stem *karo-*.

17. *dukkaraṁ* — **gerundive**, bare-*-a* type, of *karoti*, with the prefix *du-* 'hard, ill' in the role a suffix fills everywhere else. 'Hard to do.'

18. *asantaṁ* — **present participle**, negated, of *atthi* 'is, exists'. **Trap:** it looks like an ordinary negative adjective (compare *asanta* 'bad, wicked', a separate lexical item built the same way) and is, grammatically, the negation of the present participle *santaṁ* 'being'. The two readings coexist in the dictionary, and only the sentence decides which is meant.

19. *sotāpanno* — **past participle**, substantivised, of *sotaṁ āpajjati* 'enters the stream'. A compound past participle used as a noun, the pattern the chapter's "as nouns" section describes for *Buddho*. Item 1 has it in its own sentence and item 28 asks what voice it carries.

**Build the form.**

20. *dinnaṁ* — *-na* type past participle of *dadāti*, the root *dā-* taking *-na* directly. 323 root-text occurrences. The bare stem *dinna* occurs as a free word only eleven times and inside compounds nearly two thousand, as a citation form usually does beside its word.

21. *pahātabbaṁ* — *pajahati*'s stem *pajaha-* loses its reduplication and prefix down to *pahā-* before the *-tabba* is added; the chapter's example. 564 root-text occurrences, 499 of them the Abhidhamma's *dassanena pahātabbā* formula; the bare stem *pahātabba* has ten. The canon's most consequential use of it is in item 3.

22. *vijitāvī* — the past participle *vijita* 'conquered' (from *vijayati*/*vijināti*, root √*ji*) plus *-āvī* (the *-in* type past active participle). Forty-three root-text occurrences, most of them the standard epithet of a righteous wheel-turning monarch, *cāturanto vijitāvī* 'conqueror of the four quarters'.

23. *jānanto* — ordinary *-nt* type on the present stem *jānā-*, with the stem vowel shortened before the ending as it is throughout the *-a* declension. Sixty-nine root-text occurrences.

24. *vedayamāno* — *vedeti*'s alternative stem *vedaya-* (the chapter's "*vedayamāno*" table entry) plus *-māna*. Fifty-five root-text occurrences.

**Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — penetrated by view.**

**Put it back into Pāli.**

25. **Gahakāraka diṭṭhosi.** *Diṭṭho* + *asi* fused, as item 31 prints it. The verse opens with this line.

26. **Pāṇo na hantabbo.** The chapter's DN 17 quotation gives this precept's neighbours; DPD's own citation for *hantabbo* happens to be a different sutta's identical line (the *Mahāsudassanasutta*), which is further evidence that the sentence is fixed, formulaic Pāli and not a paraphrase.

**Read at length.**

27. "'Suppavāsā, when a female noble disciple gives food, she gives the recipients four things. What four? She gives life, beauty, happiness and strength. Having given life, she becomes a partaker of life, whether divine or human. Having given beauty, she becomes a partaker of beauty, whether divine or human. Having given happiness, she becomes a partaker of happiness, whether divine or human. Having given strength, she becomes a partaker of strength, whether divine or human. Suppavāsā, when a female noble disciple gives food, she gives the recipients these four things.'"

    - **Dentī** is a **present participle**, feminine, of *deti* 'gives', and its feminine ending (*-antī*, the strong stem the chapter's grammar section names) shows before a single verb is read that the passage concerns a woman: Suppavāsā the lay disciple, addressed by name in the vocative in the same clause. The participle's agreement shows what a finite verb's ending could not have shown.
    - **Datvā** is an **absolutive**, 'having given', and not a participle at all, though it looks close enough to one that a reader might guess. Chapter 11 gives the absolutive a chapter of its own; here it is enough to notice that Pāli reaches for the absolutive, not a participle, when one action is finished before the next one starts.
    - **Bhāginī** 'sharer, partaker' *looks* like a past active participle in *-in* and is not. DPD roots it in *bhāga* 'portion, share' plus the ordinary possessive-adjective suffix *-in*, the device the chapter's *bhuttāvī*/*vijitāvī* class uses, but built on a noun instead of a verb's past participle. The resemblance is real, since both classes decline the same way, on the *ī*/*in*-stems of Appendix A.1. It is the kind of look-alike that Chapter 9's "recognising a derived stem" taught you to suspect, transposed here to participles.
    - No gerundive appears because the passage reports what happens, not what ought to. A gerundive would turn "she gives four things" into "four things are to be given", a rule instead of an observation, and this passage is built as a chain of consequence (*datvā … hoti*, 'having given, she becomes'), which needs a narrative sequence, not an obligation. Item 3 is the opposite case: there the obligation is the subject, and every second turning is a gerundive.

**Test the voice rule.**

28. Five of the six sort the way the rule predicts, and the sixth makes the rule need stating more carefully.

    Passive, from transitive verbs: ***pariññātaṁ*** (from *parijānāti*, 'has been fully understood'), ***pahīnaṁ*** (from *pajahati*, 'has been abandoned'), ***kataṁ*** (from *karoti*, 'has been done'). Each of these has a patient (the truth that is understood, the craving that is abandoned, the work that is done), and the participle describes the patient, not the doer.

    Active, from an intransitive verb: ***khīṇā*** (from *khīyati*, 'has run out'). English says "birth is exhausted" and the passive is English's, not Pāli's: nothing exhausted birth, birth ran out. DPD's gloss is 'consumed, spent, finished', and the verb takes no object at all.

    *Sotāpanno* looks like a counter-example and is not. *Āpajjati* takes an accusative (*sotaṁ āpajjati*, *āpattiṁ āpajjati*), so on a test of "does it take an object" it is transitive, and its participle is nevertheless active: a stream-enterer is one who has entered, not one who has been entered. The rule as the chapter states it survives, because the chapter states it in terms of the *patient*: "the thing described is the patient rather than the agent". The stream is a destination, not a patient, and a verb of motion takes an accusative of goal without there being anything for a passive to act on. *Gacchati* is the same: *gāmaṁ gacchati* takes an accusative and gives the active *gata*.

    So the sharper form of the rule is to ask whether the verb has a patient, not whether it can take an accusative. DPD's gloss for *āpanna* is 'fallen into, entered into', from √*pad*, the dictionary entry of a verb of motion.

    *Vusitaṁ* is hard, and it is better to say so than to force it. *Vivasati* 'lives' is not obviously transitive, and *brahmacariyaṁ* is a cognate accusative: the life that is lived, not a thing acted on. The participle agrees with *brahmacariyaṁ* in the neuter, as a passive participle would, and DPD glosses *vusita* as 'fulfilled, completed, finished'. English has no choice but "has been lived". Whether that makes the Pāli passive is a question the six-word formula cannot settle, and to leave it open is to read it correctly.

**Reaching back.**

29. It means 'having something made, causing to be done': the causative middle participle of *karoti*, built from *kāreti* + *-māna* the same way *bhāveti* + *-anta* gives *bhāvento*. Chapter 9 explains this. It taught *kāreti* as the causative and gave the rule that a causative's stem, not its ending, carries the extra sense. So *kārayamāna* is the wrong word for 'doing' because it already says 'causing to be done', and the Vinaya uses it of a monk having a hut or a robe made for him.

    As for the lookup: the root texts print this participle only in oblique cases, and overwhelmingly in the instrumental, *kārayamānena*, the agent by whom the thing is had made. There is no nominative *kārayamāno* in them at all. A search made in the citation form a dictionary files a word under therefore returns nothing, and returns it for a reason that has nothing to do with whether the word exists. Chapter 10's footnote records that this chapter's first draft made that mistake.

30. It inflects by the (*m/v*)*ant*-stems of Appendix A.1, the paradigm every other *-nt* present participle in this chapter uses, *bhagavant-* and *gacchant-* included. It is not the surprise it looks like, because *atthi*'s irregularity in Chapter 7 (*asmi*, *amhi*, *amha*) is confined to its **finite** conjugation, built on endings peculiar to a handful of ancient athematic verbs; its participle is derived the ordinary way, on the ordinary *-nt* stem *sant-*, and ordinary participles inflect by one paradigm regardless of how irregular the verb underneath them is.

**Say it aloud.**

31. "House-builder, you are seen! You will not build a house again. All your rafters are broken, the roof-peak demolished; the mind has gone to the unconditioned, it has reached the ending of craving."

    Both words share the root *saṅkhāra*/*saṅkharoti* 'put together, construct, condition', the root that also names the fourth aggregate and the whole doctrine of conditioned existence. *Visaṅkhataṁ* 'demolished' describes an ordinary roof-peak, built and now unbuilt. *Visaṅkhāragataṁ* 'gone to the unconditioned' describes the mind at the moment of awakening, in the same vocabulary. The verse uses the *same word twice on purpose*, so that the literal demolition of a house and the doctrinal "unconditioning" of a mind read as one event, the house-builder's trade supplying the vocabulary in which awakening itself is described. It is a striking piece of construction, and the tradition has been reciting it for two and a half thousand years.

    Set 08 took this verse for its tenses: *kāhasi*, the contracted future, and *ajjhagā*, the aorist with its augment showing. Coming back to it for its participles is the same discipline as the *sajjhāya* stages themselves: a line that has been said often enough has more in it than the thing you came for.

## Answers: Absolutives and Infinitives

**Sotānugata — met by ear.**

1. Idhāvuso, bhikkhu satthāraṁ upanissāya viharati … (DN 34 — D iii 284)

    *Upanissāya* is the word, an absolutive of *upanissayati* that no longer behaves like one. An ordinary absolutive is adverbial: it names a completed action and hands its agent to the main verb. This one is a postposition. It governs *satthāraṁ*, an accusative, and means 'depending on, close to'.

    So the form has crossed word classes without changing a letter, and the tell is that it has an object. *Nissāya* at item 18 is the same word without the prefix, and *ārabbha* 'concerning' and *paṭicca* 'dependent on' are two more of the class. What an absolutive-as-postposition buys is compression: one word carries both the relation and its object, where English needs a preposition and then a noun.

    Parse *upanissāya* as an ordinary absolutive and you will look for the monk to have done some depending and then done something else, and will not find the second verb until *viharati*, four words later, with *satthāraṁ* stranded.

2. Kicchena me adhigataṁ, halaṁ dāni pakāsituṁ; Rāgadosaparetehi, nāyaṁ dhammo susambudho. (SN 6.1 — S i 136)

    *Pakāsituṁ* is the infinitive, of *pakāsati* 'reveals, makes known': DPD's *pa* + √*kās* + *ituṁ*. It completes *halaṁ*, which DPD resolves as *hi* + *alaṁ*, 'there is indeed no need'. So the construction is impersonal, like *sakkā* + infinitive at item 19 and *sukaraṁ* + infinitive at item 4: a predicate of possibility, ease or sufficiency with no grammatical subject, completed by an infinitive.

    What English has to supply is the agent. "There is no need to reveal it" leaves the revealer unnamed and English tolerates that; a translator who wanted to name him would have to add "for me", and the Pāli has not. *Pakāsituṁ* is indeclinable and carries no person, and the impersonal *halaṁ* has no subject to lend it one.

    *Adhigataṁ* in the line above does have its agent, in the instrumental: *me*, 'by me'. That is the ordinary construction for a passive past participle, which set 09 taught: a transitive verb's participle is passive, and its agent stands in the instrumental. So the verse holds both patterns one line apart: an instrumental agent where the form can take one, and no agent at all where it cannot.

    The verse itself is the hesitation that the Brahmā request answers, so if you worked item 28 of set 09 you have now met both halves of that exchange. *Adhigataṁ* is from *adhigacchati*, whose aorist *ajjhagā* closed the verse in set 08's item 2.

3. Atha kho te bhikkhū sāvatthiyaṁ piṇḍāya caritvā … ekamantaṁ nisīdiṁsu. (AN 10.27 — A v 49)

    The subject is *te bhikkhū*, and it is expressed once. Everything else in the sentence, three absolutives and two finite verbs, takes its agent from that one nominative plural. *Caritvā*, *upasaṅkamitvā* and *abhivādetvā* have no agent of their own and cannot have one, because they are indeclinable and have nothing to agree with; the reader supplies the monks and is right.

    That is the default the chapter states, and it makes a sentence of this length readable at speed. A chain of absolutives is Pāli's ordinary way of stringing actions together, and once you see that they all belong to the one subject you can run the whole chain without stopping. Set 08 took this same sentence for its aorists and asked which type each was; the three words between them are this chapter's.

    Notice also *piṇḍapātapaṭikkantā*, a past participle agreeing with *bhikkhū* in the nominative plural. Its function is like that of the absolutives around it, but it works differently: it *agrees*, and so it says something about the monks, not about the action. Item 30 is that difference stated as a rule.

4. Na dāni sukaraṁ amhehi lābhasakkārasiloke pariccajituṁ. (MN 76 — M i 524)

    **Instrumental**, *amhehi*, 'by us', and not nominative, because *sukaraṁ … pariccajituṁ* is an impersonal construction with no expressed grammatical subject of its own. There is nothing nominative for *amhehi* to compete with, and the instrumental steps in to mark the one who would do the giving-up.

    It is the same case set 09's passive uses for its agent, now with the equivalent function beside an **active** infinitive: *pariccajituṁ* means 'to give up', not 'to be given up'. So the instrumental here marks an impersonal predicate, not the passive, and if you take it for a passive you will find nothing to be passive.

    Item 2's *halaṁ* is the same construction with a different predicate, and item 19's *sakkā* the same again.

**Vacasā paricitā — rehearsed aloud.**

5. Sakkā bhoge ca bhuñjituṁ puññāni ca kātuṁ. Yannūnāhaṁ … (AN 4.122 — A ii 125)

    ***Bhuñjati* and *karoti* appear four times between them and never twice in the same form. The first sentence has the two infinitives, *bhuñjituṁ* and *kātuṁ*, completing the impersonal *sakkā*. The second has the two optatives**, *bhuñjeyyaṁ* and *kareyyaṁ*, first person singular — Chapter 7's mood, and the one Chapter 8 names for a hypothetical the speaker is entertaining about himself.

    The facts stay the same; who is in them changes. The first sentence states a bare possibility with no person in it at all: it *can* be done. The second puts a first person singular in the middle of that same possibility and wishes it. Nothing in the world has moved between the two sentences; the grammar has moved from an impersonal predicate to a personal mood, and that is all the slide consists of.

    Both sentences are **the same man's thought**, and the second follows the first immediately in the sutta. This is a monk reasoning himself out of the robe, and the passage is said aloud because the reasoning sounds so reasonable: the possibility is stated first, as a fact about the world, and the wish is then hung on it. Two absolutives carry the actual renunciation, *paccakkhāya* and *āvattitvā*, and they are the two words he says fastest.
