6. Bhagavāpi, bhante, etarahi … abhisambuddho'ti. (DN 16 — D ii 83)

    The predicate is *abhisambuddho*, a past participle, and there is no finite verb in the sentence at all. The copula is understood, as it regularly is with a nominative predicate. So the whole is held together by a chain of three non-finite forms, the two absolutives *pahāya* and *bhāvetvā* and the participle at the end, all sharing the one nominative subject *bhagavā*.

    In a sentence of thirty words, almost everything comes from this chapter. *Pahāya* is the *-ya* absolutive of *pajahati* and is item 22's build target; *bhāvetvā* is the *-tvā* absolutive of *bhāveti* and is item 20's. Both are tenseless, and item 27 depends on that: the same two words serve a past sentence, a future sentence and a present one without changing.

    *Supatiṭṭhitacitto* is a fourth non-finite element, a compound built on the past participle *patiṭṭhita*, agreeing with *bhagavā* in the nominative singular. Item 27 asks what it contributes.

**Manasānupekkhitā — gone over in the mind.**

**Recall.**

7. **Indeclinable.** An absolutive takes no case, gender or number ending at all, so it cannot agree with any noun. It does not describe a person or thing as an adjective or participle does; it modifies the *action* of the main verb adverbially: "having done X" attaches to the whole clause, not to any one noun in it.

8. **Default:** the absolutive's understood agent is the same as the main verb's subject (nominative if the main verb is active, instrumental or genitive if it is passive). **The documented exception:** a dative or genitive noun can govern the absolutive on its own, naming an agent distinct from the main clause's grammatical subject — *paññāya cassa disvā āsavā parikkhīṇā honti* (MN 25 — M i 160), where *cassa* ('and for him') governs *disvā* while *āsavā* is the separate nominative subject of *honti*. Item 28 sets the two cases side by side.

9. ***-tvā*** (with the variants *-tvāna* and *-ttā*) and ***-(i)ya***. The sound change: a root ending in a short vowel inserts a *-t-* before the suffix, and *-tya-* then assimilates to *-cca-* (*paṭi* + *i* + *tya* → *paṭicca*). Oberlies counts eight suffixes in all; the rest are in Appendix A.4.

10. The **gerund** is this chapter's absolutive: a completed action, indeclinable, standing before the main verb. The **gerundive** is Chapter 10's future passive participle: an obligation still to be discharged, declined like an ordinary adjective. They share nothing but a look-alike name.

11. Because the cases, chiefly the dative, already cover much of the ground English covers with "to." Where English must reach for an infinitive ("in order to give"), Pāli often has a dative noun in *-āya* in the same role; Chapter 15 gives that overlap its own treatment.

**Identify the formation.**

12. *gantvā* — **absolutive**, *-tvā* type, of *gacchati* 'goes'. 'Having gone.'

13. *paccakkhāya* — **absolutive**, *-(i)ya* type (assimilated), of *paccakkhāti* (*paṭi* + *ā* + √*khā*). 'Having renounced, given up.' Item 5 has it doing the actual renouncing.

14. ***jahetvā***. It does not occur in the root texts and appears once in the commentaries, in a gloss in the Mahāniddesa commentary. Nor can it be hiding behind a sandhi, since it does not begin with a vowel. *hitvā* and *jahitvā* are both solidly canonical.

15. *desetuṁ* — **infinitive** of *deseti* 'teaches'. 'To teach.'

16. *cassa* — sandhi of *ca* + *assa*, 'and for him / and his'. The third-person pronoun's genitive and dative singular are identical (Appendix A.2), so the case cannot be settled from the form, and the sentence does not settle it either. For the construction it makes no difference: whichever it is, it is neither the nominative nor *āsavā*. The whole different-agent reading depends on this word, the tradition's *asamānakattuka*, 'having a different agent'.

17. *amhehi* — instrumental plural of *ahaṁ*, 'by us'. Marks the infinitive's agent in an impersonal *sukara*-type construction, even though *pariccajituṁ* itself reads actively ('to give up', not 'to be given up'). Item 4 is the sentence.

18. *nissāya* — absolutive of *nissayati*, here functioning as a **postposition**, 'depending on, close to', governing the accusative. Item 1 is its prefixed twin in a sentence.

19. *sakkā* — an old optative form, now an **indeclinable** meaning 'possible, one might be able'. Itself neither an absolutive nor an infinitive, it is the impersonal predicate an infinitive most naturally completes (*sakkā … kātuṁ*, 'it is possible to do'). Item 5 opens with it.

**Build the form.**

20. *bhāvetvā* — confirmed at item 6, part of the standard formula for developing the awakening factors.

21. *bhāvetuṁ* — already in the chapter's word list.

22. *pahāya* — *pa* + √*hā* + *ya*, the root taking the suffix directly since it ends in long *ā* (the same rule that gives *vihāya* 'having withdrawn'); the present stem's reduplication (*pajaha-*) belongs only to the finite conjugation and does not carry over, as Chapter 10 already showed for the gerundive *pahātabbaṁ*.

23. *sotuṁ* — √*su* + *tuṁ*, strengthened root, parallel to *netuṁ* from √*nī*.

24. *datvā* — already in the chapter's word list, and set 10's item 27 has it four times in one passage.

**Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — penetrated by view.**

**Put it back into Pāli.**

25. **Na sukaraṁ paresaṁ dhammaṁ desetuṁ.** Following the word order and vocabulary of AN 5.159 — A iii 184, minus the vocative. Item 4 is the same impersonal frame with its agent expressed.

26. **So taṁ dhammaṁ sutvā tathāgate saddhaṁ paṭilabhati.** DN 2 — D i 62, word for word, and DPD's citation for *paṭilabhati* independently quotes this sentence from a different sutta (the *Attantapasutta*), a second sign that it is fixed, formulaic phrasing and not a one-off composition.

**Read at length.**

27. "Whatever arahants, fully awakened ones, there have been, venerable sir, in the past — all those Blessed Ones, having abandoned the five hindrances, the defilements of the mind that weaken wisdom, with minds well established in the four applications of mindfulness, having truly developed the seven awakening factors, fully awakened to unsurpassed full awakening.

    And whatever arahants, fully awakened ones, there will be, venerable sir, in the future — all those Blessed Ones … will fully awaken to unsurpassed full awakening.

    And the Blessed One too, venerable sir, who is at present the arahant, the fully awakened one — … has fully awakened to unsurpassed full awakening."

    - The three finite predicates are *abhisambujjhiṁsu* (**aorist**, 'awakened': past buddhas), *abhisambujjhissanti* (**future**, 'will awaken': buddhas yet to come), and *abhisambuddho* (**past participle** standing as the predicate, with the copula understood: 'has awakened', read against *etarahi* 'now' as the present case). Three tenses, one sentence template.
    - The absolutives themselves carry no tense at all. *Pahāya* and *bhāvetvā* mean only that the abandoning and the developing are complete before whatever finite verb follows, so the same wording can serve a past-tense sentence, a future-tense sentence and a present-tense sentence without changing a single letter. Only the one finite (or participial) predicate at the end has to change; the whole non-finite chain in front of it is tenseless by nature and so is reusable across all three.
    - *Supatiṭṭhitacitto/supatiṭṭhitacittā* is built on ***patiṭṭhita***, the past participle of *patiṭṭhahati* 'stands firm, is established' (√*ṭhā*), compounded with *su-* 'well' and *-citta* 'mind'. The participle contributes the resultant, already-accomplished sense Chapter 10 taught for the intransitive past participle: "a mind that has become established," not "a mind that establishes", read as a standing state, not an ongoing action.
    - Saying it three times turns a claim about one teacher into a claim about the nature of awakening itself: whichever buddha is in view, past, future, or the one Sāriputta is speaking to, the same five hindrances are abandoned and the same seven factors developed. The repetition makes the argument. Sāriputta's declaration of faith (traditionally called his "lion's roar") rests on a pattern that holds regardless of which buddha fills in the blank, and the sentence's own grammar, one template running through three tenses, embodies that claim before a word of doctrine is drawn from it.

**Whose action is it.**

28. In item 3, *caritvā* is the monks', and you know because they are the only candidate. *Te bhikkhū* is the nominative subject of both finite verbs, no other word in the sentence is nominative, and monks are the sort of thing that can wander for alms. The default holds, and it holds so comfortably that the question does not arise on a first reading.

    In MN 25 — M i 160, *disvā* is the monk's, and the nominative subject of the sentence is not the monk. *Āsavā parikkhīṇā honti*, "the defilements come to an end": *āsavā* is nominative plural and is what *honti* agrees with. So the default reading would make the defilements see with wisdom, which is impossible. The test is plausibility, not word order or a rule about position. Defilements do not see. When a main clause's subject is an abstract noun and the sentence describes a consequence, not a sequence, the absolutive's agent is somebody else.

    The word that supplies him is *cassa*, *ca* + *assa*, 'and for him'. Its case cannot be settled from its form, because the third-person pronoun's genitive and dative singular are identical, and Appendix A.2 gives *assa* for both; the sentence does not disambiguate them either. For the construction it makes no difference. It is **not** nominative and **not** *āsavā*, and the tradition has a name for the pattern: *asamānakattuka*, 'having a different agent'.

    So the rule has two halves, and item 3 and this sentence show them. Ordinarily the agent is the main verb's subject, and to assume so is right nearly every time. Where the subject could not plausibly have done it, look for a genitive or dative, and read the absolutive as giving a cause, not a preceding event.

**Reaching back.**

29. The absolutive *labhitvā* ('having gained') serves as the protasis, read as "if they were to gain" and not "having gained", since the sentence as a whole is hypothetical. The optative *parihareyyuṁ* ('they would carry') carries the apodosis, matching Chapter 8's protasis/apodosis division for a conditional sentence. Here, though, the "if" half is an absolutive and not a verb in the conditional mood, and that is what this chapter adds to Chapter 8.

30. Neither *vatvāna* nor any absolutive carries a voice distinction; *vutta* does. *Vatvāna* is indeclinable. It has no gender, case or number slot to carry agreement in, and so nothing for a voice reading to attach to either; it says only that the saying is complete, and stays the same whether the sentence around it is active or passive. *Vutta*, by contrast, is a verbal *adjective*. Chapter 10's whole voice rule (a transitive verb's participle is passive) applies to it because it agrees with a noun and therefore has to say something about that noun's relationship to the action. The absolutive's freedom from voice follows directly from its freedom from agreement: a form with nothing to agree with has nothing to be active or passive *about*. Item 3 has both kinds standing in one sentence: three absolutives and one agreeing participle, *piṇḍapātapaṭikkantā*.

**Two subordinations in one sentence.**

31. *tena kho pana samayena* now at that time · *aññataro* a certain (m. nom. sg.) · *bhikkhu* monk (m. nom. sg.) · *saṅghassa* by the Sangha (m. gen. sg.) · *cīvare* robe-cloth (nt. loc. sg.) · *bhājīyamāne* being distributed (nt. loc. sg., pres. part. of the passive *bhājīyati*) · *theyyacitto* with theft in mind (m. nom. sg.) · *kusaṁ* the lot (m. acc. sg.) · *saṅkāmetvā* having shifted (absol. of the causative *saṅkāmeti*) · *cīvaraṁ* robe-cloth (nt. acc. sg.) · *aggahesi* took (3 sg. aor.)

    "Now at that time a certain monk, while robe-cloth was being distributed by the Sangha, shifted the lot with theft in mind and took a robe."

    The two constructions are a locative absolute and an absolutive, and the sentence uses each where the rule requires it. *Saṅghassa cīvare bhājīyamāne* is the locative absolute: its subject is *cīvare*, the cloth, and its agent is the Sangha in the genitive, neither of them the subject of the main verb. *Saṅkāmetvā* is the absolutive, and its doer is the monk, who is also the doer of *aggahesi*.

    That is Bodhi's rule, quoted in Chapter 16, applied in both directions in one sentence: where the prior action belongs to the subject of the main verb, the absolutive; where it belongs to someone else, the locative absolute with a participle. Here the distribution is the Sangha's business and the shifting is the monk's, and the grammar keeps the two apart without a pronoun anywhere. It is the same question item 28 asks, answered by the choice of construction, not by inference.

    *Saṅkāmetvā* is indeed a causative, of *saṅkamati* 'goes together, moves across', literally 'having caused to go across'. What he moved was a *kusa*, which DPD gives in this sense as a lot or ticket: the slips drawn to decide who receives which piece of cloth. He shifted his own and took a better robe, so the case is filed under theft and not under greed.

## Answers: Indeclinables and Particles

**Sotānugata — met by ear.**

1. Tesaṁ divā ca ratto ca, sadā puññaṁ pavaḍḍhati. (SN 1.47 — S i 33)

    *Divā* is an old instrumental; *ratto* is an old locative. The pairing is unremarkable because neither is behaving as a case any more. Both have frozen into indeclinable adverbs, and once a form has stopped taking part in the case system there is nothing left for a mismatch to be a mismatch *in*.

    To make the two agree is to apply a rule to words that have left its jurisdiction, and that is the difficulty of this whole chapter in one line: from here on, a word's form no longer shows its function. Item 21 asks for *divā* again, out of its sentence, and the answer is the same.

    *Ca* has its ordinary joining function here (*samuccaya*), between two words that are not the same case. That is a second small demonstration of the same point, since *ca* joins two adverbs, not two nominatives.

2. Ruppatī'ti kho, bhikkhave, tasmā 'rūpa'nti vuccati. (SN 22.79 — S iii 86)

    The first *iti* is causal (*hetu*); the second is the ordinary quotative. The first turns the clause 'it is deformed' into the *reason* for what follows; the second marks *rūpaṁ* as the name being applied.

    The first answers to *tasmā*. 'Therefore' picks the reason up and delivers the conclusion, and if the first *iti* is read as a quotation mark, *tasmā* answers to nothing at all. So when a sentence has a *tasmā* in it, look back for what supplies the 'because'.

    The "quotation mark" account of *iti* cannot reach this sense. Set 09 read the same sentence for its two passives, *ruppati* and *vuccati*; that reading and this one concern the same eight words and have nothing else in common.

3. Kiṁ nu kho, āvuso gotama, taṁ jīvaṁ taṁ sarīraṁ, udāhu aññaṁ jīvaṁ aññaṁ sarīran'ti? (DN 7 — D i 159)

    The two particles are ***nu***, which marks the whole utterance as a question, and ***udāhu***, which introduces the second of exactly two alternatives. Together they build what logic calls an exclusive disjunction: either the soul and the body are one thing, or they are two, and no third answer is on offer.

    So the question is one of the undeclared ones, a classic case, and the Buddha's refusal to answer it is a refusal of the frame. Here the frame is built out of two words that do not inflect. *Kiṁ* is a third: it is the interrogative pronoun, but standing at the head with *nu kho* it does little more than open the question.

4. Selo yathā ekaghano, vātena na samīrati; Evaṁ nindāpasaṁsāsu, na samiñjanti paṇḍitā. (Dhp 81 — Dhp p. 12)

    *Yathā* and *evaṁ* carry the simile, and the verse has no verb of comparison in it at all. English needs one ("as … so", or "is like", or "resembles"); Pāli sets the two clauses side by side with a correlative pair holding them together. Neither word inflects and neither refers to anything, yet between them they make the whole analogy.

    The two *na*s could not have been reduced to one, because they negate two different verbs in two different clauses: the rock is not shaken, the wise are not moved. Take the second away and the second clause says the opposite of what the verse means. *Na* is the word on the page a reader most easily skips, and here the sense depends on it twice.

    DPD gives *samīrati* as *saṁ* + *īra* + *ti* from √*īr* and *samiñjanti* as *saṁ* + *iñja* + *ti* from √*iñj*, two different roots of nearly the same meaning, one for the rock and one for the wise. The verse avoids repeating itself.

**Vacasā paricitā — rehearsed aloud.**

5. Ayaṁ kho me, samma, sāṇabhāro dūrābhato ca susannaddho ca, alaṁ me tvaṁ pajānāhī'ti. (DN 23 — D ii 351)

    *Alaṁ* refuses. It means 'enough of that', a declining of the offer, and not 'enough' in the sense of a quantity being sufficient; the dative *me* names the person for whom it is enough. English needs a clause where Pāli needs a syllable.

    The chapter files *alaṁ* under *paṭisedha*, denying and forbidding, alongside *na*, *no* and *mā*, because it closes something down instead of modifying anything. What follows it here, *tvaṁ pajānāhi*, "you know for yourself", is the polite equivalent of "suit yourself", and the two together are one of the canon's most human exchanges.

    Two smaller things for the rebuilding. *Me* appears twice in eleven words, genitive the first time and dative the second, with nothing in the form to tell them apart; and *ca … ca* joins two past participles, the simplest kind of *samuccaya*.

6. "Na sūpaṁ vā byañjanaṁ vā odanena paṭicchādessāmi bhiyyokamyataṁ upādāyā"ti sikkhā karaṇīyā. (Bu Sk 36 — Vin iv 192)

    Three of the four jobs are here, and the fourth is absent because a rule has nothing to join. *Na* is *paṭisedha*, denying; the pair *vā … vā* is *vikappana*, separating, in the arrangement 'A *vā* B *vā*' this chapter gives for *ca*; and *ti* is the quotative, closing what the monk is to say to himself. There is no *samuccaya* particle here and no filler, since a training rule has no line of verse to fill out and nothing to add.

    The *ā* of *upādāyā* is the quotative at work: *upādāya* + *iti* lengthens the final vowel and swallows the *i*, giving *upādāyāti*, which editors print with the mark inside the join. There is no form *upādāyā*, and to take the long vowel for part of the absolutive is to look for a stem that does not exist. The same junction makes every *sekhiya* rule end in a lengthened vowel, since all seventy-five close a first-person future with *ti*.

    *Upādāya* is itself an absolutive serving as a postposition. It is not one of the six this chapter names but belongs to that class, and it governs the accusative *bhiyyokamyataṁ*, as the rule for the class says. Item 35 sets the two chapters' accounts of that class against each other.

**Manasānupekkhitā — gone over in the mind.**

**Recall.**

7. *Samuccaya*, joining: *ca*, *pi*, *atha*. *Vikappana*, separating: *vā*, *udāhu*. *Paṭisedha*, denying and forbidding: *na*, *no*, *mā*, *alaṁ*. *Pūraṇattha*, filling: *kho*, *hi*, *vata*, *sudaṁ*, *khalu*. The scheme is the Saddanīti's, and the notable thing about it is that the fourth category exists at all: a Western list of parts of speech has nowhere to put a word whose job is to fill out a line.

8. Both Oberlies and Geiger are historical-comparative grammars, organised by inflection. Their method tracks sound changes through sets of endings, and an indeclinable has no endings for it to work on. So a word class with no morphology has no place in a description built entirely out of morphology. The gap belongs to the genre and is no oversight, and it meant that every form in this chapter had to be checked against the corpus instead of a grammarian.

9. It has *kiriyāvisesana*, 'specification of the action', with the subclass *bhāvanapuṁsaka*, the neuter accusative used adverbially. The expectation changes: in Pāli an adverb is a *use* of a word, not a kind of word, so there is no ending to look for, and the same string may be an adverb in one sentence and an ordinary case form in the next. You identify it by asking what it modifies. Item 1 is the shortest demonstration.

10. *Mā* governs the **aorist**. It has to be known because it inverts the reading of a form the reader has already learnt as a past tense: *mā avaca* means 'do not say', not 'you did not say'. Item 31 shows that even this is too narrow.

11. Derivative adverbs (formed by suffix), case-form adverbs, and pure adverbs. A word you cannot analyse belongs to the third group by definition. Duroiselle's "pure adverbs" are those which are neither derived nor case forms, and the inability to break them down is the criterion, not a failure of analysis.

**Identify the formation.**

The three traps are 14, 18 and 19.

12. *tato* — adverb, 'from there, thence, therefore'. Built on the distal pronominal stem *ta-* with the ablative-sense suffix *-to*. A derivative adverb; in most of its uses it has the force of a conjunction, drawing a consequence, as it does in item 36.

13. *sudaṁ* — a filler (*padapūraṇa*), emphatic, with no reliable English rendering. Not analysable as anything else, so a pure adverb in Duroiselle's terms. DPD records a second *sudaṁ* which is the sandhi of *su* + *idaṁ*, and the two are told apart only by what the sentence needs.

14. **Trap.** *kira* — a reportive particle, 'it is said, apparently', marking the sentence as not the speaker's own assertion. The trap is that *kira* is also an inflected form of the verb *kirati* 'scatters'. Nothing in the shape distinguishes them. The second trap inside the first: some grammars print *kila* beside it as though it were a Pāli variant, but *kila* is the Sanskrit form and occurs nowhere in this corpus.

15. *cirassaṁ* — 'at long last', a case-form adverb. Historically the genitive *cirassa* with a neuter accusative ending built on top of it, so it looks like two case endings at once. The plain genitive *cirassa* is also used adverbially but is rare; *cirassaṁ* is the form to expect.

16. *tikkhattuṁ* — 'three times', a derivative adverb: the numeral stem *ti-* with the suffix *-khattuṁ* 'times'. The suffix is fully productive: *sattakkhattuṁ* 'seven times' is built the same way. Set 06 met it as a numeral; item 34 sets it beside an adverb of the other kind.

17. *iṅgha* — an interjection, 'come on!, please!'. Analysable, and the item is here for that reason: DPD parses it as a second-person singular imperative of *iñjati* 'stirs, moves', frozen. It has stopped conjugating and become an indeclinable.

18. **Trap.** *bho* — an address particle, 'sir', and not an indeclinable at all. It is the vocative of *bhavant*, a declined form used as an interjection. To list it among the *nipātā* is to classify it by behaviour instead of morphology, and for this class that is the wrong way round.

19. **Trap.** *ekopi* — not a word to be looked up but two words written as one: *eko* 'one' with the enclitic *pi* 'even' fused onto it. The trap is that no dictionary carries it. This is the ordinary spelling of an enclitic in these editions, and the item tests whether you recognise the join.

20. *nanu* — an interrogative particle expecting the answer yes. Analysable as *na* + *nu*, a negative folded into a question, as English 'don't you …?' folds one in. A pure adverb by Duroiselle's classification, a filler by nobody's. Item 27 is it in a sentence.

21. *divā* — 'by day', a frozen instrumental, so a case-form adverb. Its companion *ratto* 'by night' is a frozen locative, and neither is any longer functioning as a case. Item 1 is the line they stand in.

**Build the form.**

22. *sabbattha*.

23. *kadā*.

24. *sattadhā*. (Built as *pañcadhā* and *bahudhā* are; the doubling in *sattakkhattuṁ* belongs to the *-khattuṁ* suffix, not to this one.)

25. The form the rule produces is ***mā gacchi***, and the item is really about the corpus check. *Mā gacchi* occurs once in a commentary and once in a grammarian's own conjugation table, which prints the whole run: *mā agami, mā gamā, mā gamī, mā gametu. Tvaṁ mā gaccho, mā gacchi, mā gacchāhi*. The canon has ***mā agamāsi*** 'do not go', in the Vinaya, with the augment kept.

    Two things follow. First, the rule is right: *mā* takes the aorist, and *mā gaccha* with an imperative, which is what an English speaker's instinct produces, is not the construction. Second, a correctly built form may still be unattested, and you cannot see the difference until you look. The book runs this check on every form it teaches.

26. *ito*. The proximal stem with *-to*, parallel to *tato* at item 12.

**Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — penetrated by view.**

**Read the sentence.**

27. *nanu* surely (interrog.) · *tvaṁ* you (nom. sg.) · *sālavatikaṁ* Sālavatikā (f. acc. sg.) · *ajjhāvasasi* you dwell in · *ti* (quotation)

    "Surely you live in Sālavatikā?"

    The questioner expects **yes**, and *nanu* shows it. The discourse bears it out: Lohicca answers *Evaṁ, bho Gotama*, 'Just so, master Gotama'. Had the particle been *nu kho*, as in item 3, the question would have been open. The two particles mark the difference between asking and checking.

28. The word is ***kudāssu***, and the particle inside it is ***ssu***, an emphatic that never stands as a free word in the root texts. Printed apart the two would be *kudā ssu*, and *kudā* 'when' does not stand alone in the root texts either, so a reader will meet either half only in the compound. The whole means 'when, oh when'.

    *kudā* when · *ssu* (emphatic) · *nāma* indeed · *imassa* of this (n. gen. sg.) · *dukkhassa* of suffering · *nissaraṇaṁ* escape (n. nom. sg.) · *paññāyissati* will be discerned (3 sg. fut. pass.) · *jarāmaraṇassa* of ageing-and-death · *ti* (quotation)

    "When, oh when will an escape be discerned from this suffering, from ageing and death?"

    This is the Bodhisatta before the awakening, and the force of the question lies in the emphatic. Item 19's *ekopi* is the same join in a less consequential place.

**Put it back into Pāli.**

29. *Mā evaṁ, āvuso sāti, avaca, mā bhagavantaṁ abbhācikkhi.* (MN 38 — M i 256)

    Watch the mood: both verbs are aorists, *avaca* and *abbhācikkhi*, and the prohibition is carried by *mā* alone. An imperative here would be wrong.

30. *Handa dāni, bhikkhave, āmantayāmi vo.* (DN 16 — D ii 155)

    *Handa* the interjection, *dāni* the adverb of time, and *vo* the enclitic second-person plural pronoun. Only *āmantayāmi* and the vocative *bhikkhave* inflect. Set 08 took this sentence for the two forms of its eighth-class stems; here, once the verbs are accounted for, almost the whole sentence is left.

**Read at length.**

31. "Come, Kālāmas. Do not go by oral tradition, by lineage of teaching, by hearsay, by a collection of scriptures, by logical reasoning, by inferential reasoning, by reasoned consideration, by acceptance of a view after pondering it, by the appearance of competence, or because 'the ascetic is our teacher'."

    - There are ten *mā*s and no aorist at all. *Mā* here governs an **instrumental**, and the verb it would have governed is left to be supplied: 'do not [go / be led] by …'. The chapter taught *mā* with the aorist because a reader meets that construction most; this passage shows that the particle forbids, and that what it attaches to varies.
    - *Anussavena*, *paramparāya*, *ākāraparivitakkena* are all **instrumental**, and the case expresses *means*: these are the grounds by which one might be persuaded. The sentence rests on the instrumental of means, and *mā* refuses every instance of it.
    - *Takkahetu* and *nayahetu* are not instrumentals at all. They are compounds ending in ***hetu*** 'cause', which this chapter listed among the nouns used prepositionally: 'by reason of logic', 'by reason of inference'. DPD parses *takkahetu* as *takka* + *hetu* and glosses it literally 'because of logic'. So the series is less uniform than it first looks. Check before assuming a pattern holds across a long list.
    - *Mā samaṇo no garū'ti*: "nor because 'the ascetic is our teacher'." The *iti* is **causal**, as in item 2: it closes a quoted reason and hands it to *mā*. Read as a plain quotation mark, it leaves the clause with no verb to attach to.
    - The repetition makes each item a separate refusal. Written once with a list, the sentence would say 'do not go by [this whole set]', leaving open the possibility that some smaller combination would do. Ten *mā*s close each door individually. The repeated form is also easier to recite and to remember, and in an oral literature that counts for as much.

**Reaching back.**

32. The rule is that the aorist after *mā* is **prohibitive**, not past. Remove the particle from item 29 and *evaṁ, āvuso sāti, avaca* becomes 'you spoke thus, friend Sāti', a report of what happened and the opposite of what the monks are saying. Here the particle in front of the form decides its tense, and that sums up this whole chapter fairly.

33. Chapter 5 describes what *tasmā* and *tato* **are**: oblique case forms of the pronoun *ta(d)*, sitting in its paradigm, ablative singular and *-to* form respectively. This chapter describes what they have **become**: words that no longer refer to anything and function as 'therefore' and 'thence'. Both are true because the freezing is a historical process, not a reclassification: the form kept its shape and lost its function. In practice *tasmā* in a real sentence usually needs no antecedent, and looking for one is the commonest way of misreading it. Item 2 is the exception, for there *tasmā* does answer to something, and the something is a particle.

34. This chapter adds the other five oblique cases, and the traditional name for the whole phenomenon (*kiriyāvisesana*). The sentence showing both at once is **DN 16 — D ii 163**, *tikkhattuṁ citakaṁ padakkhiṇaṁ katvā*, where *tikkhattuṁ* 'three times' is an adverb built with a suffix and *padakkhiṇaṁ* 'keeping to the right' is an ordinary accusative used adverbially.

35. Chapter 11 teaches what they are and how they are formed: absolutives of *pacceti*, *nissayati*, *uddisati*, which have stopped naming a prior action and become postpositions. This chapter teaches what case they govern, the accusative, and places them among the four things that took over the vanished preposition's work. Neither chapter is complete without the other, so each cross-refers to the other instead of repeating it. Item 6's *upādāya* is a fourth member of the same class, met in this chapter and not in Chapter 11.

**Say it aloud.**

36. "Mind precedes all things; mind is their chief, they are mind-made. If with a corrupted mind one speaks or acts, from that suffering follows, as the wheel follows the ox's foot."

    The four indeclinables are the verse's whole logical apparatus. *Ce* 'if' marks the protasis, and it is enclitic, so it stands second in its line, not first as English 'if' does. *Vā … vā* offers two ways the condition may be satisfied, speech or act, without preferring either. *Tato* 'from that' draws the consequence and makes the second half an apodosis instead of a new sentence. And *va*, the reduced *iva*, makes the simile, as *yathā* and *evaṁ* do in item 4, here with one syllable.

    Strip them out and six lines remain that are almost entirely nouns and two verbs, with no relation stated between any of them. That is a fair measure of how much of a Pāli sentence depends on the words that do not inflect.

## Answers: Compounds

**Sotānugata — met by ear.**

1. jātipaccayā jarāmaraṇaṁ sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsā sambhavanti. (SN 12.1 — S ii 1)

    The one ending is on *-upāyāsā*, and it is a masculine nominative plural. It is the last member that carries it, as *ekavibhattitā* says it must, and it therefore settles three things at once for a word of twenty-two letters: the case, the number and the gender of the whole. Everything to its left stands in the bare stem. So the compound is an *itaritara dvanda*, declined as a plural in the gender of its last member, and it is a subject of *sambhavanti*.

    The other two words are compounds as well, of two further classes. *Jātipaccayā* is *jāti* + *paccaya*, a *tappurisa* standing in the ablative of cause, 'with birth as condition'. *Jarāmaraṇaṁ* is *jarā* + *maraṇa*, a *dvanda* of the other kind: collective, neuter singular, the two treated as one thing. Three compounds, three classes, four words.

    The five members fail to look up because a stem is not a word. The compound contains *soka*, *parideva*, *dukkha*, *domanassa*, *upāyāsa*; a page of canon has *soko*, *paridevo*, *domanassaṁ*, *upāyāso*. Only *dukkha* has an independent life, because it is also an adjective: *dukkha* has 303 root-text occurrences where the other four have almost none outside the Visuddhimagga. A search for a member spelled as the compound spells it will usually find nothing, and the search is not at fault.

2. 'sambādho gharāvāso rajopatho, abbhokāso pabbajjā. (MN 27 — M i 179)

    Two compounds, one word that looks like a third, and no verb anywhere. *Gharāvāso* is a locative *tappurisa*, 'dwelling in a house'. *Rajopatho* is a *kammadhāraya*, 'a path that is dust', standing here in apposition to *gharāvāso*.

    The trap is *abbhokāso*. It looks like *abbha* 'cloud' plus *okāsa* 'space', which would make it a compound and a rather beautiful one. DPD gives *abhi* > *abbh-* + *ava* + √*kās*, Sanskrit *abhyavakāśa*: a root with two prefixes on it, Chapter 14's material and not this chapter's at all. A plausible split may not be a real one, and only the dictionary can tell them apart.

3. Tirokuṭṭesu tiṭṭhanti, sandhisiṅghāṭakesu ca; Dvārabāhāsu tiṭṭhanti, āgantvāna sakaṁ gharaṁ. (Kp 7)

    *Tirokuṭṭesu* is an *avyayībhāva*, here inflected as a locative plural instead of frozen in the usual *-aṁ*; item 23 asks for the same word cold. That shows the class is defined by its first member and not by its ending. An *avyayībhāva* is a compound whose governing member is an indeclinable, and the usual frozen accusative is the normal result of that, not its definition.

    *Sandhisiṅghāṭakesu* is a *dvanda*; *dvārabāhāsu* a genitive *tappurisa*. Note the spelling: Collins gives this word as *tiro-kudda*, and both romanised editions consulted print *kuṭṭa* with the cerebral. That is an orthographic question and was checked against editions, not against a corpus, as it must be whenever the disagreement is about spelling.

4. Tena kho pana samayena aññataro bhikkhu rajakattharaṇaṁ gantvā mahagghaṁ dussaṁ passitvā theyyacittaṁ uppādesi. (Bu Pj 2 — Vin iii 56)

    Tena kho pana samayena aññataro bhikkhu rajakattharaṇaṁ gantvā mahagghaṁ dussaṁ passitvā theyyacitto āmasi. (Bu Pj 2 — Vin iii 56)

    ***Theyyacittaṁ*** is a genitive *tappurisa*, neuter accusative singular, the object of *uppādesi*: it names a thought, and *citta* is neuter. ***Theyyacitto*** is the same two members standing masculine and nominative, agreeing with *bhikkhu*: a *bahubbīhi* by use, and it names no thought at all. It describes the monk who has one. Inside the compound nothing changed. Only the ending did, and there the canon demonstrates the whole of the *bahubbīhi* rule on itself.

    The second sentence has no word for a thought because it does not need one: the thought has been absorbed into an adjective and hung on the man. This is what the chapter means by saying the class points outwards. DPD records the pair as two entries under one construction (the neuter noun 'intention to steal' and the adjective 'intending to steal, with theft in mind', both of them *theyya* + *citta*), so the same compound is entered twice: the same point, reached from a dictionary's side. *Theyyacitto* has 87 root-text occurrences and *theyyacittaṁ* 2, and the masculine can only be the adjectival use.

    The Vinaya then does what a grammar cannot: it attaches a consequence to the difference. The first case ends *anāpatti, bhikkhu, cittuppāde*, "there is no offence, monk, in the arising of a thought" (Bu Pj 2 — Vin iii 56). The second, where the monk touched the cloth with theft in mind, ends *āpatti dukkaṭassā* (Bu Pj 2 — Vin iii 56), an offence of wrong conduct. One ending on one compound is the difference between a thought that is not an offence and a man who has committed one.

**Vacasā paricitā — rehearsed aloud.**

5. Sabbapāpassa akaraṇaṁ, kusalassa upasampadā; Sacittapariyodapanaṁ, etaṁ buddhāna sāsanaṁ. (Dhp 183 — Dhp p. 27)

    Four compounds, three classes. *Sabbapāpassa* is a *kammadhāraya* governed by the genitive relation inside *sabbapāpassa akaraṇaṁ*, which is itself an uncompounded genitive *tappurisa* written as two words, and the question asks for that relation. The same relation could have been written *sabbapāpākaraṇaṁ* and was not. So compounding is a choice, not an obligation: an *anicca samāsa* is assembled for the occasion, so the occasion may equally decline to assemble one, and a two-word genitive means nothing by staying two words.

    *Akaraṇaṁ* is the negative prefix compounded with an action noun. *Sacittapariyodapanaṁ* is three members deep, and its first member is the reflexive possessive *sa-* (DPD's Sanskrit is *svacetas*), so *sacitta* is 'one's own mind' and the whole is a genitive *tappurisa* over it. *Buddhāna* is a metrical genitive plural for *buddhānaṁ*, which is the verse's business, not this chapter's.

6. svākkhāto bhagavatā dhammo sandiṭṭhiko akāliko ehipassiko opaneyyiko paccattaṁ veditabbo viññūhī'ti. (AN 6.10 — A iii 285)

    Only two of the four have a compound underneath, and the dictionary is the only thing that will tell you which.

    - ***Akāliko***: *akāla* + *ika*, and *akāla* is *na* > *a* + *kāla*, the negative prefix compounded with a noun. A compound, and the suffix has made an adjective of it as the chapter says.
    - ***Ehipassiko***: *ehi* + *passa* + *ika*, two second-person imperatives with the suffix on the end. A compound, and of the strangest kind in the chapter.
    - ***Sandiṭṭhiko***: DPD gives *saṁ* + √*dis* + *ti* + *ka*, that is *sandiṭṭhi* + *ka*. A prefix, a root and two suffixes: no compound anywhere in it.
    - ***Opaneyyiko***: *upaneyya* + *ika*, where *upaneyya* is *upa* + √*nī* with a gerundive suffix. Again no compound, and this one shows the vowel change: *upa* strengthens to *opa* before the suffix, which is the same process that turns *punabbhava* into *ponobhavika*.

    This does not make the chapter's statement wrong; it makes it narrower than it sounds. The claim is that *-ika* turns a finished compound into an adjective, and both compounds here confirm it. The other two add that the suffix does not belong to compounds alone: it takes a prefixed root-derivative just as readily, and *svākkhāto* at the head of the line is a third such word without the suffix at all, *su* > *sv* + *ā* + √*khā* + *ta*. So the suffix is evidence of nothing on its own. A word ending in *-ika* is a word to look up, not a compound to split.

    *Ehipassiko* fits no class because its members are finite verb forms, and all six classes assume nominal members. Collins files it under compounds built out of sentences, a heading he takes from Western scholarship, not from the tradition, and the tradition's silence is itself informative: this is a word the language made and the grammarians did not provide for. The Dhamma is a 'come-and-see!' thing.

**Manasānupekkhitā — gone over in the mind.**

**Recall.**

7. ***Ekapadatta***, 'the fact of being one word', and ***ekavibhattitā***, 'the fact of having one inflection'. A reader uses the second, because it says where to look: the ending is on the last member alone, so the last member gives the case, the number and the gender of the whole.

8. **Number and case.** The uninflected members carry neither. *Buddhasāsanaṁ* is 'the teaching of the Buddha' or 'of the Buddhas' indifferently, and the genitive relation that makes it 'of' is nowhere written; it has to be recovered from sense and context. A third thing could fairly be added: a compound does not always keep its first member to itself, since a word elsewhere in the sentence may construe with it.

9. **Copulative and determinative.** In a copulative compound, the *dvanda*, the members are equals and would have been joined by *ca*: *devamanussā* 'gods and humans'. In a determinative compound the first member qualifies the second in some way, and everything else in the chapter is a subdivision of how.

10. Because a *bahubbīhi* is a **use**, not a formation. Any compound of any class becomes one the moment it is used as an adjective describing something outside itself. *Bahu* + *vīhi* is an ordinary descriptive compound meaning 'much rice'; said of a field it means 'having much rice', and the rice is no longer what the word is about. Collins, Bodhi and Thānuttamo all say this outright, and Duroiselle demonstrates it by running each of the other classes in turn under the heading 'used relatively'.

11. A *nicca samāsa* has a dictionary entry and an *anicca samāsa* does not. A permanent compound is one whose members do not occur apart in that sense (*kammakāro*, *paṭiccasamuppādo*, the name *Arindamo*), so it is a lexical item and is listed. An impermanent compound was assembled for the occasion and could always have been said as separate words; these are the vast majority, and looking for one in a dictionary is a waste of time. When a long word fails to resolve, do not first suspect the dictionary.

**Split the compound.**

12. *vijjā* + *caraṇa* + *sampanna*. No sound change: the *-ā* of *vijjā* stands, and the join before *sampanna* is clean.

13. *pubbe* + *nivāsa* + *anussati* + *ñāṇa*, with *nivāsa* + *anussati* → *nivāsānussati* by ordinary vowel sandhi. The trap is *pubbe*. It is a locative singular and it has kept its ending inside the compound, an *alutta* compound, standing in the middle of a long word where it looks like an error.

14. *appa* + *rajas* + *akkha* + *jātika*, with *rajas* → *raja-* by the *-as* stem rule. Four members, not three; DPD's construction field gives this.

15. *mahā* + *phala*, with the initial *ph-* doubled at the join and the *ā* of *mahā* shortened in consequence, since a long vowel and a following double consonant cannot both stand. Reading it as *mahap* + *phala* and hunting for *mahap* is the mistake this item exists to prevent.

16. *bahula* + *kata*, with the stem-final *-a* becoming *-ī-* before a form of √*kar*. This is a pattern, not an exception, and so many canonical words end in *-īkata* and *-ībhūta*. Meeting an unfamiliar one, split it there.

17. *hatthin* + *go* + *assa* + *vaḷavā*, a four-member *dvanda*: 'elephants, cattle, horses and mares'. The *-in* stem drops its nasal to give *hatthi-*, and *go* becomes *gav-* before the vowel of *assa*.

18. **Not a compound.** *Ekamantaṁ* 'to one side' is the sandhi of two separate words, *ekaṁ antaṁ*, which the editions happen to print joined. Nor is this an isolated oddity: Oberlies points out that words in close syntactical juncture are quite generally treated as though they were members of a compound, and gives *na-ccaje* and *tatra-ssu*, where the negative particle doubles its verb's initial as a compound join would. Compounding and habitual sandhi are two points on one scale, and where a given text falls on it is often an editor's decision.

**Name the class.**

19. ***Dvanda***: 'the teaching and the training'. Separated it would be *dhammo ca vinayo ca*. It is declined as a singular in the gender of its last member, which is one of the three patterns a *dvanda* may take.

20. ***Tappurisa*, genitive**: *bhikkhūnaṁ saṅgho*, 'the assembly of the monks'. The genitive is much the commonest relation, so an unfamiliar *tappurisa* should be tried as a genitive first.

21. ***Kammadhāraya***, adverb plus noun: *sammā* 'rightly' modifying *diṭṭhi* 'view'. Separated, the two would not have stood in different cases, because the first is an indeclinable. Every factor of the noble eightfold path is of this shape.

22. ***Digu***, and specifically a collective (*samāhāra*) *digu*: *cha* + *āyatana*, with *cha* in its compounding form *saḷ-*. The six are collected into one concept and the compound is a neuter singular: 'the sixfold sense base', not 'six sense bases'. Had it been an *asamāhāra digu* it would have stood in the plural.

23. ***Avyayībhāva***: *tiro* 'across, beyond' plus *kuṭṭa* 'wall'. Collins resolves it as *kuṭṭānaṁ tiro*. It is an adverb by formation, and the form here is a locative plural, not the usual frozen *-aṁ*, one of the ways this class varies.

24. ***Bahubbīhi***, from a *tappurisa*: *khīṇa* 'exhausted' + *āsava* 'corruption', hence 'one whose corruptions are exhausted'. Note that it stands alone, with no *bhikkhu* beside it, the second of the three consequences the chapter draws from the *bahubbīhi* being a use.

25. The trap is that the case ending looks like a mistake and is not. *Manasikāro* is an *alutta* compound: *manasi* is a locative singular of *mano*, kept intact, plus *kāra* 'making'. 'Work in the mind', hence 'attention'. It occurs a hundred and thirty-seven times in the root texts, so it is an ordinary word, not an oddity, built on a class many coursebooks pass over.

**Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — penetrated by view.**

**Build the form.**

26. *Dukkhassa samudayo*, 'the origin of suffering': a genitive *tappurisa*. If you supplied a different case, say which and why; several relations are formally possible here and the sense settles it.

27. *Sīlaṁ eva dhanaṁ*, 'the wealth which is virtue itself'. The two members are in **apposition**, not in a genitive relation: virtue does not possess the wealth, it *is* it. Rendering it 'the wealth of virtue' turns an identity into a possession, as rendering turns *avijjāpaccayā* 'with the condition that is ignorance' into 'the condition of ignorance', and *satisambojjhaṅgo* 'the awakening factor that is mindfulness' into 'the awakening factor of mindfulness'. Neither rendering is quite wrong, and neither is what the compound says.

28. ***Subhāvitacitto***, masculine nominative singular. The point of the exercise is the gender: *cittaṁ* is neuter, but a *bahubbīhi* agrees with the noun it describes and not with its own last member, so a neuter noun at the end of the compound comes out masculine here. Six occurrences in the root texts, and the same thing happens in reverse with a feminine last member. Almost all *bahubbīhis* follow the *-a* declension whatever they end in.

29. The canon has *hatthacchinno*. *Chinnahattho* occurs nowhere in any layer of the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana; *hatthacchinno* occurs five times in the root texts, in the Mahāvagga's list of those who may not be ordained, beside *pādacchinno* and *hatthapādacchinno*. Oberlies names this as a general phenomenon, "compounds with an inversion of the expected word-order", and gives *akkhacchinno*, *aṅgulicchinnaṁ*, *veṇikatā* and *vaṅkaghastā* alongside it. The implication needs stating carefully: a grammar's rules and its illustrations are not equally reliable. Duroiselle's seven-way scheme of *bahubbīhi* case-relations is sound and is taught in this chapter; not one of the five compounds he illustrates it with occurs in the canon, because they are the standing examples of the Sanskrit grammatical tradition reproduced without a mark to say so. Take the rule, check the form.

**Read the sentence.**

30. - Saḷāyatanañcidaṁ, bhikkhave, kiṁnidānaṁ kiṁsamudayaṁ kiṁjātikaṁ kiṁpabhavaṁ? (MN 11 — M i 67)
    - *saḷāyatanañcidaṁ* < *saḷāyatanaṁ* the sixfold sense base + *ca* and + *idaṁ* this · *bhikkhave* monks · *kiṁnidānaṁ* having what as its source · *kiṁsamudayaṁ* having what as its origin · *kiṁjātikaṁ* having what as its birth · *kiṁpabhavaṁ* having what as its production
    - And this sixfold sense base, monks — what is its source, what its origin, what its birth, what its production?

    Four *bahubbīhis* in a row on the interrogative pronoun, each agreeing with *saḷāyatanaṁ*. The trap is that they look like nouns: *kiṁnidānaṁ* means 'having what as its source?', not 'what source?', so the whole phrase is a question about *saḷāyatanaṁ*, not a list of four questions. The Pāli answers itself two segments later in the same form, *saḷāyatanaṁ nāmarūpanidānaṁ nāmarūpasamudayaṁ*, where the same compounds are built on *nāmarūpa* instead.

31. - Santi sattā apparajakkhajātikā, assavanatā dhammassa parihāyanti. (SN 6.1 — S i 137)
    - *santi* there are · *sattā* beings · *apparajakkhajātikā* < *appa* little + *rajas* dust + *akkha* eye + *jātika* of the kind · *assavanatā* through not hearing · *dhammassa* of the teaching · *parihāyanti* they fall away
    - There are beings with little dust in their eyes who are falling away through not hearing the teaching.

    This is one of the chapter's examples, chosen on purpose, and doing the analysis unaided differs from following it. Four members, the *-as* rule operating in the middle, and the whole a *bahubbīhi* describing *sattā*: nothing inside the compound names a being. Look again at *assavanatā* too: DPD parses it as *na* + *savana* + *tā*, an abstract noun in the instrumental, so the causal sense is carried by the case and not by anything in the compound.

**Put it back into Pāli.**

32. Idaṁ kho pana, bhikkhave, dukkhasamudayaṁ ariyasaccaṁ (SN 56.11 — S v 421). *Dukkhasamudayaṁ* is a genitive *tappurisa*; *ariyasaccaṁ* is a *kammadhāraya*; and the first qualifies the second, so a compound serves as an adjective, a *bahubbīhi* by use, inside one of the most familiar sentences in the canon.

33. **Tirokuṭṭesu tiṭṭhanti** (Kp 7).

**Read at length.**

34. The passage is MN 27 — M i 178: the arising of a Realised One, his teaching, and a householder's going forth. It recurs, with small variations, in dozens of suttas, so the twenty minutes are well spent.

    - The compound whose first member keeps its ending is ***majjhekalyāṇaṁ*** 'good in the middle', at 11.3. *Majjhe* is a **locative** singular, kept intact, and it stands between *ādikalyāṇaṁ* and *pariyosānakalyāṇaṁ*, neither of which keeps its ending, so the contrast is visible on one line. It is Perniola's example of the *alutta* compound, and here it is in a formula every reader knows.
    - They are ***bahubbīhis*** built with ***saha*** 'with' reduced to *sa-*: 'having gods', 'having Māras', 'having Brahmās', 'having ascetics and brahmins', 'having gods and humans'. Each agrees with the noun it describes (*lokaṁ* for the first three, *pajaṁ* for the last two), and two of them have a *dvanda* inside the *bahubbīhi*. This is the *sa-* possessive that `04-gender-and-adjectives` meets from the other end.
    - Because *vā … vā* is 'or' and a *dvanda* is 'and'. Compounding *gahapati* and *gahapatiputta* would have said that a householder **and** a householder's son heard it; the passage means either one. A *dvanda* can occasionally be read disjunctively, but where the disjunction is the point Pāli writes *vā*.
    - An *itaritara dvanda* would have given ***kesamassūni*** or similar, a plural in the gender of the last member, and the sense would have shifted from 'hair-and-beard' as one thing to be shaved to hairs and beards counted separately. The collective is the right choice and the canon makes it a hundred and forty-four times.

**The canon's compound list.**

35. "'Kings' means: a king of the earth, a king of a region, a ruler of a district, a ruler of a border area, judges, government officials, or whoever passes sentence of mutilation and execution. That is what 'kings' means."

    ***Pathabyārājā*** is *pathabyā* 'of the earth' + *rājā* 'king', a **tappurisa** in the genitive relation: a king *of* the earth. Its first member is the genitive of *pathavī*, not the stem, in the irregular form *pathabyā* that DPD gives beside *pathaviyā*, so it is also an ***alutta*** compound: the first member keeps the ending it would have as a separate word. ***Padesarājā*** is the same class on the same second member: *padesa* 'region' + *rājā*, a king of a region. Both keep the gender and declension of *rājan*, their last member, as a *tappurisa* does.

    ***Chejjabhejjaṁ*** is a **dvanda**, not a *tappurisa*: *chejja* 'what is to be cut' and *bhejja* 'what is to be broken', two gerundives joined by an understood 'and'. It is the collective kind, taking a neuter singular ending for the pair instead of agreeing with either member, and so it is *chejjabhejjaṁ* and not a plural. The test is whether the members stand in a relation to each other or merely side by side, and here neither cuts the other: they are two punishments named together.

    The relative clause at the end, *ye vā pana … anusāsanti*, does what a list cannot: it catches whoever else holds the power, without naming them. Chapter 17 treats the construction; here it is enough that the canon reaches for a relative clause where its compounds run out.

**Reaching back.**

36. All three are compounds; two of the three are *bahubbīhis* and one is disputable, and that makes the item useful. *Manopubbaṅgamā* is *mano* + *pubbaṅgama* 'going before', a *tappurisa* used as an adjective describing *dhammā*, hence 'having mind as their forerunner'. *Manoseṭṭhā* is the same shape: *mano* + *seṭṭha* 'chief', describing *dhammā*, 'having mind as their chief'. Both are *bahubbīhis* because the mind they name is not what the word is about; *dhammā* is. *Manomayā* is different: DPD parses it as *mano* + *maya*, 'made of mind', where *-maya* is a suffix meaning 'consisting of', not an independent word, so it is an adjective by formation and does not need the *bahubbīhi* analysis at all. How you know, in every case: ask whether the thing the compound names is the thing the compound is about. If it is not, the compound is exocentric.

    Chapter 2's point still holds alongside all of this. The *-o* of *mano* stands unchanged before each of the three following consonants; no rule applies and none did.

37. **A noun's work.** *Sammāsambuddho* stands in a list of epithets, all nominative singular masculine, in apposition to *tathāgato*: 'a Realised One arises in the world, worthy, fully awakened, …'. The past participle *sambuddha* is here a substantive, the title, not a predicate saying that something has been awakened. The syntax decides it, not the form: there is already a finite verb, *uppajjati*, and *sammāsambuddho* is not it. Chapter 10 makes the general point that a past participle may serve as adjective, as noun, or as the verb of its sentence, and that only the sentence shows which.

38. Because *avyayībhāva* is a statement about how the word was **made** and 'indeclinable' is a statement about what it now **is**. The name says so itself: *avyayībhāva* is 'becoming indeclinable'. Chapter 12 is interested in *yathābhūtaṁ* as a word a reader meets, a fixed adverb, nearly two thousand times in the root texts, modifying a verb of knowing; this chapter is interested in where its form came from.

**Say it aloud.**

39. The odd compound is ***maggāmaggassa***, *magga* 'path' compounded with its own negation *amagga* 'non-path'. Read as an ordinary *dvanda* it says 'of path and non-path', which is close to meaningless; read as the type Oberlies calls the *phalāphala* type, it names the whole question of the path: one skilled in what is the way and what is not. The canon has others of the shape: *kusalākusalaṁ* 'the wholesome and the unwholesome', *piyāppiyaṁ* 'the liked and the disliked'. Each names a field, not two items.

    Sound out two more compounds in the verse as you read. *Gambhīrapaññaṁ* is a *bahubbīhi*, 'of deep wisdom', accusative because it agrees with the *taṁ* of the last line. And *uttamatthamanuppattaṁ* hides a sandhi as well as a compound: *uttamattham* + *anuppattaṁ*, 'who has attained the highest goal', where *uttama* + *attha* is itself a *kammadhāraya*. Three of the verse's four lines carry a compound, which is a fair sample of the Dhammapada.

## Answers: Word Formation

**Sotānugata — met by ear.**

1. Sāmaññañca vo, bhikkhave, desessāmi, sāmaññaphalāni ca. Taṁ suṇātha. Katamañca, bhikkhave, sāmaññaṁ? (SN 45.35 — S v 25)

    The base is *samaṇo* 'ascetic' and the suffix is the secondary *-ya*. Two changes happen at once, and item 18 asks for them again cold: the first vowel is strengthened, *sa-* to *sā-*, and the *y* assimilates to the preceding *ṇ* to give *-ñña*. So the word is filed under *sā-* and its base under *sa-*, and without the rule there is no way to get from the one to the other. DPD writes the construction as `samaṇa + *ya`, with the asterisk marking the suffix that strengthens, the same notation that separates *kāra* from *kara*.

    There are two bases, not one, and several numbered senses under each. Beside the *sāmañña* built on *samaṇo* (Sanskrit *śrāmaṇya*), DPD carries a *sāmañña* built on *samāno* 'same, equal', Sanskrit *sāmānya*, meaning 'similarity, accord, agreement', and it is canonical Pāli too: DPD illustrates it from the suttas, the Sandaka Sutta among them. The front vuddhi has taken *sama-* to *sāma-* and *samaṇa-* to *sāmañña-*, and the two arrive at the same spelling from different words.

    Here the sentence settles it, not the word. The Buddha is addressing monks; the second noun is *sāmaññaphalāni*, and the fruits he goes on to name at SN 45.35 — S v 25 are the four stages of awakening; and the definition at 1.5 is the noble eightfold path. The morphology cannot decide it and the context decides it at once: the chapter's whole position in one word.

2. Asevanā ca bālānaṁ, paṇḍitānañca sevanā; Pūjā ca pūjaneyyānaṁ, etaṁ maṅgalamuttamaṁ. (Snp 2.4 — Sn p. 46)

    Three formations in four lines. *Sevanā* and *asevanā* are one action noun in *-anā* used twice, the second time with the negative particle, and the verse's whole shape is that opposition. *Pūjā* is the same kind of noun made with a bare *-ā*. *Pūjaneyyānaṁ* is the future passive participle in *-aneyya*, 'to be honoured', declined as an ordinary *-a* stem in the genitive plural.

    What the verse gains by building its opposition out of one noun and a particle is that the two halves are the same act, and only the *a-* separates them. Two different nouns would have made two different subjects; one noun negated makes association itself the thing at issue, and leaves the choice of company as the only variable. The verse argues about one act, and it does so without a verb.

3. Patirūpadesavāso ca, pubbe ca katapuññatā; Attasammāpaṇidhi ca, etaṁ maṅgalamuttamaṁ. (Snp 2.4 — Sn p. 46)

    *-tā* has been added to *katapuñña*, a finished compound adjective, not a stem: 'who has made merit', *kata* + *puñña*, and DPD lists it as an adjective in its own right and gives the abstract as *katapuñña* + *tā*. That is the property of *-tā* the chapter singles out: it attaches to things much larger than a stem, to a compound as readily as to a noun, and English does the same with *-ness*.

    There is no vuddhi, for the reason item 27 gives about a different suffix. The front strengthening belongs to the *ṇ*-marked *taddhita* affixes (*ṇa*, *ṇeyya*, *ṇika*, *ṇiya*, *ṇaka*, *ṇya*), and *-tā* is not among them. *Kata-* stays *kata-*; contrast *bāhusaccaṁ* in item 4, where *-ya* is one of them and the base is unrecognisable afterwards. Two secondary suffixes on two compounds, two verses apart, and only one of them moves the front of the word. That teaches better than the rule stated abstractly.

    Two smaller things in the verse. *Paṇidhi* is an action noun in *-i* (DPD's *pa* + *ni* + √*dhā* + *i*), a type the chapter's four do not include, and a reader will meet it in *sandhi* and *vidhi* and *upadhi*. And the first word is spelled with a dental: *patirūpa-*, not *paṭirūpa-*. That is an orthographic question and was settled against the editions, not against a corpus, as it must be whenever the disagreement is about spelling; all three romanised editions print the dental in their running text, and only the PTS prints the retroflex, and once.

4. Bāhusaccañca sippañca, vinayo ca susikkhito; (Snp 2.4 — Sn p. 47)

    *Bāhusaccaṁ* is the chapter in one word. The base is a compound, *bahussuto* 'one who has heard much'; the suffix is *-ya*; the first vowel is strengthened, *ba-* to *bā-*; and the *y* assimilates to the preceding dental to give *-cca*. Nothing of *suta* is visible in the result. *Susikkhito* shows *su-* used as an ordinary prefix on a past participle.

5. Mātāpitu upaṭṭhānaṁ, puttadārassa saṅgaho; (Snp 2.4 — Sn p. 47)

    Two action nouns on prefixed roots, one with *-ana* and one with the bare *-a*, and both with the prefix in a form the reader has to undo: *upa-* is transparent, *saṁ-* has become *saṅ-* before the guttural.

**Vacasā paricitā — rehearsed aloud.**

6. svākkhāto bhagavatā dhammo sandiṭṭhiko akāliko ehipassiko opaneyyiko paccattaṁ veditabbo viññūhī'ti. (AN 6.10 — A iii 285)

    **Only *opaneyyiko* moves.** Its base is the prefix *upa-*, filed under *u-*; the word is filed under *o-*. The other three are looked up under the letter their base begins with, and from those three alone no one would discover that there was a rule.

    - ***Sandiṭṭhiko***: under *sa-*, and its base *sandiṭṭhi* is under *sa-*. The first vowel is long by position, a cluster following it, and a vowel long by position is not strengthened. That is the same exception that leaves *paṇḍiccaṁ* unstrengthened in item 25.
    - ***Akāliko***: under *a-*, and its base *akāla* is under *a-*. The first vowel is the negative particle, which the chapter says does not take the strengthening; the canon has *akāliko* and not *ākāliko*.
    - ***Ehipassiko***: under *e-*, and its base *ehi* is under *e-*. The first element is an imperative, not a stem, and in any case *e* is where the ladder for *i* ends: there is no step above it.
    - ***Opaneyyiko***: under *o-*, and its base *upa-* is under *u-*. This demonstrates the whole of the chapter's practical claim inside a formula most readers already know by heart.

    Item 28 takes the same word further, to the *-eyya-* that Warder's resolution leaves out.

7. 'Idaṁ dukkhaṁ ariyasaccan'ti me, bhikkhave, pubbe ananussutesu dhammesu cakkhuṁ udapādi, ñāṇaṁ udapādi, paññā udapādi, vijjā udapādi, āloko udapādi. (SN 56.11 — S v 422)

    Four of the five nouns are derivatives on prefixed or bare roots, and *ñāṇaṁ* and *paññā* are two suffixes on the **same** root, a small demonstration of what item 13 says. *Udapādi* shows where the augment goes in a prefixed verb: between the prefix and the stem, not at the front of the word.

8. "Cattārome, bhikkhave, iddhipādā bhāvitā bahulīkatā mahapphalā honti mahānisaṁsā". (SN 51.20 — S v 276)

    *Bahulīkatā* is the formation treated in the chapter's section on verbs made from nouns and adverbs: a noun or adjective takes a final *-ī* and is compounded with a form of √*kar* or √*bhū*. It is a fixed phrase behaving as one word, neither an ordinary compound nor a denominative verb. The verb *bahulīkaroti* occurs 134 times in the root texts, almost always in this pairing with *bhāvitā*.

**Manasānupekkhitā — gone over in the mind.**

**Recall.**

9. ***Kita***, the primary derivation, whose suffixes are added **directly to a root**; and ***taddhita***, the secondary derivation, whose suffixes are added to a stem that already exists, an ordinary noun or itself a primary derivative. So √*kar* + *-aka* → *kāraka* is *kita*, and *sīla* + *-vant* → *sīlavant* is *taddhita*. The two names have to be held because every grammar uses them, and the division is softer than it looks: Perniola says outright that it "is not of great importance since the same suffixes may be used both as primary and as secondary".

10. The first syllable of the word, not the syllable next to the suffix. *A* becomes *ā*, *i* becomes *e*, *u* becomes *o*. This is the chapter's whole practical point: the derivative is filed in the dictionary under a different letter from its base. *Nipuṇo* is under *ni-* and *nepuññaṁ* under *ne-*; *upa-* is under *u-* and *opaneyyiko* under *o-*. Without the rule you look in the wrong place and conclude the word is not in the dictionary.

11. It may **modify or intensify** the sense of the root (*dhātvatthavisesako*), it may **reverse** it (*dhātvatthabādhako*), or it may **add nothing at all** (*dhātvatthānuvattako*). The third has to be pointed out, because without a name for it a reader treats every prefix as meaningful and produces glosses the language does not support. Naming the category converts a recurring puzzle into a recognised fact.

12. It is an ***anubandha***, an "exponent": a fictitious letter attached to a suffix to distinguish it from another suffix of the same shape, *ṇ* being chosen because no Pāli word begins with it. It signals **vuddhi**: Kaccāyana calls the *ṇ*-marked affixes the *kārita* affixes because the root or the first syllable is strengthened before them. So a grammar's *ṇa* is a reader's *-a* with strengthening, against a plain *-a* without it, which is the whole difference between *kāra* 'maker' and *kara* 'making'.

13. Any two of three. **First**, words built on one root diverge in sense until nothing connects them: √*ñā* gives *saññā* 'perception' and *viññāṇa* 'consciousness' and *paññā* 'wisdom', and no arithmetic on the prefixes would produce those three senses. **Second**, a prefix often contributes nothing, so a prefixed verb has to be learnt as a word, not assembled; Bodhi's advice is to take the meaning from a dictionary and the nuance from the context. **Third**, a derivative may be identical in shape with its base, as *Gotamo* the clan name is with *Gotamo* the ancestor, so the absence of a visible suffix is not evidence that no derivation happened.

**Identify the formation.**

14. ***-anā***, primary, an action noun: 'feeling' as the *doing* of feeling. DPD's construction is √*vid* > *ved* + *anā*, literally 'causing to know': the suffix goes on the **root**, strengthened, and not on the finished verb *vedeti*. DPD's entry does relate the noun to *vedeti*, 'from *vedeti*', but its construction is built on the root. Deriving the noun from the verb itself is a common habit of teaching, and it claims a derivation nobody has checked.

15. ***-ti***, primary, an action noun, feminine: √*kham* + *ti*, 'patience, endurance'. The root is in its weak grade, so the vowel is short.

16. ***-aka***, primary, an agent noun: 'doer'. DPD writes it `√kar + *aka`, the asterisk marking the strengthening; the tradition writes the same suffix *ṇvu*, its *ṇ* saying the same. The strengthening separates *kāraka* from *kara*.

17. ***-mant***, secondary, possessive: 'having eyes'. This is the nominative singular; the stem is *cakkhumant* and a dictionary lists it that way, the usual mismatch between how a grammar cites a word and how a text prints it. The trap here is the direction of the derivation: a possessive in *-mant* or *-vant* is built on a *noun*, whereas a present participle in *-ant* is built on a present stem, and the *-m-* or *-v-* is the only visible difference.

18. ***-ya***, secondary, abstract: 'the ascetic life, the state of being a *samaṇa*'. Two changes happened at once: the first vowel was strengthened, *sa-* to *sā-*, and the *y* assimilated to the preceding *ṇ* to give *-ñña*. Neither change is optional and together they leave nothing of *samaṇo* visible on the surface.

19. **Nothing.** *Ācariyaka* is *ācariya* 'teacher' with a *-ka* that does not change the meaning at all. **This is the trap**, the commonest one in the language: the pleonastic *-ka*. There is no test for it, and the working rule is that when a *-ka* word will not read as a diminutive or an agent, it is probably the plain word. DPD analyses the whole thing back to *ā* + √*car* + *iya* + *ka*, which is accurate and no help at the point of reading.

20. ***-ka***, contemptuous: 'shaveling'. This is the same suffix as in item 19 and as in *puttaka* 'little son', in its third sense. The passage decides whether a given *-ka* is affectionate, dismissive or empty, not the word. The trap is thinking the suffix decides.

21. ***-ya***, secondary, abstract, with vuddhi: 'skill', from *nipuṇo* 'skilful'. DPD's construction is `ni > ne + √puṇ + ya + *a`, and the `ni > ne` is the front strengthening written out. This is the word the chapter opens with, because looking it up is the whole lesson.

22. ***-ika***, secondary, relational, with vuddhi: 'belonging to the ordinary person', from *puthujjano*. The *pu-* has become *po-*. Note that the base is itself a compound, *puthu* + *jana*, so the suffix operates on a finished compound. That is the *-ika* Chapter 13 treats, there and not here because it works on a compound, not on a stem.

23. ***-ū***, primary, an agent noun: 'gone to the far shore'. The trap is that it cannot stand alone. *Pāragū* is *pāra* + *gū*, where *gū* is a bound form of √*gam* that occurs only at the end of a compound and is in no dictionary as a word. The same is true of *-ga*, *-ja*, *-da*, *-ññū* and the rest of the family Chapter 13 listed and this chapter accounts for.

24. ***-ti***, primary, an action noun, feminine: 'attainment', DPD's *pa* + √*ap* + *ti*. The trap is that it looks like a third-person singular verb, and in the nominative singular nothing on the word distinguishes the two. Any oblique case declares itself (*pattiyā*, *pattiṁ*), so only the bare nominative has to be read from the sentence, a good reason to be suspicious of a "verb" that has no subject and sits in a list of nouns.

**Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — penetrated by view.**

**Build the form.**

25. ***Paṇḍiccaṁ***. Two changes, and they are independent of each other. The final vowel of the base is elided before the suffix, which is Duroiselle's general rule for *taddhita*; and the *y* of *-ya* assimilates to the preceding dental, giving *-cca*. There is no vuddhi here, because the first vowel of *paṇḍita* is already long by position: a cluster follows it. DPD gives the construction as simply *paṇḍita* + *ya*, with Sanskrit *pāṇḍitya* alongside, and the Sanskrit shows the vuddhi that Pāli has traded for the doubled consonant.

26. ***Gāravo***. The root is in its ***vuddhi*** grade: DPD gives `√gar + u + *a`, so the base is *garu* and the suffix is the strengthening *-a*. **Note that the base is *garu* and not *guru***: the *u*-vowel Sanskrit has in the first syllable is not what Pāli prints, and looking for a canonical *guru* returns the grammarians' texts and the later literature only.

27. ***Vicikicchī***, with **no** strengthening, not *vecikicchī*. Six root-text occurrences against one. **This is the trap**: a common teaching box on "vowel lengthening" gives *vicikicchā* + *in* → *vecikicchin* as an example of the rule. What the result shows is that the rule of item 10 belongs to the *ṇ*-marked *taddhita* affixes and not to every suffix. The possessive *-in* is not one of them, and neither are the primary suffixes, which strengthen the root instead if they strengthen anything.

28. The canon has ***opaneyyiko***, fifty-five times in the root texts; *opanayiko* occurs once, in the Visuddhimagga. A second suffix that Warder's resolution leaves out accounts for it: DPD gives `upa + √nī + *eyya + *ika`, so there is an *-eyya-* between the root and the *-ika*. His rule, front strengthening, *upa-* to *opa-*, is right and produces the right first syllable; the ending is a step short. The moral is the chapter's, drawn twice: a source's rules and a source's specimen forms are different things, and the corpus adjudicates the second.

29. ***Ponobhaviko***, or in the feminine plural *ponobhavikā* as the canon most often prints it. The **first** vowel changed, *pu-* to *po-*; the *-a-* of *-bhava-* did not, because the strengthening falls on the first syllable of the word and nowhere else. That is the same rule as item 10 and this is the item that shows what "first syllable" excludes.

**Read the sentence.**

30. - "Yadākhvāhaṁ, āvuso, santiṭṭhāmi tadāssu saṁsīdāmi; yadākhvāhaṁ, āvuso, āyūhāmi tadāssu nibbuyhāmi. (SN 1.1 — S i 1)
    - *yadā* when · *kho* indeed · *ahaṁ* I, the *o* of *kho* becoming the semivowel of *khvāhaṁ* · *āvuso* friend · *santiṭṭhāmi* < *saṁ-* + √*ṭhā* — I stand still · *tadā* then · *assu* indeed · *saṁsīdāmi* < *saṁ-* + √*sad* — I sink · *āyūhāmi* < *ā-* + √*yūh* — I strive, I swim · *nibbuyhāmi* < *nī-* + √*vah* + *-ya-* (passive) — I am swept away
    - When I stood still, friend, then I sank; when I struggled, then I was swept away.

    Four verbs and four prefixes, and the sentence rests on them: the roots are 'stand', 'sit', 'strive' and 'carry', and what makes the pairs into opposites is what sits in front of each. *Nibbuyhāmi* is also a passive (DPD gives *nī* + √*vah* + *ya* + *ti*), so the speaker is carried, not carrying, and that is the point of the simile.

**Put it back into Pāli.**

31. Bāhusaccañca sippañca, vinayo ca susikkhito (Snp 2.4 — Sn p. 47). If you produced *bahussutañca* you have written good Pāli but not this line; the exercise is the abstract in *-ya*, and the verse has *bāhusaccaṁ*.

32. **Yadākhvāhaṁ, āvuso, santiṭṭhāmi tadāssu saṁsīdāmi; yadākhvāhaṁ, āvuso, āyūhāmi tadāssu nibbuyhāmi** (SN 1.1). The four prefixes are *saṁ-*, *saṁ-*, *ā-* and *nī-*. Getting the roots right and the prefixes wrong is the ordinary failure here, the failure the chapter predicts in the other direction.

**Read at length.**

33. **The reading.**

    - ***Abhi-***, both times, with a different sense in each. In *abhikkantāya rattiyā* the participle means 'far gone, advanced': the night was well on. In *abhikkantavaṇṇā* it means 'surpassing, excellent': the devatā's beauty was outstanding. DPD lists both senses under one headword and gives the literal sense as 'gone forward'. One prefix, one root, two senses that a reader cannot get from the parts, which is item 13 again in the first sentence of the sutta.
    - ***Upa-* then *saṁ-*, on √*kam***: DPD's construction is *upa* + *saṁ* + √*kam* + *a* + *ti*. The *saṁ-* has assimilated to the following guttural and is written *-saṅ-*. Two prefixes on one root is ordinary, and three is not rare.
    - *Santiṭṭhāmi* and *saṁsīdāmi*, *āyūhāmi* and *nibbuyhāmi*; then *appatiṭṭhaṁ* and *anāyūhaṁ*. The prefixes are *saṁ-*, *saṁ-*, *ā-*, *nī-*, and then the negative *a-*/*an-* on top of *paṭi-* and *ā-*. Note that the negated pair are **not** the same words negated: *appatiṭṭhaṁ* is built on *paṭi-* + √*ṭhā*, not on *saṁ-* + √*ṭhā*, and *anāyūhaṁ* is a present participle, not a first-person verb. The Buddha answers in a different formation from the one the question was posed in.
    - *Tattha* + *eva* + *antaradhāyi* + *ti*, 'right there she vanished'. No, *antara* is not a prefix; it is a noun, 'interval', standing unchanged in front of the verb. This is the last section of the chapter: an adverb or noun compounded with a verb to make a fixed expression, alongside *pātubhavati* and *purakkharoti*.

**Reaching back.**

34. Because a compound and a derivative are different operations and a word may undergo both. *Ehi* + *passa* is the compound, two second-person imperatives, and *-ika* is the suffix added to the finished compound to make an adjective of it; DPD's construction, *ehi* + *passa* + *ika*, contains both. The *-o* is neither chapter's: it is the masculine nominative singular ending of an *-a* stem, agreeing with *dhammo*, and it belongs to Chapter 3. Three layers of morphology in one familiar word.

35. *Paṇītataro* is built on the adjective *paṇīta* 'excellent'; *uttaritaro* is built on *uttara*, which is itself the comparative of the prefix *ud-* 'up'. So *uttaritaro* is a comparative of a comparative, and the difference shows in the extra syllable, *uttari-* instead of *uttara-*. Chapter 4 records three more of the same kind, *seyyataraṁ*, *pāpiṭṭhataraṁ* and *purimatarāni*, and draws the same moral: the suffix had stopped being felt as a suffix, so it could be added again.

36. Only the vowel of the suffix. *Diṭṭha* is √*dis* + *-ta*, a past participle, 'seen'; *diṭṭhi* is √*dis* + *-ti*, an action noun, 'view'. The root has behaved identically in both, with the same assimilation of *s* + *t* to *ṭṭh*, so the only signal is *-a* against *-i*. So a bare *-ti* form in a verse can be confused with a third-person singular verb, and the resolution is syntactic: look for a subject, and look at the functions of the other words in the line. A noun in a list of nouns is a noun.

37. The prefix is ***nī-***, which the grammars also write *nir-* and which has a consonant to contribute: the point of Warder's bracketed notation *u(d)*, *du(r)*. Before *v* it gives *bb* (*nī-* + √*vā* → *nibbāna*, *nī-* + √*vid* → *nibbindati*), and before *k* it gives *kkh*, *nī-* + √*kam* → *nikkhamati*, with the aspiration that the cluster produces. It is one process with three outputs, and a reader needs it because *nikkhamati* is not filed under *kh*.

**Say it aloud.**

38. **Three derivatives, three suffixes.** ***Aṭṭhaṅgiko*** is *-ika* on *aṭṭha* + *aṅga*, secondary, relational: 'having eight factors'. ***Virāgo*** is the strengthening *-a* on √*raj* with the prefix *vi-*, primary, an action noun: 'fading away'. And ***cakkhumā*** is *-mant*, secondary, possessive: 'the one with eyes', which is item 17 again, now standing on its own as a noun and meaning the Buddha. *Seṭṭho* deserves a glance too: it is an irregular superlative and belongs to Chapter 4, not here.

    Read the verse aloud and the geminates carry most of the metre: *-ṭṭh-* twice, *-cc-* in *saccānaṁ*, *-ṭṭh-* again. Holding them makes the line scan.

**One root, three formations.**

39. *nissajjitabbaṁ* is to be relinquished (nt. nom. sg., fpp.) · *saṅghassa* to the Sangha (m. dat. sg.) · *vā* or · *gaṇassa* to a group (m. dat. sg.) · *vā* or · *puggalassa* to an individual (m. dat. sg.) · *vā* or

    "It is to be relinquished to the Sangha, or to a group, or to an individual."

    All three words are built on *nissajjati* 'lets go, relinquishes', which is itself *nis-* 'out, away' prefixed to the root √*sajj*. The prefix's *s* has assimilated to the following consonant, so the words show *-ss-* and not *-s-s-*.

    - ***Nissajjitabbaṁ*** is the **gerundive**, the *-tabba* suffix on the stem with a connecting *-i-*. It is a verbal adjective and it declines like one; here it is neuter nominative singular, agreeing with the robe-cloth understood from the sentence before. Its sense is obligation: the thing is to be relinquished.
    - ***Nissaggiya*** is an adjective built on the action, and the whole class of offence is named after it: *nissaggiya pācittiya*, an offence entailing relinquishment. The formation is on the root's other grade, *sagga-*, not *sajj-*, with the *-iya* suffix.
    - ***Nissaṭṭha*** is the **past participle**, the *-ta* suffix straight onto the root with the cluster assimilating to *-ṭṭh-*. It reports the act as done: *nissaṭṭhaṁ*, 'has been relinquished'.

    The three between them cover obligation, class and completion, which a rule needs: what must happen, what kind of offence it is, and that it has happened. A root in Pāli is a supply of words more than a word, and the Vinaya draws on the supply harder than the suttas do because a procedure has to name each stage of itself.

## Answers: Case Syntax

**Sotānugata — met by ear.**
