1. Atha kho bhagavā kismiñcideva pakaraṇe bhikkhusaṅghaṁ paṇāmetvā pubbaṇhasamayaṁ nivāsetvā pattacīvaramādāya kapilavatthuṁ piṇḍāya pāvisi. (SN 22.80 — S iii 91)

    Four accusatives with three functions: the chapter's opening demonstration. *Bhikkhusaṅghaṁ* and *pattacīvaraṁ* are objects. The Saṅgha is what was dismissed, the bowl and robe are what was taken. *Kapilavatthuṁ* is the goal of motion: nothing is done to Kapilavatthu, and the accusative marks it as the place the going is towards. *Pubbaṇhasamayaṁ* is adverbial, of time when.

    The fourth is not a participant in the action at all. It has the ending of an object and is not one; it names when, and no *kāraka* role is involved. Items 15 and 16 ask for those two cold, and item 16 is one of the five traps.

2. Puttā matthi dhanaṁ matthi, iti bālo vihaññati; (Dhp 62 — Dhp p. 9)

    The literal shape is 'of me there are sons, of me there is wealth', and the possessor is carried by the **genitive**, or the dative, which is the same form. Pāli has no verb 'to have' and this is the gap the genitive fills: a form of *atthi* or *hoti* with the possessor in the genitive.

    At the join, *me* has lost its vowel before the *a-* of *atthi* and what is left of it is a single consonant welded to the front of the verb: *me* + *atthi* → *matthi*. So the possessor is the *m-*, not something at the front of the word. Scan for *me* and you will not see it, and a corpus search for *me atthi* will not find this line either. This is the mirror of the fifth caution: there a word's initial vowel is eaten by the word before it, and here a word is reduced to its first consonant by the word after. Either way the search term differs from what stands on the page.

    Whether the *me* is genitive or dative cannot be settled, and need not be: the two are identical in form for this pronoun and the sense supports both. That is the merger described at the head of the chapter, met in a real sentence, and item 37 shows how the tradition handles it.

3. Ajjeva rattiyā pacchime yāme tathāgatassa parinibbānaṁ bhavissati. (DN 16 — D ii 139)

    The genitive is *rattiyā*, in the ordinary partitive sense: the watch is a part of the night. It sits inside a locative phrase and belongs to the noun, not to the verb, as a genitive always does.

    *Rattiyā* settles nothing on its own because a feminine oblique in *-iyā* is shared out among the cases, as *-āya* is on an *ā*-stem. The proof is in this chapter and not outside it. At SN 1.1 — S i 1 the same chapter prints the same word and glosses it a locative absolute, "when the night was far gone"; here it glosses it a genitive. One word, one chapter, two cases, and the form is identical in both.

    Here the company decides it. *Pacchime yāme* is already the locative of time when and the sentence does not want a second one; and a watch is a part of a night, which is the relation a genitive states. The ending narrows and the sentence decides: the chapter says so in the abstract, and this line shows it.

4. Ayaṁ, deva, pūraṇo kassapo saṅghī ceva gaṇī ca gaṇācariyo ca ñāto yasassī titthakaro sādhusammato bahujanassa rattaññū cirapabbajito addhagato vayoanuppatto. (DN 2 — D i 47)

    A string of nominatives all in apposition to one another, and a single genitive where an instrumental would ordinarily stand.

    The odd word is *bahujanassa*, where the instrumental would ordinarily stand. It is the agent of the past participle *sādhusammato*, 'held in high regard', and the agent of a passive goes into the instrumental, which is item 12's rule. Here it is genitive instead. That is the merger working the other way round: where two cases have run together in function, each turns up in the other's territory, and an agent in the genitive is one of the commonest places to meet it.

    Apposition alone holds the eleven together. There is no verb in the sentence for them to be governed by; they agree with *pūraṇo kassapo* in case, number and gender, and that agreement is the whole of the syntax. Pāli says "is" with a list of nominatives.

**Vacasā paricitā — rehearsed aloud.**

5. Musāvādā veramaṇīsikkhāpadaṁ samādiyāmi. (Kp 2)

    The ablative is governed by the *veramaṇī* buried inside the compound, not by *samādiyāmi*. What is undertaken is a training rule; what the false speech is separated from is the abstaining. *Veramaṇī* 'abstaining' takes an ablative of the thing abstained from, and the ablative here is the sense of separation, release and abstention in which the precepts are phrased throughout.

    That is also how the phrase survives being compounded. The word governing the ablative has been swallowed into *veramaṇīsikkhāpadaṁ* and has taken its ablative with it: the ablative stands outside the compound and points at a member inside it. An ablative governed by the verb could not have done that, because *samādiyāmi* does not take one.

    Thānuttamo lists 'abstention from' among the ablative's senses and names *ārati* 'abstinence' as another noun that governs it. Wijesekera would add the caution that adnominal ablatives in the Nikāyas are few and mostly presuppose a verb that has dropped out, which is a fair description of what has happened here.

6. Etaṁ nibbidāya virāgāya nirodhāya upasamāya abhiññāya sambodhāya nibbānāya saṁvattati. (DN 9 — D i 189)

    Five of the seven, not all seven. *Virāga*, *nirodha*, *upasama* and *sambodha* are masculine *a*-stems and *nibbāna* is neuter, so on those five the *-āya* can only be the dative ending, the only place in the language where a dative is visible as a dative (item 25's rule). *Nibbidā* and *abhiññā* are feminine *ā*-stems, and on them *-āya* is *the* ending, instrumental, ablative, dative, genitive and locative at once. Two of the seven are as ambiguous as *vedanāya* in item 7.

    They are readable for the reason any ending in this chapter is. *Saṁvattati* governs a dative, and the two ambiguous words stand in a series of seven whose other five can only be datives. The list carries them. *Abhiññāya* has a further claim on a reader's attention, because it is also the absolutive of *abhijānāti*, 'having directly known', a second word of the same spelling, and only the series rules it out.

    Do not take the seven to be datives merely because each looks like an *a*-stem. *Sambodhāya* is from *sambodha*, masculine (DPD's `saṁ + √budh > bodh + *a`, from *sambujjhati*), and not from the feminine *sambodhi*, which is an *i*-stem and cannot give *-āya* at all. The trap is the chapter's subject: a run of seven identical endings invites a reader to stop looking.

7. Evaṁ passaṁ, bhikkhave, sutavā ariyasāvako rūpasmimpi nibbindati, vedanāyapi nibbindati, saññāyapi nibbindati, saṅkhāresupi nibbindati, viññāṇasmimpi nibbindati. (SN 22.59 — S iii 68)

    Three of the five endings can only be locative; two could be five cases each. The three carry the two.

    So the chapter calls the sentence the whole chapter in one line. *Rūpasmiṁ*, *saṅkhāresu* and *viññāṇasmiṁ* are unmistakable. *Vedanāya* and *saññāya* are feminine *ā*-stems and say nothing on their own. They are locative because the other three are, and because *nibbindati* takes a locative. The construction decides, and the ending only narrows.

8. Tiṭṭhatu, bhante, bhagavā kappaṁ, tiṭṭhatu sugato kappaṁ bahujanahitāya bahujanasukhāya lokānukampāya atthāya hitāya sukhāya devamanussānaṁ. (DN 16 — D ii 103)

    Six datives of purpose, an accusative of extent, and a genitive at the end governing the last three of them.

    The accusative is *kappaṁ*, and it is extent of time, not an object: nothing is done to the aeon, and the sentence asks the Blessed One to remain *for* one. It is the same adverbial use as *pubbaṇhasamayaṁ* in item 1, measuring time instead of locating it.

    *Devamanussānaṁ* reaches back over the last three datives, *atthāya hitāya sukhāya*, the benefit and welfare and happiness **of gods and humans**. It stops there because the first three already carry their own possessor: *bahujana-* is compounded into two of them and *loka-* into the third, so they have no room for a second one. A genitive reaches back until it meets a phrase that does not need it.

**Manasānupekkhitā — gone over in the mind.**

**Recall.**

9. Eight ***vibhatti***, 'divisions': the sets of endings, numbered, not named: *paṭhamā*, *dutiyā*, and so on to *sattamī*, with the vocative counted as a variety of the first. Six ***kāraka***, 'factors of action': the roles a participant can play in what the verb describes: *kattā* agent, *kamma* object, *karaṇa* means, *sampadāna* recipient, *apādāna* point of departure, *okāsa* place. The genitive and the vocative are the two that are left over, and the *Saddanīti* says so in one line: *na chaṭṭhīvihitattho kārakaṁ, yathā mantanaṁ*. The reason given is that neither has a connection to the action. A genitive relates one noun to another and reaches the verb only at second hand; a vocative has no action to be connected to yet, since being addressed makes a person available to be told to do something.

10. **Instrumental and ablative** are identical throughout the plural (*-ehi*, *-āhi*, *-ūhi*) and in the *-ā* singular of the *a*-stems; they stay apart in the *a*-stem singular, *-ena* against *-asmā* and *-amhā*. **Dative and genitive** are identical everywhere, in both numbers, with one exception: the *a*-stem dative singular in *-āya*. The consequence is the chapter's structural point. Where the forms merged, the functions bled into each other and Pāli now uses each of the pair in senses that belong to the other; where the forms stayed apart, the functions stayed comparatively clean. So a beginner finds the accusative and locative easy while the other four seem to shade into one another. They do, and the reason is morphological, not conceptual.

11. **First the verb**, because most verbs take one case and not another: *nibbindati* takes a locative, *tasati* a genitive, *saṁvattati* a dative, *viramati* an ablative. **Then the parallel**: Pāli is a language of lists, and a form whose case is unclear is very often standing in a series with forms whose case is not. Where neither helps, say that the sentence is open.

12. They come apart **in the passive** (and in the causative), which Pāli uses constantly. The agent then goes into the **instrumental** and the nominative is the thing acted on: *svākkhāto bhagavatā dhammo*, where *dhammo* is nominative and *bhagavatā* is the agent. The tradition has a name for each half: *abhihitakattā*, the agent expressed by the verb's own ending, and *anabhihitakattā*, the agent the ending does not express and which must therefore be named separately.

13. It uses the **genitive** (or dative; the two are the same in form) with *atthi* or *hoti*, giving a sentence of the shape 'of me there is X'. *Puttā matthi dhanaṁ matthi* (Dhp 62) is *me* + *atthi* twice: "of me there are sons, of me there is wealth", which we render "I have sons, I have wealth."

**Name the case and its job.**

14. **Dative, of purpose.** The *-āya* dative is the only true dative form left in the language, and purpose is overwhelmingly what it expresses.

15. **Accusative, goal of motion.** Nothing is done to Kapilavatthu; the accusative marks it as the place the going is towards.

16. Accusative, adverbial, of time when, and **this is a trap**. It has the ending of an object, but it is not a participant in the action at all. DPD in fact files *pubbaṇhasamayaṁ* twice, once as the noun *pubbaṇhasamaya* and once as an indeclinable adverb in its own right, "acc sg of *pubbaṇhasamaya*", because the accusative has hardened into a word.

17. **Instrumental, of agent.** The sentence is passive: the Dhamma is what was proclaimed, and the Blessed One is who proclaimed it.

18. **Instrumental, of agent** again, this time plural, and governed by the gerundive *veditabbo*, not by a finite verb: it is to be known by the wise.

19. Genitive, with a verb of fearing. English wants an object or a preposition here; Pāli puts the thing feared in the genitive. The parallel line, *sabbe bhāyanti maccuno*, does the same with a second verb of the same class.

20. **Locative, of contact**, with a verb of seizing: the *opasilesika* type in the tradition's fourfold division, contact between two separately established things. Where English says 'by the hair', Pāli puts the part taken hold of in the locative.

21. **Ablative singular, of cause**, and **this is a trap**. *Jātipaccayā* looks like a feminine nominative or an *a*-stem nominative plural and is neither: it is the ablative singular of the masculine compound *jātipaccaya*, 'the condition that is birth'. DPD, like the other examples in this set, files it as an indeclinable adverb which is an ablative singular. Getting it wrong turns the sentence about conditionality into a sentence about a list.

22. **Genitive plural, partitive**: the whole out of which one is taken. Subhadda became one of the arahants, not the arahants' something.

23. **Instrumental**, and **this is a trap**, because English says "possible *for* me" and the reader reaches for a dative. The impersonals *sakkā*, *labbhā*, *dujjānaṁ*, *sukaraṁ* take an infinitive and put the person in the instrumental. The Buddha's reply in the same passage does it in the second person: *dujjānaṁ kho etaṁ … tayā*.

24. **Genitive, objective**, and **this is a trap**, on two counts. The ending *-iyā* on a feminine *ā*-stem could be any of five cases; and even once one has decided it is genitive, there are two genitives on the same noun, with opposite functions. *Tathāgatassa* is subjective, the one who sees; *vesāliyā* is objective, the thing seen. Nothing in the endings distinguishes them. What distinguishes them is that a city cannot look at a person.

**Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — penetrated by view.**

**Choose the case.**

25. **Dative of purpose**, ending *-āya* on an *a*-stem: *bahujanahitāya bahujanasukhāya*. In a feminine *ā*-stem you could not identify it with the same confidence, because there *-āya* is *the* ending, instrumental, ablative, dative, genitive and locative singular all at once. The masculine and neuter *a*-stems are the only place in the language where a dative is visible as a dative.

26. **The genitive.** *Tasati* 'trembles' and *bhāyati* 'fears' are the two in Dhp 129; Perniola's fuller list adds *sarati* and *sumarati* 'remembers', *saddahati* 'believes', *pūrati* 'is filled with', *maccharāyati* 'is stingy about', and the verb of envying, which occurs in the canon only in the plural, as *pihayanti*.

27. The **suffix *-to***, which is invariable and which no other case wears, so a word carrying it can only be ablative. Wijesekera declines to call it a proper case ending because it is not one even in Sanskrit, where *-taḥ* is likewise a suffix, not an inflection; in the Nikāyas it attaches to every kind of stem and is mostly adverbial in force. Three from this chapter: *jātito*, *nāmato* and *gottato* in the Mahāpadāna's list of what the Buddha recollects (*jātitopi anussarati, nāmatopi anussarati, gottatopi anussarati*), where every one of them is printed with the enclitic *pi* welded on. The Dhammapada sequence adds *piyato*, *pemato* and *kāmato*.

28. This is a trap, set by the source itself. There is no such verb as *appamaññati*. The canon has *Māvamaññetha pāpassa* (Dhp 121 — Dhp p. 18) and *Māvamaññetha puññassa* (Dhp 122 — Dhp p. 18), and the verb is *avamaññati*, which DPD gives as *ava* + *mañña* + *ti*, √*man*, 'thinks lightly of'. The grammar's form would have to be built on *appa-* 'little', which is not what the line says. What has happened at the join is that *mā* and *avamaññetha* have run together and the vowel has lengthened (*mā* + *avamaññetha* → *māvamaññetha*), so the verb is not visible at the front of the word, and a corpus search for *avamaññ* returns nothing because what stands there is *āvamaññ*. The form to search for is *vamaññetha*, stripped of its own initial vowel.

29. **Dative singular** of the neuter abstract noun *itthatta*, which DPD gives as *ittha* 'thus, here' plus the abstract suffix *-tta*, Sanskrit *itthatva*: 'the state of being thus'. A search returns almost nothing because the word almost never stands bare in the canon. It stands before the quotation marker *ti*, and the final vowel lengthens at the join, so what is printed is *itthattāyāti*, 114 times in the root texts against one for *itthattāya*.

**Read the sentence.**

30. - Seyyathāpi, bho gotama, puriso purisaṁ narakapapātaṁ patantaṁ kesesu gahetvā uddharitvā thale patiṭṭhapeyya. (DN 12 — D i 234)
    - *seyyathāpi* just as · *bho gotama* good Gotama (voc.) · *puriso* a man (m. nom. sg.) · *purisaṁ* a man (m. acc. sg.) · *narakapapātaṁ* into a pit (m. acc. sg.) · *patantaṁ* falling (m. acc. sg., pres. part.) · *kesesu* by the hair (m. loc. pl.) · *gahetvā* having seized · *uddharitvā* having pulled out · *thale* on firm ground (nt. loc. sg.) · *patiṭṭhapeyya* would set down
    - Just as, good Gotama, a man might seize by the hair another man falling into a pit, pull him out, and set him on firm ground.

    Both locatives are the *opasilesika* type of the chapter's fourfold table, contact between two separately established things, the hand on the hair and the man on the ground. The two accusatives beside them differ too: *purisaṁ* is the object of *gahetvā* and *narakapapātaṁ* is the goal of *patantaṁ*, the four-accusative lesson of item 1 in a sentence with only two. The participle *patantaṁ* agrees with *purisaṁ*, and that shows whose falling it is.

**Put it back into Pāli.**

31. *Khīṇā jāti, vusitaṁ brahmacariyaṁ, kataṁ karaṇīyaṁ, nāparaṁ itthattāyā ti.* The last clause is *na* + *aparaṁ* + *itthattāya*, and the whole weight of it is on the dative.

32. *Sabbe tasanti daṇḍassa, sabbe bhāyanti maccuno.* The nouns are genitive, not accusative and not ablative.

**Read at length.**

33. **On Ud 1.10.** *Diṭṭhe, sute, mute, viññāte* are **locative singulars** of past participles used as nouns: 'in the seen, in the heard, in the sensed, in the cognised'. Which of the tradition's four kinds of locative they are is harder than it looks, because the fourfold division was built for physical supports: oil in sesame, a man on a mat, a dwelling near a city, fish in water. The seen is not a place, and this is the locative of **reference**, the *nimitta-sattamī* the grammarians name separately, which the fourfold division does not cover. The second inflected word in each clause, *diṭṭhamattaṁ* and its fellows, is a **nominative** singular neuter, the subject of *bhavissati*: literally "in the seen there will be merely-the-seen". Bāhiya's request at Ud 1.10 — Ud p. 7 is *desetu me, bhante, bhagavā dhammaṁ*: *desetu* imperative, *bhante* vocative, *bhagavā* nominative, *dhammaṁ* accusative, and *me* open between dative and genitive, the *me* the chapter on pronouns discusses at *evaṁ me sutaṁ*. The chapter would not force a choice: the two cases are identical in form for this pronoun and the sense supports both, 'teach the Dhamma to me' and 'let there be a teaching of the Dhamma for me'. Finally, *yattha* and *tattha* are not case forms at all. They are the relative and demonstrative pronominal stems *ya-* and *ta-* with the adverb-forming suffix *-ttha*. The formation is in the chapter on indeclinables, and the correlative use of the pair is in the chapter on relative clauses.

**Reaching back.**

34. Because the fundamental sense of the instrumental is **accompaniment**, not the instrument: Brugmann's *Mit-Kasus*, the with-case, and Wijesekera notes that some have preferred to call it the sociative for this reason. If 'with' is the case's core sense, then 'without' is its negation and takes the same case; *saddhiṁ* and *vinā* are two ends of one relation.

35. *Bhikkhusaṅgho* resolves as *bhikkhūnaṁ saṅgho*, a **genitive** relation: the assembly of the monks. *Kāmataṇhā* resolves as *kāmesu taṇhā*, a **locative** relation. The recovered cases differ because the case inside a *tappurisa* is decided by the second member, and *saṅgha* takes a genitive while *taṇhā* takes a locative. This chapter's first rule, taken one step further, decides it: look at the governing word, here the compound's second member, where the chapter's wording is "look at the verb first". English translation conceals this one, for 'craving *for* sensual pleasures' sounds like a dative, and Pāli words for desire and for views take their object in the locative.

36. *-āya* in a feminine *ā*-stem singular is instrumental, ablative, dative, genitive and locative (with *-āyaṁ* as an alternative locative). What settles *vedanāya* in *vedanāyapi nibbindati* is both of the chapter's tests at once: *nibbindati* governs a locative, and the word stands in a series of five whose other three members, *rūpasmiṁ*, *saṅkhāresu*, *viññāṇasmiṁ*, carry endings that can only be locative. The sentence decides, not the word.

37. Buddhaghosa distinguishes three senses in which *me* is met (instrumental, dative, genitive) and then **declines to choose** for this passage: *idha pana "mayā sutan"ti "mama sutan"ti ca atthadvaye yujjati*, "but in this place both 'heard by me' and 'my hearing' fit." This chapter recommends saying so. Where neither the verb nor a parallel settles an ending, the sentence is open, and to demand that Pāli be more determinate than its own commentators found it is to assert things the language does not say.

**A chain of genitives.**

38. *aparikkhittassa* unenclosed (m. gen. sg.) · *gāmassa* of an inhabited area (m. gen. sg.) · *gharūpacāre* at the access to a house (m. loc. sg.) · *ṭhitassa* standing (m. gen. sg. p.p.) · *majjhimassa* of middling stature (m. gen. sg.) · *purisassa* of a man (m. gen. sg.) · *leḍḍupāto* a stone's throw (m. nom. sg.)

    "A stone's throw of a man of middling stature standing at the access to a house of an unenclosed inhabited area."

    The nesting is the same as in the chapter's sentence, and the pivot word has changed. *Aparikkhittassa gāmassa* hangs on the locative *gharūpacāre*, and *majjhimassa purisassa*, with *ṭhitassa* agreeing with it, hangs on *leḍḍupāto*. Where the enclosed village was measured from its gate-post, an unenclosed one has no gate-post, so the measurement starts from the edge of the last house instead. One word moves and the whole definition changes its starting point, so the two sentences are printed one after the other.

    The endings cannot decide, being five identical *-assa* forms. What can sensibly own what decides it: a village has an access to a house, and a stone's throw does not. Five genitives in seven words, at two depths, and the reader supplies the depth.

**Say it aloud.**

39. **Ratiyā** and **taṇhāya**. The company they keep makes them readable. *Piyato*, *pemato* and *kāmato* carry the *-to* suffix, which is not a proper case ending at all and can only mean 'from'; the five verses are the same sentence five times over; and *jāyati* takes an ablative of the source in all five. Three unambiguous endings, one repeated frame, and one verb settle the two ambiguous words, though the words themselves say nothing. That is how a case is decided in practice.

## Answers: Clause and Sentence Syntax

Three items carry a trap: **17**, **18** and **27**. Each is named where it falls.

**Sotānugata — met by ear.**

1. Sīho, bhikkhave, migarājā sāyanhasamayaṁ āsayā nikkhamati. Āsayā nikkhamitvā vijambhati. Vijambhitvā samantā catuddisā anuviloketi. Samantā catuddisā anuviloketvā tikkhattuṁ sīhanādaṁ nadati. (AN 4.33 — A ii 33)

    The condition is that the subject does not change. The absolutive carries no person and no number, only priority, so it cannot say whose action it is, and the language may use it only where that question does not arise. One lion does all four things, so *nikkhamitvā*, *vijambhitvā* and *anuviloketvā* are available. Had somebody else been doing the coming out while the lion stretched, the absolutive would have been unusable and an absolute construction would have had to take its place: that is item 9's rule, and this passage is the easy half of it.

    Four finite verbs, and each sentence opens by repeating in the absolutive what the sentence before ended with as a finite verb. This is Bodhi's third pattern, and it is a fact about oral composition, not grammar: each sentence is joined to the last action of the one before, and that makes a long passage recitable. Whether one prints it as four sentences or as one is a decision about punctuation, and the Pāli has no punctuation to consult.

2. Parinibbute bhagavati saha parinibbānā mahābhūmicālo ahosi bhiṁsanako salomahaṁso. (DN 16 — D ii 156)

    The locative absolute carries it, and no other word in the sentence shares the load. *Parinibbute bhagavati* is a clause of its own with its own subject, and the only thing marking it off is the case: the Blessed One stands in the **locative**, which he would not be if he were the subject of *ahosi*. Translate the two words as they stand, 'in the extinguished Blessed One', and you have read the case correctly and the construction not at all.

    The tense of the participle settles the order. *Parinibbute* is past, so the subordinate action is complete before the main one: the *parinibbāna* is over before the earth moves. A present participle there would have said the opposite, that the two ran together, and item 3 is that sentence. Neither contains a word meaning 'when', and the chapter states the rule once and never repeats it: past participle, subordinate action first; present participle, the two concurrent.

3. Imasmiñca pana veyyākaraṇasmiṁ bhaññamāne āyasmato koṇḍaññassa virajaṁ vītamalaṁ dhammacakkhuṁ udapādi. (SN 56.11 — S v 423)

    Only the tense of the participle has changed, and it changes the timing entirely. A **locative absolute** with a **passive present participle**, so the two actions are concurrent: the discourse is still being delivered when Koṇḍañña's insight breaks. The tense of the participle carries all of that, and no word in the sentence means 'while'.

    Learn the pair together, because between them they exhaust the rule. Two sentences, one construction, one difference, two time relations; once the pair is fluent there is no more to remember about which is which.

4. Pavattite ca pana bhagavatā dhammacakke bhummā devā saddamanussāvesuṁ. (SN 56.11 — S v 423)

    The added word is *bhagavatā*, and its case is the instrumental. A **locative absolute** with a **past passive participle** (so this time the subordinate action is complete before the main one), and with the agent added in the instrumental, *bhagavatā*. The grammatical subject stays in the locative and the logical subject stands beside it in a third case. Compare item 3: same construction, different participle, different time relation, and the pair is the clearest demonstration of the tense rule in the chapter.

    Follow the instrumental to find out who did the thing. *Dhammacakke* is the grammatical subject of the absolute and it did nothing; it is what was set rolling. The participle is passive, so the doer cannot be in the locative with it, and a passive puts its agent in the instrumental: Chapter 15's rule, inside a subordinate clause. Item 2 has no agent at all because *parinibbute* is not passive: nobody extinguished the Blessed One.

**Vacasā paricitā — rehearsed aloud.**

5. Yā pana bhikkhunī lasuṇaṁ khādeyya, pācittiyaṁ. (Bi Pc 1 — Vin iv 259)

    The optative carries the 'if' and a case ending carries the 'then'. There is no *sace*, no *ce* and no *yadi*; *khādeyya* carries the condition by itself. The consequence then arrives as a single noun in the nominative, *pācittiyaṁ*, with *hoti* understood, as in *appamādo amatapadaṁ* at the head of the chapter. A rule is a conditional with the 'if' left to a mood and the 'then' left to a case ending, and most of the Pātimokkha is built that way.

    *Yā pana bhikkhunī* gives the rule its **generality**: any nun there may be, not some particular nun. A name would have decided a case instead of making a rule, and a plural would have said something false, for the offence is incurred one nun at a time. The generic relative is Chapter 17's material, and *yā* is its feminine nominative singular; that a rule of law opens with a relative pronoun and no antecedent states that chapter's central point in one sentence.

6. **No cetaṁ**, bhikkhave, **abhavissa** ajātaṁ abhūtaṁ akataṁ asaṅkhataṁ, nayidha jātassa bhūtassa katassa saṅkhatassa nissaraṇaṁ **paññāyetha**. (Ud 8.3 — Ud p. 80)

    **An unreal condition.** *Abhavissa* is the conditional, third singular, of *bhavati*; *paññāyetha* is a third-singular optative in the middle voice, built on the passive stem *paññāyati*. The protasis is contrary to fact and the apodosis draws the consequence that would follow if it were not.

    With indicatives the sentence would assert what it exists to deny. 'There is no unborn, and no escape is discerned here' is a well-formed Pāli sentence, and it says the opposite of what the passage exists to say. The vocabulary does not separate the two readings (the nouns, the negatives and the word order are the same), and two verb endings carry the entire difference. So the conditional tense, rare everywhere else in the language, is at home here: this construction needs it, and the chapter on tenses and moods had little occasion to show it.

7. It denies the false premise and states the true one, using the causal correlative **_yasmā … tasmā_**: 'but because there is an unborn … therefore an escape is discerned'. And the verbs change with it: the conditional and optative of 3.2 become the plain indicatives *atthi* and *paññāyati* of 3.3. Bodhi identifies this as the regular sequel to a counterfactual in the Nikāyas. Only the mood of the verbs marks the difference between the two sentences, and the whole force of the passage lies in that difference.

**Manasānupekkhitā — gone over in the mind.**

**Recall.**

8. The other verbs are in the **absolutive**, which is indeclinable and carries no person, number or tense, only priority. The condition is that the subject does not change. The absolutive cannot say whose action it is, so it can only be used where that question does not arise.

9. If the prior action belongs to the subject of the main verb, use the absolutive; if it belongs to somebody else, use an absolute construction. That is Bodhi's formulation, and nowhere else in the collection are the two set against each other instead of described separately.

10. A **present participle** or a **past passive participle** may stand as the predicate of an absolute. The **future passive participle** may not, and Wijesekera gives the reason: it cannot agree with a substantive in an oblique case in the required way. He rules out the **past active participle** too, and there the rule is too strong: *bhuttāvissa* at DN 17 — D ii 195 is a past active participle in *-āvin*, standing in a genitive absolute with its own object, and it has thirteen root-text occurrences.

11. The tradition has a **case-substitution rule**, sitting in the *kāraka* section beside the other substitutions. Kaccāyana 305, *Anādare ca*, and its *vutti*: *anādare chaṭṭhīvibhatti hoti, sattamī ca*, "in the sense of *anādara* the sixth case-ending occurs, **and the seventh**." Both cases are named in one breath, as alternatives, and no construction is named at all.

12. **Real** conditions put both verbs in the indicative (present or future), with an imperative sometimes standing in the apodosis. **Possible** conditions put the optative in both. **Unreal** conditions put the conditional in the protasis (usually *abhavissa*) and the optative in the apodosis, and only the third gives the conditional tense a regular home in the language.

13. *Hoti* and *atthi*. When a substantive stands where the verb would be, concord of gender and number lapses: *appamādo* is masculine and *amatapadaṁ* neuter, and neither shifts to match the other. Where an adjective or a past participle is the predicate, concord holds.

**Whose action is it?**

14. **The subject's own.** The Blessed One dresses, takes his bowl and robe, and enters, one actor throughout, so the first two verbs go into the absolutive and the third carries the tense and the person for all three. This is the absolutive chain.

15. **Somebody else's**, and it is a **locative absolute**. Ānanda leaves; Māra approaches. Two subjects, so the absolutive is unavailable and the case must carry the relation.

16. **Somebody else's**, and it is a **genitive absolute**: four words in the genitive making a clause with its own subject, the parents. Note that the same sentence then uses two absolutives, *ohāretvā* and *acchādetvā*, for the actions that *do* belong to the speaker. Every device in the chapter is in this one sentence.

17. **Somebody else's**, and it is not an absolute at all: this is a trap. *Sandhānaṁ gahapatiṁ* and *āgacchantaṁ* are both accusative, and together they are the *object* of *addasā*: "he saw that the householder Sandhāna was coming from afar." This is Pāli's real indirect statement, an accusative with a participle agreeing with it, and it is easy to mistake for an accusative absolute because the shape is the same.

18. The subject's own: a second trap. *Daharova samāno*, 'while still young', is nominative, agreeing with the *so kho ahaṁ* that is the subject of *pabbajiṁ*. Wijesekera calls this a **semi-absolute**: the temporal sense is real, but nothing is detached, because the participle's subject and the sentence's subject are the same person. It stands four words before the genitive absolute in item 16, in the same sentence, which makes that sentence the best single test in the chapter.

19. **The subject's own.** The absolutive again, this time in Bodhi's second pattern, rhetorical repetition, the same absolutive chained across a list of four, with the four matching main verbs following.

**Name the construction and its sense.**

20. **Locative absolute, conditional sense.** No word in the sentence says 'if' or 'because'; the construction says only that one state is the mark of another. Aggavaṁsa illustrates his own rule for the locative absolute with the parent formula, *imasmiṁ sati idaṁ hoti*: the tradition's grammar and the tradition's doctrine reach for the same sentence.

21. **Locative absolute, concessive.** Five nouns and four participles in one clause, and the concessive force comes from none of them: it comes from the negative in the main clause. Rendered 'when the rain is falling … he would not hear' the sentence says nothing; rendered 'even with the rain falling' it says what it means.

22. **Locative absolute, causal.** The disappearance of the wheel-treasure is why the king is displeased, and a verb of feeling in the main clause is the commonest signal for this reading.

23. **Locative absolute, attendant circumstance.** The torches are not the cause of his setting out and not a condition of it; they accompany it. Note that the same sentence carries two absolutives, *paṭiyādāpetvā* and *āropetvā*, for the brahmin's own prior actions: the division of labour is exact.

24. **Locative absolute, hypothetical**, and the signal is that the main clause is a question. Wijesekera's rendering for this class is 'supposing' or 'now if'. The exchange runs: how are we to conduct ourselves towards women? — By not looking, Ānanda. — But supposing there is looking, how should one conduct oneself? — By not talking, Ānanda.

25. **Genitive absolute**, and the sense shades between temporal and concessive: 'even while my body is afflicted, my mind will be unafflicted'. Wijesekera notes that *anādara* is faintly implied here, and on Aggavaṁsa's definition of *anādara*, the simultaneous occurrence of the two actions, the temporal and the concessive readings are one, not two.

26. Genitive absolute with a past active participle, which is the exception to Wijesekera's rule stated in answer 10. *Bhuttāvissa* is genitive singular, it has its own object *bhojanaṁ*, and the drowsiness belongs to the householder, not to the participle's own clause.

27. Locative absolute, conditional, and this is a trap. *Hatthesu* and *pādesu* are locative **plural**; *sati* is locative **singular**. The participle does not agree with its subject, and this is regular, not an error: *sati* stays masculine singular against feminine subjects and plural ones alike. The Digital Pali Dictionary has given up on it and files two entries for the same three letters, one singular and one plural.

**Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — penetrated by view.**

**The sentence types.**

28. **A possible condition**, and the optative in both clauses is how you know. *Sace* by itself would not tell you: it introduces real conditions too. Meghiya is floating a request, not reporting a fact, and the two optatives say so.

29. The **locative absolute** *āditte … cele vā sīse vā* carries it, with no particle at all. Translate it '**if**', not 'when'. Bodhi's reason is the right one: the locative absolute with a past participle normally means 'when X has happened', but nobody's turban is actually on fire, and the situation is offered as a hypothesis, not reported as an event.

30. The verb is missing: *hoti* or *atthi*, understood, not written. And **concord has lapsed**: *appamādo* is masculine nominative, *amatapadaṁ* is neuter nominative, and they sit in apposition without either shifting. That lapse is regular whenever a substantive stands in the predicate; it is not a licence taken by the verse.

31. It is marking **purpose**, not quotation. Read as a quotation the sentence is nonsense, because nobody is speaking; read as a purpose clause it is exact: the wise one guards his virtue *so that* praise may come to him. An imperative or optative closed with *ti* is the ordinary Pāli way of saying 'in order that', and it sits beside the infinitive and the dative of an action noun as the third means of expressing purpose.

32. The **simile frame** *seyyathāpi … evameva kho*; a **locative absolute** *tasmiṁ agāre āditte* carrying a condition; and an **infinitive of purpose**, *sotthiṁ kātuṁ*. The verb is optative because the first term of a simile is imagined, not reported; that is the regular convention, and knowing it stops a reader hunting for the occasion on which a man with an eleven-doored house escaped a fire.

33. The **word order** (*atthi* stands first) and the **particle** *nu kho*. Trust the particle. Fronting is real and Perniola is right that it can mark a question, but his own second example, *anuññātosi mātāpitūhi agārasmā anagāriyaṁ pabbajjāya* at MN 82 — M ii 60, has the verbal form first and is a statement, not a question: Raṭṭhapāla's friends telling him he has his parents' permission. Word order in Pāli is a rhetorical instrument, not a grammatical one.

**Adjudicate.**

34. An **accusative absolute**: *santaṁ*, the accusative singular of the present participle of *atthi*, with *paraṁ lokaṁ* in the accusative beside it. The evidence that the tradition recognised it is the commentary's gloss on the parallel phrase at DN 9 — D i 186: *evaṁ santan ti evaṁ sante. Bhummatthe hi etaṁ upayogavacanaṁ* — "'*evaṁ santaṁ*' means '*evaṁ sante*'; for this is an accusative in the sense of the locative." Buddhaghosa does what a grammarian does, identifying an unexpected ending by naming the one it stands for. It should be added that no premodern Pāli grammarian classified the accusative with the locative and the genitive here; the category is a modern Western one, which does not make it wrong but does mean a reader will not find it in Kaccāyana.

35. Wijesekera reads it as a probable **accusative absolute**: 'there being inward ill-will, he knows that there is inward ill-will'. Bodhi reads the parallel line as an ordinary direct object with a participle agreeing with it: 'he understands the ill-will existing internally'. Both fit the Pāli, and the commentary supports each in a different place: *bhummatthe* at DN 9, but *itthambhūtākhyānatthe upayogavacanaṁ*, an accusative describing a state of being, at DN 4, where it resolves the phrase with a genitive instead. The main verb would settle it: where the accusative could be the verb's object, it probably is. *Pajānāti* can take an object, so Bodhi's reading is the safer one here, and *saṅkappeti* in item 34 cannot take *paraṁ lokaṁ* as its object, so that one is an absolute.

36. The category is the **nominative absolute**, and the reader should conclude that it is not established for Pāli. Every case offered for it either has a competing reading in another edition or admits an ordinary analysis. This one turns on a single word in a single edition: *saṁvaṭṭamāno* occurs nowhere in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana in any of its four layers, and the Buddha Jayanti reads the locative with the Burmese. Collins's other instance, DN 19, is not variation at all — every edition prints the locative absolute *Govinde brāhmaṇe kālaṅkate*, and the nominative *Govindo brāhmaṇo kālakato* is twenty lines away inside the king's quoted lament, where it is an ordinary verbless sentence. And the tradition's two verses do not show what they are offered for: in both, the nominative participle agrees with the subject of the main verb, which is a participle qualifying a subject and not an absolute of any kind. A claim about spelling or about a reading is settled against editions, not against a corpus, and this item really tests that.

37. The first is a **genitive absolute** and the second a **locative absolute**, with the same subject, the same participle and the same sense: 'he went forth although the child was crying'. They are printed together because Kaccāyana's rule names both cases in one breath (*anādare chaṭṭhīvibhatti hoti, sattamī ca*), and the *vutti* illustrates each half. A reader should not take either as canonical Pāli because neither occurs in any root text: *rudantasmiṁ dārake* appears once in the whole Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana, in this line of this grammar. The *Saddanīti* has the tradition's word for illustrations of this kind: ***lokikappayoga***, 'worldly usage', as against the *suttapadāni* it quotes elsewhere.

**Read the sentence.**

38. "**That** the Realised One should recollect, of past Buddhas who have attained final Nibbāna, even their birth, even their name, even their clan …" ***Yatra hi nāma*** with a **future**, carrying astonishment, not result; the future there is not future time. And inside it sits a second construction, the **locative absolute** *atīte buddhe parinibbute*, and inside that a run of ablatives in *-to* used adverbially. The sentence is the monks' exclamation in the very conversation that supplied this chapter's genitive-absolute example twelve segments earlier.

**The case law.**

39. *tena kho pana samayena* now at that time · *aññataro* a certain (m. nom. sg.) · *bhikkhu* monk (m. nom. sg.) · *saṅghassa* by the Sangha (m. gen. sg.) · *odane* rice (m. loc. sg.) · *bhājīyamāne* being distributed (m. loc. sg., pres. part. of the passive *bhājīyati*) · *aparassa* for another (m. gen. sg.) · *bhāgaṁ* a share (m. acc. sg.) · *dehi* give! (2 sg. imper.) · *ti* end of quotation · *amūlakaṁ* groundlessly (nt. acc. sg., adverbial) · *aggahesi* took (3 sg. aor.)

    "Now at that time a certain monk, while rice was being distributed by the Sangha, took a share groundlessly, saying 'Give a share for another'."

    The three constructions are a locative absolute, a quotation closed by *ti*, and an adverbial accusative. *Saṅghassa odane bhājīyamāne* is the absolute: its grammatical subject *odane* stands in the locative, its participle agrees with it, and its agent, the Sangha, is in the genitive, not the instrumental, which this chapter notes as the ordinary alternative. *"Aparassa bhāgaṁ dehī"ti* is direct speech with no verb of saying anywhere in the sentence, the *ti* alone marking it; Chapter 12 treats that omission, and a reader has to supply 'saying' from nothing but the particle. *Amūlakaṁ* is a neuter accusative used adverbially, which Chapter 15 sets out.

    The locative absolute shows that the monk was not distributing the rice. Had he been, the sentence would have used an absolutive, whose doer is always the doer of the main verb, and the whole offence depends on his not having been: he was a recipient claiming a second share under another monk's name. The construction has a legal function before a grammatical one, and so the Vibhaṅga reaches for it so much more often than the suttas do.

**The reading passage.**

40. The exchange runs: "How, sir, are we to conduct ourselves towards women?" — "By not looking, Ānanda." — "But supposing there is looking, sir, how should one conduct oneself?" — "By not talking, Ānanda." — "But when talking, sir, how should one conduct oneself?" — "Mindfulness should be established, Ānanda."

    The second and third questions are carried by **locative absolutes** (*dassane … sati* and, in the third, the instrumental *ālapantena*, with a comparable function), and the sense is **hypothetical**, because the main clause is a question: 'supposing there is looking'. Wijesekera identifies the interrogative main clause as the regular signal for that reading, and it makes 'when there is looking' a thinner rendering than 'supposing there is'.

    *Sati* is masculine locative singular and *dassane* is neuter locative singular, so they agree in case and number but not in gender. That is the regular behaviour set out in item 27: *sati* does not trouble to agree, and the reader should stop expecting it to.

    What the exchange demonstrates is how much a Pāli sentence can leave unsaid. Three of the four answers are single words in an oblique case with no verb at all (*adassanaṁ*, *anālāpo*), and the questions carry their subordination on the ending of one participle. There is not a conjunction in the passage. Trained to look for 'if', 'when' and 'although', you will find nothing to look at, and will have to do what this chapter has asked throughout: read the case, read the participle, and supply the relation.

## Answers: Relative Clauses

Six items carry a trap: **15**, **16**, **17**, **19**, **20** and **27**. Each is named where it falls.

**Sotānugata — met by ear.**

1. Atha kho bhagavā yena dvārena nikkhami, taṁ gotamadvāraṁ nāma ahosi. (DN 16 — D ii 89)

    *Taṁ*, the nominative subject of *ahosi*. The pair is *yena … taṁ*: same word, same gender and number, different cases, because the gate is what one leaves *by* in the first clause and what *is named* in the second.

    The two halves of the rule divide the labour cleanly. Gender and number come from the antecedent, and both words point at the same gate, so both are neuter singular and there the sameness ends. Case comes from each word's function where it stands: instrumental in the relative clause, because the going out was by that gate; nominative in the main clause, because there the gate is the subject. Sameness is the antecedent's doing and difference is the clause's.

2. Yo, bhikkhave, rāgakkhayo dosakkhayo mohakkhayo — idaṁ vuccati, bhikkhave, asaṅkhataṁ. (SN 43.12 — S iv 362)

    *Idaṁ*. It is not from the *ta-* stem at all, and that is ordinary, not irregular: any demonstrative can answer.

    *Yo* is masculine singular from *rāgakkhayo*, the first of the three nouns it opens; *idaṁ* is neuter singular from *asaṅkhataṁ*, the word it is equated with in the main clause. Each half agrees with its own clause's noun and not with the other half, which is the general rule that the fixed pair *yo … so* conceals by usually satisfying it twice over. Item 4 is the same fact again with the genders the other way round.

3. Yāni kānici, bhikkhave, bhayāni uppajjanti sabbāni tāni bālato uppajjanti, no paṇḍitato. (AN 3.1 — A i 101)

    *Sabbāni tāni*. The generalised relative *yāni kānici* is closed by *sabba* with the demonstrative attached to it, which is the standard completion for a generalisation.

    The two additions are ***kānici*** on the relative and ***sabbāni*** on the correlative: the first turns 'which dangers' into 'whatever dangers', the second turns 'those' into 'all those'. A generalised relative needs its answer generalised because the correlative states the scope of the claim. *Yāni kānici … tāni* would leave the main clause saying something about some dangers, not about every one, and the sutta insists that there is no exception.

4. **Yā** kho, bhikkhave, imesaṁ tiṇṇaṁ dhammānaṁ saṅgati sannipāto samavāyo, **ayaṁ** vuccati cakkhusamphasso. (SN 35.93 — S iv 68)

    *Yā* takes feminine singular from *saṅgati*, the **first** of the three nouns in the series, even though *sannipāto* and *samavāyo* after it are masculine; it takes nominative from being the subject of its own clause. *Ayaṁ* takes masculine singular from *cakkhusamphasso* in the main clause, and nominative from being the subject there. The two pronouns refer to the same thing and differ in gender, and the rule has been obeyed in both.

    **Vacasā paricitā — rehearsed aloud.**

5. Yaṁ kiñci samudayadhammaṁ sabbaṁ taṁ nirodhadhammaṁ. (SN 56.11 — S v 423)

    Generalisation. *Yaṁ kiñci … sabbaṁ taṁ*, "whatever … all that". There is no finite verb in the sentence; the whole content sits in the relation between the two halves.

    In place of a verb, the pairing itself holds it together: two nominatives, one opened by the relative and one closed by the correlative, and the relation between them is the predication. It is item 3's construction, *kiñci* generalising the relative and *sabbaṁ* generalising the answer, in the singular instead of the plural and with the verb dropped as well. The two sentences are built alike, and a reader is more likely to have the shorter by heart already.

6. Yathā yathā vā panassa kāyo paṇihito hoti tathā tathā naṁ pajānāti. (MN 10 — M i 57)

    Manner, generalised by doubling. *Yathā yathā … tathā tathā*, "in whatever way … in just that way". The doubling matters, for the instruction is that the knowing tracks the posture whatever the posture is.

7. Yasmā ca kho, ānanda, suññaṁ attena vā attaniyena vā tasmā suñño lokoti vuccati. (SN 35.85 — S iv 54)

    Cause. *Yasmā … tasmā*, "because … therefore". Note that the reason clause comes first and the conclusion second, which is the ordinary order and the reverse of the English habit of stating the claim and then defending it.

    **Manasānupekkhitā — gone over in the mind.**

    **Recall.**

8. Number and gender from the antecedent; case from its function in its own clause. The first half identifies what the pronoun points at, and nothing else lets a reader find the antecedent when it stands several words away, or in the clause on the other side. The case half says nothing about the antecedent at all; it gives the relative's function where it stands.

9. "Of undetermined meaning": undetermined, that is, until the correlative determines it. It is the better name because it says what the word *does*, not what it is related to: it opens a term and leaves it open, and the sentence is not readable until something closes it. "Relative" names a relation; *aniyamattha* names the state the reader is left in.

10. Both facts have one cause. *Yad-* is the stem with its old final *d*, and that *d* only survives where a following vowel gives it somewhere to attach. So it is a real element of the paradigm and never a free word: the corpus has *yadidaṁ*, *yadā*, *yadeva*, *yadaniccaṁ* and never *yad*. A junction form *should* return zero occurrences.

11. *Yaṁ* first, then *yathā*. It is a difficulty ranking because a relative that means "where" or "when" announces what kind of clause is coming and what to look for; a relative that means nothing shows only that a clause has begun and leaves the relation to be reconstructed. The emptiest word is the hardest, and it is much the commonest.

12. *Paccattavacane* nominative, *upayogavacane* accusative, *karaṇavacane* instrumental, *bhummatthe* locative. Together they amount to the claim that the empty relative is never actually empty: it always stands for some case, the case is recoverable from the sentence, and recovering it is what construing the sentence consists of. That is considerably stronger than saying the ambiguity does not matter in translation.

13. *Yato* and *yasmā*. In the root texts *yato* leads 1,370 to 374; in the commentaries the ratio reverses to 3,317 for *yasmā* against 590, and in the sub-commentaries 1,605 against 327. *Yasmā … tasmā* is the exegetes' connective and *yato* is the canon's.

14. Any three of: a participle agreeing with the noun; a *tappurisa* compound; a *kammadhāraya* compound; a *bahubbīhi* compound.

**Find the answering word.**

15. **Trap.** There is no answering word, and there is no relative pronoun either: the trap is that the missing word is the *relative*, not the correlative. *Bhikkhu* serves at once as the complement of *vuccati* in the main clause and as the subject of *aggahesi* in the subordinate one, and the two are simply set down side by side. Wijesekera §18.d records the construction and supplies the missing *yo* in brackets in his own rendering. English cannot do it without one.

16. **Trap.** Nothing answers *yāvakīvañca*. The expected *tāva* is absent, and the main clause, *vuddhiyeva … pāṭikaṅkhā*, simply states the consequence. The construction is complete as it stands; the correlative is optional and is very often dropped. Note also *bhavissanti*: a future in a sentence that is not about the future, which item 39 returns to.

17. **Trap.** This is *yena* the connective, in the sense "whereby, in that", and **not** the *yena … tena* idiom of motion taught in the chapter on case syntax. Two signs show it: there is no verb of motion, and the Buddha's reply at AN 4.80 — A ii 83 closes the sentence with *yena* again, not with *tena*, so the word attaches a clause to *hetu* and *paccayo*, not marking a destination.

**What agrees with what.**

18. *Yo* takes masculine singular from *satto* and nominative from being the subject of *upapanno*. *Tassa* takes masculine singular from the same being, and **genitive** because of *tassa evaṁ hoti*, "to him it occurs", the idiom that puts the one who thinks in the genitive or dative. *Yo so* together is the emphatic combination, "that very being who".

19. **Trap.** *Yāvatakaṁ* is a relative **adjective**, not a pronoun, so the item is not asking about the *ya-* paradigm at all. *Taṁ* is neuter singular agreeing with *yāvatakaṁ* and with the neuter *pattabbaṁ*; it is nominative because the main clause is passive (*anuppattaṁ taṁ mayā*, "that has been attained by me"), so the thing attained is the grammatical subject and the attainer stands in the instrumental.

20. **Trap.** The concord rule is broken here, and nowhere else in the chapter. *Vinicchayā* is picked up by neuter plural *yāni* in one line and by masculine plural *te* in the next. Oberlies's explanation is that the masculine and neuter nominative and accusative plural endings had already fallen together through dialect mixture, so the distinction the rule depends on was not being maintained. DPD shows the same thing lexically: it carries *vinicchaya* as masculine at this verse and as neuter at Snp 4.12. Do not emend.

21. *Yā* takes feminine plural from *kulitthiyo kulakumāriyo* and nominative from being the subject of its own clause; *tā*, the second one, takes feminine plural from the same nouns and is the object of *vāsessanti*: accusative, identical in form to the nominative in this declension, so the sentence needs reading, not pattern-matching. The first *tā*, standing immediately after *yā*, is the emphatic combination of the kind seen at item 18.

Supply the case of the empty relative.

22. Locative. *Yaṁ vipassī* = *yasmiṁ kappe vipassī*, "in which eon Vipassī [arose]". This is Buddhaghosa's resolution, and it makes the sentence cohere: *ekanavutikappe* is the antecedent, not a floating adverbial, and the *yaṁ* clause says which eon it was.

23. Instrumental, on Buddhaghosa's reading: *kāraṇatthe paccattaṁ*, glossed *yena kāraṇena*, "by which reason". Resolved: *aṭṭhānaṁ etaṁ … yena kāraṇena ekissā lokadhātuyā dve arahanto … uppajjeyyuṁ*. Wijesekera reads it instead as an adverbial accusative; see item 31.

24. Accusative. The *yaṁ* clause is the content of the demonstrative accusative *etaṁ*: "this is your misdeed, namely that you did not entreat the Realised One". Resolved, the *etaṁ* and the *yaṁ* are the same thing named twice, once forward and once in full.

25. Locative again, and here the sentence supplies the antecedent itself: *so samayo yaṁ mahāvātā vāyanti* = *yasmiṁ samaye mahāvātā vāyanti*, "that is the time at which the great winds blow". This is the construction from which the *ekaṁ samayaṁ* of every sutta opening descends.

**Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — penetrated by view.**

**Name the clause and its sense.**

26. Time, shading into cause: *yato … atha*, "when, once … then". The answering word is *atha*, not *tato*, as is common with *yato*.

27. **Trap.** *Kittāvatā* is **interrogative**, not relative, and it opens no subordinate clause: this is a question, and it is the main clause. It belongs beside the others because the reply at 1.4 answers it with *yasmā … tasmā*, so the three shapes of one stem stand within two segments of each other: *kittāvatā* asking, *yasmā* subordinating, *tasmā* concluding.

**What the counts license.**

28. He is right about the root texts, and the margin is wide: *yato* 1,370, *yasmā* 374. What the counts add is that the claim does not hold of Pāli generally: in the commentaries *yasmā* leads 3,317 to 590 and in the sub-commentaries 1,605 to 327. So "rare" is not sayable without naming a layer, and Warder noticed because he was reading the Dīgha. It also explains how other grammars come to teach *yasmā … tasmā* as the ordinary causal pair: a grammar written from commentarial Pāli is describing the language it read.

29. *Yāvatak-* 117 root-text occurrences and *tāvatak-* 78; *yattak-* 12 and *tattak-* 15, of which four are *tatta-* "heated" in a compound and not the pronoun at all, leaving eleven. Learn *yāvataka*/*tāvataka* and learn *yādisa*/*tādisa*; recognise *yattaka* if it appears and do not spend time on it. None of the four ever occurs in its bare stem form, so the counts had to be taken on *yāvatak-*, not on *yāvataka*: the form a word is looked up in belongs to the grammar, and not to the text.

30. Seven occurrences, all in the later literature, and none in the root texts, commentaries or sub-commentaries. Six of the seven are declension runs in Kaccāyana, Padarūpasiddhi and the *Saddanīti*, grammars listing a paradigm, and the seventh is a verse in the Thūpavaṁsa, a mediaeval chronicle. That licenses you to say it is a grammarians' cell, completed by rule and used by nobody, the verdict Oberlies reaches by hand for *amuyo* and *amūsānaṁ*. It does not license you to say the ending is unattested: *katamesānaṁ* stands twelve times in the root texts, and *sabbesānaṁ* and *etesānaṁ* once each. What is unattested is this stem carrying that ending.

**Adjudicate.**

31. Buddhaghosa reads the *yaṁ* as a nominative used in the sense of an instrumental (*kāraṇatthe paccattaṁ*) and glosses it *yena kāraṇena*, "by which reason", which fits the sense of the sentence well: it is impossible that two Buddhas arise at once, and the *yaṁ* clause states the thing that is impossible. Wijesekera holds that it is instead an adverbial accusative on the Sanskrit model, and adduces sentences where the *yaṁ* clause is clearly the content of a demonstrative accusative: *tuyhevetaṁ dukkaṭaṁ … yaṁ tvaṁ … na tathāgataṁ yāci*, DN 16. Follow Wijesekera, for economy, not authority: his analysis accounts for all four of Buddhaghosa's uses by one mechanism, where Buddhaghosa needs a separate case-substitution for the third. Both give the same translation, so counting cannot settle it: the disagreement is about the grammar behind an agreed reading.

32. The canon has ***yadaniccaṁ*** solid, everywhere the formula occurs, so *Yad 'aniccaṁ* is a form no text prints and does not belong on a quotation line. The impulse is a teacher's and is sound: the reader does need to see that the word is *yad* + *aniccaṁ*, and that the *d* is the stem's old final surfacing before a vowel. The resolution belongs on the gloss line, which exists for it.

33. The sentence does not occur anywhere in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana; it is Perniola's own construction. That shows the *illustration* is invented, which is his habit, and every form taken from him is checked against the canon first for that reason. It shows **nothing** about the construction: the *kammadhāraya* used attributively is ordinary Pāli, and the right response is to replace his sentence with a canonical one, not to doubt the category. An unattested example is evidence about a grammarian, not about a language.

**Read the sentence.**

34. Literal: "Easy are the things not good and unbeneficial to oneself; but what is beneficial and good, that is supremely hard to do." Idiomatic: "It is easy to do what is bad and harmful to yourself; what is good and beneficial is the hardest thing of all." The relative works like an English cleft: *yaṁ … taṁ* isolates the thing being talked about and holds it up for the predicate that follows, which in English needs "what … is" or "it is … that".

35. Literal: "Of whom the utter unvirtuousness, spread over like a *māluvā* creeper over a sal tree, he does to himself what an enemy wishes for him." Idiomatic: "One whose immorality overspreads him as a creeper overspreads a sal tree does to himself what an enemy would wish." Two relatives, nested: *yassa … so* carries the whole verse, and inside the main clause a second pair, *tathā … yathā*, is inverted so that the demonstrative comes first: *tathattānaṁ* is *tathā* + *attānaṁ*. The verse also has no finite verb in its first half, the participle *otthataṁ* standing where one would be.

The relative with nothing to hold on to.

36. "Whoever should rub, there is an offence of wrong-doing."

    *Yo* agrees with nothing, and nothing answers it. There is no noun in the relative clause for it to modify and no *so* in the main clause to close it: the main clause is *āpatti dukkaṭassa*, 'an offence of wrong-doing', which has no verb either and names an offence, not a person. So the rule that governs every other relative in this chapter, that the pronoun takes its gender and number from its antecedent, has nothing here to work on.

    What is left is the masculine singular itself, and it carries the sense: a bare *yo* means 'whoever', and in the Khandhaka it means whichever monk. The reader supplies *bhikkhu* from the book they are holding, where the Pātimokkha's own rules supply it in words. This is the limiting case of the construction, a relative clause with no antecedent, no correlative, and no finite verb in the clause it depends on. It is set here because anyone who has learnt *yo … so* will look for three things and find none of them.

37. "Whoever should have it made, there is an offence of wrong-doing."

    *Kārāpeyya* is a causative, an optative of *kārāpeti* 'has made, causes to be made', where *ugghaṁseyya* is a plain optative of *ugghaṁseti* 'rubs up', an *-e-* stem of Chapter 7's eighth class with a causative's vowel but not a causative. The difference decides whom the rule catches: *yo kārāpeyya* is the one who arranges for the thing to be made, not the one who makes it. A causative in a rule moves the offence up a step, from the hand to the instruction. So the Vinaya reaches for one often, and Chapter 9's rule against digging pairs *khaṇati* with *khaṇāpeti* instead of stating either alone.

**The reading passage.**

38. The three run *ye te vajjīnaṁ vajjimahallakā, te sakkarissanti …*; *yā tā kulitthiyo kulakumāriyo, tā na … vāsessanti*; *yāni tāni vajjīnaṁ vajjicetiyāni …, tāni sakkaronti …*. In each, the outer relative is *yāvakīvañca* and the inner is the *ya-* pronoun doubled with its own demonstrative. The answering word in a different case is *tesañca*, genitive plural, in the first and third: *tesañca sotabbaṁ maññissanti*, "and they will think them worth listening to", and *tesañca … baliṁ no parihāpessanti*, "and they will not neglect the offerings to them". It is a **second** correlative to the same relative, in the case its own clause requires, which is the concord rule applied twice over to one antecedent.

39. Ānanda is reporting a present fact, that the Vajjians do these things, so the present is right. The Buddha is stating a rule about a particular case whose outcome is certain if the condition holds, and by the tense rule that takes the future in **both** clauses: *sakkarissanti* in the condition and *pāṭikaṅkhā* with a future sense in the consequence. "As long as they honour" leaves out that certainty: the English present reads as a general truth, and the Pāli future makes a commitment about this case.

40. The main clause closes it: *vuddhiyeva, ānanda, vajjīnaṁ pāṭikaṅkhā, no parihāni*, "growth alone is to be expected for the Vajjians, not decline". The construction is not defective because the correlative is optional throughout the system: Warder records that the demonstratives are "fairly free, and quite frequently they are omitted altogether", and *tadā* after *yadā* is the clearest case. The omission is safe here because *yāvakīvañca* is unambiguous on its own: an extent clause needs no answering word to be recognised as one.

## Answers: Metre

Seven items carry a trap: **8**, **17**, **19**, **20**, **26**, **27** and **35**. Each is named where it falls.

Every scansion below has been produced by rule and checked against the line as the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana prints it, so where your marks differ from these, chase the difference instead of shrugging at it.

**Sotānugata — met by ear.**

1. The five are *dā*, *ve*, *pā*, *van* and *dham*, and they fall into three groups. ***Dā*** and ***pā*** are heavy because the vowel is **long**, the plainest of the three conditions and the only one most readers expect. ***Van*** and ***dham*** are heavy because a **conjunct** follows: *-nt-* in *pātubhavanti*, *-mm-* in *dhammā*. Neither of those four syllables is a surprise.

***Ve*** is the fourth, and it catches many readers. Its vowel is written with a single letter, its syllable is open, and it is heavy, because *e* is **long by origin**. A reader working from "one letter, one short vowel" marks it ⏑, and the line then has a light syllable where the Tuṭṭhubha's opening will not take one. Item 11 states this fact as a rule; here it is met before it is stated, the right order.

2. All but one of the heavy syllables are heavy because the vowel is long. *Ā*, *tā*, *jhā* and *brāh* have *ā*, and nobody misreads those. The two that would have caught you are ***no*** and ***to***, whose vowel is *o*: written with a single letter, standing in an open syllable, and long by origin as the *e* of item 1 is. The exception is ***ṇas***, heavy because of the conjunct *-ss-* of *brāhmaṇassa*. So this line is almost entirely a line of long vowels, where item 1 divided its weight three ways.

    *——⏑—¦—⏑—¦—⏑—×*, and against item 1 the marks differ at **position 1** and **position 7**. The first does not matter: the opening syllable of a Tuṭṭhubha is anceps, free to be either, and its being heavy here and light there has no consequence. The second matters. Positions five to seven are the **break**, and item 1 has the normal —⏑⏑ where this line has —⏑—. That is neither a fault nor a variation to be explained away; it is the commonest of the alternatives, and item 27 turns the same difference into a trap by putting it where a reader will read it as damage.

3. It is evidence that the two lines are in the same metre, and that is all it is. Eleven positions, the same cadence, the same break: a metre is a pattern of weights, and weight is indifferent to which words carry it. Line four has the same eleven marks with a third set of words again, so three of the four lines of this verse are metrically identical and lexically unrelated.

It is *not* evidence about how the lines are **cut**. The **¦** in these scansions marks the Tuṭṭhubha's own division (opening, break, cadence), not a word break, and the two do not coincide. Item 1 divides its words after the fourth syllable, *Yadā have* ¦ *pātubhavanti dhammā*; this line divides after the fifth, *Athassa kaṅkhā* ¦ *vapayanti sabbā*. Identical marks, different cuts. Nor is it evidence that the lines sound alike: they share a rhythm and nothing more. Item 36 is the case where that difference between marks and cuts decides an analysis.

4. The unmarked syllable is ***ti***, the quotation marker closing the udāna. It is outside the metre and is not counted: the fact item 26 asks you to find for yourself, met here as given information. Note also that the canon does not have the word before it as *sahetudhammaṁ* standing free: the niggahīta has assimilated to *n* before the *t* of *ti*, which is sandhi and has nothing to do with the metre.

Eleven syllables to each of the four lines, once *ti* is set aside. That settles the family at once: the count is fixed, the cadence is fixed, and the middle of the line varies: the **flexible syllabic** family, *vaṇṇacchandas* in its first sense. Eleven syllables within that family makes it **Tuṭṭhubha**. Item 35 is the same metre with one line of twelve in it, and item 15 is the family list this answer has just used.

**Vacasā paricitā — rehearsed aloud.**

5. *⏑———¦⏑——×* — eight. *Ma-no-pub-baṅ-ga-mā-dham-mā*. *Pub-* is heavy for *-bb-*, *-baṅ-* for *-ṅg-*, *dham-* for *-mm-*.

6. *⏑———¦⏑—⏑×* — eight. Identical to item 5 in the first half and different in the seventh syllable, which is the whole difference between a Siloka odd line and an even one.

7. *⏑⏑——¦⏑——×* — eight. *Na* and *hi* are both light, which is the ⏑⏑ opening the metre normally avoids in second and third position but tolerates in first and second.

8. **Trap.** *—⏑——¦⏑—⏑×* — eight. The trap is the first syllable: *esa* begins with *e*, which is heavy although the syllable is open and the vowel is written with one letter. A reader working from "short vowels are light" marks it ⏑ and then cannot make the line fit.

**Manasānupekkhitā — gone over in the mind.**

**Recall.**

9. Of a vowel: *rassa* short, *dīgha* long. Of a syllable: *lahu* light, *garu* heavy. The first pair is about **length**, the second about **weight**, and they do not track each other: a syllable with a short vowel is heavy whenever a conjunct or the niggahīta follows it, which is most of the time. Duroiselle glosses *lahu* and *garu* as "short" and "long" syllables, and that single slip makes the subject unlearnable.

10. Heavy if the vowel is **long**; or if a **conjunct** follows it, in the same word or the next; or if the **niggahīta** follows it. Light if none of the three holds, that is, a short *a, i, u* with at most one consonant after it.

11. *E* and *o* are long by origin, so they make a syllable heavy in an open syllable; where a conjunct follows they are pronounced short, but the syllable is then heavy twice over, once for the vowel and once for the conjunct. A syllable containing *e* or *o* is never light by rule. It can be scanned light by licence, as in the chapter's Ratanasutta case.

12. *Vaṅka*, "bent", for the heavy syllable; *uju*, "straight", for the light (Vuttodaya 7). The European convention is the reverse (a bent **⏑** for light and a straight **—** for heavy), so the same pattern printed under the two systems looks like its own mirror image. Nothing in the marks says which system is in use.

13. A **word break**. The Vuttodaya glosses it *padaccheda*, "the cutting of words" (v. 10). It is **not a pause**: reading it as one produces a recitation no tradition uses.

14. DN 20 — D ii 255, the Mahāsamaya Sutta, *Silokamanukassāmi*. The commentary glosses *siloka* there as *akkharapadaniyamitaṁ vacanasaṅghātaṁ*, "an assemblage of words regulated by syllable and by line": as a metre, that is, and not in the word's commoner sense, "fame, praise".

15. Flexible syllabic (*vaṇṇacchandas* 1), fixed by the **number of syllables** with the cadence fixed and the rest free. Measure (*mattāchandas*), fixed by the **number of measures**, the syllable count varying. Bar (*gaṇacchandas*), the measures grouped into bars of four. Fixed syllabic (*vaṇṇacchandas* 2), fixed by the weight of nearly every syllable.

16. The last syllable of a line counts as **two measures** whatever its actual weight. It matters in the **measure metres** and the bar metres, where the arithmetic is the definition of the metre: without it every even line of every *mattāchandas* metre comes out one measure short.

**Scan the line.**

17. **Trap.** *——⏑—¦—⏑⏑×* — eight. Two traps in one line, in fact. *Dham-mas-sa-va-naṁ* has its heavy syllables from *-mm-* and *-ss-*, not from any long vowel; and the cadence is **—⏑⏑×**, which is not the *pathyā*'s ⏑——× and not the even line's ⏑—⏑×. The line is neither broken nor an even line: it is a named variation, and item 34 asks for its name.

18. *——⏑—¦———×* — eight, and every syllable of the cadence heavy.

19. **Trap.** *——⏑⏑—⏑—⏑×* — **nine**, and the question is the wrong one. This is the opening of the Jarāsutta, a **Vetālīya**, and Vetālīya is a measure metre: the syllable count varies from line to line by design and tells you nothing. Count the measures instead and the line is fourteen, as the metre requires of an odd line. Counting syllables in a *mattāchandas* metre is the commonest way to conclude that a sound line is corrupt.

20. **Trap.** *——⏑—¦—⏑⏑¦—⏑—×* — eleven, a textbook Tuṭṭhubha. The trap is *-bhi-* in *bahunābhichanno*: it is followed by *ch*, which is a **digraph and one sound**, so the syllable stays open and light. Read *ch* as a conjunct and the syllable comes out heavy, the break reads —⏑— instead of —⏑⏑, and the line looks like a variation when it is the normal form.

**Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — penetrated by view.**

**Now say what disturbed the count.**

21. Nine as written. *Cariyā* carries a **sarabhatti** vowel; drop it and the word is *car-yā*, restoring the conjunct that makes position. *——⏑—¦⏑——×* — eight, and a *pathyā* odd line.

22. Nine as written, and the same accommodation: *kiriyā* is *kir-yā*, from Sanskrit *kriyā*. *——⏑—¦⏑——×* — eight, *pathyā* odd line. Note that the two lines end up with identical scansions, as a fixed cadence ensures.

23. Nine as written, and this one needs **two** accommodations, not one. Release *br-* from making position, so that *ca* stays light; *brahma-* is on Ānandajoti's list of words where *br* regularly fails. Then drop the sarabhatti vowel of *cariya*. *⏑—⏑—¦⏑——×* — eight.

24. Nine as written, and again two. The sarabhatti vowel of *ariya* goes, and the line has **already** lost the niggahīta of *ariyasaccānaṁ*: the editor's apostrophe marks it. *—⏑——¦⏑—⏑×* — eight, an even line. Each accommodation has a separate effect: keep the vowel and the line has nine syllables; keep the niggahīta and it has eight but closes ——⏑× instead of the required ⏑—⏑×.

25. Nine as written, and nothing has been altered: this is **resolution**, not licence. The first syllable is realised as two light ones, which the Siloka's profile allows in that position: *[⏑⏑]—⏑—¦⏑——×*. Read that way the line is a *pathyā* odd line of eight positions carried by nine syllables.

26. **Trap.** Ten as printed, against the refrain's eight, and the two extra syllables have two different explanations. The quotation marker ***ti* is outside the metre** and is not counted, which removes one. The other is not a licence at all: *Taṁ tesaṁ* simply has one syllable more than the *Etaṁ* of the refrain, and the line runs to nine by resolution as item 25's does. The trap is expecting a single cause. A line can be long for two unrelated reasons at once, and finding one of them is not finishing.

27. **Trap.** Eleven syllables, the right number, and the line scans *——⏑—¦—⏑—¦—⏑—×*. Note two points. The division is *pa-gā-ḷho* and **not** *pa-gāḷ-ho*: *ḷh* is a digraph and one sound, so it cannot close the preceding syllable. Getting that wrong happens to give the right total here, and that makes it a trap: the count checks out and the analysis is wrong. And the break is —⏑— where the Tuṭṭhubha's normal break is —⏑⏑. That is no fault: Ānandajoti lists —⏑—, ⏑⏑— and ⏑⏑⏑ as patterns that occur in this position, the normal one accounting for about eighty-five per cent of cases and no more.

**Metre as the cause.**

28. In full the line is *etaṁ buddhānaṁ sāsanaṁ*, which scans *——————⏑×*. Its last four positions are ——⏑×. The Siloka's even line requires the cadence **⏑—⏑×** and admits no other, so the full form does not scan. Drop the niggahīta and *-na* is light: *————¦⏑—⏑×*, as required. One nasal is the difference between the best-known verse in the Dhammapada being in metre and being out of it.

29. ***Ghātvā*** should not be taught. It does not occur in the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana at all, in any text, and neither does any word containing *ghātv*, while the ordinary *ghāyitvā* stands eighty-eight times in the root texts. *Daṭṭhu* is sound, with sixteen root-text occurrences. The canon's usage settles it, and the point of checking is that fidelity to a source cannot catch a source being wrong: everything around this sentence in Ānandajoti is correct.

30. Lengthen or shorten a **vowel**; double or simplify a **consonant**; add or drop the **niggahīta**. The commonest is the change of niggahīta to labial *-m* at the end of a word, and it is the hardest to demonstrate for the reason that makes it common: the same change happens in prose, where the metre is not a consideration, so no individual instance can be shown to have happened *because of* the metre.

31. In *upekhā* the *e* stands in an open syllable and is heavy because *e* is long by origin. In *upekkhā* the *e* is pronounced short, but the syllable is closed by the conjunct *-kkh-* and is heavy for that reason instead. The weight is the same and the cause is different, so the four terms in answer 9 have to be kept apart.

**Name the pattern.**

32. The **seventh**. The odd line ends ⏑——× and the even ⏑—⏑×. If the two were identical the verse would be four equivalent lines with nothing marking where a unit ended, and the pairing that makes a Siloka read as couplets, not as a list, would disappear. The Vedic Anuṭṭhubha was that four-identical-line metre; the Siloka is what it became when the two lines diverged.

33. Both scan *——⏑—¦———×*, and both are ***mavipulā***: positions two to four are —⏑—, and the cadence is four heavy syllables. Both also have the word break after the fifth syllable that the chapter says the *mavipulā* normally takes: *Ākaṅkhamānā | sotthānaṁ*, *Sabbattha sotthiṁ | gacchanti*.

34. *——⏑—¦—⏑⏑×* — ***bhavipulā***. Positions two to four are —⏑—, and the cadence is —⏑⏑×. Someone who had learnt only the *pathyā* would find that three lines of a sutta they have recited for years (two *mavipulā* and this *bhavipulā*) end in patterns the rule forbids, and would have to conclude either that the text was corrupt in three places or that the rule was wrong. Both conclusions are available and both are wrong, so learn the table of variations, not only the norm.

35. **Trap.**

    Yathindakhīlo pathavissito siyā  *⏑—⏑—¦—⏑⏑¦—⏑—⏑×*  twelve — **Jagatī**\
    Tathūpamaṁ sappurisaṁ vadāmi    *⏑—⏑—¦—⏑⏑¦—⏑—×*   eleven — **Tuṭṭhubha**

    The difference is one light syllable in penultimate position, and that is the whole difference between the two metres: opening and break are identical and the cadence differs by that syllable alone.
