It is not a fault. A Jagatī line inside an otherwise Tuṭṭhubha verse was always acceptable and in the middle period is so ordinary that it is better to speak of a single Tuṭṭhubha-Jagatī. The trap is that only the count marks the switch, so a reader scanning for a fixed eleven concludes the first line has a syllable too many.

36. ***Ravipulā***, or a ***pathyā*** with the sixth syllable resolved. The marks cannot decide it, because both analyses produce the same weights. The caesura, the word break, decides it. If it falls after the fourth syllable the line is *ravipulā*; if after the fifth, the run of three light syllables is a resolution inside a *pathyā*. No case in the chapter shows more clearly that the *yati* is evidence, not punctuation.

**What the evidence licenses.**

37. The Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana prints *Chaccābhiṭhānāni **abhabba** kātuṁ*, and its own apparatus records *abhabbo* against the siglum *sī.*, the Sinhalese edition. The Buddha Jayanti and the Pali Text Society both print *abhabbo*. **The licence disappears.** With *-a* the syllable is light by nature and the line is an ordinary Tuṭṭhubha needing nothing; the first syllable is anceps, so both readings scan. The general lesson is that a claim about a metrically shortened vowel is always also a claim about which edition is in front of you, and the apparatus should be read before the licence is asserted.

38. **The tradition calls it *Vatta***, and Duroiselle, transmitting the Vuttodaya, calls it *Vatta* too. This book calls it the Siloka because that is the name in universal modern use, and because *siloka* is the word the Buddha uses at DN 20 — D ii 255, Ānandajoti's reason for changing to it between his first and second editions. Both names are defensible; they answer different questions, one about the canon and one about the tradition that analysed it.

39. The name in universal modern use is not a Pāli word at all. *Tuṭṭhubha* is a modern Pālicisation of Sanskrit *Triṣṭubh* found in no Pāli text; the tradition's form of the name has a different vowel. The chapter uses the modern name anyway, for the same reason it uses Siloka, and says so.

40. *—⏑——¦⏑—⏑×* — identical to item 8's, mark for mark. Nothing has changed: *e* is long by origin, *sa* is light, *dham* is closed by *-mm-*, *mo* is long, and none of that depends on where the space goes. *Dh* is a digraph and one sound, so it makes no position on either analysis, and no conjunct straddles the join.

So the metre cannot decide it. Scansion settles weight, and weight is a fact about vowels and about the consonants that follow them, not about word boundaries. It would have something to say if a conjunct straddled the join, since the rule counts a conjunct "in the same word or the next", so a division that put two consonants together would make position and one that separated them would not. Here it does not arise.

The one feature where a division becomes metrical evidence is the **yati**, and item 36 is the case where it decides between two analyses the marks cannot separate. Here it falls both ways at once. The Siloka wants its break after the fourth syllable, and both divisions give one there, *esadhammo* ¦ *sanantano* and *esa dhammo* ¦ *sanantano* alike. The divided form has a further break after the second syllable, and the metre neither asks for one nor forbids it.

The division is therefore an editor's decision and not a metrical fact. *Esadhammo* has 1 root-text occurrence, and that one is this line of the Vinaya; everywhere else the root texts print the two words apart. The lesson is item 37's, about licences, in a smaller key: before asking what the metre proves, find out what it is capable of proving.

**Put it back into Pāli.**

41. The operation is **vowel sandhi**: *sammanti* + *idha*, final *-i* and initial *i-* coalescing into a single long *ī*. The line as the canon has it is

    Sammantīdha kudācanaṁ; (Kd 10 — Vin i 349)  *———⏑¦⏑—⏑×*

Eight syllables, and the even line's required cadence ⏑—⏑× at the end of it.

It is none of the three. Item 30's three changes (vowel length, consonant doubling, the niggahīta) each alter the **weight** of a syllable and leave the count where it was. This alters the **count**, by making two syllables into one, and it does so by a rule that operates throughout the language and takes no notice of verse: the same coalescence happens in prose, where no metre is asking for it.

Take that point away. The metre did not make this line; it selected it. Pāli offers the poet a resolved form and a joined form of the same two words, and the metre's whole contribution was to rule out the one that came to nine syllables. A great deal of what looks like metrical shaping is the ordinary language, chosen from, not bent, and the licence lies in the residue that will not yield to that explanation, as at item 28.

**The reading passage.**

42. The six lines scan:

    ```
          ⏑    —    —    —  ¦  ⏑    —    —    ×
          Ma   no   pub  baṅ   ga   mā   dham  mā

          ⏑    —    —    —  ¦  ⏑    —    ⏑    ×
          ma   no   seṭ  ṭhā   ma   no   ma   yā

          ⏑    ⏑    —    —  ¦  ⏑    —    —    ×
          Ma   na   sā   ce    pa   duṭ  ṭhe   na

          —    ⏑    ⏑    —  ¦  ⏑    —    ⏑    ×
          bhā  sa   ti   vā    ka   ro   ti   vā

          ⏑    —    —    —  ¦  ⏑    —    —    ×
          Ta   to   naṁ  duk   kha  man  ve   ti

          —    —    ⏑    ⏑  ¦  ⏑    —    ⏑    ×
          cak  kaṁ  va   va    ha   to   pa   daṁ
    ```

    Eight syllables to the line, every line. That fixes the family at once: it is a flexible syllabic metre, and eight syllables makes it Siloka.

    Lines 1, 3 and 5 are odd lines; 2, 4 and 6 are even, and the marks say so without reference to position. Every odd line closes ⏑——× and every even line closes ⏑—⏑×, and that opposition alone identifies them. All six are *pathyā*; there is no variation anywhere in the verse.

    The verse has six lines, not four, and the chapter's description of the metre accounts for it. A Siloka is dissimilar pairs ("an odd or *prior* line and an even or *posterior* line, the pair repeated"), and here the pair has been repeated three times instead of twice, and because the pair is what the metre is built on, nothing about the structure has to change to accommodate it.

## Answers: Review — Sounds and Script

**Sotānugata — met by ear.**

1. *Suṇātu* ends in the vowel *-u* and *me* begins with the consonant *m*; *me* ends in *-e* and *bhante* begins with *bh*; *bhante* ends in *-e* and *saṅgho* begins with *s*. All three boundaries are a vowel followed by a consonant, and at this junction alone the rules have nothing to say. The vowel rules need a vowel on both sides, and the consonant rules need a consonant on the left, which no Pāli word can supply, because no Pāli word ends in one. Item 9 is that derivation; this line shows it on the page.

    For a rule to fire, the second word of a pair would have to begin with a vowel. Then *-u a-*, *-e a-* and *-e a-* would each be a candidate for contraction or elision, and the line would come out shorter than it does. As it stands nothing is lost anywhere in it, and so every word of a formal act of the Sangha can be heard at speed.

2. The two are ***sabbapāpassa akaraṇaṁ***, where a short *a* meets a short *a*, and ***kusalassa upasampadā***, where a short *a* meets a short *u*. Nothing has happened at either, and item 10 asks for the name of that.

    The arithmetic comes out differently for the two, and that is why it is done twice. In the first, the regular treatment of two like vowels is contraction to the long: *-passa akaraṇaṁ* would become *-passākaraṇaṁ*, one syllable instead of two, and the long *ā* is worth two *mattā* where the two short vowels were worth one each. Same *mattā*, one syllable fewer; in the second the vowels are unlike, and the regular treatment is elision of the first: *kusalassa upasampadā* would become *kusalassupasampadā*, where *-ssu-* is a single short open syllable. One syllable fewer and one *mattā* fewer: the elision has thrown a measure away.

    Part I cannot say which of the two spellings this verse requires, and the metre chapter settles it. But the canon settles half of it for us. Both contractions are made elsewhere in the canon, and each time the result is a complete eight-syllable line. *Kusalassupasampadā* has 2 root-text occurrences, both of them whole *pādas* in the Apadāna; and *pāpassākaraṇaṁ* has 1 root-text occurrence, a whole *pāda* in the Jātaka verses. Neither is a form anybody invented for this answer: they are what the same words look like when a poet needed eight syllables instead of nine.

    That observation underlies the item: a junction is neither free nor compulsory. Every one costs a syllable, some cost a *mattā* as well, and the canon makes the same junction in one verse and declines it in another. Think of sandhi as a spelling convention and you will keep being surprised by lines that will not come out to the length they should.

3. The verb is ***upasaṅkami***. It stands inside *tenupasaṅkami*, which is *tena* + *upasaṅkami* with the *a* of *tena* elided before the *u*; and it stands free two words later in the absolutive *upasaṅkamitvā*.

    No junction could form at the second because there is no word in front of it. The sentence ends at *tenupasaṅkami* and *upasaṅkamitvā* opens the next one. Sandhi is a fact about what happens inside a breath group, not about which letters are adjacent on the page, and a full stop is the end of a breath group by definition. Keep this in front of you before the reading items, because the commonest reason a hunted-for junction is not there is that a sentence boundary is.

    *Ekamantaṁ* is the other case and item 15 takes it apart. The difference lies in what became of the result, not in the junction, which is the same niggahīta-before-a-vowel as anywhere else. A junction you are expected to undo leaves both its pieces with their own functions; *tena* is still an instrumental and *upasaṅkami* is still the verb. A junction you are expected to look up has stopped meaning what its pieces mean: *ekamantaṁ* is an adverb, "to one side", and neither *ekaṁ* nor *anta* keeps a recognisable function inside it. The dictionaries list the second kind and not the first, and there is no line between them, only a gradient.

**Vacasā paricitā — rehearsed aloud.**

4. The two are ***bhagavā arahaṁ*** and ***lokavidū anuttaro***. At both a long vowel meets a short one across the boundary and **nothing happens**.

    At the other eleven the word for what has happened is ***pakatisandhi***. Every one of them is a vowel or a niggahīta followed by a consonant (*itipi so*, *so bhagavā*, *arahaṁ sammāsambuddho*, and so on down to *buddho bhagavā*), and, as item 1 showed, that combination has no rule to fire.

    So the most recited formula in the Theravāda world contains thirteen junctions and not one letter has changed at any of them. That is the answer to the expectation the sandhi chapter creates. Rules are written about the cases where something happens, so a chapter of them leaves a reader believing that something usually does; and the ratio in running canonical prose is nothing like that. Recite this formula once with the question in mind, because it is the clearest demonstration in the language that the default is for nothing to occur.

5. *Ādimetaṁ* is *ādiṁ* + *etaṁ*. Apart, the pieces divide **ā·diṁ** and **e·taṁ**, and *-diṁ* is heavy, closed by its niggahīta. Joined, the word divides **ā·di·me·taṁ**, and *-di-* is **light**: open, with a short *i* and nothing after it. The niggahīta has not disappeared: it has surfaced as the *m* that now opens the following syllable, and in moving from the end of one syllable to the start of the next it has stopped adding weight. The junction has turned a heavy syllable light.

    The same thing happens in *mukhametaṁ* and in *pamukhametaṁ*, both from a niggahīta meeting the *e-* of *etaṁ*. It does **not** happen in *pātimokkhanti*, and the reason is that this junction has two steps where the others have one. *Iti* loses its own initial *i* first, as it does everywhere; what the niggahīta then meets is the *t* of the remaining *-ti*, a consonant, so it has nowhere to move to and assimilates in place, as the *n* that a following *t* requires. *-khan-* stays closed and stays heavy.

    So a reciter feels a junction before a reader sees one. On the page these look like letters rearranged. Aloud, three of the four have taken a beat out of the line.

**Manasānupekkhitā — gone over in the mind.**

**Recall.**

6. A syllable may not hold more than two *mattā*: a short vowel is one, a long vowel two, and a vowel closed by a consonant two as well. A long vowel with a cluster after it would come to three, so one of the two gives way: either the vowel shortens and the consonant is doubled, or the vowel keeps its length and the cluster simplifies. Both directions occur, and *kattabbaṁ* beside *kātabbaṁ* is one word showing both.

7. Any closed syllable with a short vowel: *at-* in *attā*, *sam-* in *sammā*, *-haṁ* in *arahaṁ*. The vowel is short and the syllable is heavy, because the consonant or the niggahīta that closes it is worth the second *mattā*. Weight is a property of the syllable, not of the vowel, and running the two together is the commonest error in reading a verse line.

8. Either the niggahīta is a **nasalised vowel** (the vowels of French *genre*, *vin*, *un*: Thānuttamo's reading, with Collins, Geiger and Levman behind it) or it is a pure nasal segment after a vowel that is not itself nasalised (Oberlies's reading), which he distinguishes from the truly nasalised *sānunāsika* vowel that no Pāli script has a sign for. Neither disputes that a syllable closed by a niggahīta is heavy. That is a fact about metre, not a reconstruction of a sound, so it can be checked and the rest cannot.

9. Because a Pāli word may **begin** with a vowel or a consonant, but may **end** only in a vowel or the niggahīta; no Pāli word ends in a consonant. Two possible endings times two possible beginnings is four, and there is no fifth.

10. ***Pakatisandhi***, 'natural junction'. A chapter of rules makes a reader worse at it because rules train the expectation that something must have applied: shown two vowels side by side, a reader who has just learned six patterns of change will hunt for the one that fired instead of concluding that none did. Oberlies's way of putting it corrects this: hiatus is "generally permitted", and the question to ask is what would have had to happen for something to, not why nothing happened.

11. They shorten when a **cluster of consonants** closes their syllable, because a closed syllable cannot hold a long vowel: *mettā*, *bhāseyya*. The disputed class is clusters formed with a group nasal: *ṇḍ*, *nd*, *ñc* and the rest. Buddhappiya holds that *e* and *o* stay long before those, so *meṇḍo* and *soṇḍo* have long first vowels; Kaccāyana holds that *e* and *o* are simply always long; Moggallāna holds that if they are ever short they must be different letters, and counts the alphabet differently in consequence.

12. Either a **glide**, a consonant with no history that closes an awkward gap (the *m* of *aññamañña*, the *y* of *diyaḍḍha* and *nayidaṁ*), or a **relic**, an Old Indic word-final consonant reappearing now that the word is no longer final: the *d* of *yāvadeva* from *yāvad*, the *r* of *punareti* from *punar*. No test distinguishes them from inside Pāli, and Oberlies says why: once a language has a stock of words whose *d* or *r* comes back, it extends the habit by analogy to words that never had one. Knowing the older form settles a particular case; nothing settles the class.

13. **Forty-one**, **forty-two** or **forty-three**, and each is counting something different. Forty-one is the PTS dictionary's list and Kaccāyana's: eight vowels, thirty-two ordinary consonants, and the niggahīta counted among the consonants. Forty-two is Perniola's: the same inventory with ***ḷh*** admitted as a letter in its own right and the niggahīta counted separately. Forty-three is Moggallāna's: short *e* and short *o* counted as distinct from long *e* and long *o*, on the argument that one sound cannot have two durations.

Say what the form is, and what decides it.

Say what the form is, and what decides it.

14. *natthi* — *na* + *atthi*, a vowel meeting a vowel. The length of the surviving vowel makes it a review question, not a sandhi question. Two identical short vowels contracting should give a long one, and here they do not: the *a* stays short because the syllable *nat-* is closed by the *t* and could not hold a long vowel. The sandhi rule and the *mattā* rule both apply, and the second overrides the first.

15. *ekamantaṁ* — *ekaṁ* + *anta*, with the accusative singular ending. The *m* in the middle is a niggahīta that met a vowel, not a stem consonant, so this extremely common adverb is a frozen junction. DPD's construction is *ekaṁ* + *anta* + *aṁ*: the pieces are *eka* and *anta*, and the *m* belongs to neither. The junction has since hardened into a stem of its own (DPD files the adverb as the accusative of *ekamanta*, and the Therīgāthā declines it, with the locative *ekamante* four times), but the *m* inside it is still the niggahīta.

16. *svākkhāto* — *su* + *akkhāto*, 'well proclaimed'. Two changes at once. The sonant *u* takes its consonantal form *v* before the following vowel, which is *ādeso*; and the resulting first syllable *svāk-* has a long vowel and a closing consonant, the exception to the two-*mattā* ceiling, not an instance of it. Thānuttamo cites this word as his counter-example ("long vowels are usually not followed by conjunct consonants (one exception out of many is: *svākkhāto*)"), so the right answer names the rule *and* says that this form breaks it.

17. *diyaḍḍha* 'one and a half' — *di-* + *aḍḍha*, with a *y* between. The *y* is a glide, not a relic: there is no Old Indic *diy* behind it, and the parallel form *duvaḍḍha* shows the same gap closed with a *v* instead, as a glide can be and a relic cannot. Compare *yāvadeva* in question 12, where the consonant is fixed because it is inherited.

18. *yaṁnūna* — and this item concerns the difference between a rule and a spelling. The rule says a niggahīta before a nasal is absorbed into it, so *yaṁ* + *nūna* is analysed *yannūna*. The Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana prints *yaṁnūna*, ninety-four times in the root texts, with the niggahīta intact. Both are correct: the rule describes what a reciter does and the spelling records what an editor chose. Expect the two to agree and you will be surprised in both directions.

19. *upekkhā* — or *upekhā*, and the point is that the variation is between editions. The Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana prints the double consonant 5,246 times against four; the Pali Text Society prints the single one 460 times against 106; the Buddha Jayanti is divided almost evenly against itself, 565 to 540. Three editors, three policies, and one of them without a policy. The form also shows the *a* + *i* → *e* merger (it is *upa* + *ikkhā*), so it belongs to both chapters twice over.

**Read the line.** Heavy syllables are in bold.

**Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — penetrated by view.**

**Read the line.** Heavy syllables are in bold.

20. Namo tassa bhagavato arahato sammāsambuddhassāti. (MN 91 — M ii 140)

    *namo* homage · *tassa* to him · *bhagavato* to the Blessed One · *arahato* to the perfected one · *sammāsambuddhassa* to the fully awakened one · *ti* < *iti*, closing the quotation

    "Homage to him, the Blessed One, the perfected one, the fully awakened one — so [he said]."

    na·**mo** **tas**·sa bha·ga·va·**to** a·ra·ha·**to** **sam**·**mā**·**sam**·**bud**·**dhas**·**sā**·ti

    The junction is at the end, and it explains why this passage was chosen instead of the two identical ones before it. Brahmāyu says the homage three times, and the third is closed by *iti*, the quotation particle. *Sammāsambuddhassa* + *iti* gives *sammāsambuddhassāti*: the *i* of *iti* drops and the *a* before it lengthens, which is the regular behaviour of *iti* and one of the most useful junctions in the language to recognise, since *iti* closes nearly every quotation in the canon.

    Note what the lengthening does to the weights. *-ssa* was light in the two earlier repetitions and is heavy here, because it is now *-ssā*. The junction has changed the rhythm of the line, and a silent reader will never notice that.

21. Manopubbaṅgamā dhammā, manoseṭṭhā manomayā; (Dhp 1 — Dhp p. 1)

    *manopubbaṅgamā* < *mano* mind + *pubbaṅgama* going before (m. nom. pl.) · *dhammā* mental states (m. nom. pl.) · *manoseṭṭhā* < *mano* + *seṭṭha* chief · *manomayā* < *mano* + *maya* made of

    "Mind precedes mental states; mind is their chief, they are made by mind."

    ma·**no**·**pub**·**baṅ**·ga·**mā** **dham**·**mā**, ma·**no**·**seṭ**·**ṭhā** ma·**no**·ma·**yā**

    Three junctions and all three are *pakatisandhi*. *Mano* meets *p*, *s* and *m*, and keeps its *-o* every time. The line is the best short answer there is to question 10.

    The weights deserve attention. *No* is heavy three times, each time because the syllable is open and *o* is long: the reason the *ne* of *anelagalāya* is heavy, and the same trap. *Baṅ* is heavy because *ṅ* closes it, question 7's answer in a real line: a short *a* in a heavy syllable.

22. Esevanto dukkhassā. (SN 12.51 — S ii 84)

    *esevanto* < *eso* this (m. nom. sg.) + *eva* just + *anto* end (m. nom. sg.) · *dukkhassā* of suffering (n. gen. sg.)

    "Just this is the end of suffering."

    **e**·**se**·**van**·**to** **duk**·**khas**·**sā**

    Three words in one, joined by two plain elisions: the final vowel of *eso* goes, then the final vowel of *eva*, and *anto* keeps its own. DPD lists the string as a sandhi entry and prints it *es'ev'anto*, the apostrophes marking those two.

    Every syllable in the line is heavy, and that is a fact on its own: after two elisions there is no short open syllable left anywhere. Sandhi does not only change spellings; it changes the weight of the line, and in verse that is the point of it.

    The last word explains why this book's three-layer format exists. *Dukkhassā* is a genitive with its vowel lengthened before the closing quotation mark, and *esevanto* is a word no dictionary lists except as a junction. Both have to be undone before anything can be looked up, and both have to stay as printed on the quotation line, because *eso eva anto* is a sequence no manuscript contains.

**Put it back into Pāli.**

23. **bhikkhūti**, **bhikkhakoti**, **nivāsessāmīti**, **pātimokkhanti**, **panāti**. Every one stands in the canon: *bhikkhūti* has 243 root-text occurrences and *bhikkhakoti* 2.

    Three of them lengthen a short final vowel, which is the regular behaviour of *iti*: *bhikkhu* → *bhikkhū·ti*, *nivāsessāmi* → *nivāsessāmī·ti*, *pana* → *panā·ti*. In each the last syllable of the first word was light and is now heavy, and the *i-* of *iti* has gone.

    *Bhikkhako* has nothing to lengthen. Its final *-o* is long already, so the only visible change is the loss of the *i-*, and the weight of *-ko* is what it always was. A rule that appears not to have applied has applied, with nothing to change.

    *Pātimokkhaṁ* is the fifth and differs from the rest. It ends in a niggahīta, not a vowel, so there is no vowel for *iti* to lengthen. Instead the second half of the same rule applies: the *i-* of *iti* drops as it does in all five, and the niggahīta, now facing the *t* of *-ti*, assimilates to it as *n*. *-khan·ti*, heavy before and heavy after, and the count neither gains nor loses. Item 5 is the same word at the other kind of junction, where a vowel follows and the *m* surfaces instead.

    And this junction matters more than any other because the joined form is not in the dictionary. *Bhikkhūti* is two words, not one: meeting it, strip the *-ti* and then *restore the short vowel* before looking anything up, because *bhikkhū* is not the entry either; *bhikkhu* is. Two operations, and the second gets forgotten, because the form left after the first looks like a perfectly good nominative plural.

## Answers: Review — The Noun Phrase

**Sotānugata — met by ear.**

1. The three nouns are ***ratti*** (f., an *i*-stem), ***yojanaṁ*** (n., an *a*-stem) and ***saṁsāro*** (m., an *a*-stem), and they hand *dīgha* the endings *-ā*, *-aṁ* and *-o*.

    Two of the three match and one does not. *Dīghaṁ yojanaṁ* and *dīgho saṁsāro* are the same ending on the same declension, adjective and noun in step. ***Dīghā ratti* is not.** *Ratti* is an *i*-stem whose nominative singular is the bare stem; *dīghā* is an *ā*-stem feminine, because an adjective in *-a* makes its feminine in *-ā* and that is a different declension altogether. The two words agree in gender, number and case and share no ending at all.

    Take that from the verse, for this is the commonest unnoticed error in reading Pāli. Agreement is agreement in categories, never in endings. Learn concord by matching the letters at the ends of words and you will read *dīghā ratti* as a mismatch and start looking for the mistake. Item 12 is the rule this depends on; here is the rule with a noun standing next to it.

2. Three of the four nouns wear their gender and one does not.

    *Patto*, *uttarāsaṅgo* and *antaravāsako* all end in *-o*, which is the masculine nominative singular of the *a*-stems, and for these three the ending decides, with the standing qualification of item 13 that an *-o* in the nominative singular may also be an old *-as* stem on its way into the masculine. *Saṅghāṭi* decides nothing. It is an *i*-stem, and the *i*-stem nominative singular is the bare stem in both genders: *aggi* is masculine and *ratti* is feminine and the two look identical. It happens to be feminine, and nothing on this page says so.

    *Ayaṁ* is no help either, four times over. The nominative singular of *ima-* is *ayaṁ* in the masculine and *ayaṁ* in the feminine, so the only word agreeing with all four nouns cannot distinguish two of the three genders. It agrees correctly with a feminine and with three masculines and reports the fact in the same three letters.

    So gender is visible in three places in this sentence, all of them the *-o* endings, and nowhere for the robe. A reader must then do as item 13 recommends and look for something that agrees. Here nothing does, for the sentence has no adjective, no participle and no verb with a gendered form anywhere near *saṅghāṭi*, and when that fails there is only the dictionary. In general, Pāli marks gender far less often than a reader coming from a European language expects, and a fair proportion of the time there is nothing to read it off at all.

3. *Sahassa* is a **noun** in *sahassaṁ candānaṁ*: neuter, nominative singular, with *candānaṁ* a genitive plural hanging on it. Literally "a thousand of moons", which is the second of the four constructions item 9 lists. In *mahāsamuddasahassāni* it is the second member of a compound, *mahāsamudda* + *sahassa*, neuter nominative plural: the fourth construction.

    *Cattāri* agrees with ***mahāsamuddasahassāni***, the thousands, and not with the oceans. It is neuter nominative plural because *sahassāni* is, and the oceans are inside a compound where nothing can reach them to agree with.

    Put the two together and the whole design of the higher numerals falls out. From nineteen up a numeral is a noun with a gender of its own, and a noun cannot agree with what it counts, because it is not describing it, so the thing counted has to be attached some other way, and Pāli has two: put it in the genitive, or compound it. Hence the genitive keeps appearing where an English reader expects a plain plural. And a number larger than a thousand is built by counting the thousands themselves, with a low numeral agreeing, as low numerals do, with a high one. *Cattāri … sahassāni*, four thousands. The two systems are working in the same phrase, one inside the other.

**Vacasā paricitā — rehearsed aloud.**

4. *Tvaṁ* and *ahaṁ* are not filling subject slots, because *rakkha* and *rakkhissāmi* have already said who is acting. They are there for **contrast**, as item 6 predicts of a pronoun subject that appears when nothing requires one.

    Take them out and the sentence still works: *samma medakathālike, mamaṁ rakkha, taṁ rakkhissāmi* is good Pāli and means the same thing. What has gone is the **opposition**, you against me, and with it the design of the line. The Pāli is a chiasmus, *tvaṁ … mamaṁ, ahaṁ … taṁ*, second person outside and first person in, then the reverse; the four pronouns are the four corners of it and the sentence is built so that a listener hears the two people being set against each other before the verbs arrive.

    That answers the recall question in concrete form: a Pāli pronoun subject is never required by the grammar and is always there for rhetoric. The acrobat is proposing a bargain, and the pronouns serve the bargain. The Buddha's reply, a few lines later, refuses the terms of it.

5. *Cattāro* is masculine nominative plural, agreeing with ***dhammā***, which is masculine nominative plural. The four things it counts are *āyu* (**neuter**), *vaṇṇo* (**masculine**), *sukhaṁ* (**neuter**) and *balaṁ* (**neuter**): three neuters and one masculine.

    Had the numeral agreed with the list and not with the word that heads it, three of the four being neuter, a reader would expect ***cattāri***. It is *cattāro*, and the reason is that the numeral never sees the list. *Cattāro dhammā* is a complete noun phrase and the four items stand in apposition to it, naming what the *dhammā* are. Agreement runs to the syntactic head and stops.

    So the general instruction is: when the members of a list disagree in gender with the word that names them, everything agrees with the word that names them, and the members agree with nothing: they are in apposition, and apposition requires case, not gender. Try to make a numeral or an adjective agree with the contents of a list and you will find sentences that cannot be parsed at all.

    The odd stem is ***āyu***, historically a stem in *-us*, the sibling of the *-as* stems of item 13, with the same history: an old neuter consonant stem drifting into a vowel declension, so that the canon writes *āyunā* and *āyuno* where the older language would have had *āyusā*. The nouns chapter takes it as its clearest single case of that drift.

**Manasānupekkhitā — gone over in the mind.**

**Recall.**

6. Because the verb carries person, a pronoun subject is never structurally required, and it follows that when one appears it is there for emphasis, contrast, or the drawing of a distinction. The prediction holds: *ahaṁ* and *tvaṁ* turn up where a speaker is set against someone else, not where English would simply need a subject. Treat them as ordinary subject pronouns and half of them will be inexplicable and the other half translated flat.

7. Up to eighteen a numeral is an **adjective**, with no gender of its own, taking the gender of what it counts and standing in that noun's case. From nineteen upwards it is a **noun**, with its own gender, normally singular however many things it counts, and the thing counted attached by some means other than agreement. The boundary falls at nineteen, not twenty, because nineteen is *ekūnavīsati*, 'twenty less one', a word built on 'twenty': it belongs with what follows it.

8. *Eka* 'one' declines as a pronominal adjective throughout. The form that goes further is the feminine genitive/dative and locative singular *ekissā*, which follows the *ki-* / *ima-* type and not the plain *-assā* the rest of the pattern would predict.

9. Agreement; the counted noun in the genitive plural; no inflection at all; and a determinative compound. A reader assumes **agreement** is the only one, because the low numerals agree and a reader meets the low numerals first. That assumption founders on the genitive construction.

10. *Ubha* 'both', whose nominative and accusative *ubho* is a true dual ending generalised across the rest of its inflection. Its *-o* looks like a masculine nominative singular and is **not** one. And 'two' has no proper paradigm because the dual exists above all for a numeral meaning 'two': when the category went, the word had to be reassembled. *Dve* is an old dual transferred to the masculine, and everything else in its column is borrowed from 'three'.

11. **Nominative, vocative and accusative.** Everywhere else (instrumental, ablative, genitive, dative, locative, in both numbers) masculine and neuter are identical, so the appendix gives them one table with two columns instead of two tables. What follows for a reader is blunt: an oblique ending **cannot** tell you the gender. *Buddhassa* and *cittassa* are the same ending on the same declension; *purisesu* and *rūpesu* likewise. If the gender matters, it has to come from the dictionary, or from an adjective agreeing with the noun, or from a nominative somewhere else in the passage; gender cannot be read off *-ena*.

12. A **noun** in *-a* takes *-ī*: *dāsa* → *dāsī*. An **adjective** in *-a* takes *-ā*: *dīgha* → *dīghā*. The difference is grammatical because the same stem shape gives two different suffixes according to the word's part of speech, which no rule about sounds could predict, since the phonetic environment is identical. The best evidence is the pair of words that do both, according to which they are being at the time: *kalyāṇa* gives the adjective *kalyāṇā* 'beautiful' and the noun *kalyāṇī* 'a beautiful woman'. One stem, one sound, two suffixes, and the only thing that has changed is whether the word is naming or describing.

13. Those words, *manas*, *cetas*, *tapas* and the rest, are historically neuter and are migrating into the masculine *a*-declension, because their old nominative *mano* looks like an *a*-stem masculine nominative in *-o* and attracts masculine agreement accordingly. So the grammars are not contradicting each other so much as describing different moments: Perniola and Ñāṇatusita record where the class came from, DPD where it has arrived. The sense-base that gives it away is **the mind**. At SN 35.2 the Buddha runs through the six, and five take the agreement their own gender requires (*cakkhuṁ dukkhaṁ*, *jivhā dukkhā*, *kāyo dukkho*), while the sixth is *mano dukkho*, masculine. The adjective is the witness, and that is the general moral of this whole group: when a noun's own ending is ambiguous or contested, look at what agrees with it.

Say what the form is, and what decides it.

Say what the form is, and what decides it.

14. *ekissā* — the numeral *eka* 'one', feminine singular, genitive/dative **or** locative. The form belongs to the numeral chapter and its *shape* belongs to the pronoun chapter: *-issā* is the *ki-* / *ima-* ending, not the *-assā* that the ordinary pronominal-adjective pattern would give. And the case cannot be recovered from the form, since genitive, dative and locative fall together here; whatever governs the word decides it. A verb of giving or a noun of possession takes the first two; a locative sense, or a locative absolute, the third.

15. *tissannaṁ* — the numeral *ti* 'three', **feminine** genitive/dative plural. The masculine and neuter is *tiṇṇaṁ*, and the split matters: *ti* distinguishes all three genders in the nominative and accusative but only masculine-neuter against feminine in the genitive. The gender of the noun counted decides the reading, and the information exists nowhere else. The doubled *-ss-* is taken over from the feminine nominative *tisso*, so this one cell looks unlike its neighbours.

16. *paṭhamaṁ* — the ordinal *paṭhama* 'first', and it has two readings that no ending will separate. As an **adjective** it is neuter nominative or accusative singular (or masculine accusative singular), agreeing with its noun. As a **temporal adverb** it is 'the first time, first of all', an ordinary neuter accusative used adverbially. Only the sentence decides, and sometimes not even that: sentence 22 below is a case where both readings are available and the sense comes out the same. An adverbial reading is wanted when there is no neuter singular for it to agree with.

17. *dvīhi* — the numeral *dvi* 'two', instrumental **or** ablative, and all genders at once, since *dvi* makes no gender distinction anywhere. This is the form that teaches the general lesson: a numeral whose instrumental and ablative have fallen together shows nothing about the case of its phrase, and neither does a noun in *-ehi*, which is syncretic the same way. The case has to be read off whatever else the phrase governs. The chapter's example is *dvīhi dhammehi niyyāti abhijjhāya ca byāpādena ca*, where the whole phrase is settled as instrumental by *byāpādena* alone.

18. *tāya* — the distal pronoun *ta(d)* 'that, she', **feminine singular**, and then instrumental, ablative, genitive, dative **or** locative. Five cases in one form. It is here because this is the same five-way collapse the feminine *ā*-stem nouns have in *kaññāya*, and the same one the feminine *ī*-stems have in *nadiyā* — pronoun and noun behaving identically, for once. The same things decide it as for the nouns: the verb, any governing indeclinable, and whether there is something for a genitive to possess. Being a pronoun does not help at all, and that runs against the grain, because pronouns are usually *more* informative than nouns and here they are not.

19. *manasā* — the *-as* stem *manas* 'mind', **instrumental or ablative singular**, with the old consonant-stem ending *-asā* in place of the *a*-stem *-ena*. It belongs to the stem class that is halfway into the *a*-declension, and *manena* exists beside it. Its ending is one of the four (*mano*, *manasā*, *manaso*, *manasi*) that mark the old declension still working. And instrumental and ablative have fallen together here as they do throughout the plural of every class, so the form does not choose between them. In practice the instrumental is far likelier, because *manasā* stands in the standing phrases of Dhp 1 and 2, *manasā ce paduṭṭhena* and *manasā ce pasannena*, where the adjective *paduṭṭhena* agreeing with it is itself an *a*-stem instrumental: one word in the old declension and its adjective in the new one, in the same phrase.

**Read the sentence.**

**Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — penetrated by view.**

**Read the sentence.**

20. Ime kho, bhikkhave, tayo niruttipathā adhivacanapathā paññattipathā asaṅkiṇṇā … (SN 22.62 — S iii 72)

    *ime* these (m. nom. pl.) · *kho* indeed · *bhikkhave* bhikkhus (m. voc. pl.) · *tayo* three (m. nom. pl.) · *niruttipathā* ways of speech (m. nom. pl.) · *adhivacanapathā* ways of designation (m. nom. pl.) · *paññattipathā* ways of description (m. nom. pl.) · *asaṅkiṇṇā* uncorrupted (m. nom. pl.)

    "These, bhikkhus, are the three ways of speech, of designation, of description, and they are uncorrupted …"

    The plainest case, and it is here to be the control. *Ime* and *tayo* are both masculine nominative plural and so are all four nouns: pronoun, numeral and noun in one case, one number, one gender. This is the **agreement** construction, the first of the four, and the reason a reader comes to expect it everywhere. Notice that the numeral does nothing a pronoun could not do, for both simply qualify the noun, and the next sentence is set for that reason.

21. Yā kho, bhikkhave, imesaṁ tiṇṇaṁ dhammānaṁ saṅgati sannipāto samavāyo, ayaṁ vuccati cakkhusamphasso. (SN 35.93 — S iv 68)

    *yā* which (f. nom. sg.) · *kho* indeed · *bhikkhave* bhikkhus (m. voc. pl.) · *imesaṁ* of these (m. gen. pl.) · *tiṇṇaṁ* of three (m. gen. pl.) · *dhammānaṁ* of things (m. gen. pl.) · *saṅgati* meeting (f. nom. sg.) · *sannipāto* coming together (m. nom. sg.) · *samavāyo* concurrence (m. nom. sg.) · *ayaṁ* this (nom. sg.) · *vuccati* is called · *cakkhusamphasso* eye-contact (m. nom. sg.)

    "The meeting, coming together and concurrence of these three things is what is called eye-contact."

    Three genitive plurals in a row, *imesaṁ tiṇṇaṁ dhammānaṁ*, and they **agree**. This is ordinary agreement that happens to be in the genitive, not the genitive *construction* of the numeral chapter, where a numeral-noun governs the thing counted. Be able to state the difference. In *sahassaṁ candānaṁ* the numeral is nominative and the noun genitive, and they cannot be a unit; here all three words share case, number and gender, and the whole phrase then depends on *saṅgati*. Suspicion of numerals with genitives will over-correct here.

    Then the two pronouns, which behave differently from each other. *Yā* is unambiguously **feminine**, and it agrees with *saṅgati*, the nearest of the three subjects, and not with the masculine *sannipāto* and *samavāyo* that follow it. *Ayaṁ* is the only form of *ima-* that serves masculine and feminine nominative singular alike, so it is untroubled by the mismatch and can answer to *cakkhusamphasso* without strain. A syncretic form is usually a nuisance; here it is a convenience, and the sentence would be harder to write without it.

22. Mano tesaṁ dhammānaṁ paṭhamaṁ uppajjati, anvadeva akusalā dhammā. (AN 1.56 — A i 11)

    *mano* mind (n. nom. sg.) · *tesaṁ* of those (m. gen. pl.) · *dhammānaṁ* of things (m. gen. pl.) · *paṭhamaṁ* first (n. nom./acc. sg., or adverbial) · *uppajjati* arises · *anvadeva* immediately after (ind.) · *akusalā* unskilful (m. nom. pl.) · *dhammā* things (m. nom. pl.)

    "Mind arises first of those things, and unskilful things follow immediately after."

    This is item 16 in a sentence, and it does not resolve. *Paṭhamaṁ* can be a neuter nominative singular agreeing with *mano*, which is neuter: 'mind is the first of those things to arise'. Or it can be the adverbial accusative the chapter met among the ordinal's non-counting uses, 'mind arises first', which is how Bodhi renders it. Both are available, both are good Pāli, and the sense is the same either way, so nothing in the sentence forces a choice, and that is no defect in the exercise. Expect every ambiguity to be resolved by context and you will be puzzled by a great many sentences like this one.

    *Tesaṁ dhammānaṁ* is a genitive plural of the distal *ta(d)*, agreeing with its noun and depending on *paṭhamaṁ*, 'first **of** those things', and this reading makes the adjectival analysis attractive. On the adverbial analysis it depends on nothing in particular and is simply the domain within which the arising happens first. The two readings differ in the function of the genitive, a cleaner way to see the ambiguity than arguing about the ordinal.

    One further thing, which was not visible when this review covered only two chapters. *Mano* is glossed neuter above, and item 13 has just established that the grammars divide over its gender. The adjectival reading of *paṭhamaṁ* **depends** on taking *mano* as neuter, because a masculine would want *paṭhamo*. So the two ambiguities are not independent: settle the gender and you have half-settled the ordinal. Nothing here settles either: the sentence has one reading in which *mano* is neuter and *paṭhamaṁ* agrees with it, and another in which the question of gender never arises because *paṭhamaṁ* is an adverb.

23. Tīṇimāni, bhikkhave, titthāyatanāni yāni paṇḍitehi samanuyuñjiyamānāni … parampi gantvā akiriyāya saṇṭhahanti. (AN 3.61 — A i 173)

    *tīṇi* three (n. nom. pl.) · *imāni* these (n. nom. pl.) · *bhikkhave* mendicants (m. voc. pl.) · *titthāyatanāni* sectarian tenets (n. nom. pl.) · *yāni* which (n. nom. pl.) · *paṇḍitehi* by the wise (m. inst. pl.) · *samanuyuñjiyamānāni* being cross-examined (n. nom. pl., passive present participle) · *paraṁ* further · *api* even · *gantvā* having gone · *akiriyāya* in inaction (f. dat./loc. sg.) · *saṇṭhahanti* they come to rest

    "There are these three sectarian tenets, mendicants, which — when cross-examined by the wise and pressed even further — come to rest in inaction."

    Four words in a row all carrying the neuter nominative plural, from four different chapters: the numeral *tīṇi*, the demonstrative *imāni*, the noun *titthāyatanāni*, and the relative *yāni*. Add the participle *samanuyuñjiyamānāni* and it is five. This is the agreement construction at full stretch, and it is here because it shows what agreement buys a reader: five words, and the case-number-gender of all five is established the moment any one of them is.

    Notice that *tīṇi* is the **neuter** form of 'three'. It could not be *tayo*, which is masculine, and the choice is forced by *titthāyatanāni*. That is the numeral chapter's first construction, behaving as the review's item 9 said it does.

    *Paṇḍitehi* is the odd one out. It is masculine instrumental plural, agreeing with no other word in the sentence, and it is an adjective used as a noun: 'the wise', *paṇḍita*, standing on its own without a noun to describe, the pattern the gender-and-adjectives chapter set out. It is instrumental because it is the agent of a passive participle.

24. Sabbapāpassa akaraṇaṁ, kusalassa upasampadā; Sacittapariyodapanaṁ, etaṁ buddhāna sāsanaṁ. (Dhp 183 — Dhp p. 27)

    *sabbapāpassa* of all evil (n. gen. sg.) · *akaraṇaṁ* the not-doing (n. nom. sg.) · *kusalassa* of the wholesome (n. gen. sg.) · *upasampadā* the undertaking (f. nom. sg.) · *sacittapariyodapanaṁ* the cleansing of one's own mind (n. nom. sg.) · *etaṁ* this (n. nom. sg.) · *buddhāna* of the Buddhas (m. gen. pl.) · *sāsanaṁ* the teaching (n. nom. sg.)

    "Not doing any evil, undertaking the wholesome, cleansing one's own mind — this is the teaching of the Buddhas."

    Three subjects, then a predication, and every chapter of Part II is in it.

    *Sabba* 'all' is a **pronominal adjective**: it declines on the pronoun pattern, not the noun pattern, and here it is the first member of a compound with *pāpa*. *Kusalassa* is an adjective used as a noun and neuter, which is the gender such a noun takes when it names a quality and not a person, the same word that was an ordinary adjective in *kusalaṁ ṭhānaṁ*. *Upasampadā* is a feminine *ā*-stem nominative singular standing as one of three parallel subjects, two of which are neuter; nothing agrees with anything across the three, because they are a list, not a phrase. And *etaṁ*, neuter nominative singular, answers to *sāsanaṁ* and gathers all three.

    The form to stop on is ***buddhāna***. It is a genitive plural, 'of the Buddhas', with the niggahīta dropped: *buddhānaṁ* without its *-ṁ*. This is a metrical licence, common in verse and rare in prose, and it is canonical: 23 occurrences in the root texts against 148 for the full form. If you have met only *-ānaṁ* you will not recognise it, yet it can be nothing else.

**Put it back into Pāli.**

25. ***buddhāna*** (item 24), ***imesaṁ tiṇṇaṁ dhammānaṁ*** (item 21), ***sahassaṁ candānaṁ*** (item 3).

    The chapters behind them are not the same. *Buddhāna* draws on the nouns chapter alone, for the *a*-stem genitive plural, with the metre chapter accounting for the nasal it has dropped. *Sahassaṁ candānaṁ* draws on two: the numerals chapter for the construction, a noun taking a genitive, not an adjective agreeing, and then the same *a*-stem genitive plural again for the ending on the moons. The middle one draws on three at once. *Imesaṁ* is the pronouns chapter's *imesaṁ*; *tiṇṇaṁ* is the numerals chapter's *tiṇṇaṁ*; and *dhammānaṁ* is an ordinary *a*-stem genitive plural out of the nouns chapter. Three words in a row, in the same case, from three different paradigms. A Pāli noun phrase is routinely like this, and so the four chapters of Part II are one part, not four.

    The short one is *buddhāna*, for *buddhānaṁ*. To be sure it is not an error you would have to go outside Part II entirely. The line is verse, and a final niggahīta is regularly dropped there for the metre. That is the metre chapter's business, and item 28 of that set is this very line, asked from the other side: what the missing niggahīta buys. The editions also agree on the reading: the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana, the Buddha Jayanti and the Pali Text Society all print *buddhāna* here. A form that looks short is a claim about what an editor printed before it is a claim about what a poet did, and the way to check that is the apparatus, not the grammar.

## Answers: Review — The Verb

**Sotānugata — met by ear.**

1. *Tasanti* and *bhāyanti* are third person plural presents and belong to **Chapter 7**; *haneyya* is an optative and belongs to **Chapter 8**; *ghātaye* is an optative built on a causative stem, and the stem is **Chapter 9**'s. Four finite verbs and the three chapters of this part, in a verse of sixteen words.

    *Haneyya* and *ghātaye* are the same mood, person and number, and the whole difference between them is the **causative**. *Haneyya* is the optative of the simple verb, "should kill"; *ghātaye* is the optative of a causative stem, "should cause to kill". One affix, and what it moves is the agent: the first forbids the hand, the second forbids the order.

    So the two halves of the line forbid two different things, and only the morphology says so. Take them as elegant variation, Pāli doubling a word for emphasis as it very often does, and you get "one should not kill, one should not kill": the second half of the precept disappears with the affix. The second half catches the one who never touches the rod. A syllable carries the difference between a rule about violence and a rule about violence and its procurement.

2. The finite verbs are *asi*, *kāhasi* and *ajjhagā*. Three clauses have no verb in them at all, and each is a past participle standing as its own predicate: *sabbā te phāsukā **bhaggā***, *gahakūṭaṁ **visaṅkhataṁ***, ***visaṅkhāragataṁ** cittaṁ*. None of the three is inflected for tense. The participle supplies the tense, since a past participle is past by its own form, together with a copula the reader has to understand and the poet has not written.

    The fourth is *diṭṭho·si*, which is the same construction with the copula printed: a past participle plus *asi*, the second person singular present of *atthi*. Note what that does to the tense. The copula is present and the sense is a completed act ("you are seen", meaning "you have been found out"), because the tense of the clause is in the participle and the copula supplies only the person.

    *Kāhasi* is the trap, and Chapter 8 explains it. It is a second person singular future of *karoti* and it has no *-ssa-* in it: in verse the *-ss-* of the future weakens to *-h-*, so that beside the ordinary *karissasi* the canon also has *kāhasi*, and beside *karissati*, *kāhati*. The affix has been worn away, not omitted, and these forms exist because they are a syllable shorter. Meeting a future you cannot cut into a stem and an affix, stop cutting. The *h*-futures are a short closed list for the two commonest verbs in the language, and they are learnt as vocabulary, not derived.

3. *Harāyati* is a **denominative**, from *hiri* 'shame'; *jigucchati* is a **desiderative**, from *gup-*; and *aṭṭīyati* is left unlabelled in Chapter 9's gloss, though the chapter discusses it. Perniola files it among the *-īya-* denominatives, and the chapter's box is about how the word is spelt, not how it is formed. So: two named formations and one the sources leave open.

    All three end in *-ti*, and that ending is Chapter 7's. It is the third person singular primary ending and it says the same thing on all three verbs: third person, singular, present, active endings. The stems are Chapter 9's, and they say something entirely different on each.

    Carry that sentence out of this part. A Pāli verb's ending shows person, number and which of the two series it belongs to, and nothing more. Whether the verb is passive, causative, denominative, desiderative or none of them is a fact about the stem, and no ending anywhere in the language will report it. Item 13 is the same lesson in one word, and item 18 shows what happens when the stem is ambiguous too.

    One thing to notice on the way past: this is a stock phrase, and Chapter 9 prints it from a different sutta. Three words that always travel together, in the canon's usual manner.

**Vacasā paricitā — rehearsed aloud.**

4. *Vijānanti*, *vijānanti* and *sammanti* take the ordinary **active** endings, *parassapada*, third person plural *-anti*. *Yamāmase* is the odd one: first person plural, and its ending belongs to the middle, the *attanopada*.

    The middle does there what it always does: it turns the action back on the people performing it. "Let us restrain **ourselves**": English needs the reflexive pronoun to say what the Pāli says with four letters at the end of the verb. The active first person plural would have given "let us restrain", and the verse would then be about restraining somebody.

    This is voice in the first of item 8's two senses, and the item exists because the word does double duty. Active against middle is a fact about the *endings*, and *yamāmase* is the whole of the evidence for it in this verse. Active against passive is a fact about the *stem*, and no ending will ever report it: item 3's sentence again, reached from the other side. Run the two together and a middle ending starts looking like a passive, which it is not, and a passive stem with an active ending starts looking like an error, which it is not either.

5. "Flung open for them are the doors to the deathless; let those who have ears release their faith. Perceiving trouble, Brahmā, I did not speak the refined, sublime Dhamma among human beings."

    ***Pamuñcantu*** is a **third person plural imperative**, 'let them release', on the stem *pamuñca-*. That stem is Chapter 7's second conjugation class in its purest form. The root is *muc-*, and the *-ñ-* is a nasal pushed inside the root before the class vowel, which is what *rudhādigaṇa* means. So the specimen verb of the class, *muñcati*, turns up prefixed in the most famous verse of the sutta.

    ***Bhāsiṁ*** is an *s*-aorist, first person singular, 'I spoke', negated here, 'I did not speak'. It lacks the augment. Chapter 8's graded rule says the root aorist always takes the augment and the *a*- and *s*-aorists generally do; the augmented form would be *abhāsiṁ*, and Sanskrit has *abhāsīt* for the third singular. The last clause of that rule decides it here: in verse, the metre decides. The line is a *tuṭṭhubha* pāda of eleven syllables, and the augment cannot be accommodated either way. Written out, *na abhāsiṁ* gives twelve. Elided to *nābhāsiṁ* it keeps eleven and breaks the cadence instead: as printed the line closes —⏑—×, which is the Tuṭṭhubha's cadence, and with the long *nā* it would close ———×, which no Tuṭṭhubha does. So the absence is neither a variant reading nor a defective form. The poet has dropped a prefix he is entitled to drop.

    A note on saying it. Hold *apārutā* and *paṇītaṁ* at their length, and sound both halves of the doubled consonant in *pamuñcantu* and *vihiṁsasaññī*. The *ññ* is a held palatal nasal, not an *n*, and rushing it is the commonest fault in recitation. *Sajjhāya*, recitation, is the tradition's instrument for holding a text, and it works for the same reason spaced retrieval works: it makes you produce the thing, not merely recognise it.

**Manasānupekkhitā — gone over in the mind.**

**Recall.**

6. The present stem is the third person singular of the present indicative with the *-ti* taken off: *gacchati* gives *gaccha-*, *deseti* gives *dese-*, *jānāti* gives *jānā-*. Since a dictionary cites a verb in that third singular, the stem comes free, with no analysis needed. Three forms are built on it, the **present indicative**, the **imperative** and the **optative**, so those three make one chapter. The rest is built on the root instead, or on a stem made from the root: the aorist, the future and the conditional, so they make the next chapter. The division reflects Oberlies's point that in Pāli the centre of the verb has moved from the root to the present stem, so that everything regular is derived from the stem and what is not so derived is a historical relic, and the past tense is full of relics.

7. The **primary** endings serve the present indicative and the future. The **secondary** endings serve the aorist and the conditional. The imperative has its own set. The **optative takes both**. That sentence of Perniola's says more than the tables it summarises: the future is the present endings with *-(i)ssa-* pushed in front of them, not a new paradigm, and the conditional is the future stem with the aorist's endings.

8. **Active against middle**, *parassapada* against *attanopada*, is carried by the **personal endings**: *-ti* against *-te*, *-nti* against *-nte* or *-re*. **Active against passive** is carried by the **stem**: a *-ya-* stem is passive and then takes whatever endings it likes, active-looking ones included. The endings can never tell you the second one. *Vuccati* has an ordinary active *-ti* and means 'is called'; *pacati* has the same ending and means 'cooks'. The ending cannot distinguish them, so Chapter 9's recognition procedure works on stems and not on endings.

9. *Mā* takes the aorist, and the aorist then loses its past sense entirely: *mā bhāyi* 'do not fear', a survival of the old injunctive with nothing to do with the past. *Na* takes the optative for the same English: *na seveyya* 'one should not resort to'. The difference is more than politeness. *Mā* forbids **a particular act now** (stop, don't do that), while *na* with the optative **lays down a precept**, a standing rule about what one ought not to do. Render both as "don't" and you lose the difference between a command and a training rule, which in a monastic text matters a great deal.

10. The **fourth**, the *is*-aorist, is the living one: it is formed from the present stem and, in Perniola's words, can be formed from any present stem ending in *-a*. That is a claim about productivity, and it is useful as well as historical because it runs the other way for a reader: if you know a verb's present stem you already know its most likely aorist, without knowing anything about the verb's history. And it turns the other three types into **a list of exceptions**, not a parallel system to be learnt alongside it. The root aorist in particular is five verbs; it feels large only because those five are among the commonest words in the language.

11. Productive: the **passive**, the **causative** and the **denominative**. Unproductive: the **desiderative** and the **intensive**. Productivity licenses you to meet a causative of a verb no grammar lists, work it out from the rule, and be right. It forbids the reverse move: a word that looks like a desiderative of a verb no grammar lists is almost certainly not one, because Pāli stopped making them before the canon was composed. So meeting a reduplicated stem, look the word up; do not derive it. Oberlies names five surviving forms for the two categories together (*jigucchati*, *tikicchati*, *sussūsati*, *lālappati*, *jaṅgamati*) against the hundreds of causatives in the canon, and each of them has gone its own way.

Say what the form is, and what would settle it.

Say what the form is, and what would settle it.

12. ***Desesi***: two readings, the ambiguity that recurs across the whole *-e-* class. It is either the third person singular *s*-aorist, 'he taught', or the second person singular present indicative, 'you teach'. The *-si* of the *s*-aorist third singular and the *-si* of the present second singular are simply identical. The person of the surrounding discourse settles it: an *s*-aorist third singular sits in narrative about somebody else, and a present second singular sits in speech addressed to someone, usually with a vocative nearby. In practice the aorist reading is far commoner, because *deseti* is a verb of narrative. Note the third thing about this form, which belongs to Chapter 9: the stem is itself a **causative**, *dis-* 'point out' strengthened to *dese-*, so the word is a causative stem in an aorist ending with a present-tense look-alike. Three chapters in six letters.

13. ***Assa***: the ambiguity spans a Part, not a chapter. It is either the third person singular optative of *atthi*, 'it would be, there might be', the *-eyya* ending contracted onto the root *as-*, or the genitive or dative singular of the demonstrative *ta(d)* / *ima-*, 'of him, to him, of this'. Both are extremely common, and the corpus cannot separate them: the string *assa* stands 812 times in the root texts, and that figure is the two readings added together, because no search can tell an optative from a genitive. Take it as a measure of how often the question arises, not of how often the answer is 'optative'. Whether the clause already has a finite verb settles it. If it does, *assa* is the pronoun; if it does not, *assa* is carrying the predicate. A second cue is a hypothetical frame (*sace*, *ce*, *yadi*, or a question about what would follow), which pulls it towards the optative.

14. ***Khomhi***: two words, not one, *kho* + *amhi*, 'I am indeed'. *Amhi* is the first person singular of *atthi*, the doublet of *asmi*, and the junction has swallowed its initial vowel. Context is not needed here, because once resolved the form is unambiguous; the difficulty lies wholly in seeing the junction. The form is not rare (*khomhi* alone stands 134 times in the root texts), and the whole of the first singular behaves this way. Neither *asmi* nor *amhi* is normally printed as a free-standing word.

15. ***Avoca***: the *a*-aorist of *vac-* 'speak', and it is either second or third person singular, since that type has the same *-a* in both. 'You said' or 'he said'. The presence of an addressee settles it: this verb is overwhelmingly the third person of reported narrative, and it is the word that stands at the end of the formula introducing direct speech, usually inside *etadavoca*. Two further things belong to it. Nothing of the root is visible on the surface (Perniola derives it through a reduplication that then weakens and contracts), and the verb has no present in *vac-* at all: its present is *vadati* and its passive is *vuccati*. The three have to be learnt as a set, which is a fair sample of what "the past tense is where the irregularity lives" means in practice.

16. ***Dissati***: the **passive** of *dassati* 'sees', so 'is seen', third person singular present. It is on this list because it looks like an ordinary present of some root *diss-*, and because it is easily glossed 'sees'. The clue is the doubled consonant where the root has a single one, *dis-* + *-ya-* assimilating to *-ss-*; the confirmation is that the simple-consonant active stands beside it in the language and that the sense is one of undergoing. The case of the surrounding words settles it: a passive has the thing acted on in the nominative and the agent, if named at all, in the instrumental. Note also what its own past tense does not show: the aorist of the active verb is *addasā*, from which no reader would ever guess *dissati*.

17. ***Sādiyati***: the trap. It has the form of a passive, an *-iya-* stem on the present stem, and the sense of an **active** verb, 'consents to, accepts'. It is not a rarity: two hundred occurrences in the root texts, and it keeps company with *vediyati* 'feels', *ādiyati* 'takes up', *samādiyati* 'undertakes' and *ruccati* 'is pleased with'. Oberlies's account is that these inherited part of the dead middle's function, an action done for oneself, which is close to what these verbs mean. Only the sense of the sentence settles it, and so Chapter 9 says the form raises a question and does not answer one. Trust the *-iya-* and you will make the Vinaya's *sādiyati* mean the opposite of what a rule needs it to mean.

18. ***Labbhetha***: the most heavily loaded form in this review. It is the third person singular optative middle on the **passive stem** of *labhati* 'gets'. So: *labh-* + *-ya-* assimilating to *labbha-*, which is Chapter 9; then the middle optative ending *-etha*, which is Chapter 7's second column. 'It would be possible, one could obtain.' There is nothing to settle here, for the form is unambiguous, and it is included for the opposite reason. Chapter 7 says the middle survives only in scattered forms and that a reader's practical need is only to recognise it. Here is a form to recognise: seventeen occurrences in the root texts, one of them in the second discourse, with a sense no other form gives.

**Build the form.**

**Build the form.**

19. Third singular: *abhavissa*. Third plural: *abhavissaṁsu*. Three steps come before the ending. Take the future stem, *bhavissa-*; prefix the **augment** *a-*; and then use the **secondary** endings, not the primary ones. That is the whole of the conditional. The sense follows from the form: a future seen from a point in the past is something that was going to happen and did not. Both forms are canonical, 143 and 27 occurrences in the root texts, and *bhavati* is the verb to peg this paradigm on because the display paradigms the grammars print (Perniola inflects *labhati*) return nothing at all from the root texts.

20. ***Siyā*** and ***assa***. They come from the two different optative series Chapter 7 sets out. *Siyā* is the *-yā* type, the older ending that survives in a few common verbs beside *vajjā* and *dajjā*. *Assa* is the ordinary ***-eyya*** contracted onto the root *as-*. Both are third person singular and both mean 'it would be, there might be'. *Siyā* has 3,843 occurrences in the root texts, and it is unambiguous, so that is the figure to trust: this is the busiest optative in the language, as it must be, since so much of the Buddha's conditional and hypothetical speech runs through 'would be'. *Assa*'s 812 cannot be read the same way; see item 13, where the same string is also a pronoun.

21. ***Gaccha*** and ***gacchāhi***, and they are the same command. One is the ending *-hi* with the stem vowel lengthened before it, by the rule that lengthens *gaccha-* before *-hi*, *-mi* and *-ma*; the other is the bare stem with no ending at all. Only stems in *-a* can take the shorter form, because in a stem with a long vowel nothing would distinguish the imperative from the stem itself: *dese* and *jānā* would be indistinguishable from the material they are built on, whereas *gaccha* at least sits in a sentence where a command is expected. Both are well attested; so are *karohi* and *suṇohi*, where the *-o-* stem shows the ending clearly.

22. ***Vuccati***. The passive suffix *-ya-* goes straight onto the consonant-final root and is swallowed by it: Perniola takes it through *vac-ya-ti* > *uc-ya-ti* > *uccati*, with a *v-* prefixed to the initial *u-*. The result has no visible *y* at all and looks like an ordinary first-conjugation present. The aorist is no help, because the aorist of this verb is *avoca*, which is a reduplicated and contracted *a*-aorist with nothing of the root left on its surface either. Present *vadati*, aorist *avoca*, passive *vuccati*: three forms, one verb, and none of them predicts another. No case in the book shows more clearly that a verb has to be learnt as a set, not derived.

23. Present third plural: *bhāventi*. Future third singular: *bhāvessati*. The trap is in the second. The causative stem is ***bhāve-***, the root *bhū-* pushed to the *vuddhi* grade and given the causative *-e-*, and the future infix attaches to *that* stem: *bhāve-* + *-ssa-* + *-ti*. So the form is *bhāvessati* with an *e*, and *bhāvissati* is not a Pāli word. It returns nothing from any layer of the corpus except the grammarians' texts, because it has been built on the present stem of the base verb instead of on the causative stem. *Bhāvessati* has 75 root-text occurrences. The general point is Chapter 9's thesis stated negatively: a derived stem adds no new endings, but it does replace the stem, and every later formation must be built on the replacement.

24. ***Mā, bhikkhave, bhāyittha.*** *Mā* with the **aorist**, second person plural, and the aorist has no past sense in this construction. Chapter 7 would have offered the imperative *mā bhāyatha*, which the canon does not use here; the aorist is the standing form after *mā*, and the reason is historical. It is really the old injunctive, never a past tense, which now merely looks like an aorist.

**Read the sentence.**

**Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — penetrated by view.**

**Read the sentence.**

25. Word by word: *rūpaṁ* form (n. nom. sg.) · *ca* and · *hi* for · *idaṁ* this (n. nom. sg.) · *bhikkhave* monks (m. voc. pl.) · *attā* self (m. nom. sg.) · *abhavissa* were (3 sg. cond.) · *na* not · *ayidaṁ* this · *rūpaṁ* form (n. nom. sg.) · *ābādhāya* to affliction (m. dat. sg.) · *saṁvatteyya* would lead (3 sg. opt.) · *labbhetha* it would be possible (3 sg. opt. mid., passive stem) · *ca* and · *rūpe* with regard to form (n. loc. sg.). "For if form were self, monks, this form would not lead to affliction, and it would be possible with regard to form …"

    The three verbs, in order. *Abhavissa* is the **conditional**, the form built in item 19. *Saṁvatteyya* is an ordinary **active optative**, third singular. *Labbhetha* is the middle optative on a passive stem, item 18. So one sentence carries the rarest finite form in the language, the commonest mood in the canon, and the survival of a voice that is otherwise almost extinct.

    The protasis is *rūpañca hidaṁ … attā abhavissa*, and the apodosis is everything after it. This follows the normal division of labour **exactly**: conditional in the clause carrying the condition, optative in the clause carrying the consequence. It is the textbook instance, and the next item is not.

26. *no* not · *ce* if · *taṁ* that (n. acc. sg.) · *padaheyya* one were to strive (3 sg. opt.) · *na* not · *ayidaṁ* this · *saccaṁ* the truth (n. acc. sg.) · *anupāpuṇeyya* one would arrive at (3 sg. opt.). "If one were not to strive, one would not arrive at the truth."

    The condition is marked by ***ce***, the particle, not the verb, and **both verbs are optatives**. There is no conditional in the sentence at all.

    What the pair teaches is a proportion. Chapter 8 says the conditional is the rarest finite form in the language and that the optative usually stands in for it; item 26 shows what "usually" looks like on the page. You will build the conditional once and meet the optative in conditional clauses constantly, so the form to have cold is *padaheyya*, and *abhavissa* is a form to recognise. Note also *saccamanupāpuṇeyya*, which is *saccaṁ* + *anupāpuṇeyya* with the niggahīta becoming *m* before a vowel. The verb is a *-uṇā-* stem of Chapter 7's fourth class, and if you do not undo the junction you will hunt for a verb *manupāpuṇāti*.

27. *atha kho* then · *bhagavā* the Blessed One (m. nom. sg.) · *bhikkhū* the monks (m. acc. pl.) · *āmantesi* addressed (3 sg. aor.) · *handa* come now · *dāni* now · *bhikkhave* monks (m. voc. pl.) · *āmantayāmi* I address (1 sg. pres.) · *vo* you (acc./dat. pl., enclitic) · *vaya·dhammā* subject to decay (m. nom. pl.) · *saṅkhārā* conditions (m. nom. pl.) · *appamādena* with diligence (m. inst. sg.) · *sampādetha* accomplish (2 pl. imper.). "Then the Blessed One addressed the monks: 'Come now, monks, I address you: conditions are subject to decay; accomplish it with diligence.'"

    *Āmantesi* and *āmantayāmi* are one stem in its two shapes. The eighth conjugation class takes the affix ***-e-* or *-aya-***, one affix written contracted and uncontracted, *āmante-* and *āmantaya-*, not two. Chapter 7's table of the classes has both in a single row, against *deseti*. So *āmantesi* is that stem with the *s*-aorist third singular ending, and *āmantayāmi* is the same stem uncontracted with the primary first singular *-mi*, the stem vowel lengthened before it by the rule of item 21.

    *Sampādetha* is a **causative**: DPD gives it as the causative of *sampajjati*, *saṁ* + *pāde* + *ti*, and the root vowel strengthening to *-pāde-* is the visible mark of it. The mood is the **second person plural imperative**, whose ending is identical to the present indicative's, so the form could as easily be read 'you accomplish'; the vocative and the sense settle it. Sit with the fact that the last sentence the Buddha is recorded as speaking ends on a causative imperative: *cause it to be accomplished, by means of diligence*, more than "be diligent".

28. The sense: "In the same way, monks, in a future time there will be monks who, when those discourses spoken by the Realized One — deep, profound in meaning, transcendent, dealing with emptiness — are being recited, will not want to listen, will not lend an ear, will not apply their minds to understand, and will not think those teachings worth learning and mastering."

    Five verbs are in the **future**, formed by inserting *-(i)ssa-* between the stem and the ordinary primary endings: *bhavissanti*, *sussūsissanti*, *odahissanti*, *upaṭṭhāpessanti*, *maññissanti*.

    Now the stems underneath them, and this is the item's point:

    - *bhavissanti*: the plain present stem *bhava-*.
    - *sussūsissanti*: a **desiderative**, *sussūsa-*, 'desires to hear', one of the five that survive.
    - *upaṭṭhāpessanti*: a **causative** in *-āpe-*, from *upaṭṭhahati*, 'causes to stand near'. Note *-essanti* and not *-issanti*, for the reason given in item 23.
    - *maññissanti*: a ***-ya-* stem** of the third conjugation class, *mañña-* from *man-*.

    And a fifth kind is present without being finite: *bhaññamānesu* is a **passive** stem, *bhaṇ-* + *-ya-* assimilated, in a locative absolute. So a desiderative, a causative, a class sign and a passive are all carrying the same tense, with the same endings, in one sentence. That demonstrates Chapter 9's argument (one set of endings, several stems) in Chapter 8's tense, better than any table could.

    *Maññissanti* is **not** a passive. The doubling is the third conjugation's *-ya-* class sign, not the passive's, and those are the same morph, so Chapter 9 demotes the doubled consonant from a test to a reason to look. Two of the three checks fail here: there is no simple-consonant active *manati* standing beside it in the canon, and the sense is not one of undergoing. 'They will think', not 'they will be thought'.

29. *uṭṭhehi* rise! (2 sg. imper.) · *vīra* hero (m. voc. sg.) · *vijita·saṅgāma* victor in battle (m. voc. sg.) · *sattha·vāha* caravan leader (m. voc. sg.) · *anaṇa* debtless one (m. voc. sg.) · *vicara* wander! (2 sg. imper.) · *loke* in the world (m. loc. sg.) · *desassu* teach! (2 sg. imper. mid.) · *bhagavā* Blessed One (m. voc. sg.) · *dhammaṁ* the Dhamma (m. acc. sg.) · *aññātāro* those who understand (m. nom. pl.) · *bhavissanti* there will be (3 pl. fut.). "Rise, hero, victor in battle! Caravan leader, debtless one, wander the world! Let the Blessed One teach the Dhamma — there will be those who understand."

    *Uṭṭhehi* and *vicara* are both second person singular imperatives and they look nothing alike because the imperative has two shapes in that one cell: the ending *-hi*, and the bare stem with no ending. *Uṭṭhehi* takes the ending; *vicara* is the naked stem *vicara-*. Item 21 is this distinction met in the wild, one line apart.

    *Desassu*: the stem is the **causative** *dese-*, from *dis-* 'point out'; the mood is the **imperative**; the person and number are **second singular**; and the voice is **middle**. DPD parses it as *dese* + *ssu*, calling it an irregular form of the expected *desessu*. The middle imperative second singular in *-ssu* is in Chapter 7's second column, where a reader is told they need only recognise such forms, and here is one, in a verse every Buddhist knows, saying what no active form could.

    *Desetu*, the reading of the Syāmese, Cambodian and PTS editions, is the **third person singular active** imperative: 'let him teach'. So the two readings differ in person and in voice at once, and what changes is who Brahmā is talking to. *Desetu* keeps the deference of the prose section three verses earlier, where Brahmā speaks about the Buddha in the third person: *desetu, bhante, bhagavā dhammaṁ*, 'sir, let the Blessed One teach'. *Desassu* addresses him directly and in the middle voice: **teach, thou**. Read with the two imperatives before it, *uṭṭhehi* and *vicara*, which are unambiguously second person, the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana's reading is the harder and the more coherent one, for the verse has been urging him on for three lines. No doctrine depends on it. But the editions disagree here about whether a god is addressing the Buddha or speaking about him, not about a spelling, and that is a fact about the apparatus.

30. *ekaṁ samayaṁ* at one time · *bhagavā* the Blessed One (m. nom. sg.) · *uruvelāyaṁ* at Uruvelā (f. loc. sg.) · *viharati* dwells (3 sg. pres.) · *najjā* of the river (f. gen. sg.) · *nerañjarāya* Nerañjarā (f. gen. sg.) · *tīre* on the bank (n. loc. sg.) · *ajapāla·nigrodha·mūle* at the root of the goatherd's banyan (m. loc. sg.) · *paṭhama·abhisambuddho* newly awakened (m. nom. sg.); *atha kho* then · *bhagavato* to the Blessed One (m. gen. sg.) · *raho·gatassa* gone into seclusion (m. gen. sg.) · *paṭisallīnassa* withdrawn (m. gen. sg.) · *evaṁ* thus · *cetaso* of mind (n. gen. sg.) · *parivitakko* a reflection (m. nom. sg.) · *udapādi* arose (3 sg. aor.).

    *Viharati* is a **present indicative** and *udapādi* an **aorist**, and both describe the same afternoon some centuries ago. This is the **historic present**, not an inconsistency, and Pāli uses it freely where English can manage it only in a consciously colloquial register. Every sutta opens this way, and translators render it as a past for that reason. The event has not moved; only the tense has.

    Look again at *udapādi*, because there the augment can be seen behaving as Chapter 8 says it does. The word is *ud* + *a* + *pad* + *i*: the prefix, then the **augment**, then the root. The augment is **buried in the middle** of the word, not at the front, and there a reader is most likely to lose it and read *-apād-* as part of the stem.

**Put it back into Pāli.**

**Put it back into Pāli.**

31. Ahañceva kho pana dhammaṁ deseyyaṁ; pare ca me na ājāneyyuṁ. (SN 6.1 — S i 136) Two optatives, one first person singular and one third person plural, and no conditional anywhere: item 26's proportion again. *Ahañceva* is *ahaṁ* + *ca* + *eva*, with the niggahīta assimilating to the following palatal. If you produced *sace ahaṁ dhammaṁ deseyyaṁ* you have written good Pāli and not this sentence, which is the exercise: the condition here is carried by *ca … ca* and the mood, without *sace*.

32. **Mā, bhikkhave, puññānaṁ bhāyittha.** (AN 7.62 — A iv 88) *Mā* with the second person plural aorist, as in item 24. The thing feared stands in the **genitive**, *puññānaṁ*, which is not what an English speaker expects and is the ordinary construction with this verb. The line is worth carrying whole for its sense as much as its grammar: it introduces the Buddha's account of his own past merit, and it is a prohibition against a fear a monastic audience might well have.

33. **Bhārañca vo, bhikkhave, desessāmi bhārahārañca bhārādānañca bhāranikkhepanañca.** (SN 22.22 — S iii 25) The future of the causative *deseti*, first person singular: *dese-* + *-ssa-* + *-mi*, with the stem vowel lengthened before the first-person ending.
