Note again *-essāmi* and not *-issāmi*, for item 23's reason. *Vo* is the enclitic, on which see item 36, and the four accusatives are strung with *ca* after each, not only after the last, as the canon does and English does not.

**Read at length.**

**Read at length.**

34. Brahmā's speech is the argument, and the three verbs are its three moves. ***Parihāyanti*** is a **present indicative** stating what is the case now: there are beings with little dust in their eyes, and they are declining for want of hearing. That is the evidence. ***Desetu*** is an **imperative**, third person singular, and it is the request; the third person makes it deferential, not a command, since Pāli has no politeness marker of its own and the person serves instead. ***Bhavissanti*** is a **future**, and it is the inducement: a flat prediction, not a wish, *there will be those who understand*. Evidence, request, consequence, in three sentences and three moods. It also demonstrates Chapter 7's remark that the third-person imperative covers instruction, invitation, blessing and entreaty alike, and that which one is meant depends on who is speaking to whom. A god entreating a Buddha is at the far end of that range.

    *Pasāreyya* and *samiñjeyya* are optatives in a **simile**, and neither wishes nor supposes anything. This is the optative's second use, the polite or provisional statement: here the hypothetical of comparison, "just as a strong man *might* stretch out his bent arm or bend his outstretched arm". English does the same thing with "would" or "might" in a simile. It matters because, having learnt the optative as the mood of conditions, you will look for a condition, and there is none: the whole simile is a single suspended supposition with no consequent clause at all.

    *Nassati* and *vinassati* are presents used to state what is always the case or what is now unavoidably in train, and Bodhi's "is lost" and "is to perish" render the sense, not the tense. Neither is a present progressive, and the two are not even rendered alike: the first is done and the second is coming, which is the range a single Pāli present covers here. Chapter 7 treats this under what the present tense does: a general truth has no particular time, and Pāli puts it in the present, reaching into the future without any future form. Brahmā is not reporting that the world is at this instant perishing; he is saying that this is how it goes, given what he has just learnt.

    The aorists are *etadahosi*, *pāturahosi*, *etadavoca*, *idamavoca* and *etadavoca* again: five instances, and only two verbs between them. Three of the five are *avoca*. Both *etadahosi* and *pāturahosi* are *ahosi*, the *s*-aorist of *hoti*. A Pāli narrative is built so heavily out of two verbs because the canon was composed for recitation, and its narrative frame is formulaic by design: somebody thinks something, somebody appears somewhere, somebody says something. *Etadahosi*, *pāturahosi*, *etadavoca* are the joints of that frame, and to learn those three phrases whole is to learn the framework of several thousand suttas. Note that all three are junctions (*etaṁ* + *ahosi*, *pātu* + *ahosi*, *etaṁ* + *avoca*), the subject of item 35.

**Reaching back.**

**Reaching back.**

35. *Etadavoca* is *etaṁ* + *avoca*, with the old stem-final *-d-* of *etad* surfacing before the vowel. *Idamavoca* is *idaṁ* + *avoca*, with the same thing happening to the *-m* of *idam*. *Tenañjaliṁ* is *tena* + *añjaliṁ*, the two *a*-sounds merging. *Khomhi* is *kho* + *amhi*.

    ***Khomhi*** defeats a search of a digital text, and the reason needs stating carefully. In the first three the verb survives the junction intact: *avoca* and *añjaliṁ* are still there as substrings, so searching for the word inside a longer word finds them. In *khomhi* the junction has eaten the initial vowel of the second word, so what is left is *-mhi* and not *-amhi*. A search for *amhi* as a word standing alone finds nothing; and the usual remedy of searching for it inside longer words finds nothing either, or worse, finds the pronominal locatives *tamhi* and *yamhi*, which are a different ending on a different word class and make the empty result look like a finding. The rule to carry: when a form begins with a vowel, look for it without that vowel too, because a preceding word ending in a vowel will have absorbed it.

36. *Vo* and *me* are the **enclitic** forms of the second and first person pronouns, *vo* standing for *tumhe*, *tumhākaṁ* or *tumhaṁ*, and *me* for *mayā*, *mama* or *mayhaṁ*. They are preferred because they are unstressed and short: the canon reaches for the enclitic wherever the pronoun is carrying no weight of its own, and reaches for the full form where it is being contrasted or emphasised. That is the principle behind Chapter 5's account of why a pronoun subject appears at all: a full form adds emphasis, an enclitic is merely present. The rule of position they both obey is that an enclitic can never stand first in its sentence. It has no accent to begin a sentence with, and it leans on the word before it, which is what "enclitic" means. So *āmantayāmi vo* and *pare ca me na ājāneyyuṁ* both place them second or later, and to tell whether a *no* or a *vo* is a pronoun or a particle, begin by asking where in the clause it sits.

37. *Cattāro* joins *iddhipādā* by **agreement**, the first of Chapter 6's four constructions, and the one a reader most readily assumes is the only one. *Catu* is a numeral below nineteen, so it behaves as an adjective: no gender of its own, taking the masculine of *iddhipādā*, and standing in the same case and number, nominative plural.

    *Tiṭṭheyya* is the **optative**, and it marks the difference between a claim and an entitlement. An indicative, *tiṭṭhati*, 'he lives on', would assert that the Buddha does in fact live for the full lifespan, which is not what is being said. The optative with *ākaṅkhamāno*, 'wishing', says he **could** if he wished, which leaves the wishing unexercised. The whole of the passage that follows in DN 16, and Ānanda's failure in it, depends on the sentence having been a statement of capacity, not of fact. This is the optative's second use again, the provisional statement, and a good instance of a mood carrying doctrinal weight.

38. The sentence works without a finite verb because *desitā* is a past participle, and a past participle plus a nominative subject is a complete Pāli sentence. Chapter 9 makes the point in an aside: the commonest way of saying in Pāli that something is done is a past participle, not a passive verb, and by a long way.

    *Mayā* is the **instrumental** of the first person pronoun, and it is in that case because it is the agent of a passive construction. That is the whole syntax of the passive: the thing acted on stands in the nominative, and the agent, when it is named at all, stands in the instrumental. Here it is named because it matters who taught them.

    *Desitā* has to agree with ***dhammā***, and it does: masculine nominative plural, as a participle used as an adjective would. That agreement checks the reading, for if *desitā* were not predicated of *dhammā*, it would not carry that ending. "These, monks, are the things taught by me from my own direct knowledge."

**Say it aloud.**

## Answers: Review — The Sentence and the Verse

**Sotānugata — met by ear.**

1. *Nisinno* is the odd one. *Tiṭṭhaṁ*, *caraṁ* and *sayāno* are present participles (the first two active, the third middle), and *nisinno* is a past participle. The difference of kind is a difference of aspect and not of time: standing, walking and lying down are postures being held, while sitting down is an act completed, and Pāli says "having sat" where English says "sitting". Render all four alike and nothing of the sense is lost, but the reason the fourth word looks unlike the other three is lost entirely.

    What all four have in common is that not one of them is the subject of anything. There is no finite verb in this half of the verse at all. All four are masculine nominative singulars, and they wait for a clause that has not arrived yet.

    It arrives in the other half: *Etaṁ satiṁ adhiṭṭheyya*, "he should undertake this mindfulness". The four participles are adverbial to that optative, and *yāvatā assa vitamiddho* is the further condition on it. So the whole verse is one clause with four alternative circumstances stacked in front of it, and the stacking costs four words. English cannot do it that way: "whether standing, walking, seated or lying down" needs a subordinating conjunction and a copula that Pāli simply does without, because a nominative participle agreeing with the subject already says "while being thus".

    That is the method of this whole stretch in its plainest form. A participle is a clause that has given up its verb, and what it gives up in exchange is the need for anything to join it on.

2. The three compounds are of three different classes, and the line is set for that reason. ***Tirokuṭṭesu*** is an *avyayībhāva*, a compound governed by an indeclinable, and it is inflected here as a locative plural instead of standing frozen in the usual *-aṁ*. That is useful to see: the class is defined by its first member and not by its ending. ***Sandhisiṅghāṭakesu*** is a *dvanda*, junctions and crossroads. ***Dvārabāhāsu*** is a genitive *tappurisa*, the posts of a door.

    All three are **locative plural**, and the job is the plainest the case has: **place where**. Three compounds, three classes, one case, and the case does what English would need a preposition for three times over, without ever being repeated.

    *Āgantvāna* is an absolutive, "having come", and the ordinary form is ***āgantvā***. The longer *-tvāna* is the same absolutive with a syllable more, and here it fills out a line of verse. At this point the absolutives chapter and the metre chapter have to be held together: a form that looks like a dialect variant or a scribal fullness is very often a metrical choice, and the way to find out is to scan the line and see whether the shorter form would fit. Here it would not.

3. Everything after *kassapo* is in the **nominative**, and the relation is **apposition**: each epithet renames the same man and depends on nothing. Apposition holds a verbless sentence together: *ayaṁ pūraṇo kassapo* is the subject and every word that follows is another way of saying it, so no verb is needed and none is supplied. The courtier is piling up names for one person, and the case shows that they all belong to him.

    The epithets sort into three kinds. **Past participles**: *ñāto*, *sādhusammato*, *cirapabbajito*, *addhagato*, *vayoanuppatto*, five of them, and every one of the last three is a compound with a participle as its second member, so it belongs to two chapters at once. **Possessive suffixes**: *saṅghī*, *gaṇī*, *yasassī*, *rattaññū*, words meaning "having a community", "having a following", "having renown", and the suffix is the word-formation chapter's business. **Compounds** proper: *gaṇācariyo*, *titthakaro*, and the compound halves of the participial epithets.

    ***Bahujanassa*** is the word to get right, and it is a genitive. It looks like a possessive, "of many people", and a reader will attach it to whatever noun is nearest. In fact it is attached to no noun: it is the agent of the past participle *sādhusammato*, "held in high regard **by** many people". The case syntax chapter treats that construction. Pāli uses a genitive where an instrumental would seem the obvious choice because a past participle is not quite a passive verb, and it takes the agent of a noun, not the agent of a verb.

    So one sentence of flattery needs four chapters and has no verb in it, and that is a fair description of what reading canonical prose is actually like.

**Vacasā paricitā — rehearsed aloud.**

4. *Jarāmaraṇaṁ* is a ***dvanda***, "old age and death", and *sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsā* is a *dvanda* too, of five members, "sorrow, lamentation, pain, dejection and distress". A *dvanda* makes the list one thing, as a string of separate words would not. *Jarāmaraṇaṁ* is singular and neuter: old age and death are a single condition with two names here, and the ending says so.

    *Jātipaccayā* is an **ablative singular**, and the job is **cause**: birth being the condition, this comes to be. Spelt out, the relation is *jātiyā paccayā*, "from the condition that is birth", and compounding it removes the second ending and joins the condition to what conditions it. Twelve links, each stated in two words, make the formula recitable, and this matters for more than its sound: the chain has to be held in the memory forwards and backwards, and the compound makes each link short enough to be a single hand-hold.

    Note also that *sambhavanti* is plural where *jarāmaraṇaṁ* is singular. The verb is agreeing with the five-member *dvanda*, not with the two-member one, which is the ordinary Pāli habit when a verb has two subjects of different number: it takes the nearer, or the larger, and here it is both.

5. "Not doing any evil, undertaking the wholesome, cleansing one's own mind: this is the teaching of the Buddhas." Read it aloud, holding the long vowels of *-pā-*, *-pa-dā* and *-sā-* and the closed syllables *-raṇaṁ*, *-sam-* and *-naṁ* to their full weight, and sounding the doubled consonants of *sabbapāpassa* as two each, not one. Note that *pariyodapanaṁ*, for all its length, has not a single double consonant in it: the third *pāda* is one whole word, nine syllables of it, and six of the nine are light — ⏑ — ⏑ ⏑ ⏑ — ⏑ ⏑ ×. Read aloud beside the heavy opening of the fourth, it is the most audible thing in the verse.

    ***Akaraṇaṁ*** is the negative prefix *a-* (a particle, not an *upasagga*, in the tradition's terms, and treated in Chapter 14) on the action-noun suffix *-ana*, built on the root *kar-* 'to do'. Literally 'not-doing', the same *-ana* type as *dassana* and *ṭhāna*. ***Sacittapariyodapanaṁ*** is a genitive *tappurisa*. Chapter 13 advises trying an unfamiliar *tappurisa* as a genitive first, and this one answers to it: *sacitta* 'one's own mind' stands to *pariyodapana* as *dukkha* stands to *samudaya* in *dukkhasamudayo*, 'the origin of suffering'. So: 'the cleansing **of** one's own mind', with the same *-ana* action-noun suffix on the final member as *akaraṇaṁ* carries.

    The last word's full form is *buddhānaṁ*, the genitive plural taught in Chapter 3 and displayed in Appendix A.1: it had to **keep** its case ending as far as the grammar goes, since nothing else in the sentence marks whose teaching this is, and it had to **lose** its niggahīta as far as the metre goes, since the Siloka's even-line cadence has no room in its final four syllables for the extra weight a closed *-naṁ* would carry. This verse closed a question in the second review of this book, chapters ago, as a plain sentence to be read and translated. It took the whole of Chapter 18 to say why its last four syllables scan the way they do, and the fact needed to say so had been in the declension table of this book's third chapter all along.

**Manasānupekkhitā — gone over in the mind.**

**Recall.**

6. A transitive verb's past participle is passive; an intransitive verb's is active. *Desita* 'taught' and *kata* 'done' describe what was done *to* something; *gata* 'gone' and *bhūta* 'been' describe what the subject itself did, and there is no patient for a passive reading to act on. English uses the same *-en/-ed* form for both ("a **broken** pot" is passive, "a **fallen** leaf" is active, not "a leaf that fell upon"), so the rule has to be stated and cannot be left to be noticed.

7. Ordinarily the absolutive's agent is the main verb's subject, and it stops being so the moment the main clause's own subject cannot plausibly have performed the prior action, typically because that subject is an abstract noun and the sentence reports a consequence, not a sequence. The commentators' name for it is ***asamānakattuka***, 'having a different agent', and Dhammapāla's gloss is sharper than any Western rendering: the construction marks two actions occurring *at the same time*, one the mark of the other, and so it can carry both a causal sense ('because he saw') and a temporal one at once.

8. ***Samuccaya*** (joining), ***vikappana*** (separating), ***paṭisedha*** (denying and forbidding), and ***pūraṇattha*** (filling). The fourth changes how a page is read, because it names a class of word (*kho*, *hi*, *vata*, *sudaṁ*, *khalu*) that carries no translatable meaning at all. Calling *kho* "emphatic" says what it feels like; calling it a filler says what it is for, and permits the only sound response to it: leave it untranslated and watch its effect on the movement of the sentence instead.

9. ***Ekapadatta***, 'the fact of being one word', and ***ekavibhattitā***, 'the fact of having one inflection': only the last member of a compound takes an ending. The second catches beginners out, because most compounds encode a case relation, and the ending that would state it is not written anywhere. *Dukkhakkhandha* means 'mass **of** suffering', but the genitive that says so has to be recovered, not read off the word.

10. It strengthens the first vowel of the whole word, not the vowel beside the suffix: *a* becomes *ā*, *i* becomes *e*, *u* becomes *o*. That fact decides where a dictionary entry sits: *nipuṇo* 'skilful' gives *nepuññaṁ* 'skill', and a reader looking for it under *ni-* will not find it. The word is filed under *ne-*, because the dictionary is alphabetised on the written form and the written form has moved.

11. The **genitive** and the **vocative**. The tradition's line is *na chaṭṭhīvihitattho kārakaṁ, yathā mantanaṁ*: "what is expressed by the sixth case is not a factor of action, nor is address." A genitive relates one noun to another and reaches the verb only at second hand, if at all; a vocative has no connection to the action at the moment it is uttered, since calling someone makes them available to be told to go or to eat, and only then can the action begin.

12. When a run of actions shares a subject, every verb but the last goes into the absolutive. It stops working the moment the prior action belongs to somebody else, for the absolutive carries no person, number or case of its own and so cannot say whose action it is. Pāli then reaches for the absolute construction, which has a subject of its own because the absolutive cannot supply one.

13. A relative pronoun takes its number and gender from the antecedent, and its case from its own clause. Number and gender are **agreement**: they say what the relative is pointing at. Case is **function**: it says what the relative does inside the clause it opens. The two halves answer different questions, so they can pull in different directions in the same short sentence and both still be correct.

14. A syllable with *e* or *o* counts heavy whether it is open or closed, because the two vowels are long by origin. Before a conjunct they are pronounced short (*upekkhā*'s *e* is shorter than *upekhā*'s), but the syllable is heavy either way, once for the vowel and once for the conjunct if there is one. There is no position in which *e* or *o* is light by rule, and every other vowel in the language differs: *a*, *i* and *u* are light in an open syllable and heavy only when closed, and *ā*, *ī* and *ū* are simply long and heavy throughout.

15. ***Kita***: suffixes added directly to a root. Participles, absolutives and infinitives are all *kita* formations, so the chapter on word formation can say that three chapters of this book have been teaching *kita* suffixes without using the name. What unites them is that each lets a single non-finite word carry an action or a state without inflecting for person at all (a participle agreeing like an adjective, an absolutive and an infinitive not inflecting even for that), and that is how a long Pāli sentence can run on one finite verb.

16. It is any compound of any of the preceding classes used as an adjective describing something outside itself, and no separate kind of compound at all. That is Collins's formula, and Bodhi's and Thānuttamo's independently. The suffix with the same structural function, turning a finished unit into a word that describes something else, is ***-ika*** (or its variant *-iya*) added to a whole compound, as in *anuloma* 'with the hair' giving *anulomika* 'in accordance with'. And it is treated in the **chapter on compounds**, not the chapter on word formation, because it operates on a finished compound and not on a bare stem. It is the only piece of suffix machinery this book files by what it attaches to, not by which chapter usually owns suffixes.

17. The genitive is outside the system because it "relates one noun to another and reaches the verb only at second hand, if at all": it is read against the noun beside it, not against the verb. The vocative is outside it because at the moment of address there is no action yet for it to be connected to. An absolute construction, by contrast, **is** read against a verb of its own, its own participle, and is named after its case for a related reason: only the case marks the construction off. Strip the case and nothing is left to say a locative absolute is a clause and not an ordinary phrase.

18. ***Resolution*** replaces one heavy syllable with two light ones, and it is common, particularly at the start of a line. ***Replacement*** replaces two light syllables with one heavy, and it is much rarer, occurring only after a word break. Both obey the same constraint in mirror image: resolution operates across the first two syllables of a word, and replacement across the first two syllables of a word standing right after a break, which shows that the two light syllables being replaced were themselves the opening of a word.

Say what the form is, and what decides it.

19. ***Disvā***: an absolutive, 'having seen', and the question is whose seeing it is. The main clause's subject is *āsavā*, 'the defilements', and defilements do not see with wisdom; the seer is the monk, present in the sentence only as *assa*. This is the *asamānakattuka* construction of item 7. The main clause's subject cannot plausibly have performed the action, and that forces the absolutive's agent outward, onto whoever is grammatically present only in an oblique case. Dhammapāla's commentary calls this reading causal, 'because of seeing', and not merely sequential.

20. ***Sati***: the locative singular of the present participle of *atthi*, 'there being', standing beside the plural noun *pādesu* and not agreeing with it in number. This is Chapter 16's one standing exception to ordinary participle agreement: *sati* is the workhorse of the whole absolute construction and is used in the singular "even when the corresponding locative noun is plural," as Bodhi puts it of the same word beside *saṅkhāresu*. Nothing settles it; a reader has to know the idiom, not derive it, and the Digital Pali Dictionary's two separate entries, one locative singular and one locative plural, record the same fact from the lexicon's side.

21. ***Santaṁ***: the accusative singular present participle of *atthi*, and the question is whether it is an ordinary participle agreeing with an accusative noun or an accusative absolute in its own right. Here *saṅkappeti* already has its object: the quoted thought, *'natthi paro loko' ti*. Since the verb's object slot is taken, *santaṁ yeva … paraṁ lokaṁ* cannot also be filling it, and the test Chapter 16 gives for this construction generally decides it: where the accusative could be the main verb's own object, it probably is; where it could not, it is absolute. Here it could not, so the reading is 'although the other world does exist, he forms the thought that there is no other world', the concessive sense the commentary's gloss of the parallel formula, *bhummatthe upayogavacanaṁ*, 'an accusative in the sense of the locative', supports.

22. ***Taṇhāya***: a feminine *ā*-stem ending that could be instrumental, ablative, dative, genitive or locative. Chapter 15's stated method settles it in two steps. First, the verb: *jāyati* 'is born' takes an ablative of source, as *tasati* takes a genitive and *saṁvattati* a dative. Second, the parallel: this line is the fifth in a run of five near-identical verses in the same chapter of the Dhammapada, and three of the other four carry an ending that can only be ablative: *piyato*, *pemato* and *kāmato*, all in *-to*. Framed by three unambiguous ablatives, the two ambiguous forms (*ratiyā* and *taṇhāya*) are read as ablative too.

23. ***Bhuttāvissa***: a **past active participle** in *-āvin*, genitive singular, standing in a genitive absolute. This is doubly exceptional: Wijesekera's stated rule restricts the absolute construction to a present participle or a past *passive* one, ruling out both the future passive participle and the past active one. Yet here a past active participle stands in the genitive, taking its own object (*bhojanaṁ*) and marking a state (having eaten) that belongs to someone other than *hoti*'s subject, *bhattasammado*, 'drowsiness after a meal'. The case is genitive, not instrumental or dative, because the participle has its own object and its own logical subject, as a genitive absolute does. It is rare (thirteen root-text occurrences), and Thānuttamo had to go looking for it, so it is stated here and not merely noted.

24. ***Yena*** has two different functions, and in each sentence what governs it decides which. In **SN 1.1 — S i 1**, *yena bhagavā tenupasaṅkami*, it is the instrumental of *ya(d)-* in the fixed idiom "*yena* X, *tena* Y", 'to where X is, there Y', and it is governed by a verb of motion, *upasaṅkami*. Chapter 15 files the whole idiom under the instrumental, not as a relative clause proper. In **AN 4.80 — A ii 82**, *yena mātugāmo … na kammantaṁ payojeti*, there is no verb of motion at all; *yena* follows the nouns *hetu* and *paccayo*, 'cause' and 'condition', and works as a **connective**, 'whereby, in that, such that': Wijesekera's term, and the function the empty accusative *yaṁ* has elsewhere. The test is whether a verb of motion governs it or it attaches a clause to a noun asking for a reason.

25. Every grammar in the collection but one treats *yato* and *yasmā* as interchangeable synonyms for 'because'. Warder alone calls *yasmā* "a rarely used synonym of *yato*", and the corpus shows he is right about the **canon** specifically: root-text *yato* stands 1,370 times against *yasmā*'s 374. But the ratio reverses, and reverses harder, outside it — commentaries 3,317 against 590, sub-commentaries 1,605 against 327. *Yasmā … tasmā* is the exegetes' connective; the canon, which argues less and narrates more, reaches for *yato*. Which a reader should expect depends on the layer of the text, and a grammar written mainly from commentarial Pāli will teach *yasmā* as ordinary and will be describing accurately the language it read.

26. ***Ye te*** is one unit, not two levels: a relative phrase, masculine nominative plural, 'those who are the Vajjian elders'. What makes the sentence hard is what is around it. There are two relative clauses here, one inside the other, and their answering words are of different kinds.

    The **outer** clause is opened by *yāvakīvañca*, 'and so long as', and it is answered by nothing at all. No *tāva* arrives; the main clause simply states what is to be expected, *vuddhiyeva … pāṭikaṅkhā*. The **inner** clause is *ye te vajjīnaṁ vajjimahallakā*, and it *is* answered, by the *te* that follows, accusative plural this time, the object of the four verbs *sakkarissanti garuṁ karissanti mānessanti pūjessanti*. So one pair is complete and the other is not, in the same sentence.

    A correlative can be answered by silence, and that decides it. Look only for *yāva … tāva* and you will hunt for the missing *tāva*, find the nominative *ye te* and the accusative *te* instead, and conclude the sentence is broken. It is not: the inner pair behaves as it should, and the outer one uses the language's freedom to leave the answering word out.

27. Both are the quotation particle *ti*, with two different functions, only one of which is quoting. The **first**, after *ruppati*, is ***hetu***, causal, and it is the first of the seven senses Aggavaṁsa distinguishes for *iti* over and above the ordinary quotation account: it attaches to the clause and makes it the reason for what follows, and it is answered by *tasmā*, 'therefore': 'because it is deformed'. The **second**, after *rūpaṁ*, is the ordinary naming sense that closes an act of calling something something: 'it is therefore called "form"'. The *tasmā* directly after the first tells them apart: only a causal *iti*-clause is answered by a 'therefore', and the ordinary quotation-naming use never is.

Take the compound or the word apart.

28. ***Sokaparidevadukkhadomanassupāyāsā*** is five members (*soka* 'sorrow', *parideva* 'lamentation', *dukkha* 'pain', *domanassa* 'dejection', *upāyāsa* 'distress'), of which only the last, *-upāyāsā*, is inflected: masculine nominative plural, agreeing with *sambhavanti* alongside *jarāmaraṇaṁ*. Four of the five members cannot be found independently in that form in the root texts; when they stand free the canon has *soko*, *paridevo*, *domanassaṁ*, *upāyāso*. *Dukkha* is the exception, and an instructive one: it stands free three hundred times, because it is an adjective and a neuter noun as well as a member of this compound, and its bare stem is therefore a real word in its own right. The other four are stems used inside a compound and nowhere else. *Jātipaccayā* is not part of the compound at all; it is the **ablative of cause** Chapter 15 gives for the whole formula of dependent origination, *paccayā* 'as condition'. The same sentence is quoted in both the compounds chapter and the case-syntax chapter, for a different purpose in each: one reads the long compound, the other reads the ablative in front of it.

29. Read from the right, as the compounds chapter's method requires. ***Ñāṇāya*** is the outer layer: *pubbenivāsānussati* + *ñāṇa*, a **genitive *tappurisa***, 'knowledge **of** recollection', in the dative of purpose (*cittaṁ abhininnāmeti*, 'he directs the mind towards'). ***Pubbenivāsānussati*** is the middle layer: *pubbenivāsa* + *anussati*, another genitive *tappurisa*, 'recollection **of** the former abode'. ***Pubbenivāsa*** is the innermost layer, and the one to pause on: *pubbe* + *nivāsa*, an ***alutta*** compound. The first member **keeps** the locative ending it would have had as a separate word, *pubbe* 'in the past', and does not reduce to the bare stem *pubba-*. That is Chapter 13's exception to the rule that only the last member of a compound is inflected, sitting on top of the ordinary locative sense (dwelling *in* the past) that Chapter 15 gives that case.

30. Both are masculine nominative singular in *-vā* and both decline as the *(m/v)ant*-stems of Appendix A.1, but they are built two different ways. ***Vusitavā*** is Chapter 10's **past active participle**: *-vant* added to an **already-formed past participle**, *vusita* 'lived'. ***Sīlavā*** is Chapter 14's ordinary **possessive**: *-vant* added directly to a **noun**, *sīla* 'virtue'. The base decides which is which: is the element in front of *-vant* itself a verbal past-participle shape (a root already carrying *-ta* or *-na*, an event already completed) or a plain noun naming a quality? *Vusita* is a *pp.*; *sīla* is not.

31. Both end in nominative *-iko*, and the *-ika* has a different function in each. ***Ehipassiko*** is Chapter 13's closing formation: *-ika* added to a **finished compound** (here an unusual one, two second-person imperatives, *ehi* 'come!' plus *passa* 'see!') to make it an adjective, 'a come-and-see thing'. That whole operation is filed in the **chapter on compounds**, not the chapter on word formation, on the explicit ground that it works on a compound already assembled and not on a bare stem. ***Dhammiko*** is Chapter 14's ordinary *taddhita* suffix, *-ika* attached directly to the noun *dhamma*, in one of the sixteen senses Thānuttamo catalogues for it after Kaccāyana, here 'righteous'. So the same three letters mean two different operations depending on what stands in front of them, and the more surprising of the two locations is where the compound-final use is taught.

32. Four operations, in order. **(1)** A compound base, Chapter 13's material: *bahu* 'much' + *suta* 'heard', the same shape as *bahussuta* 'much-learned'. **(2)** Chapter 14's *taddhita* suffix *-ya*, added to that compound base to make an abstract noun. **(3)** Chapter 14's front-*vuddhi* rule, strengthening the **first** vowel of the whole word, not the vowel next to the suffix: *ba-* becomes *bā-*. **(4)** Chapter 14's assimilation rule for *-ya*: the *-ty-* produced where the suffix meets the base's final *-t* (from *suta*) assimilates to *-cc-*. The result, *bāhusaccaṁ*, carries nothing of *bahussuta* recognisable on its surface, and the word-formation chapter uses this example to make that point.

**Diṭṭhiyā suppaṭividdhā — penetrated by view.**

**Read the sentence.**

33. *So kho ahaṁ* I, that one (m. nom. sg.) · *akāmakānaṁ* unwilling (m. gen. pl.) · *mātāpitūnaṁ* mother and father (m. gen. pl.) · *assumukhānaṁ* with tear-stained faces (m. gen. pl.) · *rudantānaṁ* weeping (m. gen. pl., pres. part.) · *kesamassuṁ* hair and beard (nt. acc. sg.) · *ohāretvā* having shaved off · *kāsāyāni vatthāni* ochre robes (nt. acc. pl.) · *acchādetvā* having put on · *agārasmā* from home (nt. abl. sg.) · *anagāriyaṁ* to homelessness (nt. acc. sg.) · *pabbajiṁ* I went forth (1 sg. aor.). "And I, though my mother and father were unwilling and were weeping with tear-stained faces, shaved off my hair and beard, put on ochre robes, and went forth from home into homelessness."

    *Ohāretvā* and *acchādetvā* are ordinary **absolutives**, sharing their subject with *pabbajiṁ*: the going-forth is what "I" did, in three stages, one finite verb at the end. The four genitives (*akāmakānaṁ mātāpitūnaṁ assumukhānaṁ rudantānaṁ*) are a **genitive absolute**: they report the parents' weeping, an action belonging to somebody else entirely, running at the same time as the going-forth, not before it. It could not have been an absolutive, because an absolutive has no case, gender or person of its own to say whose action it reports. Once the action belongs to someone other than the main verb's subject, the absolutive cannot mark it, and Pāli reaches for the case-substitution rule instead. This one sentence demonstrates Chapter 16's whole argument, with every device in the chapter at once.

34. *Evaṁ passaṁ* thus seeing (m. nom. sg., pres. part.) · *bhikkhave* monks (voc. pl.) · *sutavā ariyasāvako* a learned noble disciple (m. nom. sg.) · *rūpasmiṁ* regarding form (nt. loc. sg.) · *pi* also · *nibbindati* becomes disenchanted · *vedanāya* regarding feeling (f. loc. sg.) · *saññāya* regarding perception (f. loc. sg.) · *saṅkhāresu* regarding volitional formations (m. loc. pl.) · *viññāṇasmiṁ* regarding consciousness (nt. loc. sg.). "Seeing thus, monks, a learned noble disciple becomes disenchanted with form, with feeling, with perception, with volitional formations, with consciousness."

    *Rūpasmiṁ*, *saṅkhāresu* and *viññāṇasmiṁ* carry endings that can be nothing but locative. *Vedanāya* and *saññāya* are feminine *ā*-stems whose ending could be instrumental, ablative, dative, genitive or locative. Chapter 15's two-step method settles them, in order. Look at the verb first: *nibbindati* takes a locative of what one is disenchanted with. Then look for the parallel: the two ambiguous forms stand in a series between three unambiguous locatives. The verb alone would have been enough; the parallel confirms it.

35. *Seyyathāpi* just as · *purisassa* of a man (m. gen. sg.) · *agāraṁ* a house (nt. nom. sg.) · *ekādasadvāraṁ* with eleven doors (nt. nom. sg., *bahubbīhi*) · *so* he (m. nom. sg.) · *tasmiṁ agāre āditte* that house being ablaze (nt. loc. sg., past part.) · *ekamekena dvārena* by any one door (nt. ins. sg.) · *sakkuṇeyya* would be able (3 sg. opt.) · *attānaṁ sotthiṁ kātuṁ* to make himself safe. "Just as, sir, if a man had a house with eleven doors, and that house were ablaze, he would be able to get himself to safety by any one of the doors."

    Three constructions. **The simile frame**, *seyyathāpi* opening the comparison (Chapter 16, "The simile"). A locative absolute carrying a condition with no particle at all, *agāre āditte*, 'if that house were ablaze' (Chapter 16, the locative absolute's hypothetical sense, the past participle *āditte* marking the state where no word means 'if'). **An infinitive of purpose**, *sotthiṁ kātuṁ*, 'to make safe', sharing its agent with the main verb, formed in Chapter 11 and put to use in Chapter 16, so the chapter on clause syntax can call all three of these its own. Chapter 16 calls this sentence "a good measure of how much of the grammar a reader has to have available to follow one ordinary sentence of the canon at reading speed", and this review takes it at its word.

36. First sentence: *no cetaṁ … abhavissa ajātaṁ abhūtaṁ akataṁ asaṅkhataṁ* 'if there were no unborn, unbecome, unmade, unconditioned' (*abhavissa*, 3 sg. **conditional**) · *nayidha … nissaraṇaṁ paññāyetha* 'no escape … would be discerned' (*paññāyetha*, 3 sg. mid. **optative**, passive stem). Second sentence: *yasmā ca kho … atthi ajātaṁ …* 'but because there is an unborn …' (**present indicative**) · *tasmā … nissaraṇaṁ paññāyati* 'therefore … is discerned' (**present indicative**).

    The first sentence is an **unreal condition**: the false premise goes into the conditional, its consequence into the optative, Chapter 16's third type. The second sentence is two plain indicatives. The shift changes the function of *yasmā*: in the first sentence's shape it would answer to *tasmā* as an ordinary 'because … therefore' stating a real reason; here it opens the sentence that **corrects** the counterfactual just entertained, restoring the true state of affairs: 'but because [in fact] there is an unborn …, therefore [in fact] an escape is discerned'. The whole doctrinal weight of the passage, that an escape from the conditioned is possible because the unconditioned exists, rests on this contrast of mood between the two sentences alone.

37. *Ito so, bhikkhave, ekanavutikappe yaṁ vipassī bhagavā … loke udapādi* 'ninety-one eons ago, monks, the Blessed One Vipassī … arose in the world'.

    Buddhaghosa resolves the empty relative instead of leaving it: *Tattha yaṁ vipassīti yasmiṁ kappe vipassī*, "'*yaṁ vipassī*' means 'in which eon' Vipassī [arose]", assigning *yaṁ* the **locative** sense (his *bhummatthe*, the fourth of four uses he distinguishes by case), even though on its face the word looks accusative and appears to modify nothing at all. For a reader to accept this as a method rather than a one-off convenience, the empty relative would always have to carry a case, recoverable from the sentence, and never be case-less. Buddhaghosa's four-way classification makes that claim, each use cited to a real passage (nominative, accusative, instrumental, locative). It is a stronger and more demanding position than the shrug that such relatives merely "do not pose a problem" for translation; it says that recovering the case *is* what reading the sentence consists of.

38. *Yaṁ kiñci samudayadhammaṁ sabbaṁ taṁ nirodhadhammaṁ.* "Whatever is of the nature to arise, all that is of the nature to cease."

    There is no finite verb; *hoti* is understood, and the two compounds (*samudayadhammaṁ*, *nirodhadhammaṁ*) stand as predicate to their subjects with nothing supplying tense. Chapter 16 calls this a **nominal sentence**, one of the four kinds of predicate Duroiselle lists, and here concord holds, since *taṁ* and *nirodhadhammaṁ* are both neuter singular in agreement, not two nouns merely placed in apposition. In place of a *yo … so* pair, the sentence is held together by Chapter 17's device for generalising a relative clause by adding an indefinite: *yaṁ kiñci* 'whatever' opens a class with no member named, and *sabbaṁ taṁ* 'all that' closes it and states what holds of every member. Every word in the sentence is either a pronoun or a compound of *dhamma*, and the whole of its content lies in the relation between the two halves, so the chapter on relative clauses calls it the canon's shortest statement of insight and a pure specimen of the construction at once.

**Scan the line.**

39. The full, un-elided word is ***buddhānaṁ***, the **genitive plural** of the *-a* stem masculine noun *buddha*, the *-ānaṁ* ending given for every masculine *-a* stem in Chapter 3's declension and displayed across Appendix A.1. Dropping the niggahīta, which is Chapter 2's material generally, leaves the syllable *-na* light where *-naṁ* would have closed it and made it heavy; the Siloka's even-line cadence needs ⏑—⏑× and no other pattern, so this one dropped nasal keeps the most-quoted verse of the Dhammapada inside the metre it is written in.

40. The third *pāda*, *Ākaṅkhamānā sotthānaṁ*, scans ——⏑—¦———×, which is the ***mavipulā***: syllables two to four run —⏑—, and the cadence is four heavy syllables running straight through where the *pathyā* would require ⏑——. It even carries the *mavipulā*'s normal word break after the fifth syllable, at *Ākaṅkhamānā | sotthānaṁ*. Someone who had learnt only the *pathyā* would take the opening line of the Maṅgalasutta, one of the best-known suttas in the canon, for a corrupt text.

41. The first *pāda*, *Tapo ca brahmacariyañca*, needs two accommodations at once: *br-* fails to make position, so the syllable in front of it (*ca*) stays light; and the sarabhatti vowel of *cariya* is dropped, so it scans as the two syllables *car-ya*, not three. Together these turn what a literal reading gives as nine syllables in no recognisable shape into the regular eight-syllable Siloka opening ⏑—⏑—¦⏑——×. The second *pāda*, *ariyasaccāna' dassanaṁ*, needs two more, and each does separate work. *Ariyasaccānaṁ* has a sarabhatti vowel of its own, as *cariya* does, so it scans *ar-ya-sac-cā-* and not *a-ri-ya-sac-cā-*; keep it and the line has nine syllables where eight are wanted. And the niggahīta of *ariyasaccānaṁ* is dropped, as in Dhp 183 above; keep it and the line has its eight syllables but closes ——⏑× where the Siloka's even line requires ⏑—⏑×. Neither accommodation can replace the other, so they are named separately. That makes four separate departures from the written text in two lines, in an ordinary verse, not a difficult one.

42. The Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana's reading, *Chaccābhiṭhānāni abhabba kātuṁ*, needs **no licence at all**: *abhabba* has a short final *-a*, light by its own nature, and the line scans ——⏑—¦—⏑⏑¦—⏑—× as a regular Tuṭṭhubha. Ānandajoti's text prints *abhabbŏ* instead, which requires the final *-o* to be read light against its own weight: a true licence, and one that changes the line's opening from heavy to light in that position. This is the same question the book has raised twice before: once in Chapter 1, on *aneḷagalāya*/*anelagalāya*, where a variant recorded only in the apparatus turned out to be the reading the corpus carries; and once in Chapter 16, on *saṁvaṭṭamāno*/*saṁvaṭṭamāne*, where the nominative absolute rested on a single word in a single edition against two others. In all three cases, a claim about the language turned out on inspection to be a claim about which edition one had opened.

43. Line a, *Satto guhāyaṁ bahunābhichanno*, has the normal break, —⏑⏑ (opening ——⏑—, break —⏑⏑, cadence —⏑—×). Line b, *Tiṭṭhaṁ naro mohanasmiṁ pagāḷho*, has the break —⏑— instead, one of the documented alternatives to the ordinary —⏑⏑, and otherwise identical to line a in every other position. Hold the three-part pattern (opening, break, cadence) in mind and this is a variation within the metre, not an error; a reader scanning by *gaṇas* alone would have no ready name for what has changed.

44. Line a, *Appaṁ vata jīvitaṁ idaṁ*, has nine syllables (—— ⏑⏑ ¦ —⏑—⏑×) totalling fourteen measures: 2+2+1+1 for the opening, then 2+1+2+1+2 for the cadence. Line b, *Oraṁ vassasatāpi miyyati*, has ten syllables and totals sixteen measures by the same arithmetic: 2+2+2+1+1 for the opening, then the identical cadence. The cadences are the same eight measures in both; the whole difference sits in the opening, which is two measures heavier in the even line. So the two lines are not equal to each other, and the metre never asked them to be. The Vetālīya fixes a total per position, fourteen measures in every odd line and sixteen in every even one, and because it counts *mattā*, not syllables, a poet can reach that total by any arrangement of lights and heavies they please. So line a can spend nine syllables and line b ten while both fit the metre. The last piece of the arithmetic is the *pādantagaru* rule: the even line's final syllable is light and is counted two anyway, without which every even line in every measure metre would come out one measure short.

**Read at length.**

45. *Imasmiñca pana veyyākaraṇasmiṁ bhaññamāne āyasmato koṇḍaññassa virajaṁ vītamalaṁ dhammacakkhuṁ udapādi.* "And while this exposition was being spoken, the dust-free, stainless eye of the Dhamma arose in the venerable Koṇḍañña." *Yaṁ kiñci samudayadhammaṁ sabbaṁ taṁ nirodhadhammaṁ.* "Whatever is of the nature to arise, all that is of the nature to cease." *Pavattite ca pana bhagavatā dhammacakke bhummā devā saddamanussāvesuṁ.* "And when the wheel of Dhamma had been set rolling by the Blessed One, the earth-dwelling gods raised a cry."

    The first sentence's construction is a **locative absolute**, and its participle is present, not past: *bhaññamāne*, 'being spoken', not *bhaṇite*, 'having been spoken'. Chapter 16's rule for the tense of the participle in this construction is exact: a past participle where the subordinate action precedes the main one, a present participle where the two run together. So Koṇḍañña's insight is stated as arising **while** the discourse was still being delivered, not after it closed.

    The second sentence has no finite verb (the predicate is a bare compound, *nirodhadhammaṁ*) and no *yo … so* pair. The generalising is done by Chapter 17's device of adding an indefinite to the relative: *yaṁ kiñci* 'whatever', closed by *sabbaṁ taṁ* 'all that', which opens a class with no member named and then says what holds of every member of it.

    The third sentence's case would be an ordinary locative of place, 'in the wheel of Dhamma', if the sentence stopped at *dhammacakke*. What turns it into something else is *pavattite … bhagavatā*: a past participle agreeing with the locative noun, and an **instrumental** naming who did the setting-rolling. This is Chapter 16's account of the passive locative absolute: the grammatical subject stays in the locative, and the *logical* subject, the agent, is added in the instrumental, which is very often the first sign that the locative is absolute and not merely local. The agent named is *bhagavatā*, the Blessed One: it is his own turning of the wheel, reported in the case that names an instrument, not the case that names a place.

**Reaching back.**

46. The compound-seam rule, from Chapter 13: the ordinary rules of sandhi that would flatten a consonant cluster are "partly annulled at the seam of compounds in order not to obscure the initial sound of the posterior member", Oberlies's own words for it. *Duccaritaṁ* keeps its *c*, *duttaro* its *t*, *uggacchati* its *g*. Had the assimilation rules of Chapter 2 applied without restraint at these seams, none of the second members could be recognised, let alone looked up. The rule solves the reader's problem: the language protects the beginning of the second member so that it stays findable, the very difficulty a compound poses in every other respect.

47. The suffix is ***-to***, marking an ablative sense ('by birth', 'by name', 'by clan'), and it is introduced in **Chapter 3**, on the nouns. Chapter 15 reaches back to it for the point-of-view ablative: *jātitopi anussarati, nāmatopi anussarati, gottatopi anussarati* is Kaccāyana's *-to* construction, used as Chapter 3 taught it. The enclitic *pi* welded onto each word has removed the bare form from a plain search: every occurrence in the passage is printed *gottatopi*, not *gottato*, so a search for the citation form alone will find it apparently unattested: the same caution Chapter 12 states generally about enclitics that are almost never printed as separate words.

48. The stem is ***eta(d)-***, and the source is **Oberlies**, whose §42(3) has the two stems "inflect in the same way". That lets Chapter 17 give a paradigm for the relative pronoun at all, since Appendix A carries no *ya-* table of its own and the chapter's table is therefore checked against the corpus instead of the appendix. The chain starts in **Chapter 5**, on the pronouns, and it runs one link further than the question implies: *eta(d)-* has no table of its own there either, because it "declines exactly as *ta(d)* does" and needs none. So the paradigm printed in Chapter 5 is the distal ***ta(d)-***'s, and the relative pronoun reaches it at two removes: *ya(d)-* like *eta(d)-*, *eta(d)-* like *ta(d)-*. Know the distal paradigm cold and you have most of the relative pronoun's as well.

49. Chapter 4 gives *sādhukaṁ* 'well', *niccaṁ* 'constantly' and *ciraṁ* 'for a long time': the accusative singular of an adjective used adverbially. Chapter 12 gives *tikkhattuṁ* 'three times' among its own derived-by-suffix adverbs, and then explicitly returns to the promise: "the adverbial accusative was introduced in the chapter on gender and adjectives, which promised that this one would return to it; it has." On top of the single accusative case Chapter 12 builds the larger table of case-marked adverbs across all six oblique cases (accusative for manner and extent, instrumental for manner and means, dative for purpose, ablative for cause and position, genitive for time elapsed, locative for place and time), of which the Chapter 4 accusative turns out to be only the first row.

50. R3 called the conditional tense "the rarest finite form in the language", and in Chapter 16 it finally gets its one regular job: it is "the one place in the language where the conditional tense … has a settled job", carrying the false premise of an unreal condition. The standing form of that job is *abhavissa*, 'were to be', the form worked in Ud 8.3 above (item 36), where the false premise that nothing unconditioned exists is stated in the conditional and then answered, *yasmā … tasmā*, by the true state of affairs in the plain indicative.

**Put it back into Pāli.**

51. ***Tirokuṭṭesu***, an *avyayībhāva* inflected as a locative plural. ***Gharāvāso***, a locative *tappurisa*, "dwelling in a house". ***Rajopatho***, a *kammadhāraya*, "a path that is dust".

    The one you could not have built from its members is the third. Given *raja* and *patha*, the form a reader would write is *rajapatho*, and the canon does not have it.

    *Rajapatho* has 0 root-text occurrences and *rajopatho* 4. But it is not an oddity, and to call it one would be the easy answer to the wrong question. The first member is an *-as* stem, DPD's *rajas* 'dust, dirt', of the same declension as *manas* and *cetas*, and an *-as* stem regularly gives *-o-* inside a compound. Chapter 13's table of what a stem does in composition names this word, *rajas* → *rajo-* in *rajopatho*, beside *vacas* → *vacī-*, the same rule refusing to be tidy.

    So what you could not have supplied is the declension, not the compound. The question hands you *raja*, and *raja* is the adjective; the noun behind it is *rajas*, and the members as given do not show it. The classes show how the two words stand to each other and are silent about the form either takes.

    That is the cost of a compound to a reader. It gives the reciter a great deal: a five-member *dvanda* is one word to hold instead of five, one ending instead of five, and the whole of dependent origination becomes twelve short phrases instead of a paragraph. The cost is that the joins are invisible. A compound gives no signal about which class it is, which relation its members stand in, or what form a member takes inside it, and resolving one by reversing the rules gives *rajapatho*, *abbhokāso* split into *abbha* and *okāsa*, and a plausible analysis of a word that never had one. The classes show what to look for, the declension the form to look for it in, and the dictionary what is there.
